The Full-Stack Social Platform Masterclass

From absolute beginner to professional full-stack developer — by building a real, premium social-discovery app with Next.js, TypeScript, Tailwind, Supabase, and Vercel.

36 chapters · 8 sections · beginner → production · bubblegum pink Gen-Z aesthetic

Build Viby

Build Viby is a project-based, beginner-to-professional full-stack course that teaches modern web and product engineering by actually building something real. Instead of disconnected tutorials, you ship Viby — a premium, mobile-first social-discovery app — one section at a time. By the final chapter you won't just understand Next.js, TypeScript, Tailwind, and Supabase; you'll have engineered, designed, secured, and deployed a production-grade social platform with your own hands, and you'll think like the product builder behind it.

Who this book is for

  • Curious beginners who can write a little code and want a single, coherent path from "Hello World" to shipping a real app.
  • Frontend developers ready to go full-stack and own the backend, database, and realtime layers.
  • Bootcamp grads and self-taught engineers who want professional-grade patterns, not toy examples.
  • Founders and product-minded builders who want to turn an idea into a deployed, scalable MVP.

What you will be able to build

  • A real, working social platform — profiles, discovery, connections, chat, and feeds.
  • Complete full-stack applications, frontend to database, with clean architecture.
  • Responsive, modern UIs that feel native on mobile and polished on desktop.
  • Realtime systems — live chat, presence, notifications, and instant updates.
  • Scalable production apps that are secure, observable, and ready for real users.

The stack you will master

  • Next.js 16 — App Router and React 19 (Server Components, Server Actions, streaming).
  • TypeScript — end-to-end type safety from the database to the UI.
  • Tailwind CSS v4 — CSS-first theming with @theme design tokens (no tailwind.config.js).
  • shadcn-style UI primitives — composable, accessible, fully-owned components.
  • Framer Motion — fluid micro-interactions and premium motion design.
  • Zustand — lightweight client state for demo / in-memory mode.
  • Supabase — Auth, PostgreSQL, Storage, and Realtime via @supabase/ssr.
  • Vercel — zero-config, edge-ready production deployment.

Brand identity: bubblegum pink #FF6FB5 on clean white, mobile-first, with a premium Gen-Z social aesthetic woven through every screen and component.

How to use this book

This is a project-based book — you learn by building, not by reading alone.

  • Code along. Type the code yourself; don't just skim it. Muscle memory matters.
  • Do the exercises and mini-projects. Each chapter ends with hands-on work that locks the concept in.
  • Build Viby section by section. Every chapter adds a real piece of the app, so progress compounds.
  • Choose your mode. Run in demo mode (no keys, in-memory state with Zustand) to learn fast and offline, or connected mode (real Supabase Auth, database, storage, and realtime) to build the production app for real. The book guides both paths.

How the chapters are structured

Every chapter follows the same rhythm so you always know where you are:

  • Theory — the core concept explained clearly, from first principles.
  • In practice — applying it to real Viby code.
  • Diagram — a visual model of how the pieces fit together.
  • Best practices — what professionals do, and the traps they avoid.
  • Exercises — focused reps to make the concept stick.
  • Mini-project — a self-contained build that proves you've got it.
  • Takeaways — the essentials to carry into the next chapter.

Ship something real. Let's build Viby.

Chapter 1 — How the Web Works

Every app you'll ever build — including Viby — is just two computers having a polite, very fast conversation. Learn that conversation, and the whole web stops being magic.

What you'll learn

  • What "the internet" actually is, and how it's different from "the web"
  • The difference between a client and a server, and why both matter
  • How IP addresses and DNS turn viby.app into a real machine you can reach
  • The anatomy of an HTTP request and response, including headers and bodies
  • What status codes mean (200, 301, 404, 500…) and how to read them
  • What a browser actually does when you press Enter
  • The high-level split between frontend and backend, and where Viby's pieces live

Prerequisites

This is the very first chapter, so you need nothing but curiosity and a computer with a browser. We'll set up your tools (Node.js, the editor, the Viby starter) in Chapter 2 — Your Developer Toolkit, and you'll write your first real Next.js page in Chapter 3. For now, just read, picture the analogies, and try the exercises in your browser.

Theory

The internet vs. the web

People say "the internet" and "the web" as if they're the same thing. They're not.

The internet is the roads — a global network of cables, fiber, routers, and Wi-Fi that lets any two computers send data to each other. The web is one kind of traffic that drives on those roads: web pages, apps, and APIs that speak a language called HTTP. Email, video calls, and online games also use the internet, but they're different vehicles.

When you build Viby, you're building for the web — but it rides on the internet underneath.

Clients and servers: who asks, who answers

Almost everything on the web is a conversation between two roles:

  • A client asks for something. Your phone's browser opening Viby is a client.
  • A server answers. A computer in a data center that holds Viby's pages and data is a server.

Analogy: think of a restaurant. You (the client) read the menu and ask the waiter for a dish. The kitchen (the server) prepares it and sends it back. You don't walk into the kitchen — you make a polite request, and you get a response. The waiter shuttling between you is the network.

A server isn't a magic super-computer. It's just a normal computer running a program that waits for requests and knows how to reply. The same machine can be a client in one moment and a server in another.

IP addresses and DNS: finding the right machine

The internet has billions of devices. How does your request find the one server that hosts Viby?

Every device on the internet has an IP address — a numeric label like 93.184.216.34 (IPv4) or a longer 2606:2800:220:1:248:1893:25c8:1946 (IPv6). It's the street address of a computer.

But nobody wants to type numbers. So we use friendly names like viby.app. The system that translates names into IP addresses is DNS (Domain Name System).

Analogy: DNS is the phone book (or your phone's contacts). You type a person's name; it looks up the actual number to dial. When you visit viby.app, your computer quietly asks DNS, "What's the IP address for this name?" DNS replies with a number, and then your request can travel to the right machine.

sequenceDiagram
    participant You as Browser (client)
    participant DNS as DNS resolver
    participant Server as Viby server

    You->>DNS: What's the IP for viby.app?
    DNS-->>You: It's 93.184.216.34
    You->>Server: GET / (HTTP request to that IP)
    Server-->>You: 200 OK + HTML page

HTTP: the language of requests and responses

Once your browser knows the server's address, it speaks HTTP (HyperText Transfer Protocol). HTTP is just a strict format for "I'd like this" and "here you go."

Every request has:

  • A method — the verb, what you want to do:
  • GET — fetch something (load a profile page)
  • POST — create something (sign up a new user)
  • PUT / PATCH — update something (edit your bio)
  • DELETE — remove something (delete a post)
  • A path — which thing: /discover, /profile/maya, /api/posts
  • Headers — extra info about the request (who you are, what format you accept, your auth token)
  • An optional body — the data you're sending (the JSON for a new Viby post)

Every response has:

  • A status code — a 3-digit summary of how it went (more below)
  • Headers — info about the response (content type, caching rules, cookies)
  • A body — the actual content (HTML, JSON, an image)

Analogy: A request is an addressed envelope. The method is what you're asking the recipient to do, the path is the apartment number, the headers are notes on the envelope ("fragile," "I speak English"), and the body is the letter inside.

Status codes: how the server says how it went

Status codes are grouped by their first digit:

  • 2xx — success. 200 OK (here's your page), 201 Created (your post was saved).
  • 3xx — redirect. 301 Moved Permanently, 302 Found — "go look over there instead." Viby uses these to send signed-out users to the login page.
  • 4xx — you made a mistake. 400 Bad Request, 401 Unauthorized (you're not logged in), 403 Forbidden (logged in, but not allowed), 404 Not Found (no such page).
  • 5xx — the server made a mistake. 500 Internal Server Error, 503 Service Unavailable.

A quick way to remember: 4xx is on you, 5xx is on the server.

What a browser actually does

When you type viby.app and hit Enter, your browser:

  1. Resolves DNS — turns the name into an IP address.
  2. Opens a connection — and (for HTTPS) sets up encryption so nobody can read your data.
  3. Sends an HTTP request — GET / to that IP.
  4. Receives the response — usually an HTML document.
  5. Parses the HTML — builds an internal tree of the page (the DOM).
  6. Fetches more resources — CSS, JavaScript, images, fonts referenced by the HTML, each its own request.
  7. Renders — paints pixels on the screen, then runs the JavaScript that makes the page interactive.

That whole dance often happens in under a second.

Frontend vs. backend

  • The frontend is everything that runs in the browser — the HTML you see, the Tailwind styles, the React components, the animations. In Viby, this is the pink Discover feed, the profile cards, the buttons.
  • The backend is everything that runs on the server — checking passwords, saving posts to the database, deciding who's allowed to see what. In Viby, this is Supabase (auth, database, storage) plus the server-side code in Next.js.

The beautiful part of our stack: Next.js lets you write both in one project, in the same language (TypeScript). Some components render on the server (close to the data), others run in the browser (close to the user).

In practice

Let's make this real with the Viby stack. Don't worry about installing anything yet — read the code and notice the shape of request and response.

A Server Component fetching data (runs on the backend)

In Next.js 16's App Router, components are Server Components by default. This page runs on the server, talks to Supabase, and sends finished HTML to the browser — the database password never reaches the client.

// app/discover/page.tsx
import { createServerClient } from "@supabase/ssr";
import { cookies } from "next/headers";

export default async function DiscoverPage() {
  // In Next.js 16, cookies() is async — await it.
  const cookieStore = await cookies();

  const supabase = createServerClient(
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL!,
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY!,
    {
      cookies: {
        getAll() {
          return cookieStore.getAll();
        },
        setAll() {
          // Read-only in a Server Component; writes happen in proxy.ts.
        },
      },
    }
  );

  // This is an HTTP request to Supabase, made from the server.
  const { data: people } = await supabase
    .from("profiles")
    .select("username, hobby")
    .limit(10);

  return (
    <main className="mx-auto max-w-md p-4">
      <h1 className="text-2xl font-bold text-brand">Discover your people</h1>
      <ul className="mt-4 space-y-2">
        {people?.map((p) => (
          <li key={p.username} className="rounded-xl bg-white p-3 shadow-sm">
            <span className="font-semibold">@{p.username}</span> loves {p.hobby}
          </li>
        ))}
      </ul>
    </main>
  );
}

Reading an async route param

A dynamic route like /profile/[username] receives its params as a Promise in Next.js 16. Await them:

// app/profile/[username]/page.tsx
export default async function ProfilePage({
  params,
}: {
  params: Promise<{ username: string }>;
}) {
  const { username } = await params; // the path segment becomes data
  return <h1 className="text-brand">@{username}</h1>;
}

Tailwind v4 brand tokens (the frontend's look)

No tailwind.config.js. In Tailwind CSS v4 you define design tokens right in your CSS with @theme. That text-brand class above comes from here:

/* app/globals.css */
@import "tailwindcss";

@theme {
  --color-brand: #ff6fb5; /* Viby bubblegum pink */
  --color-brand-ink: #1a1a1a;
}

body {
  background: white;
  color: var(--color-brand-ink);
}

A Client Component that makes a request on click

"use client" opts a component into running in the browser, where it can use state, gestures, and animation. Here, a button sends an HTTP POST.

// app/discover/WaveButton.tsx
"use client";

import { useState } from "react";
import { motion } from "framer-motion";

export function WaveButton({ username }: { username: string }) {
  const [sent, setSent] = useState(false);

  async function sendWave() {
    // A real HTTP request leaving the browser:
    const res = await fetch("/api/wave", {
      method: "POST",
      headers: { "Content-Type": "application/json" },
      body: JSON.stringify({ to: username }),
    });
    if (res.ok) setSent(true); // res.ok is true for any 2xx status
  }

  return (
    <motion.button
      whileTap={{ scale: 0.92 }}
      onClick={sendWave}
      className="rounded-full bg-brand px-4 py-2 font-semibold text-white"
    >
      {sent ? "Waved 👋" : `Wave at @${username}`}
    </motion.button>
  );
}

Notice res.ok — that's the browser reading the status code for us. It's true for 200–299 and false for 4xx/5xx.

Architecture / diagram

Here's the full round trip when a signed-in user opens Viby's Discover feed:

  ┌────────────┐        1. DNS: viby.app -> 93.184.216.34
  │  BROWSER   │  ─────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  (client)  │                                            ▼
  │            │        2. HTTP GET /discover         ┌───────────────┐
  │  Pink UI,  │ ───────────────────────────────────▶ │  NEXT.JS app  │
  │  React,    │                                      │  on Vercel    │
  │  Tailwind  │ ◀─────────────────────────────────── │  (server)     │
  └────────────┘    5. 200 OK + HTML (the feed)        └──────┬────────┘
        ▲                                                     │ 3. query
        │ 6. browser renders + hydrates                       ▼
        │    JS, then user taps "Wave"                  ┌───────────────┐
        └──────── 7. POST /api/wave ──────────────────▶ │   SUPABASE    │
                                                        │  auth + DB +  │
                  ◀──── 201 Created ───────────────────│   storage     │
                                                        └───────────────┘
                                            4. rows come back to Next.js

The key insight: the browser never talks to the database directly. It talks to Next.js; Next.js talks to Supabase. Each arrow is an HTTP request with a method, headers, a body, and a status code coming back.

Best practices & pitfalls

  • Do read status codes before trusting a response. fetch does not throw on 404 or 500 — always check res.ok.
  • Do keep secrets on the server. Anything prefixed NEXT_PUBLIC_ is shipped to the browser; your Supabase anon key is fine there, but a service-role key never is.
  • Do use the right method for the job: GET to read, POST to create. GET requests should never change data.
  • Don't confuse 401 and 403. 401 = "we don't know who you are" (log in). 403 = "we know you, you just can't do this."
  • Don't assume the network is fast or reliable. Requests fail, time out, and arrive out of order. Build for it.
  • Don't put "use client" on everything. It ships more JavaScript and loses server-side data access. Start on the server; reach for the client only when you need interactivity.
  • Pitfall: forgetting that cookies() and params are async in Next.js 16. Always await them.
  • Pitfall: thinking HTTPS is optional. The S (encryption) is why your login password isn't readable by the coffee-shop Wi-Fi. Always use HTTPS in production.

Exercises

  1. Read the verbs. Open any website, press F12 to open DevTools, click the Network tab, and reload. Find one GET request and note its status code. (Easy)
  2. Trace a name to a number. In your terminal, run nslookup viby.app (or any site you like). Write down the IP address DNS returns. (Easy)
  3. Trigger a 404. Visit a real site and add /this-page-does-not-exist to the URL. Check the Network tab — what status code came back, and what did the body show? (Medium)
  4. Inspect headers. In DevTools, click a single request and find its Request Headers and Response Headers. Identify the Content-Type of the response. (Medium)
  5. Map Viby. On paper, draw the round trip for a user signing up: which method, which path, client → Next.js → Supabase, and what status code signals success. (Harder)

Mini-project: a request inspector for Viby

Build a tiny page that shows the anatomy of a real HTTP request — a teaching tool you'll reuse to debug Viby later. No database needed.

Create a Server Component route that inspects the request's own headers:

// app/inspect/page.tsx
import { headers } from "next/headers";

export default async function InspectPage() {
  const h = await headers(); // async in Next.js 16
  const ua = h.get("user-agent") ?? "unknown";
  const accept = h.get("accept") ?? "unknown";

  return (
    <main className="mx-auto max-w-md p-6">
      <h1 className="text-2xl font-bold text-brand">Request inspector</h1>
      <dl className="mt-4 space-y-3">
        <div className="rounded-xl bg-white p-3 shadow-sm">
          <dt className="text-sm text-gray-500">Your browser (User-Agent)</dt>
          <dd className="break-words font-mono text-sm">{ua}</dd>
        </div>
        <div className="rounded-xl bg-white p-3 shadow-sm">
          <dt className="text-sm text-gray-500">Formats you accept</dt>
          <dd className="break-words font-mono text-sm">{accept}</dd>
        </div>
      </dl>
      <p className="mt-4 text-sm text-gray-600">
        Everything above arrived on the <strong>request headers</strong> — proof
        that your browser tells the server about itself on every visit.
      </p>
    </main>
  );
}

When you can run the Viby starter (Chapter 2), open /inspect, then watch the same request in the DevTools Network tab. Seeing the same headers in your UI and in the browser's tooling is the moment HTTP clicks. Stretch goal: add a WaveButton-style client component that fetches /inspect and prints the returned status code.

Key takeaways

  • The internet is the roads; the web (HTTP) is one kind of traffic on it.
  • The web is a client asking and a server answering — request in, response out.
  • DNS turns human names (viby.app) into machine IP addresses.
  • Every HTTP request has a method, path, headers, and optional body; every response has a status code, headers, and body.
  • Status codes: 2xx success, 3xx redirect, 4xx your fault, 5xx server's fault.
  • The frontend runs in the browser; the backend runs on the server. In Viby, Next.js spans both and the browser never touches the database directly.

Up next — Chapter 2: Your Developer Toolkit. We'll install Node.js, your editor, and the Viby starter, then run your first server so all this theory starts breathing on your own machine.

Chapter 2 — Frontend, Backend & Where Code Runs

Every line of code you write lives somewhere — and knowing where is the single biggest unlock between "tutorial coder" and "engineer who ships."

What you'll learn

  • The mental model of where code runs: the browser, the server, and the database — and why it matters.
  • The request lifecycle: what actually happens between a user tapping a button and seeing a result.
  • An overview of rendering strategies (SSR, SSG, CSR, streaming) without the jargon fog.
  • How Viby — our social-discovery app — is split cleanly across these layers in Next.js 16.

Prerequisites

  • Chapter 1 (Welcome to Viby & Your Toolkit): you have Node installed, the Viby repo cloned, and npm run dev working.
  • Comfort opening a terminal and a code editor. That's genuinely all. We assume no prior web theory.

Theory

Let's start with a story, not a spec.

Imagine Viby is a hip neighborhood café where people meet others who share their hobbies. Walk through it with me, because this café is the architecture.

The browser is the front counter and dining area. It's everything the customer sees and touches: the menu, the chairs, the music, the smile of the barista. In web terms, the browser (Chrome, Safari) renders the buttons, the pink Viby logo, the animations. Code that runs here is the frontend. It runs on the user's own device — their phone or laptop.

The server is the kitchen. Customers never walk into the kitchen. They place an order at the counter, and the kitchen does the real work: checking the recipe, cooking, plating. In web terms, the server is a computer you control (in Viby's case, running on Vercel) that does trusted work: checking if someone is logged in, deciding what data they're allowed to see, talking to the database. Code that runs here is the backend.

The database is the pantry and the recipe book. It's where all the durable stuff lives: the ingredients, the inventory, the records. Nobody eats raw out of the pantry — the kitchen fetches from it. In Viby, the database is Supabase's PostgreSQL: every user profile, every hobby tag, every friend connection is stored there permanently.

Here's the crucial insight beginners miss: the same JavaScript/TypeScript can run in any of these places, and where it runs changes everything.

  • Code in the browser is fast to react to taps but completely untrustworthy — the user can open dev tools and read or change it. Never put secrets here.
  • Code on the server is trusted and private. Your database password lives here. The user never sees it.
  • Code in the database is the source of truth — slow to change, but reliable.

Why "where" matters so much

A beginner writes a "filter profiles by city" feature and thinks: I'll fetch all profiles into the browser and filter them with JavaScript. That works for 10 profiles. At 100,000 profiles it ships your entire user table to a stranger's phone — slow, expensive, and a privacy disaster. An engineer asks the database to filter, on the server, and sends only the 12 results the user needs.

Same logic. Different where. Wildly different outcome.

The request lifecycle

When a user opens viby.app/discover, here's the real sequence — the heartbeat of every web app:

  1. Browser sends a request. "GET me the /discover page, please." It includes cookies (which may carry a login session).
  2. The request travels the network to Vercel, where Viby's server lives.
  3. The server runs your backend code. It reads the session cookie, asks Supabase "who is this user, and what profiles should they see?", and gets data back.
  4. The server builds a response — usually HTML, sometimes JSON.
  5. The response travels back across the network to the browser.
  6. The browser renders the HTML, then "hydrates" it — wiring up the interactive bits (buttons, animations) with frontend JavaScript.
  7. Later interactions (liking a profile, opening a chat) may repeat a smaller version of this loop without a full page reload.

Every performance problem, every security bug, every "why is this slow" question maps back to one of these seven steps.

Rendering strategies — the 60-second tour

"Rendering" just means turning your code into the HTML the user sees. Where and when that happens is the strategy:

  • CSR (Client-Side Rendering): The server sends a near-empty page; the browser builds everything with JavaScript. Fast to navigate once loaded, but slow first paint and bad for SEO. The old default.
  • SSR (Server-Side Rendering): The server builds the full HTML per request. Great for personalized, always-fresh pages — like Viby's feed, which differs for every logged-in user.
  • SSG (Static Site Generation): HTML is built once at deploy time and served to everyone. Blazing fast. Perfect for Viby's marketing landing page and /about.
  • Streaming: The server sends HTML in chunks as it's ready, so the user sees the shell instantly and the slow parts (a heavy recommendations list) fill in after.

Next.js 16's App Router lets you mix all of these per route and even per component. You don't pick one for the whole app — you pick the right one for each screen. That's the superpower.

In practice

In Next.js 16's App Router, every component is a Server Component by default. That means it runs on the server (the kitchen) — it can talk to the database directly, and its code never ships to the browser. You only opt into the browser with the "use client" directive when you need interactivity (clicks, state, animations).

A Server Component: Viby's Discover page

This runs on the server. Notice it awaits data directly — no loading spinner, no useEffect. The user gets fully-formed HTML.

// app/discover/page.tsx  (Server Component — runs on the server)
import { createClient } from "@/lib/supabase/server";
import { ProfileCard } from "@/components/profile-card";

export default async function DiscoverPage() {
  const supabase = await createClient();

  // This query runs on the SERVER, talking straight to Supabase.
  // The browser never sees this code or the DB credentials.
  const { data: profiles } = await supabase
    .from("profiles")
    .select("id, display_name, avatar_url, hobbies")
    .limit(12);

  return (
    <main className="mx-auto max-w-2xl p-4">
      <h1 className="text-2xl font-bold text-brand">Discover your people</h1>
      <div className="mt-4 grid grid-cols-2 gap-3">
        {profiles?.map((p) => (
          <ProfileCard key={p.id} profile={p} />
        ))}
      </div>
    </main>
  );
}

The text-brand class comes from a Tailwind v4 theme token — defined in CSS, no tailwind.config.js:

/* app/globals.css */
@import "tailwindcss";

@theme {
  --color-brand: #ff6fb5;       /* Viby bubblegum pink */
  --color-brand-foreground: #ffffff;
}

The Supabase server client (runs on the server)

This is the trusted kitchen helper. It reads cookies using the modern getAll/setAll handlers from @supabase/ssr:

// lib/supabase/server.ts
import { createServerClient } from "@supabase/ssr";
import { cookies } from "next/headers";

export async function createClient() {
  const cookieStore = await cookies(); // async in Next.js 16

  return createServerClient(
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL!,
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY!,
    {
      cookies: {
        getAll() {
          return cookieStore.getAll();
        },
        setAll(cookiesToSet) {
          cookiesToSet.forEach(({ name, value, options }) =>
            cookieStore.set(name, value, options)
          );
        },
      },
    }
  );
}

A Client Component: the interactive Like button

This needs to run in the browser — it responds to taps and animates. So it declares "use client" and uses Framer Motion.

// components/like-button.tsx
"use client";

import { useState } from "react";
import { motion } from "framer-motion";
import { likeProfile } from "@/app/actions/like";

export function LikeButton({ profileId }: { profileId: string }) {
  const [liked, setLiked] = useState(false);

  return (
    <motion.button
      whileTap={{ scale: 0.85 }}
      onClick={async () => {
        setLiked(true);             // instant UI feedback in the browser
        await likeProfile(profileId); // trusted write happens on the server
      }}
      className="rounded-full px-4 py-2 text-brand"
      aria-pressed={liked}
    >
      {liked ? "✨ Vibing" : "Say hi"}
    </motion.button>
  );
}

A Server Action: the trusted write (runs on the server)

The button called this, but the code runs back on the server. The "use server" directive is the magic bridge — Next.js wires up the network request for you. The database write happens where it's safe.

// app/actions/like.ts
"use server";

import { createClient } from "@/lib/supabase/server";
import { revalidatePath } from "next/cache";

export async function likeProfile(profileId: string) {
  const supabase = await createClient();

  const {
    data: { user },
  } = await supabase.auth.getUser();
  if (!user) throw new Error("You must be signed in to vibe.");

  await supabase.from("likes").insert({
    from_user: user.id,
    to_user: profileId,
  });

  revalidatePath("/discover");
}

Edge logic with proxy.ts (runs before your page, on the server)

Need to guard routes — say, bounce logged-out users away from /discover? In Next.js 16 this lives in proxy.ts (not the old middleware.ts):

// proxy.ts  (runs on the server, before the request reaches your page)
import { createServerClient } from "@supabase/ssr";
import { NextResponse, type NextRequest } from "next/server";

export async function proxy(request: NextRequest) {
  const response = NextResponse.next();

  const supabase = createServerClient(
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL!,
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY!,
    {
      cookies: {
        getAll() {
          return request.cookies.getAll();
        },
        setAll(cookiesToSet) {
          cookiesToSet.forEach(({ name, value, options }) =>
            response.cookies.set(name, value, options)
          );
        },
      },
    }
  );

  const {
    data: { user },
  } = await supabase.auth.getUser();

  if (!user && request.nextUrl.pathname.startsWith("/discover")) {
    return NextResponse.redirect(new URL("/login", request.url));
  }

  return response;
}

export const config = {
  matcher: ["/discover/:path*", "/chat/:path*"],
};

Read it top to bottom and you can feel the layers: server fetches data, server guards routes, browser handles taps, server handles writes. Every piece in its right home.

Architecture / diagram

Here's the full Viby request lifecycle for a logged-in user opening Discover and tapping "Say hi":

sequenceDiagram
    participant B as Browser (frontend)
    participant P as proxy.ts (server)
    participant S as Server Component (server)
    participant DB as Supabase Postgres (database)

    B->>P: GET /discover (with session cookie)
    P->>P: Check auth — user logged in? ✓
    P->>S: Forward request
    S->>DB: SELECT profiles LIMIT 12
    DB-->>S: 12 rows
    S-->>B: Rendered HTML (the page)
    B->>B: Hydrate — wire up Like buttons & motion
    Note over B: User taps "Say hi"
    B->>S: Server Action: likeProfile(id)
    S->>DB: INSERT into likes
    DB-->>S: ok
    S-->>B: revalidate /discover

And the layered "where does it live" map:

┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  BROWSER (user's phone)         — untrusted, interactive  │
│  "use client" components, Framer Motion, Zustand store    │
└───────────────▲───────────────────────────┬──────────────┘
                │ HTML / Server Action calls │
┌───────────────┴───────────────────────────▼──────────────┐
│  SERVER (Vercel)                — trusted, has secrets     │
│  Server Components, Server Actions, proxy.ts, Supabase key │
└───────────────▲───────────────────────────┬──────────────┘
                │ SQL queries                │
┌───────────────┴───────────────────────────▼──────────────┐
│  DATABASE (Supabase Postgres)   — source of truth          │
│  profiles, likes, hobbies, messages + Row Level Security   │
└────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Best practices & pitfalls

Do:

  • Default to Server Components. Only add "use client" when you truly need interactivity, state, or browser-only APIs. This keeps your JavaScript bundle tiny and fast.
  • Fetch data on the server, close to the database. Filter and limit in the SQL query, not in the browser.
  • Keep secrets server-side. Anything in a Server Component, Server Action, or proxy.ts is safe. Anything in a "use client" file ships to the user.
  • Use Server Actions for writes — they're typed, secure, and need no hand-rolled API route.

Don't:

  • Don't put your Supabase service-role key in a NEXT_PUBLIC_ variable. The NEXT_PUBLIC_ prefix literally means "ship this to the browser." Only the public anon key belongs there.
  • Don't useEffect + fetch for initial page data when a Server Component can await it directly. That's the old CSR habit; it causes loading spinners and slow first paint.
  • Don't trust client-side checks for security. A disabled button is UX, not protection. Always re-check auth inside the Server Action (note our getUser() guard above).
  • Don't reach for proxy.ts for heavy work. It runs on every matched request — keep it to fast auth/redirect logic.

Exercises

  1. Trace the layers. Open app/discover/page.tsx. Write a one-sentence comment above each block stating where it runs (browser / server / database). Confirm the data fetch is server-side.
  2. Prove the boundary. Add a console.log("hello from where?") to the Discover Server Component and to the LikeButton client component. Run npm run dev, open the page, and find which log appears in your terminal versus your browser console. Explain why.
  3. Static vs dynamic. Create a static app/about/page.tsx that returns hard-coded JSX (no data fetch). Compare its behavior to /discover. Which is SSG-friendly and why?
  4. Tighten a query. Modify the Discover query to also filter .eq("city", "Berlin"). Notice you changed where the filtering happens (database) — never the browser.
  5. (Stretch) Add a second matcher route to proxy.ts protecting /profile/edit, and verify a logged-out user gets redirected to /login.

Mini-project: The "Where Am I Running?" badge

Build a tiny diagnostic that makes the frontend/backend split visible.

  1. Create a Server Component app/where/page.tsx that fetches the current time on the server and renders it, plus a heading "Server rendered at: …".
  2. Create a Client Component components/client-clock.tsx ("use client") that shows a live ticking clock using useState + useEffect, labelled "Browser time: …".
  3. Render the client clock inside the server page.
// app/where/page.tsx (Server Component)
import { ClientClock } from "@/components/client-clock";

export default function WherePage() {
  const serverTime = new Date().toLocaleTimeString();
  return (
    <main className="mx-auto max-w-md p-6 space-y-2">
      <p className="font-semibold text-brand">Server rendered at: {serverTime}</p>
      <ClientClock />
    </main>
  );
}

// components/client-clock.tsx (Client Component)
"use client";
import { useEffect, useState } from "react";

export function ClientClock() {
  const [now, setNow] = useState<string>("");
  useEffect(() => {
    const id = setInterval(() => setNow(new Date().toLocaleTimeString()), 1000);
    return () => clearInterval(id);
  }, []);
  return <p className="text-gray-500">Browser time (live): {now}</p>;
}

What you'll observe: the server line is frozen at the moment the page was built on the server. The browser line ticks because that code runs live on your device. You've just seen the boundary between backend and frontend — the whole chapter in one screen.

Key takeaways

  • Code runs in one of three places — browser (frontend), server (backend), database — and where drives speed, security, and cost.
  • The request lifecycle is a predictable seven-step loop; every bug and slowdown maps to one step.
  • Next.js 16 components are Server Components by default; opt into the browser with "use client" only for interactivity.
  • Server Actions ("use server") and proxy.ts keep trusted logic and secrets on the server, away from prying eyes.
  • Pick rendering strategy per route: SSG for marketing, SSR for the personalized feed, streaming for slow sections.

Up next — Chapter 3: HTML, CSS & the Box Model, the Viby Way. Now that you know where your UI is built, we'll learn how to actually build it — pixel by pixel, in Viby's signature pink.

Chapter 3 — React Fundamentals: Components, JSX & Props

Every screen in Viby — from a profile card to the whole feed — is just small pieces of UI snapped together like LEGO. Today you learn how to make those pieces.

What you'll learn

  • What React actually is, and why it beats hand-writing HTML and DOM updates
  • JSX: writing UI that looks like HTML but is really JavaScript
  • Components as plain functions that return UI
  • Props: passing data into a component so it can be reused
  • Composition: building big screens out of small components
  • Rendering lists with key, and conditional rendering (show/hide UI)
  • Building real Viby UI pieces: a HobbyTag, a ProfileCard, and a card grid

Prerequisites

  • Chapter 1 — your environment and the Viby Next.js 16 project are set up
  • Chapter 2 — comfortable with JavaScript basics: variables, functions, arrays, .map(), and objects

If npm run dev starts the app and you can edit a page, you're ready.

Theory

What is React, and why?

Imagine you're decorating a wall with hundreds of sticky notes. The old way (plain HTML + JavaScript) is like writing every note by hand, then every time one number changes, hunting through the whole wall to find that exact note and rewrite it. Tedious, and easy to update the wrong one.

React is a librarian for your UI. You describe what the wall should look like for the current data — "here are the notes" — and React figures out the smallest set of changes needed to make the real wall match. You stop manually touching the page (the "DOM"); you just describe the result, and React keeps the screen in sync with your data.

That single idea — UI is a function of your data — is why React scales from a tiny button to an app like Viby with feeds, profiles, and chat.

JSX: HTML that's secretly JavaScript

React lets you write markup inside JavaScript using JSX:

const greeting = <h1>Welcome to Viby</h1>;

That looks like HTML, but it isn't — it's JavaScript that React turns into UI. Because it's JavaScript, a few things differ from HTML:

  • class becomes className (because class is a reserved word in JS)
  • Attributes are camelCase: onClick, tabIndex
  • You drop values in with curly braces { }:
const name = "Aanya";
const tag = <p>Hey {name}, find your people 💖</p>;

Anything inside { } is real JavaScript: a variable, user.name, 2 + 2, or a function call. A component must return one top-level element; when you need siblings without an extra wrapper, use a Fragment <>...</>.

Components are just functions

A component is a function that returns JSX. That's the whole secret.

function HobbyTag() {
  return <span>Climbing</span>;
}

Think of a component like a cookie cutter. Define the shape once, then stamp out as many cookies as you want. The function is the cutter; each time you write <HobbyTag /> you stamp one cookie onto the screen. Component names must start with a capital letter — that's how React tells your components apart from regular HTML tags like <span>.

Props: passing data in

A cookie cutter that only makes "Climbing" cookies is useless. We want any hobby. Props ("properties") are the inputs you hand a component, exactly like arguments to a function:

function HobbyTag({ label }: { label: string }) {
  return <span>{label}</span>;
}

// Used like HTML attributes:
<HobbyTag label="Climbing" />
<HobbyTag label="Lo-fi beats" />

Props flow one direction: parent → child, like passing a note down a row of desks. A child reads its props but never edits them — props are read-only.

Composition: small pieces, big screens

You don't build a ProfileCard as one giant function. You compose it: a card contains an avatar, a name, and several HobbyTags. Small, focused components snap together into screens — the LEGO model. This keeps each piece simple, testable, and reusable across the app.

Lists and keys

Viby shows many profiles. You render a list by mapping an array to components, giving each a stable key so React can track items as they're added, removed, or reordered:

{hobbies.map((h) => <HobbyTag key={h} label={h} />)}

The key is React's name tag for each item. Use a stable unique id (a database id is ideal) — never the array index if the list can change order, or React may reuse the wrong item.

Conditional rendering

Sometimes UI should appear only sometimes — a "verified" badge, an empty state when there are no results. Because JSX is JavaScript, you just use normal logic:

{isVerified && <VerifiedBadge />}        // show only if true
{count > 0 ? <Feed /> : <EmptyState />}  // either / or

In practice

These are real Viby pieces. In Next.js 16 with the App Router, components are Server Components by default — they render on the server, which is perfect for static, data-driven UI like cards. You only add the "use client" directive when a component needs interactivity (clicks, state, animation). Our display components below need none of that, so they stay server components.

First, our Tailwind v4 brand tokens live in CSS via @theme — no tailwind.config.js:

/* app/globals.css */
@import "tailwindcss";

@theme {
  --color-bubblegum: #ff6fb5;
  --color-ink: #1a1a1a;
  --radius-card: 1.25rem;
}

Now a typed, reusable HobbyTag:

// components/hobby-tag.tsx
type HobbyTagProps = {
  label: string;
};

export function HobbyTag({ label }: HobbyTagProps) {
  return (
    <span className="rounded-full bg-bubblegum/10 px-3 py-1 text-sm font-medium text-bubblegum">
      {label}
    </span>
  );
}

A ProfileCard that composes HobbyTag, renders a list with keys, and uses conditional rendering for the verified badge:

// components/profile-card.tsx
import { HobbyTag } from "./hobby-tag";

export type Profile = {
  id: string;
  name: string;
  city: string;
  isVerified: boolean;
  hobbies: string[];
};

export function ProfileCard({ profile }: { profile: Profile }) {
  return (
    <article className="rounded-card bg-white p-5 shadow-sm ring-1 ring-black/5">
      <header className="flex items-center gap-2">
        <h3 className="text-lg font-bold text-ink">{profile.name}</h3>
        {profile.isVerified && (
          <span aria-label="Verified" className="text-bubblegum">✓</span>
        )}
      </header>

      <p className="text-sm text-ink/60">{profile.city}</p>

      <div className="mt-3 flex flex-wrap gap-2">
        {profile.hobbies.map((hobby) => (
          <HobbyTag key={hobby} label={hobby} />
        ))}
      </div>
    </article>
  );
}

A page that maps over data and handles the empty state — note the async searchParams is awaited, as required in Next.js 16:

// app/discover/page.tsx
import { ProfileCard, type Profile } from "@/components/profile-card";

const PROFILES: Profile[] = [
  { id: "u1", name: "Aanya", city: "Mumbai", isVerified: true,
    hobbies: ["Climbing", "Lo-fi beats", "Ceramics"] },
  { id: "u2", name: "Rohan", city: "Pune", isVerified: false,
    hobbies: ["Cycling", "Board games"] },
];

export default async function DiscoverPage(props: {
  searchParams: Promise<{ city?: string }>;
}) {
  const { city } = await props.searchParams;

  const profiles = city
    ? PROFILES.filter((p) => p.city.toLowerCase() === city.toLowerCase())
    : PROFILES;

  return (
    <main className="mx-auto max-w-2xl p-4">
      <h1 className="mb-4 text-2xl font-extrabold text-ink">Discover your people</h1>

      {profiles.length > 0 ? (
        <div className="grid gap-4 sm:grid-cols-2">
          {profiles.map((profile) => (
            <ProfileCard key={profile.id} profile={profile} />
          ))}
        </div>
      ) : (
        <p className="text-ink/60">No one here yet — try a different city 🌎</p>
      )}
    </main>
  );
}

Run npm run dev, open /discover, and you'll see two profile cards. Visit /discover?city=pune and the list filters to one — conditional rendering and props doing real work.

Architecture / diagram

How data flows down and UI composes up:

flowchart TD
  A["DiscoverPage (Server Component)"] -->|profile prop| B["ProfileCard"]
  A -->|profile prop| C["ProfileCard"]
  B -->|label prop| D["HobbyTag"]
  B -->|label prop| E["HobbyTag"]
  C -->|label prop| F["HobbyTag"]

  subgraph data["Data source"]
    G["PROFILES array"]
  end
  G -. ".map() + key" .-> A

Props flow strictly downward (parent → child). Each arrow is one prop handed to a child. The page owns the data; children just receive and render it.

Best practices & pitfalls

  • Do name components with a Capital letter (ProfileCard), or React treats them as HTML tags.
  • Do give every list item a stable key (a database id). Don't use the array index for lists that can reorder, filter, or grow.
  • Do type your props with a type/interface — it documents the component and catches mistakes early.
  • Don't mutate props inside a child. Props are read-only inputs; if data must change, that's state (next chapter).
  • Do keep components small and single-purpose. If a function is hard to name, it's probably doing two jobs — split it.
  • Don't add "use client" to a component that doesn't need interactivity — keep display components as Server Components for speed.
  • Watch out: {0 && <X/>} renders a literal 0. Guard with a real boolean: {count > 0 && <X/>}.
  • Remember className, not class; camelCase event names like onClick.

Exercises

  1. Warm-up: Create a CityBadge component that takes a city prop and renders it as a small grey pill. Use it inside ProfileCard in place of the plain <p>.
  2. Props + conditional: Add an optional mutualFriends?: number prop to ProfileCard. Show 🤝 {n} mutual only when n is greater than 0.
  3. Lists: Add a third profile to the PROFILES array with four hobbies. Confirm no key warning appears in the console, then deliberately remove a key to see the warning.
  4. Composition: Extract the card header (name + verified check) into its own ProfileHeader component and compose it inside ProfileCard.
  5. Stretch: Make HobbyTag accept an optional tone?: "pink" | "grey" prop and switch className accordingly using a conditional expression.

Mini-project — Viby "People Near You" strip

Build a horizontally scrolling strip of profile cards for the Viby home screen.

Requirements:

  • A PeopleStrip component that takes a profiles: Profile[] prop.
  • Render the cards in a horizontal, scrollable row (Tailwind: flex gap-4 overflow-x-auto).
  • Reuse the existing ProfileCard — no copy-paste.
  • Show a heading "People near you" and a count: "12 nearby" using conditional logic for singular/plural ("1 nearby" vs "N nearby").
  • If the array is empty, render a friendly empty state instead of the strip.
// components/people-strip.tsx
import { ProfileCard, type Profile } from "./profile-card";

export function PeopleStrip({ profiles }: { profiles: Profile[] }) {
  const count = profiles.length;

  return (
    <section className="space-y-3">
      <div className="flex items-baseline justify-between">
        <h2 className="text-xl font-bold text-ink">People near you</h2>
        {count > 0 && (
          <span className="text-sm text-ink/50">
            {count} nearby
          </span>
        )}
      </div>

      {count > 0 ? (
        <div className="flex gap-4 overflow-x-auto pb-2">
          {profiles.map((profile) => (
            <div key={profile.id} className="w-64 shrink-0">
              <ProfileCard profile={profile} />
            </div>
          ))}
        </div>
      ) : (
        <p className="text-ink/60">No one nearby yet — check back soon 💖</p>
      )}
    </section>
  );
}

Drop <PeopleStrip profiles={PROFILES} /> onto your home page and you've shipped a real, reusable Viby feature using nothing but components, props, lists, and conditionals.

Key takeaways

  • React keeps the screen in sync with your data: UI is a function of data.
  • JSX is JavaScript that looks like HTML; use { } to drop in values, className not class.
  • Components are functions that return JSX; capitalize their names.
  • Props pass data parent → child and are read-only.
  • Compose small components into big screens; map arrays to lists with a stable key.
  • Use plain JS (&&, ternary) for conditional rendering; keep display UI as Server Components.

Up next — Chapter 4: State & Events — making components remember and react to clicks, so your Viby UI comes alive.

Chapter 4 — State, Events & Effects

Your Viby screen is about to stop being a poster on a wall and start being a living thing that remembers, reacts, and breathes.

What you'll learn

  • State with useState — how a component remembers things (like how many people you've followed)
  • Event handling — making buttons, taps, and typing actually do something
  • Controlled inputs — owning every keystroke in a text field
  • Lifting state up — letting two components share one source of truth
  • useEffect — talking to the outside world, and the bigger lesson: when NOT to use it
  • Derived state — values you calculate instead of store (a beginner superpower)
  • The React render model — the mental model that makes all of the above click

Prerequisites

You should have finished Chapter 2 (Components & JSX) and Chapter 3 (Props & Composition). You need to be comfortable writing a component, passing props into it, and reading them. We'll also lean on the "use client" boundary idea from Chapter 3 — in Next.js 16 everything is a Server Component until you opt into the browser. State and events live in the browser, so this is our first heavily client-side chapter.

Theory

A component is a snapshot, not a whiteboard

Beginners usually imagine a component as a whiteboard: you draw something, then you walk back up and erase a number and rewrite it. That is not how React works, and unlearning the whiteboard is the single most important thing in this chapter.

Instead, think of a React component as a photographer. Every time something changes, React doesn't edit the old photo — it asks your component to take a brand-new photo of how things look right now. That new snapshot replaces the old one on screen. This taking-a-new-photo is called a render.

So the real question becomes: what makes React take a new photo? The answer is state.

State: the component's memory

State is data that a component remembers between renders, and that — when changed — tells React "take a new photo."

Analogy: state is the counter on a coffee shop's "now serving" sign. The number lives somewhere persistent. When the barista bumps it, the sign visibly updates. In React, useState is that sign.

const [following, setFollowing] = useState(false);

Read this left to right:

  • following — the current value (the photo you're looking at right now)
  • setFollowing — the only legal way to change it ("bump the sign")
  • useState(false) — the starting value

The golden rule: never reassign the variable directly (following = true does nothing useful). You call setFollowing(true), which tells React to re-render with the new value. The setter is the doorbell; reassigning is knocking on the window and hoping someone hears.

Events: how the user talks to your app

An event is something the user does — a tap, a click, a keystroke, a hover. In React you respond to events with handlers: functions you hand to JSX like onClick={...}.

Analogy: an event handler is a doorbell wired to an action. You don't run the action now; you connect the wire and wait. That's why you pass the function (onClick={handleClick}) instead of calling it (onClick={handleClick()} — wrong, that rings the bell immediately at render time).

Derived state: don't store what you can calculate

Here's a trap every beginner falls into: storing things in state that you could just compute.

Analogy: if you know someone's birthday, you don't keep a sticky note with their age and update it every year — you calculate age from the birthday whenever you need it. The birthday is the source of truth; the age is derived.

In Viby: if you store a members array, the count of members is members.length. Don't keep a separate memberCount state — it will drift out of sync the moment you forget to update it. Derived state means: calculate during render, don't store.

Lifting state up: one source of truth

When two components need the same piece of data, you don't give each its own copy — they'd disagree. You move the state up to their closest common parent, and pass it down as props.

Analogy: two roommates shouldn't each keep a private grocery list. You put one shared list on the fridge (the parent), and both read and write to it.

useEffect: a fire escape, not a front door

useEffect lets you run code that reaches outside React — fetching data, setting up a subscription, syncing with a non-React system. It runs after the render, as a side effect.

The crucial beginner lesson: most things you reach for useEffect for don't need it. Calculating a value? That's derived state, do it in render. Responding to a click? That's an event handler. useEffect is the fire escape — there for genuine outside-world syncing (a Supabase Realtime subscription, a browser timer), not the everyday front door.

In practice

Let's build real Viby pieces. These are client components, so they start with "use client".

1. useState + an event: the Follow button

"use client";

import { useState } from "react";

export function FollowButton({ name }: { name: string }) {
  const [following, setFollowing] = useState(false);

  function toggleFollow() {
    setFollowing((prev) => !prev); // flip based on the latest value
  }

  return (
    <button
      onClick={toggleFollow}
      className={`rounded-full px-5 py-2 text-sm font-semibold transition ${
        following
          ? "bg-white text-pink-600 ring-2 ring-pink-500"
          : "bg-pink-500 text-white hover:bg-pink-600"
      }`}
    >
      {following ? `Following ${name}` : `Follow ${name}`}
    </button>
  );
}

Notice setFollowing((prev) => !prev). When your new value depends on the old one, pass a function. React hands you the freshest value, which sidesteps a whole class of stale-value bugs.

Tailwind v4 note: there's no tailwind.config.js. Your brand pink lives in CSS via @theme. In app/globals.css:

```css @import "tailwindcss";

@theme { --color-pink-500: #ff6fb5; / Viby bubblegum / --color-pink-600: #ff4fa3; } ```

Now bg-pink-500 is on-brand everywhere — define the token once, use it forever.

2. Controlled input: the "set your vibe" field

A controlled input means React state is the single source of truth for what's in the box. The input shows value, and every keystroke calls onChange to update that value.

"use client";

import { useState } from "react";

export function VibeInput() {
  const [vibe, setVibe] = useState("");

  const MAX = 60;
  const remaining = MAX - vibe.length; // derived — not stored!

  return (
    <div className="space-y-1">
      <input
        type="text"
        value={vibe}
        onChange={(e) => setVibe(e.target.value)}
        maxLength={MAX}
        placeholder="late-night ramen + lo-fi beats"
        className="w-full rounded-xl border border-pink-200 px-4 py-2 outline-none focus:ring-2 focus:ring-pink-500"
      />
      <p className={`text-xs ${remaining < 10 ? "text-pink-600" : "text-gray-400"}`}>
        {remaining} characters left
      </p>
    </div>
  );
}

remaining is derived state in the wild: we never store the count, we compute it from vibe on every render. It can never be wrong.

3. Lifting state up: a hobby picker that talks to a preview

The picker and the preview both care about which hobbies are selected. So the parent owns the state and passes it down.

"use client";

import { useState } from "react";

const HOBBIES = ["Bouldering", "Vinyl", "Matcha", "Film photography", "D&D"];

export function HobbyPicker() {
  // State lives in the PARENT — the single source of truth.
  const [selected, setSelected] = useState<string[]>([]);

  function toggle(hobby: string) {
    setSelected((prev) =>
      prev.includes(hobby)
        ? prev.filter((h) => h !== hobby)
        : [...prev, hobby]
    );
  }

  return (
    <div className="space-y-4">
      <ChipGrid hobbies={HOBBIES} selected={selected} onToggle={toggle} />
      <ProfilePreview selected={selected} />
    </div>
  );
}

function ChipGrid({
  hobbies,
  selected,
  onToggle,
}: {
  hobbies: string[];
  selected: string[];
  onToggle: (hobby: string) => void;
}) {
  return (
    <div className="flex flex-wrap gap-2">
      {hobbies.map((hobby) => {
        const isOn = selected.includes(hobby);
        return (
          <button
            key={hobby}
            onClick={() => onToggle(hobby)}
            className={`rounded-full px-4 py-1.5 text-sm transition ${
              isOn ? "bg-pink-500 text-white" : "bg-pink-50 text-pink-700"
            }`}
          >
            {hobby}
          </button>
        );
      })}
    </div>
  );
}

function ProfilePreview({ selected }: { selected: string[] }) {
  // Reads the same lifted state via props — always in sync.
  return (
    <p className="text-sm text-gray-600">
      {selected.length === 0
        ? "Pick a few things you're into ✨"
        : `Your people love: ${selected.join(", ")}`}
    </p>
  );
}

The child ChipGrid doesn't own anything — it reports clicks up via onToggle, and the parent updates the shared state. Both children re-render from one truth.

4. useEffect done right: a live Supabase Realtime subscription

Now a legitimate effect. We subscribe to new messages in a Viby chat room. This syncs with an external system (Supabase Realtime), so an effect is correct — and we clean up when the component unmounts.

"use client";

import { useEffect, useState } from "react";
import { createBrowserClient } from "@supabase/ssr";

type Message = { id: string; body: string };

const supabase = createBrowserClient(
  process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL!,
  process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY!
);

export function RoomFeed({ roomId }: { roomId: string }) {
  const [messages, setMessages] = useState<Message[]>([]);

  useEffect(() => {
    const channel = supabase
      .channel(`room:${roomId}`)
      .on(
        "postgres_changes",
        { event: "INSERT", schema: "public", table: "messages", filter: `room_id=eq.${roomId}` },
        (payload) => {
          setMessages((prev) => [...prev, payload.new as Message]);
        }
      )
      .subscribe();

    // Cleanup: React runs this when roomId changes or the component unmounts.
    return () => {
      supabase.removeChannel(channel);
    };
  }, [roomId]); // dependency array: re-subscribe only when the room changes

  return (
    <ul className="space-y-2">
      {messages.map((m) => (
        <li key={m.id} className="rounded-lg bg-pink-50 px-3 py-2 text-sm">
          {m.body}
        </li>
      ))}
    </ul>
  );
}

Three things make this a correct effect: it touches an external system, it has a dependency array ([roomId]) so it doesn't re-run on every render, and it returns a cleanup function so we don't leak subscriptions.

5. The thing you should NOT use an effect for

Beginners often write this:

// 🚫 ANTI-PATTERN — do not do this
const [members, setMembers] = useState<string[]>([]);
const [count, setCount] = useState(0);

useEffect(() => {
  setCount(members.length); // syncing state from state — causes an extra render
}, [members]);

Delete all of it. The count is derived:

// ✅ Just calculate it during render
const [members, setMembers] = useState<string[]>([]);
const count = members.length;

No effect, no second state, no chance of drift, no extra render. If you can compute it from existing props or state, compute it — don't mirror it into useEffect.

Architecture / diagram

How a click becomes a new photo on screen — the React render loop:

flowchart TD
    A[User taps Follow button] --> B[onClick handler runs]
    B --> C["setFollowing(true) — schedules an update"]
    C --> D[React re-renders the component]
    D --> E[Derived values recalculated<br/>e.g. button label, remaining chars]
    E --> F[React compares new snapshot to old]
    F --> G[Only the changed DOM is updated]
    G --> H[useEffect runs AFTER paint<br/>if its dependencies changed]
    H --> A

And lifting state up, visually:

                 ┌──────────────────────────┐
                 │   HobbyPicker (parent)   │
                 │   state: selected[]      │  ◄── single source of truth
                 └───────────┬──────────────┘
              props ↓ selected, onToggle      props ↓ selected
        ┌───────────────────┐         ┌────────────────────┐
        │   ChipGrid        │         │  ProfilePreview    │
        │ (reports clicks ↑)│         │  (reads only)      │
        └───────────────────┘         └────────────────────┘

Best practices & pitfalls

  • Do update state with the setter, never by reassigning the variable. setX(...), not x = ....
  • Do use the updater function (setX((prev) => ...)) whenever the next value depends on the previous one.
  • Don't mutate state directly. arr.push(x) then setArr(arr) won't re-render reliably — make a new array: setArr([...arr, x]).
  • Do prefer derived state over storing computed values. If it can be calculated, calculate it.
  • Don't use useEffect to sync one piece of state from another — that's a derived-state smell.
  • Do pass the handler, not the call: onClick={handle} ✅, onClick={handle()} 🚫 (the second runs at render).
  • Do give a key to every item in a .map() — a stable ID, not the array index when the list reorders.
  • Don't forget the cleanup function in effects that subscribe or set timers — leaks cause ghost updates and warnings.
  • Do keep the dependency array honest: list every value from the component the effect reads. Lying to it causes stale data.
  • Remember: useState/useEffect only work in "use client" components. Server Components (the Next.js 16 default) have no state.

Exercises

  1. Warm-up. Add a LikeButton to a Viby post that shows a heart and a count. Tapping toggles liked/unliked and bumps the count up or down. Use one useState and the updater function.

  2. Controlled + derived. Build a BioField (textarea) capped at 150 characters. Show a live counter that turns pink when fewer than 20 remain. The counter must be derived, not stored.

  3. Lift it up. Make a RoomFilter with a search box and a RoomList below it. The typed query lives in the parent; the list shows only rooms whose name includes the query (case-insensitive). Filtering is derived state.

  4. Effect with cleanup. Build an OnlineDot that flips between "online" and "away" every 5 seconds using setInterval inside useEffect. Clear the interval in the cleanup function. (Then ask yourself: could a timer like this ever be derived? Why not?)

  5. Spot the anti-pattern. Take exercise 2 and intentionally rewrite the counter using useState + useEffect. Notice the extra render and the risk of drift, then revert. Naming the smell is half of avoiding it.

Mini-project: the "Find Your People" onboarding card

Build one polished client component, FindYourPeople, combining everything:

  • A controlled name input and a controlled vibe input.
  • A row of hobby chips (lifted state, multi-select) — reuse the pattern from In Practice #3.
  • A live derived readiness check: the "Done" button is enabled only when name is non-empty and at least 2 hobbies are picked. Compute const canFinish = name.trim() !== "" && selected.length >= 2; — no extra state.
  • A Framer Motion flourish: animate each selected chip in with AnimatePresence, and pop the "Done" button when it becomes enabled.
"use client";

import { useState } from "react";
import { motion, AnimatePresence } from "framer-motion";

const HOBBIES = ["Bouldering", "Vinyl", "Matcha", "Film", "D&D", "Running"];

export function FindYourPeople() {
  const [name, setName] = useState("");
  const [selected, setSelected] = useState<string[]>([]);

  const canFinish = name.trim() !== "" && selected.length >= 2; // derived

  function toggle(hobby: string) {
    setSelected((prev) =>
      prev.includes(hobby) ? prev.filter((h) => h !== hobby) : [...prev, hobby]
    );
  }

  return (
    <div className="mx-auto max-w-sm space-y-4 rounded-2xl bg-white p-6 shadow-lg">
      <input
        value={name}
        onChange={(e) => setName(e.target.value)}
        placeholder="Your name"
        className="w-full rounded-xl border border-pink-200 px-4 py-2 outline-none focus:ring-2 focus:ring-pink-500"
      />

      <div className="flex flex-wrap gap-2">
        {HOBBIES.map((hobby) => {
          const isOn = selected.includes(hobby);
          return (
            <button
              key={hobby}
              onClick={() => toggle(hobby)}
              className={`rounded-full px-3 py-1.5 text-sm transition ${
                isOn ? "bg-pink-500 text-white" : "bg-pink-50 text-pink-700"
              }`}
            >
              {hobby}
            </button>
          );
        })}
      </div>

      <AnimatePresence>
        {selected.length > 0 && (
          <motion.p
            initial={{ opacity: 0, y: 4 }}
            animate={{ opacity: 1, y: 0 }}
            exit={{ opacity: 0 }}
            className="text-sm text-gray-600"
          >
            Matching you with people who love {selected.join(", ")}
          </motion.p>
        )}
      </AnimatePresence>

      <motion.button
        disabled={!canFinish}
        animate={{ scale: canFinish ? 1.03 : 1 }}
        className="w-full rounded-full bg-pink-500 py-2 font-semibold text-white disabled:opacity-40"
      >
        Find my people
      </motion.button>
    </div>
  );
}

Ship it, tap around, and watch how one change to state ripples through derived values and animations — with zero useEffect needed.

Key takeaways

  • A render is React taking a new photo; state changes are what trigger it.
  • useState gives you [value, setter] — change state only through the setter, and use the updater function when the next value depends on the old.
  • Event handlers are wires you connect (onClick={fn}), not actions you run now.
  • Controlled inputs make React state the single source of truth for form fields.
  • Lift state up to the closest common parent when components must share it.
  • Derived state > stored state: if you can calculate it, don't store it.
  • useEffect is for syncing with the outside world (subscriptions, timers) — and it usually needs a dependency array and a cleanup function. Most "effects" beginners write should be derived state or event handlers instead.

Up next — Chapter 5: Data Fetching & Server Actions, where we leave in-memory state behind and start reading and writing real Viby data with Supabase, Server Components, and the "use server" directive.

Chapter 5 — TypeScript for React + Talking to APIs

Give your code a memory and a conscience: types catch your mistakes before your users ever do.

What you'll learn

  • What TypeScript is and why it makes you a faster, calmer developer
  • Core building blocks: type, interface, union types, and generics
  • How to type React props and state so your components can't be misused
  • fetch, async/await, promises, and JSON — how the browser talks to a server
  • How to describe an API response with types and build a small, fully-typed data layer for Viby

Prerequisites

  • Chapter 2 (JavaScript fundamentals — variables, functions, objects, arrays)
  • Chapter 3 (your first React components and JSX)
  • Chapter 4 (props, state with useState, and the component tree)

You don't need to have written a single line of TypeScript before. We start from zero.

Theory

Why types? The "labeled jars" analogy

Imagine a kitchen where every jar is unlabeled. One looks like sugar but is salt. You only find out when the cake is ruined. JavaScript is that kitchen — a variable can hold a number now and a string five lines later, and nobody warns you until everything's in the oven (production).

TypeScript adds labels to the jars. You say "this jar holds string," and if you ever try to pour a number in, TypeScript stops you while you're cooking — in your editor, with a red squiggle — not after a user has already tasted the result.

TypeScript is just JavaScript with a type-checker bolted on. The browser never runs TypeScript directly; it gets compiled to plain JavaScript first. The types are a safety net for you, the developer, and they vanish at runtime.

Types and interfaces — describing the shape of things

The smallest labels are the primitive types: string, number, boolean.

let displayName: string = "Aria";
let age: number = 24;
let isOnline: boolean = true;

But Viby is full of objects — a user, a hobby, a message. To describe an object's shape, we use a type or an interface. Think of them as a form template: every field is named, and every field has an expected kind of value.

type Hobby = {
  id: string;
  label: string;        // e.g. "Bouldering"
  emoji: string;        // e.g. "🧗"
};

interface does almost the same job and reads nearly identically:

interface Profile {
  id: string;
  username: string;
  bio: string;
  hobbies: Hobby[];     // an array of Hobby objects
}

Rule of thumb: use interface for object shapes (especially things others might extend), and type when you need unions, intersections, or to name something that isn't a plain object. In practice they're interchangeable for most day-to-day work — pick one and stay consistent.

Union types — "one of these, exactly"

Sometimes a value is one of a small, fixed set of options. A Viby connection request is "pending", "accepted", or "declined" — never "banana". A union (the | symbol, read as "or") expresses exactly that:

type ConnectionStatus = "pending" | "accepted" | "declined";

let status: ConnectionStatus = "pending"; // ✅
// let status: ConnectionStatus = "maybe"; // ❌ red squiggle — not allowed

Unions are like a vending machine with three buttons. You can press A, B, or C. There is no button for "whatever I feel like." This single feature eliminates a whole category of typo bugs.

Generics — a type with a blank to fill in

Here's the analogy that makes generics click: a generic is a shipping box that takes a label. The box is always the same shape, but you write on the side what's inside — "📦 of shoes," "📦 of books." In TypeScript, that label is written in angle brackets <T>, where T is a placeholder you fill in later.

You already use generics without knowing it: Hobby[] is shorthand for Array<Hobby> — "an array of Hobby."

Generics let you write one reusable function or type that works for any content while still keeping it precisely typed:

// A box that wraps any successful API result alongside a count.
type ApiList<T> = {
  data: T[];
  count: number;
};

type HobbyList = ApiList<Hobby>;    // data is Hobby[]
type ProfileList = ApiList<Profile>; // data is Profile[]

One template, infinite specific shapes. That's the power.

Talking to APIs — the restaurant model

Your React app is the customer. The server (Supabase, in Viby) is the kitchen. Between them, a waiter carries your order out and the food back. That waiter is fetch.

The catch: the kitchen takes time. You don't freeze at the table staring at the wall — you chat, you wait, the food arrives later. In code, that "later" is a Promise: an IOU that says "I'll give you a value eventually — either the data, or an error."

async/await is the polite way to wait for a Promise. await means "pause here until the IOU is fulfilled, then continue with the real value."

async function getJoke() {
  const response = await fetch("https://api.example.com/joke");
  const data = await response.json(); // parse the JSON body
  return data;
}

JSON (JavaScript Object Notation) is the universal language servers and browsers use to exchange data — basically a JavaScript object written as text. response.json() turns that text back into a usable object. Crucially, fetch gives back any/unknown shaped data — TypeScript can't know what the server sent, so we must describe it. That's the bridge between this chapter's two halves.

In practice

Let's build a small, real data layer for Viby: typed models, a typed fetch helper, and a Supabase-backed query — the exact pattern you'll reuse all book long.

1. Define the domain models

Create lib/types.ts. This is the single source of truth for what a Viby user looks like.

// lib/types.ts
export interface Hobby {
  id: string;
  label: string;
  emoji: string;
}

export type ConnectionStatus = "pending" | "accepted" | "declined";

export interface Profile {
  id: string;
  username: string;
  bio: string;
  avatarUrl: string | null; // null when no photo uploaded yet
  hobbies: Hobby[];
}

// A reusable, generic envelope for any list response.
export interface ApiList<T> {
  data: T[];
  count: number;
}

2. A typed fetch helper using generics

fetch returns untyped data. We wrap it once so every call site gets full type safety for free. The generic <T> is the "label on the box."

// lib/api.ts
export async function fetchJson<T>(url: string): Promise<T> {
  const res = await fetch(url);

  if (!res.ok) {
    // Always handle the unhappy path — servers fail.
    throw new Error(`Request failed: ${res.status} ${res.statusText}`);
  }

  const json = (await res.json()) as T;
  return json;
}

Now calling it tells TypeScript exactly what comes back:

import { fetchJson } from "@/lib/api";
import type { ApiList, Profile } from "@/lib/types";

// `discover` is now fully typed as ApiList<Profile> — autocomplete everywhere.
const discover = await fetchJson<ApiList<Profile>>("/api/discover");
console.log(discover.count, discover.data[0].username);

3. The real Viby data layer with Supabase (Server Component)

In Next.js 16, components are Server Components by default — they can talk to the database directly, no fetch round-trip needed. We use @supabase/ssr with the getAll/setAll cookie handlers.

// lib/supabase/server.ts
import { createServerClient } from "@supabase/ssr";
import { cookies } from "next/headers";

export async function createClient() {
  const cookieStore = await cookies(); // async in Next.js 16

  return createServerClient(
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL!,
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY!,
    {
      cookies: {
        getAll() {
          return cookieStore.getAll();
        },
        setAll(cookiesToSet) {
          cookiesToSet.forEach(({ name, value, options }) =>
            cookieStore.set(name, value, options)
          );
        },
      },
    }
  );
}

Now a typed data-access function. Notice the return type Promise<Profile[]> — the contract is explicit:

// lib/queries.ts
import { createClient } from "@/lib/supabase/server";
import type { Profile } from "@/lib/types";

export async function getDiscoverProfiles(): Promise<Profile[]> {
  const supabase = await createClient();

  const { data, error } = await supabase
    .from("profiles")
    .select("id, username, bio, avatar_url, hobbies")
    .limit(20);

  if (error) {
    throw new Error(`Failed to load profiles: ${error.message}`);
  }

  // Map the DB's snake_case row to our camelCase Profile model.
  return (data ?? []).map((row) => ({
    id: row.id,
    username: row.username,
    bio: row.bio,
    avatarUrl: row.avatar_url,
    hobbies: row.hobbies ?? [],
  }));
}

4. Typing props and state in a component

Here's a ProfileCard that consumes our Profile type. Props get an interface; the Server Component awaits the data layer.

// app/discover/page.tsx  (Server Component — no "use client")
import { getDiscoverProfiles } from "@/lib/queries";
import { ProfileCard } from "@/components/profile-card";

export default async function DiscoverPage() {
  const profiles = await getDiscoverProfiles();

  return (
    <main className="mx-auto max-w-md space-y-4 p-4">
      <h1 className="text-2xl font-bold text-brand">Discover your people</h1>
      {profiles.map((profile) => (
        <ProfileCard key={profile.id} profile={profile} />
      ))}
    </main>
  );
}

// components/profile-card.tsx
"use client";

import { useState } from "react";
import { motion } from "framer-motion";
import type { Profile, ConnectionStatus } from "@/lib/types";

interface ProfileCardProps {
  profile: Profile;
}

export function ProfileCard({ profile }: ProfileCardProps) {
  // State is typed by the union — only valid statuses allowed.
  const [status, setStatus] = useState<ConnectionStatus>("pending");

  return (
    <motion.article
      whileHover={{ scale: 1.02 }}
      whileTap={{ scale: 0.98 }}
      className="rounded-2xl bg-white p-4 shadow-sm ring-1 ring-pink-100"
    >
      <h2 className="text-lg font-semibold">@{profile.username}</h2>
      <p className="mt-1 text-sm text-gray-600">{profile.bio}</p>

      <ul className="mt-3 flex flex-wrap gap-2">
        {profile.hobbies.map((hobby) => (
          <li
            key={hobby.id}
            className="rounded-full bg-pink-50 px-3 py-1 text-sm"
          >
            {hobby.emoji} {hobby.label}
          </li>
        ))}
      </ul>

      <button
        onClick={() => setStatus("accepted")}
        disabled={status === "accepted"}
        className="mt-4 w-full rounded-full bg-brand py-2 font-medium text-white disabled:opacity-50"
      >
        {status === "accepted" ? "Vibing ✓" : "Connect"}
      </button>
    </motion.article>
  );
}

The brand token comes from Tailwind v4's CSS-first config — no tailwind.config.js:

/* app/globals.css */
@import "tailwindcss";

@theme {
  --color-brand: #ff6fb5;
}

Architecture / diagram

How a typed request flows through Viby, from page render to database and back — with types guarding every boundary:

flowchart TD
    A["DiscoverPage<br/>(Server Component)"] -->|await| B["getDiscoverProfiles()<br/>: Promise&lt;Profile[]&gt;"]
    B --> C["createClient()<br/>@supabase/ssr"]
    C -->|SQL select| D[("Supabase<br/>PostgreSQL")]
    D -->|snake_case rows| B
    B -->|"map → Profile[]"| A
    A -->|"props: { profile: Profile }"| E["ProfileCard<br/>(Client Component)"]
    E -->|"useState&lt;ConnectionStatus&gt;"| F["UI: Connect / Vibing ✓"]

    style A fill:#ffe4f1,stroke:#ff6fb5
    style E fill:#ffe4f1,stroke:#ff6fb5
    style D fill:#fff,stroke:#999

Notice the type annotations sitting on the edges: Promise<Profile[]> at the data layer, { profile: Profile } at the prop boundary, ConnectionStatus at the state. Each is a checkpoint where TypeScript verifies the data is the right shape before it's allowed through.

Best practices & pitfalls

  • Do define your domain models once in lib/types.ts and import them everywhere. One source of truth = no drift.
  • Do type the return value of every data-layer function (Promise<Profile[]>). It documents intent and catches mistakes at the source.
  • Do handle the error path of every fetch/Supabase call. Networks fail; res.ok and error exist for a reason.
  • Don't reach for any. It silences the type-checker and re-introduces the unlabeled-jar problem. Prefer unknown and then narrow.
  • Don't annotate what TypeScript already infers. const name = "Aria" is already string — adding : string is noise. Let inference do the work; annotate boundaries (props, returns, state) explicitly.
  • Don't forget key on mapped lists, and don't put "use client" on a component that doesn't need interactivity — Server Components are the default for a reason.
  • Pitfall: await cookies() and await params are async in Next.js 16. Forgetting the await is a common early bug.

Exercises

  1. Warm-up. In lib/types.ts, add an interface Message with id: string, senderId: string, body: string, and sentAt: string. Export it.
