  2. Unions. Add a union type Vibe = "chill" | "adventurous" | "creative" | "social" and add an optional vibe?: Vibe field to Profile. Why does the ? matter?
  3. Generics. Write a generic function first<T>(items: T[]): T | undefined that returns the first element of any array, or undefined if it's empty. Test it on a Profile[] and a Hobby[].
  4. Async + types. Using fetchJson<T>, write getProfile(id: string): Promise<Profile> that calls /api/profiles/${id}. Add proper error handling.
  5. Stretch. Make ProfileCard accept an optional onConnect?: (id: string) => void prop and call it inside the button's onClick. Type the prop precisely.

Mini-project: a typed "Hobby Match" badge

Build a small client component, HobbyMatch, that takes the current user's hobbies and another profile, computes how many hobbies they share, and renders an animated match badge.

// components/hobby-match.tsx
"use client";

import { motion, AnimatePresence } from "framer-motion";
import type { Hobby, Profile } from "@/lib/types";

interface HobbyMatchProps {
  myHobbies: Hobby[];
  profile: Profile;
}

function countShared(mine: Hobby[], theirs: Hobby[]): number {
  const myIds = new Set(mine.map((h) => h.id));
  return theirs.filter((h) => myIds.has(h.id)).length;
}

export function HobbyMatch({ myHobbies, profile }: HobbyMatchProps) {
  const shared = countShared(myHobbies, profile.hobbies);

  return (
    <AnimatePresence>
      {shared > 0 && (
        <motion.span
          initial={{ scale: 0, opacity: 0 }}
          animate={{ scale: 1, opacity: 1 }}
          exit={{ scale: 0, opacity: 0 }}
          className="inline-block rounded-full bg-brand px-3 py-1 text-sm font-semibold text-white"
        >
          ✨ {shared} shared {shared === 1 ? "hobby" : "hobbies"}
        </motion.span>
      )}
    </AnimatePresence>
  );
}

Your task: wire HobbyMatch into ProfileCard, passing a hard-coded myHobbies array for now. Confirm that: - TypeScript refuses to compile if you pass a string[] instead of Hobby[]. - The badge animates in only when there's at least one shared hobby.

You've just shipped a typed, animated, data-driven feature — the same loop you'll repeat for every Viby screen.

Key takeaways

  • TypeScript = JavaScript + labeled jars: errors surface in your editor, not in production.
  • type/interface describe object shapes; unions (|) lock a value to a fixed set; generics (<T>) make one template work for any content.
  • Annotate the boundaries — props, state, and function returns — and let inference handle the rest.
  • fetch + async/await + Promises are how your app talks to a server; you must type what comes back, because the server can't.
  • A typed data layer (lib/types.ts, lib/queries.ts) is the backbone of a maintainable app.

Up next — Chapter 6: styling Viby with Tailwind CSS v4, design tokens via @theme, and building our first polished, on-brand component library.

Chapter 6 — Next.js & the App Router

You already know React. This chapter is where React grows up, moves to the city, and gets a real job shipping a production app.

What you'll learn

  • Why we reach for a framework like Next.js instead of wiring React together by hand
  • How the App Router turns folders and files into routes — no router config, ever
  • The job of each special file: page, layout, loading, error, and route
  • The difference between Server Components (the default) and Client Components ("use client")
  • How async params, Server Actions, and proxy.ts fit together
  • How the real Viby project is organized so you can navigate it like a local

Prerequisites

  • Chapter 3 (HTML/CSS fundamentals) and Chapter 4 (modern JavaScript & TypeScript)
  • Chapter 5 (React 19: components, props, hooks, the client/server mental model)
  • A working Node.js + pnpm setup from Chapter 2, and the Viby repo cloned locally

Theory

Why a framework at all?

Plain React gives you components and a way to render them into a <div>. That's it. The moment you ship a real product, you discover a long list of things React deliberately does not solve:

  • Routing (which URL shows which screen?)
  • Server rendering for fast first paint and SEO
  • Data fetching that doesn't waterfall into a loading-spinner swamp
  • Bundling, code-splitting, image optimization, caching
  • A safe place to run secret code (API keys, database queries) that never reaches the browser

You can assemble all of this yourself with Vite + React Router + a custom Express server + a dozen config files. People did, for years. It's a part-time job to maintain.

A framework is a set of strong, well-tested decisions made for you. Think of plain React as a pile of premium car parts: a great engine, great seats, great wheels. Next.js is the assembled car — the parts are bolted together in the right order, the wiring works, and you can just drive. You give up a little freedom in exchange for a lot of speed and a lot fewer 2 a.m. bugs.

The big idea: the server is back

For a decade, the trend was "ship everything to the browser." React apps became huge JavaScript bundles that downloaded, then ran, then finally fetched data, then finally showed pixels.

The App Router flips this. By default, your components run on the server, render to HTML, and stream that HTML to the browser instantly. JavaScript is shipped only for the interactive parts. These server-by-default components are called React Server Components (RSC).

Analogy: the old way mailed customers a flat-pack and an Allen key — "assemble the page yourself." The App Router ships the furniture already built, and only includes a screwdriver for the one drawer that needs to open and close.

Folders are routes

The App Router's central trick: the file system is the router. A folder inside app/ becomes a URL segment. Drop a page.tsx in it and that URL becomes visitable. No route table, no <Route path=...> — the structure on disk is the structure of your site.

app/discover/page.tsx   →   /discover
app/profile/page.tsx    →   /profile

Server vs. Client Components

This is the single most important mental model in modern Next.js:

Server Component (default) Client Component ("use client")
Runs where On the server In the browser (and once on the server for the initial HTML)
Can do await data, read DB/secrets, big deps useState, useEffect, onClick, browser APIs
Ships JS to browser No Yes
Use for Pages, layouts, data fetching Buttons, forms, animations, anything interactive

Rule of thumb: stay on the server until you can't. Add "use client" only at the leaf where you actually need interactivity (a like button, an animated card), and keep that island as small as possible.

In practice

Let's build the real Viby pieces. We'll assume Next.js 16, React 19, Tailwind v4, and Supabase via @supabase/ssr.

A page (Server Component, the default)

No directive needed — this runs on the server and can await data directly.

// app/discover/page.tsx
import { createClient } from "@/lib/supabase/server";
import { VibeCard } from "@/components/vibe-card";

export default async function DiscoverPage() {
  const supabase = await createClient();
  const { data: people } = await supabase
    .from("profiles")
    .select("id, display_name, avatar_url, hobbies")
    .limit(20);

  return (
    <main className="mx-auto max-w-md px-4 py-6">
      <h1 className="text-2xl font-bold text-brand">Discover your people</h1>
      <div className="mt-4 grid gap-4">
        {people?.map((p) => (
          <VibeCard key={p.id} profile={p} />
        ))}
      </div>
    </main>
  );
}

Notice text-brand — that's our bubblegum pink, defined once in Tailwind v4's CSS theme (no tailwind.config.js):

/* app/globals.css */
@import "tailwindcss";

@theme {
  --color-brand: #ff6fb5;
  --color-brand-foreground: #ffffff;
  --font-display: "Clash Display", ui-sans-serif, system-ui;
}

The Supabase server client (@supabase/ssr)

The modern pattern uses createServerClient with getAll / setAll cookie handlers — never the deprecated single-cookie API.

// lib/supabase/server.ts
import { createServerClient } from "@supabase/ssr";
import { cookies } from "next/headers";

export async function createClient() {
  const cookieStore = await cookies();

  return createServerClient(
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL!,
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY!,
    {
      cookies: {
        getAll: () => cookieStore.getAll(),
        setAll: (cookiesToSet) => {
          try {
            cookiesToSet.forEach(({ name, value, options }) =>
              cookieStore.set(name, value, options)
            );
          } catch {
            // Called from a Server Component — safe to ignore; proxy refreshes.
          }
        },
      },
    }
  );
}

And the browser client for Client Components:

// lib/supabase/client.ts
import { createBrowserClient } from "@supabase/ssr";

export function createClient() {
  return createBrowserClient(
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL!,
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY!
  );
}

A layout (shared shell)

Layouts wrap every page beneath them and persist across navigation — they don't re-render or unmount when you move between child routes. Perfect for nav bars.

// app/layout.tsx — the root layout
import type { Metadata } from "next";
import "./globals.css";

export const metadata: Metadata = {
  title: "Viby — find your people",
  description: "Make friends through shared hobbies and lifestyle.",
};

export default function RootLayout({
  children,
}: {
  children: React.ReactNode;
}) {
  return (
    <html lang="en">
      <body className="bg-white text-neutral-900 antialiased">{children}</body>
    </html>
  );
}

// app/(app)/layout.tsx — shared shell for the signed-in experience
import { BottomNav } from "@/components/bottom-nav";

export default function AppLayout({
  children,
}: {
  children: React.ReactNode;
}) {
  return (
    <div className="min-h-dvh pb-16">
      {children}
      <BottomNav />
    </div>
  );
}

(app) is a route group — the parentheses mean "group these routes for layout/organization, but don't add app to the URL."

loading.tsx — instant skeletons

Drop a loading.tsx next to a page and Next.js automatically wraps it in a <Suspense> boundary. While the server component awaits data, the user sees this instead of a frozen screen.

// app/discover/loading.tsx
export default function Loading() {
  return (
    <div className="mx-auto max-w-md px-4 py-6">
      <div className="h-8 w-48 animate-pulse rounded-full bg-neutral-200" />
      <div className="mt-4 grid gap-4">
        {Array.from({ length: 4 }).map((_, i) => (
          <div key={i} className="h-32 animate-pulse rounded-2xl bg-neutral-100" />
        ))}
      </div>
    </div>
  );
}

error.tsx — graceful recovery

Error files must be Client Components (they receive a reset function and need state).

// app/discover/error.tsx
"use client";

export default function Error({
  error,
  reset,
}: {
  error: Error & { digest?: string };
  reset: () => void;
}) {
  return (
    <div className="mx-auto max-w-md px-4 py-12 text-center">
      <p className="text-lg font-semibold">That vibe didn&apos;t load.</p>
      <p className="mt-1 text-sm text-neutral-500">{error.message}</p>
      <button
        onClick={reset}
        className="mt-4 rounded-full bg-brand px-5 py-2 font-medium text-brand-foreground"
      >
        Try again
      </button>
    </div>
  );
}

Dynamic routes & async params

In Next.js 16, params is a Promise — you await it (or unwrap it with React's use() in a Client Component).

// app/profile/[username]/page.tsx
import { createClient } from "@/lib/supabase/server";
import { notFound } from "next/navigation";

export default async function ProfilePage({
  params,
}: {
  params: Promise<{ username: string }>;
}) {
  const { username } = await params;
  const supabase = await createClient();

  const { data: profile } = await supabase
    .from("profiles")
    .select("*")
    .eq("username", username)
    .single();

  if (!profile) notFound();

  return <h1 className="text-2xl font-bold">{profile.display_name}</h1>;
}

A Client Component with Framer Motion

Here's a Viby "like" button — interactive, animated, and a small island of client JS.

// components/like-button.tsx
"use client";

import { useState } from "react";
import { motion } from "framer-motion";

export function LikeButton({ initialLiked = false }: { initialLiked?: boolean }) {
  const [liked, setLiked] = useState(initialLiked);

  return (
    <motion.button
      whileTap={{ scale: 0.85 }}
      onClick={() => setLiked((v) => !v)}
      aria-pressed={liked}
      className="rounded-full p-2"
    >
      <motion.span
        animate={{ scale: liked ? [1, 1.4, 1] : 1, color: liked ? "#ff6fb5" : "#a3a3a3" }}
        transition={{ duration: 0.25 }}
        className="text-2xl"
      >
        ♥
      </motion.span>
    </motion.button>
  );
}

A Server Action ("use server")

Server Actions let a form mutate data without writing an API route. Define the function with "use server", pass it straight to a <form>.

// app/onboarding/page.tsx
import { createClient } from "@/lib/supabase/server";
import { revalidatePath } from "next/cache";
import { redirect } from "next/navigation";

export default function OnboardingPage() {
  async function saveHobbies(formData: FormData) {
    "use server";
    const hobbies = formData.getAll("hobby").map(String);
    const supabase = await createClient();
    const { data: { user } } = await supabase.auth.getUser();
    if (!user) redirect("/login");

    await supabase.from("profiles").update({ hobbies }).eq("id", user.id);
    revalidatePath("/discover");
    redirect("/discover");
  }

  return (
    <form action={saveHobbies} className="mx-auto max-w-md space-y-4 p-6">
      {["Climbing", "Vinyl", "Ramen", "Film photography"].map((h) => (
        <label key={h} className="flex items-center gap-2">
          <input type="checkbox" name="hobby" value={h} /> {h}
        </label>
      ))}
      <button className="rounded-full bg-brand px-5 py-2 text-brand-foreground">
        Save & continue
      </button>
    </form>
  );
}

A Route Handler (route.ts)

When you need a raw HTTP endpoint (webhooks, third-party callbacks), use route.ts with named exports per method. Viby uses one for the Supabase auth callback:

// app/auth/callback/route.ts
import { NextResponse } from "next/server";
import { createClient } from "@/lib/supabase/server";

export async function GET(request: Request) {
  const { searchParams, origin } = new URL(request.url);
  const code = searchParams.get("code");

  if (code) {
    const supabase = await createClient();
    await supabase.auth.exchangeCodeForSession(code);
  }
  return NextResponse.redirect(`${origin}/discover`);
}

A folder can contain either a page.tsx or a route.ts for the same segment — not both. Pages render UI; route handlers return data.

Middleware-style logic with proxy.ts

For cross-cutting logic that runs before a request resolves — refreshing the Supabase session, gating protected routes — Viby uses a root proxy.ts (the modern replacement for the old middleware.ts).

// proxy.ts
import { type NextRequest, NextResponse } from "next/server";
import { createServerClient } from "@supabase/ssr";

export async function proxy(request: NextRequest) {
  const response = NextResponse.next({ request });

  const supabase = createServerClient(
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL!,
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY!,
    {
      cookies: {
        getAll: () => request.cookies.getAll(),
        setAll: (cookiesToSet) => {
          cookiesToSet.forEach(({ name, value, options }) =>
            response.cookies.set(name, value, options)
          );
        },
      },
    }
  );

  // Refresh the session so Server Components always see a fresh token.
  const { data: { user } } = await supabase.auth.getUser();

  if (!user && request.nextUrl.pathname.startsWith("/discover")) {
    return NextResponse.redirect(new URL("/login", request.url));
  }

  return response;
}

export const config = {
  matcher: ["/((?!_next/static|_next/image|favicon.ico).*)"],
};

Architecture / diagram

How a request to /discover flows through Viby:

flowchart TD
  A["Browser requests /discover"] --> B["proxy.ts: refresh Supabase session, gate route"]
  B --> C["app/layout.tsx (root shell)"]
  C --> D["app/(app)/layout.tsx (bottom nav)"]
  D --> E["app/discover/page.tsx (Server Component)"]
  E -- "awaiting data" --> F["loading.tsx skeleton streams first"]
  E -- "queries" --> G[(Supabase / Postgres)]
  E -- "throws" --> H["error.tsx (Client) with Try again"]
  E --> I["HTML streamed to browser"]
  I --> J["Client islands hydrate: LikeButton, motion cards"]

And the folder structure that produces it:

app/
├── layout.tsx            → root layout (html/body, metadata)
├── globals.css           → Tailwind v4 @theme tokens
├── page.tsx              → /  (marketing landing)
├── (app)/                → route group (no URL segment)
│   ├── layout.tsx        → signed-in shell + <BottomNav/>
│   ├── discover/
│   │   ├── page.tsx      → /discover
│   │   ├── loading.tsx   → skeleton
│   │   └── error.tsx     → error boundary (Client)
│   └── profile/
│       └── [username]/
│           └── page.tsx  → /profile/:username
├── onboarding/page.tsx   → Server Action form
└── auth/
    └── callback/route.ts → OAuth code exchange
proxy.ts                  → session refresh + route gating
lib/supabase/{server,client}.ts
components/{vibe-card,like-button,bottom-nav}.tsx

Best practices & pitfalls

Do

  • Keep components on the server by default; push "use client" to the smallest leaf that needs it.
  • Co-locate loading.tsx and error.tsx with the pages they protect — instant UX wins for free.
  • await params (and searchParams) in Next.js 16 — they're Promises now.
  • Use Server Actions for mutations from forms before reaching for a route handler.
  • Put your secrets in env vars and only read them inside server code.

Don't

  • Don't slap "use client" on a layout.tsx or page.tsx — it forces the whole subtree to ship as client JS.
  • Don't import server-only code (the Supabase server client, next/headers) into a Client Component. It'll either error or leak.
  • Don't create a tailwind.config.js — in v4, tokens live in CSS via @theme.
  • Don't reach for useEffect + fetch to load page data; await it in a Server Component instead.
  • Don't name the middleware file middleware.ts in this project — Viby standardizes on proxy.ts.
  • Don't put both page.tsx and route.ts in one folder.

Exercises

  1. Warm-up. Create app/about/page.tsx so visiting /about shows a short "Why Viby exists" blurb. Confirm it inherits the root layout.
  2. Skeletons. Add a loading.tsx to your /profile/[username] route that mimics the profile layout with animate-pulse placeholders.
  3. Dynamic + not-found. In the profile page, call notFound() when no profile matches, then add a sibling not-found.tsx with a friendly "This person ghosted us" message.
  4. Server Action. Build a app/feedback/page.tsx form with a single textarea that writes to a feedback table via a "use server" action, then revalidatePath.
  5. Stretch. Add a route group (marketing) with its own layout (no bottom nav) for /, /about, and /pricing, while keeping (app) for the signed-in screens.

Mini-project: the Viby Discover shell

Build a working /discover route end-to-end:

  1. Create the (app) route group with a layout.tsx that renders children plus a fixed <BottomNav> (Discover · Profile).
  2. Add app/(app)/discover/page.tsx as a Server Component that fetches up to 12 profiles from Supabase and renders them in a responsive grid of VibeCards (avatar, name, top 3 hobbies as pink pills).
  3. Add a loading.tsx with shimmering skeleton cards.
  4. Add an error.tsx (Client) with a Try again button wired to reset().
  5. Make each VibeCard include a client LikeButton using Framer Motion's whileTap and a heart pop animation.
  6. Wire proxy.ts to redirect signed-out users hitting /discover to /login.

When you're done you'll have touched every special file in the App Router — and it'll look and feel like a real product, not a tutorial.

Key takeaways

  • A framework trades a little freedom for assembled, battle-tested defaults — Next.js is "React, with the car already built."
  • In the App Router, folders are routes and components are Server Components by default.
  • Master the five special files: page (UI), layout (persistent shell), loading (Suspense skeleton), error (Client boundary), route (HTTP endpoint).
  • Reach for "use client" only at small interactive leaves; keep data fetching on the server with await.
  • Use modern APIs: async params, Server Actions ("use server"), @supabase/ssr with getAll/setAll, Tailwind v4 @theme, and proxy.ts instead of middleware.ts.

Up next — Chapter 7: Styling with Tailwind CSS v4, where we turn these bare bones into the polished bubblegum-pink Viby aesthetic with design tokens, dark mode, and reusable UI primitives.

Chapter 7 — Routing, Layouts & Navigation

A great app feels like one room you never leave — you move, things change, but the walls, the lights, and your friends stay put. That's routing done right.

By now Viby has a brand, components, and state. But a social app is a place — and places have rooms: a Discover feed, a profile, a chat, a settings drawer. This chapter is where Viby stops being a single page and becomes a navigable product.

What you'll learn

  • How the App Router maps folders to URLs, and why that's a superpower
  • Nested layouts that persist across navigation (no flicker, no re-mount)
  • Route groups (app) to share a shell without polluting the URL
  • Dynamic routes with async params in Next.js 16 (await in Server Components, use() in Client Components)
  • Client navigation with <Link>, programmatic moves with useRouter, and redirect() on the server
  • Building the real Viby (app) shell + mobile tab bar with an animated active indicator

Prerequisites

  • Chapter 3 — Next.js 16 App Router fundamentals (Server vs Client Components)
  • Chapter 4 — Tailwind CSS v4 @theme tokens and the Viby brand palette
  • Chapter 5 — shadcn-style UI primitives (Button, cn helper)
  • Chapter 6 — Framer Motion basics (motion, layout animations)

Theory

Folders are your router

In the App Router, the file system is the route table. There is no routes.tsx, no <Route path=...> config. You create a folder, drop a page.tsx inside, and that folder's path becomes a URL.

app/discover/page.tsx   →  /discover
app/profile/page.tsx    →  /profile

Think of it like a building. Each folder is a room, and page.tsx is the furniture inside that room — the unique content you came to see. A layout.tsx is the room's architecture: the walls, the doorway, the lighting that wrap whatever furniture is inside.

Layouts persist; pages swap

Here's the idea that makes the App Router click: layouts don't re-render when you navigate between their children. The root layout wraps everything. A nested layout wraps a subtree. When you move from /discover to /profile, if both live under the same layout, that layout stays mounted — only the page swaps out.

This is why a music app can keep playing while you browse, and why Viby's tab bar won't flicker or reset as you hop between tabs. The shell is stable; the contents flow through it.

RootLayout  (html, body, fonts, theme — never unmounts)
  └─ (app) layout  (top bar + bottom tab bar — stays put)
        └─ page  (Discover / Profile / Chat — this is what changes)

Route groups: structure without URL noise

Sometimes you want files organized together and sharing a layout, but you don't want that folder name to appear in the URL. Enter the route group: a folder wrapped in parentheses, like (app).

(app) is invisible to the URL. app/(app)/discover/page.tsx is still just /discover. We use it to draw a boundary: "everything in here is the logged-in app shell — give it the tab bar." Meanwhile app/(auth)/login/page.tsx lives in a different group with a clean, chrome-free layout. Same URL space, two completely different shells.

Dynamic routes and the async-params shift

A profile page can't have one folder per user. Instead we use a dynamic segment: a folder named [username]. Anything matching that slot — /u/maya, /u/devon — renders the same page.tsx, and the matched value arrives as params.

The big Next.js 16 change: params is now a Promise. Routing values resolve asynchronously so the framework can stream and stay fast. In a Server Component you await it; in a Client Component you unwrap it with React's use() hook. Memorize that fork — it trips up everyone migrating from older Next.

Three ways to move

  • <Link> — declarative, for things users click. Prefetches in the background; feels instant.
  • useRouter() — imperative, for code-driven moves ("after this save, go to the new chat"). Client-only.
  • redirect() — server-side, for guarding routes ("not logged in? bounce them to /login"). Throws, so put it before your JSX.

In practice

1. The route map for Viby

app/
├─ layout.tsx                 # RootLayout: html/body, fonts, theme
├─ page.tsx                   # marketing landing  →  /
├─ (auth)/
│  ├─ layout.tsx              # centered, no tab bar
│  └─ login/page.tsx          # →  /login
└─ (app)/
   ├─ layout.tsx              # APP SHELL: top bar + bottom tab bar
   ├─ discover/page.tsx       # →  /discover
   ├─ chat/page.tsx           # →  /chat
   ├─ profile/page.tsx        # →  /profile   (your own)
   └─ u/[username]/page.tsx   # →  /u/maya    (anyone's)

2. The root layout

Defined once. Fonts and the global stylesheet (where our @theme tokens live, from Chapter 4) hang here.

// app/layout.tsx
import type { Metadata } from "next";
import "./globals.css";

export const metadata: Metadata = {
  title: "Viby — find your people",
  description: "Make friends through shared hobbies and lifestyle.",
};

export default function RootLayout({
  children,
}: {
  children: React.ReactNode;
}) {
  return (
    <html lang="en">
      <body className="min-h-dvh bg-white text-neutral-900 antialiased">
        {children}
      </body>
    </html>
  );
}

3. The (app) shell layout

This is the heart of the chapter. One layout, shared by every logged-in screen, holding the persistent tab bar. Because it's a layout, it never unmounts as you switch tabs.

// app/(app)/layout.tsx
import { redirect } from "next/navigation";
import { createClient } from "@/lib/supabase/server";
import { TabBar } from "@/components/nav/tab-bar";

export default async function AppLayout({
  children,
}: {
  children: React.ReactNode;
}) {
  // Server-side guard: no session → bounce to login.
  const supabase = await createClient();
  const {
    data: { user },
  } = await supabase.auth.getUser();
  if (!user) redirect("/login");

  return (
    <div className="mx-auto flex min-h-dvh w-full max-w-md flex-col">
      <header className="sticky top-0 z-10 flex h-14 items-center justify-center border-b border-neutral-100 bg-white/80 backdrop-blur">
        <span className="text-lg font-extrabold tracking-tight text-brand">
          Viby
        </span>
      </header>

      <main className="flex-1 overflow-y-auto pb-20">{children}</main>

      <TabBar />
    </div>
  );
}

Our Supabase server client (introduced in Chapter 6, using @supabase/ssr) looks like this — note the getAll/setAll cookie handlers, the modern API:

// lib/supabase/server.ts
import { createServerClient } from "@supabase/ssr";
import { cookies } from "next/headers";

export async function createClient() {
  const cookieStore = await cookies();

  return createServerClient(
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL!,
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY!,
    {
      cookies: {
        getAll() {
          return cookieStore.getAll();
        },
        setAll(cookiesToSet) {
          try {
            cookiesToSet.forEach(({ name, value, options }) =>
              cookieStore.set(name, value, options)
            );
          } catch {
            // Called from a Server Component — safe to ignore;
            // proxy.ts refreshes the session cookie instead.
          }
        },
      },
    }
  );
}

4. The animated tab bar

A Client Component (it reads the current path and animates). usePathname tells us which tab is active; Framer Motion's shared layoutId slides the pink pill between tabs.

// components/nav/tab-bar.tsx
"use client";

import Link from "next/link";
import { usePathname } from "next/navigation";
import { motion } from "framer-motion";
import { Compass, MessageCircle, User } from "lucide-react";
import { cn } from "@/lib/utils";

const TABS = [
  { href: "/discover", label: "Discover", Icon: Compass },
  { href: "/chat", label: "Chat", Icon: MessageCircle },
  { href: "/profile", label: "You", Icon: User },
] as const;

export function TabBar() {
  const pathname = usePathname();

  return (
    <nav className="fixed inset-x-0 bottom-0 z-20 mx-auto flex w-full max-w-md items-center justify-around border-t border-neutral-100 bg-white/90 px-2 py-2 backdrop-blur">
      {TABS.map(({ href, label, Icon }) => {
        const active = pathname === href || pathname.startsWith(href + "/");
        return (
          <Link
            key={href}
            href={href}
            className="relative flex flex-1 flex-col items-center gap-0.5 py-1.5 text-xs font-medium"
          >
            {active && (
              <motion.span
                layoutId="tab-pill"
                className="absolute inset-0 rounded-2xl bg-brand/10"
                transition={{ type: "spring", stiffness: 500, damping: 35 }}
              />
            )}
            <Icon
              className={cn(
                "relative size-6 transition-colors",
                active ? "text-brand" : "text-neutral-400"
              )}
            />
            <span
              className={cn(
                "relative",
                active ? "text-brand" : "text-neutral-500"
              )}
            >
              {label}
            </span>
          </Link>
        );
      })}
    </nav>
  );
}

A reminder from Chapter 4 — text-brand and bg-brand/10 come from our token, defined in CSS, no tailwind.config.js:

/* app/globals.css */
@import "tailwindcss";

@theme {
  --color-brand: #ff6fb5;
}

5. Dynamic profile route — async params in a Server Component

// app/(app)/u/[username]/page.tsx
import { notFound } from "next/navigation";
import { createClient } from "@/lib/supabase/server";

export default async function ProfilePage({
  params,
}: {
  params: Promise<{ username: string }>;
}) {
  const { username } = await params; // ← await: params is a Promise

  const supabase = await createClient();
  const { data: profile } = await supabase
    .from("profiles")
    .select("username, display_name, bio, vibes")
    .eq("username", username)
    .single();

  if (!profile) notFound();

  return (
    <section className="p-5">
      <h1 className="text-2xl font-extrabold">{profile.display_name}</h1>
      <p className="text-sm text-neutral-500">@{profile.username}</p>
      <p className="mt-3 text-neutral-800">{profile.bio}</p>
      <ul className="mt-4 flex flex-wrap gap-2">
        {profile.vibes?.map((v: string) => (
          <li
            key={v}
            className="rounded-full bg-brand/10 px-3 py-1 text-sm text-brand"
          >
            {v}
          </li>
        ))}
      </ul>
    </section>
  );
}

6. Same params, but in a Client Component — use()

When a route needs client interactivity and still wants params, unwrap the Promise with React's use():

// app/(app)/u/[username]/edit/page.tsx
"use client";

import { use, useState } from "react";

export default function EditVibes({
  params,
}: {
  params: Promise<{ username: string }>;
}) {
  const { username } = use(params); // ← use(): unwrap in Client Components
  const [draft, setDraft] = useState("");

  return (
    <form className="p-5">
      <h1 className="font-bold">Editing @{username}</h1>
      <input
        value={draft}
        onChange={(e) => setDraft(e.target.value)}
        className="mt-3 w-full rounded-xl border border-neutral-200 p-2"
        placeholder="Add a vibe…"
      />
    </form>
  );
}

7. Programmatic navigation with useRouter

Use this when code decides to move — e.g. after starting a chat with someone.

// components/profile/start-chat-button.tsx
"use client";

import { useRouter } from "next/navigation";
import { useTransition } from "react";
import { Button } from "@/components/ui/button";
import { createChat } from "@/app/(app)/chat/actions";

export function StartChatButton({ targetId }: { targetId: string }) {
  const router = useRouter();
  const [pending, startTransition] = useTransition();

  function handleClick() {
    startTransition(async () => {
      const chatId = await createChat(targetId); // Server Action
      router.push(`/chat/${chatId}`); // jump into the new conversation
    });
  }

  return (
    <Button onClick={handleClick} disabled={pending}>
      {pending ? "Starting…" : "Say hi"}
    </Button>
  );
}

The matching Server Action — note the "use server" directive:

// app/(app)/chat/actions.ts
"use server";

import { createClient } from "@/lib/supabase/server";

export async function createChat(targetId: string): Promise<string> {
  const supabase = await createClient();
  const {
    data: { user },
  } = await supabase.auth.getUser();

  const { data, error } = await supabase
    .from("chats")
    .insert({ a: user!.id, b: targetId })
    .select("id")
    .single();

  if (error) throw new Error(error.message);
  return data.id;
}

8. Session-refresh logic in proxy.ts (not middleware.ts)

In Next.js 16, the project-root proxy.ts replaces the old middleware.ts for request-time logic. We use it to keep the Supabase session cookie fresh on every navigation.

// proxy.ts
import { createServerClient } from "@supabase/ssr";
import { NextResponse, type NextRequest } from "next/server";

export async function proxy(request: NextRequest) {
  const response = NextResponse.next({ request });

  const supabase = createServerClient(
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL!,
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY!,
    {
      cookies: {
        getAll() {
          return request.cookies.getAll();
        },
        setAll(cookiesToSet) {
          cookiesToSet.forEach(({ name, value, options }) =>
            response.cookies.set(name, value, options)
          );
        },
      },
    }
  );

  await supabase.auth.getUser(); // refreshes the token if expired
  return response;
}

export const config = {
  matcher: ["/((?!_next/static|_next/image|favicon.ico).*)"],
};

Architecture / diagram

How a tap on the Profile tab flows through the persistent shell:

flowchart TD
    A["User taps Profile tab"] --> B["&lt;Link href='/profile'&gt; intercepts<br/>(client-side, no full reload)"]
    B --> C{"Already inside<br/>(app) layout?"}
    C -- "Yes" --> D["(app) layout STAYS mounted<br/>tab bar + header persist"]
    C -- "No (cold load)" --> E["RootLayout → (app) layout render<br/>redirect() guard runs"]
    D --> F["Only page.tsx swaps:<br/>discover/page → profile/page"]
    E --> F
    F --> G["usePathname() updates<br/>Framer Motion slides pink pill"]

URL space            File system                         Shell applied
──────────           ───────────                         ─────────────
/login        ←→     app/(auth)/login/page.tsx           centered, no tabs
/discover     ←→     app/(app)/discover/page.tsx         app shell + tabs
/u/maya       ←→     app/(app)/u/[username]/page.tsx     app shell + tabs
                            └── (app) and (auth) vanish from the URL

Best practices & pitfalls

Do

  • Put shared, stable chrome (tab bar, header) in a layout — never re-render it per page.
  • Guard protected subtrees with redirect() in the (app)/layout.tsx, and keep proxy.ts refreshing the session.
  • Type dynamic params as Promise<{ … }> and await (server) or use() (client).
  • Use <Link> for anything clickable — you get prefetching for free.
  • Call notFound() when a dynamic record doesn't exist; it renders your not-found.tsx.

Don't

  • Don't reach for useRouter().push() where a <Link> works — you lose prefetch and accessibility.
  • Don't destructure params synchronously ({ params: { username } }) — that's the old API and now throws.
  • Don't create a middleware.ts in Next.js 16 — use proxy.ts.
  • Don't add a tailwind.config.js for tokens — define them in @theme (Chapter 4).
  • Don't call redirect() inside a try/catch that swallows errors — it works by throwing.
  • Don't forget redirect() returns never; place it before any JSX so TypeScript narrows correctly.

Exercises

  1. Settings tab (easy). Add app/(app)/settings/page.tsx and a fourth tab to TabBar with a Settings icon. Confirm the active pill animates to it.
  2. Loading UI (easy–medium). Add app/(app)/u/[username]/loading.tsx showing a skeleton card while the profile query resolves. Throttle your network and watch it appear.
  3. Not-found (medium). Create app/(app)/u/[username]/not-found.tsx with a friendly "No Viber by that name" message and a <Link> back to /discover.
  4. Search params (medium). On /discover, read searchParams (also a Promise now) to support /discover?vibe=hiking, filtering the feed server-side.
  5. Nested layout (hard). Give /chat its own nested layout.tsx with a conversations sidebar that persists while /chat/[chatId] pages swap inside it.

Mini-project — The Viby app shell

Ship the navigable skeleton of Viby end to end.

Goal: a logged-in user can tap between Discover, Chat, and You; the shell never flickers; tapping a profile card opens /u/[username]; visiting any (app) route while logged out bounces to /login.

Build steps

  1. Scaffold the (app) and (auth) route groups exactly as in the route map above.
  2. Implement app/(app)/layout.tsx with the Supabase guard + redirect("/login") and mount <TabBar />.
  3. Drop in the animated TabBar with the layoutId="tab-pill" indicator.
  4. Build three stub pages (discover, chat, profile) — even just an <h1> each — to prove the shell persists across navigation.
  5. Add the dynamic u/[username]/page.tsx reading async params and querying profiles.
  6. On discover, render a few profile cards as <Link href={/u/${p.username}}>.
  7. Wire proxy.ts for session refresh.

Done when: switching tabs animates the pink pill with zero layout flash, the header/tab bar stay mounted (drop a console.log in TabBar — it should log once, not per navigation), and a logged-out visit to /discover lands on /login.

Key takeaways

  • Folders are routes; page.tsx is content; layout.tsx is persistent chrome.
  • Layouts don't re-mount on navigation — that's what keeps Viby's shell stable.
  • Route groups (app) / (auth) share layouts without touching the URL.
  • params is a Promise in Next.js 16 — await on the server, use() on the client.
  • <Link> to click, useRouter to move in code, redirect() to guard on the server.
  • Use proxy.ts, not middleware.ts, for request-time session work.

Up next — Chapter 8: Data Fetching & Server Components, where those stub pages start pulling real Vibers from Supabase, with caching, streaming, and loading states that feel instant.

Chapter 8 — Server vs Client Components & Data

The single biggest "aha" of the App Router: most of your UI never has to ship to the browser at all.

What you'll learn

  • Why Server Components are the default in Next.js 16, and what "use client" actually does.
  • How to decide — on a per-component basis — whether something runs on the server or in the browser.
  • How to fetch data directly inside Server Components (no useEffect, no loading spinner gymnastics).
  • How to pass data across the server → client boundary, and the serialization rules that govern it.
  • How to compose the two worlds: server shells with small interactive client "islands."
  • The real performance and security wins — and the traps that bite beginners.

Prerequisites

  • Chapter 4 (TypeScript fundamentals) — we lean on typed props.
  • Chapter 6 (Next.js App Router, routing & layouts) — you should know what a page.tsx and layout.tsx are.
  • Chapter 7 (Tailwind v4 & our UI primitives) — we'll style with @theme tokens.
  • A running Viby project with Supabase wired via @supabase/ssr (set up at the end of Chapter 6).

Theory

Imagine Viby's Discover page: a grid of people near you who share your hobbies. Some of that page is static structure — headings, a profile card's name, avatar, and bio, the shared-interest tags. Other parts are alive — a "Wave 👋" button that reacts to your tap, a follow toggle, a hover animation.

For years, React shipped all of that to the browser as JavaScript. The browser downloaded your components, re-ran them, and re-built the DOM the server had already described. We paid the "JavaScript tax" even on parts that never change after they render.

React Server Components (RSC) split the work. Think of it like a restaurant kitchen vs. the dining table:

  • The kitchen (server) does the heavy prep: it talks to the database, reads secrets, assembles the dish. Customers never see the kitchen, and the kitchen's tools (knives, the safe with the recipes) never leave it.
  • The table (client/browser) is where interaction happens: you cut your food, react, ask for more. It needs cutlery (JavaScript), but only for the things you actually touch.

In Next.js 16's App Router, every component is a Server Component by default. It runs once, on the server, during the request. Its output isn't HTML strings and it isn't "a bundle" — it's a serialized description of the UI (the RSC payload) that React streams to the browser and stitches into the page. That component's code never ships to the browser.

When you need real interactivity — state, effects, event handlers, browser APIs — you opt a component into the client with the "use client" directive at the top of the file. That marks the boundary: this component, and everything it imports, becomes part of the browser bundle.

The mental model that matters

"use client" is not "this code runs only in the browser." It's a boundary marker. A client component still pre-renders on the server for the initial HTML (that's SSR), then hydrates in the browser so it can become interactive. The directive's real job is: "from here down, ship the JavaScript and wire up interactivity."

Two rules follow from this, and they trip up everyone at first:

  1. Server Components can import and render Client Components. (Kitchen plates the dish, then hands it to the table.)
  2. Client Components cannot import Server Components — but they can receive them as children / props. (The table can't reach into the kitchen, but the kitchen can place something on the table.)

The serialization boundary

When a Server Component passes props into a Client Component, those props cross the network. They get serialized (turned into the RSC payload), shipped, and deserialized in the browser. So props must be serializable: strings, numbers, booleans, null, arrays, plain objects, Date, Map, Set, and — importantly — other Server Components passed as children.

What you cannot pass across that boundary: functions (except Server Actions, which we'll meet), class instances, Symbols, database clients, anything with methods. Pass the data, not the machinery.

In practice

Let's build a slice of Viby's Discover page the right way.

A Server Component that fetches data

No "use client", no useEffect, no spinner. We await the data right in the component. This file's code never reaches the browser — which is exactly why it's safe to use the server-side Supabase client (with cookies) here.

// app/discover/page.tsx  — Server Component (default)
import { createClient } from "@/lib/supabase/server";
import { ProfileCard } from "@/components/profile-card";

export default async function DiscoverPage() {
  const supabase = await createClient();

  const { data: profiles } = await supabase
    .from("profiles")
    .select("id, display_name, bio, avatar_url, interests")
    .limit(24);

  return (
    <main className="mx-auto max-w-5xl px-4 py-8">
      <h1 className="text-2xl font-bold text-ink">Find your people</h1>
      <p className="mt-1 text-muted">People near you who share your vibe.</p>

      <ul className="mt-6 grid grid-cols-1 gap-4 sm:grid-cols-2 lg:grid-cols-3">
        {profiles?.map((p) => (
          <li key={p.id}>
            <ProfileCard profile={p} />
          </li>
        ))}
      </ul>
    </main>
  );
}

Here's the server Supabase client it uses — note the getAll/setAll cookie handlers required by @supabase/ssr:

// lib/supabase/server.ts
import { createServerClient } from "@supabase/ssr";
import { cookies } from "next/headers";

export async function createClient() {
  const cookieStore = await cookies(); // async in Next.js 16

  return createServerClient(
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL!,
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY!,
    {
      cookies: {
        getAll() {
          return cookieStore.getAll();
        },
        setAll(cookiesToSet) {
          try {
            cookiesToSet.forEach(({ name, value, options }) =>
              cookieStore.set(name, value, options)
            );
          } catch {
            // Called from a Server Component render — safe to ignore;
            // cookie writes happen in Server Actions / proxy.ts.
          }
        },
      },
    }
  );
}

A mostly-server card with a small client island

ProfileCard is a Server Component — it just renders data. The only interactive bit, the Wave button, is a separate Client Component. We keep the JavaScript footprint tiny by isolating interactivity.

// components/profile-card.tsx  — Server Component
import { WaveButton } from "@/components/wave-button";

type Profile = {
  id: string;
  display_name: string;
  bio: string | null;
  avatar_url: string | null;
  interests: string[];
};

export function ProfileCard({ profile }: { profile: Profile }) {
  return (
    <article className="rounded-2xl border border-black/5 bg-white p-5 shadow-sm">
      <div className="flex items-center gap-3">
        <img
          src={profile.avatar_url ?? "/avatar-fallback.png"}
          alt={profile.display_name}
          className="h-12 w-12 rounded-full object-cover"
        />
        <h2 className="font-semibold text-ink">{profile.display_name}</h2>
      </div>

      {profile.bio && <p className="mt-3 text-sm text-muted">{profile.bio}</p>}

      <ul className="mt-3 flex flex-wrap gap-2">
        {profile.interests.map((tag) => (
          <li
            key={tag}
            className="rounded-full bg-brand/10 px-2.5 py-1 text-xs font-medium text-brand"
          >
            {tag}
          </li>
        ))}
      </ul>

      {/* Pass plain, serializable data across the boundary */}
      <WaveButton profileId={profile.id} name={profile.display_name} />
    </article>
  );
}

The Client Component (the island)

"use client" sits at the very top. This component owns state, an event handler, and a Framer Motion gesture — none of which can exist in a Server Component.

// components/wave-button.tsx
"use client";

import { useState } from "react";
import { motion } from "framer-motion";
import { wave } from "@/app/discover/actions";

export function WaveButton({
  profileId,
  name,
}: {
  profileId: string;
  name: string;
}) {
  const [waved, setWaved] = useState(false);

  async function handleWave() {
    setWaved(true); // optimistic UI
    await wave(profileId); // calls a Server Action
  }

  return (
    <motion.button
      whileTap={{ scale: 0.92 }}
      onClick={handleWave}
      disabled={waved}
      aria-label={`Wave at ${name}`}
      className="mt-4 w-full rounded-xl bg-brand py-2.5 font-semibold text-white disabled:opacity-60"
    >
      {waved ? "Waved 👋" : "Wave 👋"}
    </motion.button>
  );
}

The bridge back: a Server Action

How does the client send data back? Not by importing the database client (that would leak secrets into the bundle). Instead it calls a Server Action — a function marked "use server" that runs on the server but can be called like a normal async function from the client. This is the one kind of "function" allowed to cross the boundary.

// app/discover/actions.ts
"use server";

import { createClient } from "@/lib/supabase/server";
import { revalidatePath } from "next/cache";

export async function wave(targetProfileId: string) {
  const supabase = await createClient();

  const {
    data: { user },
  } = await supabase.auth.getUser();
  if (!user) throw new Error("Not signed in");

  await supabase.from("waves").insert({
    from_user: user.id,
    to_user: targetProfileId,
  });

  revalidatePath("/discover");
}

Passing a Server Component as children (composition pattern)

When you do need a client wrapper (say, an animated container) around server-rendered content, don't drag the content into the client bundle. Pass it as children:

// components/reveal.tsx
"use client";
import { motion } from "framer-motion";

export function Reveal({ children }: { children: React.ReactNode }) {
  return (
    <motion.div
      initial={{ opacity: 0, y: 12 }}
      whileInView={{ opacity: 1, y: 0 }}
      viewport={{ once: true }}
    >
      {children}
    </motion.div>
  );
}

// usage in a Server Component — ProfileCard stays on the server
<Reveal>
  <ProfileCard profile={p} />
</Reveal>

Reveal is a client island, but ProfileCard it wraps is still rendered on the server and slotted in as already-finished UI. Best of both worlds.

Architecture / diagram

flowchart TD
  subgraph Server["🖳 Server (Next.js + Supabase)"]
    A["DiscoverPage (Server Component)\nawait supabase.from('profiles')"]
    B["ProfileCard (Server Component)\nrenders name, bio, tags"]
    SA["wave() Server Action\n'use server'"]
    DB[("PostgreSQL\nRLS-protected")]
  end

  subgraph Wire["⇅ Network boundary (RSC payload)"]
    P["Serialized props:\nprofileId, name (strings)"]
  end

  subgraph Client["🖥 Browser"]
    W["WaveButton (Client Component)\n'use client' — useState, onClick"]
  end

  DB --> A
  A --> B
  B -- "serializable props" --> P
  P --> W
  W -- "calls" --> SA
  SA --> DB

The dashed lesson: data flows down through serialization; actions flow back up through the "use server" channel. Database access never crosses into the browser box.

Best practices & pitfalls

Do

  • Keep "use client" as low in the tree as possible. Push interactivity to leaf "islands" (a button, a toggle), not whole pages.
  • Fetch in Server Components with await. It's simpler, faster, and keeps queries off the network-tab radar.
  • Pass only serializable data across the boundary — plain objects and primitives.
  • Use Server Actions ("use server") for mutations instead of building API routes for every form.
  • Put secrets and the service-role Supabase client server-side only. Anything not prefixed NEXT_PUBLIC_ stays out of the bundle.

Don't

  • Don't slap "use client" at the top of a layout or page "just in case." It turns the whole subtree into client code and kills the RSC win.
  • Don't await data inside a Client Component — you can't make a client component async. Fetch on the server and pass it down, or use a data library / use() on a passed-in promise.
  • Don't pass functions, class instances, or a Supabase client as props to a client component. They aren't serializable. (Server Actions are the only exception.)
  • Don't reach for useEffect + fetch for initial data. That's the old SPA pattern; it reintroduces spinners and waterfalls the App Router eliminates.
  • Don't read params/searchParams synchronously. In Next.js 16 they're async: const { id } = await params; (or use(params) in a client component).

Exercises

  1. Spot the boundary. Open Viby's profile page. List which parts must be client (need state/events/browser APIs) and which can stay server. Justify each.
  2. Shrink the bundle. Find a component in your project that has "use client" but contains a large chunk of non-interactive markup. Split the interactive bit into its own island and keep the rest on the server.
  3. Server fetch. Build app/profile/[id]/page.tsx as an async Server Component that reads id via await params and fetches that profile from Supabase.
  4. Serialization error on purpose. Try passing a function as a prop from a Server Component to a Client Component. Read the error Next.js gives you, then fix it by converting the function into a Server Action.
  5. Composition challenge (hard). Wrap a server-rendered list in an AnimatePresence client wrapper so items animate on mount — without moving the list items into the client bundle.

Mini-project: "Vibe Match" spotlight card

Build a single-card feature for the Discover page that shows one suggested person and lets the user act on it.

Requirements

  1. app/discover/spotlight/page.tsx — a Server Component that fetches one random profile from Supabase (profiles table) and renders it. No client directive in this file.
  2. <MatchActions /> — a Client Component island with two buttons: "Wave 👋" and "Skip". Use useState to track which action was taken and a Framer Motion whileTap gesture for feedback.
  3. waveAction / skipAction — Server Actions ("use server") that record the choice in Supabase and call revalidatePath("/discover/spotlight") so the next render shows a fresh face.
  4. Boundary discipline — the page passes only { id, display_name, avatar_url } (plain serializable props) into <MatchActions />. No Supabase client crosses the line.
  5. Polish — style with your @theme tokens (bg-brand, text-ink), and wrap the card in a Reveal client component so it fades in on mount while staying server-rendered inside.

Stretch: add an optimistic "It's a vibe!" toast that appears instantly on Wave, before the Server Action resolves.

You'll know it works when: the page loads with zero loading spinner, the network tab shows no client-side profile fetch, and the buttons still feel snappy.

Key takeaways

  • Server Components are the default; "use client" is an opt-in boundary, not a "run in browser only" switch.
  • Server Components fetch data directly with await and keep secrets and DB access off the client.
  • Props from server → client must be serializable; pass data, not machinery.
  • Server Components can render Client Components; clients receive servers only as children/props.
  • Server Actions ("use server") are the sanctioned channel for sending work back to the server.
  • Keep client islands small and low in the tree to ship less JavaScript and load faster.

Up next — Chapter 9: Data Fetching, Caching & Streaming — loading.tsx, Suspense boundaries, and how Next.js 16 caches and revalidates your Viby feed so it stays fast and fresh.

Chapter 9 — Tailwind CSS & Mobile-First Responsive Design

Your users meet Viby on a 390px phone screen first — so that's where we start too.

What you'll learn

  • Why utility-first CSS beats hand-rolled stylesheets for product teams shipping fast.
  • How Tailwind CSS v4 works with zero config — design tokens live in your CSS via @theme, not a tailwind.config.js.
  • The mobile-first mental model and how Tailwind's responsive breakpoints encode it.
  • The device-frame approach: designing inside a phone shell so every screen feels native on mobile.
  • Building real Viby layouts — a discovery feed, profile cards, and a sticky mobile tab bar — that scale gracefully up to desktop.

Prerequisites

  • Chapter 6 (Next.js 16 App Router) — you should be comfortable with Server vs. Client Components and the app/ directory.
  • Chapter 7 (React 19 fundamentals) — props, composition, use client.
  • Chapter 8 (TypeScript for components) — typed props and tsx files.
  • A running Viby Next.js project with Tailwind v4 installed (we set this up at the end of Chapter 6).

Theory

Why utility-first at all?

Imagine you're decorating a room. The traditional CSS approach is like hiring a carpenter to build a custom piece of furniture for every wall — beautiful, but slow, and the moment you want to rearrange, you're back to the workshop. Over months, your styles.css grows into a graveyard of .card-wrapper-v2-final classes nobody dares delete.

Utility-first is like having a wall of perfectly labelled drawers: flex, gap-4, rounded-2xl, bg-brand. You compose furniture in place, in your markup, from tiny single-purpose pieces. The genius isn't that you type more classes — it's that you stop inventing names and stop context-switching between files. The styling lives where the structure lives.

Three concrete wins for a product team:

  1. No naming fatigue. mt-4 always means the same thing. You never debate whether it's .card__title or .cardTitle.
  2. Dead-code-free by construction. Delete a component and its styles vanish with it — there's no orphaned CSS.
  3. Constrained by design. You pick from a scale (gap-2, gap-4, gap-6), not arbitrary gap: 13px. That constraint is what makes a UI look consistent and "designed."

The honest trade-off: your HTML gets longer. We tame that with components (extract a <Button> once, reuse everywhere) — not by abandoning utilities.

Tailwind v4: the CSS is the config

If you've seen older Tailwind tutorials, burn the tailwind.config.js from your memory. Tailwind v4 is CSS-first. You import the engine and declare your design tokens in a special @theme block right in your stylesheet:

@import "tailwindcss";

@theme {
  --color-brand: #ff6fb5;
}

That single line does something magical: it generates utilities. Because you named a token --color-brand, Tailwind now hands you bg-brand, text-brand, border-brand, ring-brand, and friends — automatically. Tokens are the source; utilities are the output. This is the whole model.

Analogy: @theme is the brand style guide for the whole app. Designers hand you "our pink is #FF6FB5, our base radius is 1rem." You write that down once in CSS, and every utility across the codebase speaks that vocabulary.

Mobile-first: small screen is the default, not an afterthought

Here's the rule that trips up everyone coming from desktop-era CSS:

An unprefixed utility applies to every screen size. A prefixed utility (md:, lg:) applies from that breakpoint up.

So p-4 md:p-8 means: "4 units of padding everywhere, bumping to 8 from the medium breakpoint onward." You never write sm: to target only phones — phones get the unprefixed value. You layer enhancements upward.

Why design up instead of down? A phone is the constrained environment: one column, thumb-reachable, limited width. If your design survives there, widening it is easy — you have more room, not less. Start wide and you'll be forever amputating layouts to fit small screens. Viby is a social app; the overwhelming majority of sessions happen on phones, so mobile-first isn't just tidy — it's where the product actually lives.

Tailwind v4's default breakpoints (defined as --breakpoint-* tokens you can override):

Prefix Min-width Typical device
(none) 0 phones
sm: 40rem large phones / small tablets
md: 48rem tablets
lg: 64rem laptops
xl: 80rem desktops

In practice

1. The Viby theme

This is the heart of your design system. Create app/globals.css:

@import "tailwindcss";

@theme {
  /* Brand */
  --color-brand: #ff6fb5;
  --color-brand-soft: #ffe3f1;
  --color-ink: #1b1320;
  --color-muted: #7a7080;

  /* Radii & shadows tuned for a soft, premium feel */
  --radius-card: 1.25rem;
  --shadow-pop: 0 10px 30px -12px rgb(255 111 181 / 0.45);

  /* A friendly display font (loaded via next/font in layout) */
  --font-display: var(--font-grotesk), system-ui, sans-serif;
}

Now bg-brand, text-ink, rounded-card, shadow-pop, and font-display all exist as utilities — typed nowhere, available everywhere.

2. A reusable Button (extract once, reuse forever)

This is how we keep markup short without leaving utility-land. It's a Server Component — no interactivity needed, so no "use client".

// components/ui/button.tsx
import { type ComponentProps } from "react";

type ButtonProps = ComponentProps<"button"> & {
  variant?: "primary" | "ghost";
};

export function Button({ variant = "primary", className = "", ...props }: ButtonProps) {
  const base =
    "inline-flex h-11 items-center justify-center rounded-full px-6 " +
    "text-sm font-semibold transition active:scale-95 disabled:opacity-50";
  const variants = {
    primary: "bg-brand text-white shadow-pop hover:brightness-105",
    ghost: "bg-brand-soft text-brand hover:bg-brand/15",
  };
  return <button className={`${base} ${variants[variant]} ${className}`} {...props} />;
}

Note the h-11 (44px) min target — that's the accessible minimum tap size for thumbs. Mobile-first thinking shows up in sizing, not just layout.

3. The discovery feed — mobile-first grid

The Viby feed is one column on phones, two on tablets, three on laptops. Watch how the breakpoint prefixes layer upward:

// app/discover/page.tsx  (Server Component by default)
import { PersonCard } from "@/components/person-card";
import { getDiscoveryProfiles } from "@/lib/profiles";

export default async function DiscoverPage() {
  const profiles = await getDiscoveryProfiles();

  return (
    <main className="mx-auto w-full max-w-5xl px-4 pb-24 pt-6">
      <h1 className="font-display text-2xl font-bold text-ink md:text-3xl">
        Find your people
      </h1>
      <p className="mt-1 text-sm text-muted">
        People near you who love what you love.
      </p>

      <section className="mt-6 grid grid-cols-1 gap-4 sm:grid-cols-2 lg:grid-cols-3">
        {profiles.map((p) => (
          <PersonCard key={p.id} profile={p} />
        ))}
      </section>
    </main>
  );
}

grid-cols-1 is the phone default; sm:grid-cols-2 and lg:grid-cols-3 are the upgrades. max-w-5xl plus mx-auto keeps the feed from sprawling on huge monitors. pb-24 reserves room for the mobile tab bar we add below.

4. A profile card with a gesture (Client Component)

Cards have a tap animation and an interest-tag row that scrolls horizontally on small screens. Interactivity + Framer Motion means this one needs "use client".

// components/person-card.tsx
"use client";

import { motion } from "framer-motion";
import Image from "next/image";

type Profile = {
  id: string;
  name: string;
  age: number;
  city: string;
  avatarUrl: string;
  interests: string[];
};

export function PersonCard({ profile }: { profile: Profile }) {
  return (
    <motion.article
      whileTap={{ scale: 0.98 }}
      className="overflow-hidden rounded-card bg-white shadow-pop ring-1 ring-black/5"
    >
      <div className="relative aspect-4/5 w-full">
        <Image
          src={profile.avatarUrl}
          alt={profile.name}
          fill
          sizes="(max-width: 640px) 100vw, 33vw"
          className="object-cover"
        />
        <div className="absolute inset-x-0 bottom-0 bg-gradient-to-t from-ink/70 to-transparent p-4">
          <p className="text-base font-semibold text-white">
            {profile.name}, {profile.age}
          </p>
          <p className="text-xs text-white/80">{profile.city}</p>
        </div>
      </div>

      {/* Tags scroll sideways on phones, wrap-free, no scrollbar */}
      <ul className="flex gap-2 overflow-x-auto p-3 [scrollbar-width:none]">
        {profile.interests.map((tag) => (
          <li
            key={tag}
            className="shrink-0 rounded-full bg-brand-soft px-3 py-1 text-xs font-medium text-brand"
          >
            {tag}
          </li>
        ))}
      </ul>
    </motion.article>
  );
}

Two mobile-first details worth internalizing: aspect-4/5 keeps the image a consistent portrait shape at any width (no layout shift), and the overflow-x-auto tag row is a classic phone pattern — horizontal scroll where vertical space is precious.

5. The sticky mobile tab bar (and why it hides on desktop)

Phones get a thumb-friendly bottom bar; laptops don't need it. fixed + bottom-0 on mobile, lg:hidden to remove it on large screens.

// components/mobile-tab-bar.tsx
import Link from "next/link";
import { Compass, MessageCircle, User } from "lucide-react";

const tabs = [
  { href: "/discover", label: "Discover", icon: Compass },
  { href: "/messages", label: "Messages", icon: MessageCircle },
  { href: "/profile", label: "Profile", icon: User },
];

export function MobileTabBar() {
  return (
    <nav
      className="fixed inset-x-0 bottom-0 z-50 flex justify-around border-t border-black/5
                 bg-white/90 pb-[env(safe-area-inset-bottom)] backdrop-blur lg:hidden"
    >
      {tabs.map(({ href, label, icon: Icon }) => (
        <Link
          key={href}
          href={href}
          className="flex flex-1 flex-col items-center gap-1 py-3 text-muted
                     transition active:text-brand"
        >
          <Icon className="size-5" />
          <span className="text-[11px] font-medium">{label}</span>
        </Link>
      ))}
    </nav>
  );
}

pb-[env(safe-area-inset-bottom)] is a real-world must: it pushes the bar above the iPhone home indicator. This is an arbitrary value — square-bracket syntax for the rare case the design scale doesn't cover.

6. The device-frame approach

When building mobile-first, it helps to develop inside a phone shell so you're never tempted to design for the full browser width. Wrap your dev preview in a frame:

// components/device-frame.tsx
export function DeviceFrame({ children }: { children: React.ReactNode }) {
  return (
    <div className="grid min-h-dvh place-items-center bg-brand-soft p-4 lg:p-8">
      <div
        className="relative h-[844px] w-[390px] overflow-hidden rounded-[2.5rem]
                   border-8 border-ink bg-white shadow-2xl"
      >
        <div className="h-full overflow-y-auto">{children}</div>
      </div>
    </div>
  );
}

390x844 is the iPhone 14 viewport. Building inside this frame keeps your hands honest — but remember it's a development aid, not your production layout. The real app uses fluid widths and the breakpoints above; the frame just disciplines your eye.

Architecture / diagram

How a token becomes a responsive, rendered pixel:

flowchart TD
    A["@theme in globals.css<br/>--color-brand: #ff6fb5"] --> B["Tailwind v4 engine<br/>generates utilities"]
    B --> C["bg-brand · text-brand · ring-brand · ..."]
    C --> D["JSX className<br/>grid-cols-1 sm:grid-cols-2 lg:grid-cols-3"]
    D --> E{"Viewport width?"}
    E -->|"< 40rem (phone)"| F["1 column · tab bar visible"]
    E -->|"40–64rem (tablet)"| G["2 columns"]
    E -->|"≥ 64rem (laptop)"| H["3 columns · tab bar hidden"]

The flow is one-directional: tokens → utilities → markup → breakpoint-resolved layout. Change the token, and every screen updates.

Best practices & pitfalls

  • Do start every layout from the unprefixed (phone) state, then add md:/lg: enhancements. Don't write desktop CSS and patch downward.
  • Do define design decisions as @theme tokens (--color-brand, --radius-card). Don't scatter hex codes and pixel values across components.
  • Don't reach for arbitrary values like mt-[13px] reflexively — they're an escape hatch, not the norm. Stick to the scale; it's what keeps the UI coherent.
  • Do extract repeated utility clusters into components (<Button>, <PersonCard>). Don't copy-paste 12-class strings — and don't prematurely abstract @apply either; component composition is the v4 idiom.
  • Do size tap targets at h-11/size-11 (44px) minimum and honor env(safe-area-inset-*) on phones.
  • Don't forget "use client" is only for interactivity (motion, hooks, events). Keep layout components on the server — Framer Motion and event handlers force client mode, plain markup does not.
  • Do test at real breakpoints (resize the browser, use device toolbar). The device frame is a guide, not a guarantee.
  • Don't misuse sm: thinking it means "small/phones." It means "small breakpoint and up."

Exercises

  1. Token swap. Add a --color-brand-deep: #d94f93 token and use it for :hover on the primary Button. Confirm hover:bg-brand-deep works with zero config changes.
  2. Two-up bio. On the profile page, make the avatar and bio stack vertically on phones but sit side-by-side from md: up using flex-col md:flex-row.
  3. Responsive type. Make the Find your people heading text-2xl on phones, text-3xl on md, and text-4xl on lg. Eyeball it in the device toolbar.
  4. Hide & show. Add a left sidebar nav that is hidden lg:block, complementing the mobile tab bar that is lg:hidden. Only one should ever be visible.
  5. Safe-area polish. Add pt-[env(safe-area-inset-top)] to a sticky top header and verify it doesn't collide with a notch in the device frame.

Mini-project — the Viby Discover screen

Build a complete, responsive Discover screen that ties the chapter together.

Requirements

  1. Add the full Viby @theme block (Section 1) to app/globals.css.
  2. Build PersonCard (Section 4) and render a feed of at least 6 mock profiles in the responsive grid (Section 3): 1 column on phones, 2 on tablets, 3 on laptops.
  3. Add the MobileTabBar (Section 5), fixed to the bottom and hidden from lg: up. Reserve space with pb-24 on the feed so cards aren't covered.
  4. During development, wrap the page in DeviceFrame to verify the phone experience first, then remove it and resize the real browser to confirm it scales to 2 and 3 columns.

Stretch: add a horizontally-scrolling "interest filter" chip row above the feed (sticky on scroll with sticky top-0), reusing the tag styling from PersonCard.

You'll know you're done when the screen feels like a native app at 390px and a polished web app at 1280px — from the same markup.

Key takeaways

  • Utility-first trades long markup for zero naming, zero dead CSS, and built-in design constraints.
  • Tailwind v4 is CSS-first: declare tokens in @theme, and utilities are generated automatically — no tailwind.config.js.
  • Mobile-first means unprefixed = phone; md:/lg: layer enhancements upward. sm: means "and up," never "phones only."
  • The device-frame approach keeps your eye honest during development without dictating production layout.
  • Real Viby layouts — responsive grids, gesture cards, safe-area tab bars — all flow from the same tokens.

Up next — Chapter 10: shadcn-style UI Primitives & Framer Motion, where we turn these raw utilities into a polished, animated component library Viby can ship on.

Chapter 10 — Reusable Components, shadcn Patterns & Framer Motion

One great <Button /> beats a hundred copy-pasted divs — this is the chapter where Viby stops looking like a tutorial and starts feeling like a real product.

What you'll learn

  • How to design a component API that's a joy to use: small, predictable, composable.
  • How to build variants with cva (class-variance-authority) and merge classes safely with cn().
  • How to compose primitives — Button, Chip, Avatar — that the rest of Viby is built on.
  • How to add premium motion with Framer Motion: motion components, AnimatePresence for mount/unmount, gestures (whileHover/whileTap), and layout animations.
  • When a component should be a Server Component and when it earns a "use client".

Prerequisites

  • Chapter 6 (Tailwind CSS v4 and the @theme tokens that define our bubblegum-pink brand).
  • Chapter 7 (TypeScript fundamentals — generics, unions, and React.ComponentProps).
  • Chapter 9 (the Next.js 16 App Router: Server Components by default, "use client" only when needed).

You should have the Viby app running locally with the brand tokens already defined in globals.css.

Theory

Why components at all?

Imagine you run a bakery. You could hand-pipe every cupcake from scratch, but you'd never ship a hundred a day with a consistent look. Instead you build a piping nozzle — one tool, used everywhere, that guarantees the same swirl every time. A reusable component is that nozzle. You design the interface once (the shape of the swirl), and every cupcake in the shop inherits it for free.

A good component has a narrow, honest API: the props you can pass are exactly the things that should change, and nothing else. If your Button accepts twelve boolean flags, callers will combine them in nonsensical ways. If it accepts one variant prop with a closed set of values, the design system stays coherent.

The two axes of a component API

Every primitive varies along two axes:

  1. Appearance — discrete, designer-chosen looks: primary, secondary, ghost, sizes sm/md/lg. These are enumerable. You want autocomplete and type errors when someone passes variant="purple".
  2. Behaviour & passthrough — everything a native element already does: onClick, disabled, aria-label, type. You should not re-invent these; you forward them.

cva handles axis 1. React.ComponentProps<"button"> handles axis 2.

Why cva + cn()?

Tailwind is utility classes as strings. Strings are easy to mess up: duplicate classes, conflicting ones (px-2 px-4 — who wins?), and sprawling ternaries. Two small libraries fix this:

  • cva (class-variance-authority) turns "variant → class string" into a typed, declarative map. Think of it as a recipe card: base ingredients plus a table of variations.
  • cn() is our tiny helper that runs clsx (conditional joining) through tailwind-merge (last-conflicting-class-wins). So cn("px-2", "px-4") correctly yields "px-4". It's the referee that resolves class fights.

Composition over configuration

The deepest idea in this chapter: build small primitives, then compose them. A profile card isn't a giant <ProfileCard configEverything />. It's an Avatar, a few Chips, and a Button, arranged together. Small pieces compose into infinite layouts; one giant configurable component composes into nothing.

Why animate?

Motion is feedback. When a user taps "Follow" on Viby and the button springs slightly, their brain registers "it worked" before any network call returns. Framer Motion gives us this with almost no code — and crucially, it animates enter and exit (plain CSS can't animate an element that's being removed from the DOM). That's what makes a Gen-Z social app feel alive instead of clinical.

In practice

The cn() helper

This lives once, in src/lib/utils.ts, and is imported everywhere.

// src/lib/utils.ts
import { clsx, type ClassValue } from "clsx";
import { twMerge } from "tailwind-merge";

export function cn(...inputs: ClassValue[]) {
  return twMerge(clsx(inputs));
}

Install the deps:

npm install class-variance-authority clsx tailwind-merge framer-motion

Brand tokens (recap from Chapter 6)

Our variants reference these Tailwind v4 theme tokens. No tailwind.config.js — it's all CSS:

/* src/app/globals.css */
@import "tailwindcss";

@theme {
  --color-viby: #ff6fb5;          /* bubblegum pink */
  --color-viby-ink: #1b1320;      /* near-black text */
  --color-viby-soft: #fff0f7;     /* pink tint surface */
  --radius-pill: 9999px;
}

These generate utilities like bg-viby, text-viby-ink, and rounded-pill automatically.

The Button primitive

A Server Component-safe button (no hooks, no state) using cva:

// src/components/ui/button.tsx
import { cva, type VariantProps } from "class-variance-authority";
import { cn } from "@/lib/utils";

const buttonVariants = cva(
  // base: applies to every button
  "inline-flex items-center justify-center gap-2 font-medium whitespace-nowrap " +
    "transition-colors focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 " +
    "focus-visible:ring-viby/50 disabled:pointer-events-none disabled:opacity-50",
  {
    variants: {
      variant: {
        primary: "bg-viby text-white hover:bg-viby/90",
        secondary: "bg-viby-soft text-viby-ink hover:bg-viby-soft/70",
        ghost: "bg-transparent text-viby-ink hover:bg-viby-soft",
        outline: "border border-viby text-viby hover:bg-viby-soft",
      },
      size: {
        sm: "h-8 px-3 text-sm rounded-pill",
        md: "h-10 px-5 text-sm rounded-pill",
        lg: "h-12 px-7 text-base rounded-pill",
      },
    },
    defaultVariants: { variant: "primary", size: "md" },
  }
);

export interface ButtonProps
  extends React.ComponentProps<"button">,
    VariantProps<typeof buttonVariants> {}

export function Button({ className, variant, size, ...props }: ButtonProps) {
  return (
    <button
      className={cn(buttonVariants({ variant, size }), className)}
      {...props}
    />
  );
}

export { buttonVariants };

Why this is good:

  • VariantProps<typeof buttonVariants> derives the variant/size types from the recipe — they can never drift out of sync.
  • React.ComponentProps<"button"> forwards onClick, disabled, type, aria-* for free.
  • className is merged last via cn(), so a caller can override anything without specificity wars.

Usage anywhere, including Server Components:

<Button variant="primary" size="lg">Find my people</Button>
<Button variant="ghost" size="sm" aria-label="Skip">Skip</Button>

The Chip primitive (Viby hobby tags)

Chips are how Viby surfaces shared interests — "Bouldering", "Lo-fi", "Matcha".

// src/components/ui/chip.tsx
import { cva, type VariantProps } from "class-variance-authority";
import { cn } from "@/lib/utils";

const chipVariants = cva(
  "inline-flex items-center gap-1 rounded-pill px-3 py-1 text-xs font-medium transition-colors",
  {
    variants: {
      tone: {
        neutral: "bg-viby-soft text-viby-ink",
        active: "bg-viby text-white",
        muted: "bg-zinc-100 text-zinc-500",
      },
    },
    defaultVariants: { tone: "neutral" },
  }
);

export interface ChipProps
  extends React.ComponentProps<"span">,
    VariantProps<typeof chipVariants> {}

export function Chip({ className, tone, ...props }: ChipProps) {
  return <span className={cn(chipVariants({ tone }), className)} {...props} />;
}

The Avatar primitive (composing primitives)

Avatar shows a user photo, falling back to initials. It uses next/image and stays a Server Component:

// src/components/ui/avatar.tsx
import Image from "next/image";
import { cn } from "@/lib/utils";

interface AvatarProps {
  src?: string | null;
  name: string;
  size?: number; // px
  className?: string;
}

export function Avatar({ src, name, size = 40, className }: AvatarProps) {
  const initials = name
    .split(" ")
    .map((p) => p[0])
    .slice(0, 2)
    .join("")
    .toUpperCase();

  return (
    <span
      className={cn(
        "relative inline-flex shrink-0 items-center justify-center overflow-hidden rounded-pill bg-viby-soft text-viby-ink font-semibold",
        className
      )}
      style={{ width: size, height: size, fontSize: size * 0.4 }}
    >
      {src ? (
        <Image src={src} alt={name} fill sizes={`${size}px`} className="object-cover" />
      ) : (
        initials
      )}
    </span>
  );
}

Adding motion: the FollowButton

Now the fun part. A button that springs on tap needs client-side JS, so it gets "use client". We compose our existing Button styles with a motion.button. Notice we reuse buttonVariants rather than duplicating classes:

// src/components/follow-button.tsx
"use client";

import { motion } from "framer-motion";
import { useState } from "react";
import { cn } from "@/lib/utils";
import { buttonVariants } from "@/components/ui/button";

export function FollowButton({ initial = false }: { initial?: boolean }) {
  const [following, setFollowing] = useState(initial);

  return (
    <motion.button
      whileTap={{ scale: 0.94 }}
      whileHover={{ scale: 1.03 }}
      transition={{ type: "spring", stiffness: 400, damping: 25 }}
      onClick={() => setFollowing((f) => !f)}
      className={cn(
        buttonVariants({ variant: following ? "secondary" : "primary", size: "md" })
      )}
    >
      {following ? "Following" : "Follow"}
    </motion.button>
  );
}

AnimatePresence: animating things that leave
