When a Viby user dismisses a "person you might vibe with" card, we want it to fly out, not vanish. Only AnimatePresence can animate exit:

// src/components/discovery-deck.tsx
"use client";

import { AnimatePresence, motion } from "framer-motion";
import { useState } from "react";
import { Avatar } from "@/components/ui/avatar";
import { Chip } from "@/components/ui/chip";

interface Person {
  id: string;
  name: string;
  avatar?: string | null;
  hobbies: string[];
}

export function DiscoveryDeck({ people }: { people: Person[] }) {
  const [deck, setDeck] = useState(people);
  const dismiss = (id: string) => setDeck((d) => d.filter((p) => p.id !== id));

  return (
    <ul className="flex flex-col gap-3">
      <AnimatePresence initial={false}>
        {deck.map((person) => (
          <motion.li
            key={person.id}
            layout
            initial={{ opacity: 0, y: 12 }}
            animate={{ opacity: 1, y: 0 }}
            exit={{ opacity: 0, x: 80 }}
            transition={{ type: "spring", stiffness: 300, damping: 30 }}
            className="flex items-center gap-3 rounded-2xl bg-white p-4 shadow-sm"
          >
            <Avatar src={person.avatar} name={person.name} size={48} />
            <div className="min-w-0 flex-1">
              <p className="font-semibold text-viby-ink">{person.name}</p>
              <div className="mt-1 flex flex-wrap gap-1">
                {person.hobbies.map((h) => (
                  <Chip key={h}>{h}</Chip>
                ))}
              </div>
            </div>
            <button
              onClick={() => dismiss(person.id)}
              aria-label={`Dismiss ${person.name}`}
              className="text-zinc-400 hover:text-viby"
            >
              ✕
            </button>
          </motion.li>
        ))}
      </AnimatePresence>
    </ul>
  );
}

The magic word is layout: when one card exits, the remaining cards smoothly slide up to fill the gap, because Framer Motion measures their positions before and after and animates the difference (the FLIP technique). You get this for one keyword.

Wiring it into a Server Component page

The page itself is a Server Component that fetches via Supabase and passes plain data to the client deck:

// src/app/discover/page.tsx
import { createServerClient } from "@supabase/ssr";
import { cookies } from "next/headers";
import { DiscoveryDeck } from "@/components/discovery-deck";

export default async function DiscoverPage() {
  const cookieStore = await cookies();
  const supabase = createServerClient(
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL!,
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY!,
    {
      cookies: {
        getAll: () => cookieStore.getAll(),
        setAll: (toSet) =>
          toSet.forEach(({ name, value, options }) =>
            cookieStore.set(name, value, options)
          ),
      },
    }
  );

  const { data } = await supabase
    .from("profiles")
    .select("id, name, avatar, hobbies")
    .limit(10);

  return (
    <main className="mx-auto max-w-md p-4">
      <h1 className="mb-4 text-2xl font-bold text-viby-ink">People to vibe with</h1>
      <DiscoveryDeck people={data ?? []} />
    </main>
  );
}

Architecture / diagram

flowchart TD
    subgraph tokens["Design tokens (globals.css @theme)"]
      T["--color-viby, --radius-pill ..."]
    end
    subgraph recipes["Variant recipes (cva)"]
      BV["buttonVariants"]
      CV["chipVariants"]
    end
    subgraph primitives["Primitives (Server-safe)"]
      B["Button"]
      C["Chip"]
      A["Avatar"]
    end
    subgraph motion["Motion layer ('use client')"]
      FB["FollowButton (motion + buttonVariants)"]
      DD["DiscoveryDeck (AnimatePresence + layout)"]
    end
    subgraph page["Server Component page"]
      P["/discover  -> Supabase fetch"]
    end

    T --> BV --> B
    T --> CV --> C
    B --> FB
    C --> DD
    A --> DD
    P --> DD

The flow is one-directional: tokens → recipes → primitives → motion-wrapped composites → pages. Each layer only knows about the one below it, which is exactly why the system scales.

Best practices & pitfalls

Do:

  • Keep primitives Server Components (no "use client") unless they need state, effects, or motion. Button, Chip, Avatar ship zero JS to the browser.
  • Always put className last in cn() so callers can override.
  • Reuse buttonVariants inside motion components instead of re-writing classes — single source of truth.
  • Give every AnimatePresence child a stable, unique key (use the DB id, never the array index).
  • Use spring transitions for interactions (taps, follows) and tween/ease for entrances — springs feel physical, which suits gestures.
  • Respect prefers-reduced-motion: wrap motion with Framer's <MotionConfig reducedMotion="user"> at the root.

Don't:

  • Don't pass arbitrary color props (color="#abc"). Enumerate variants instead — it keeps the brand consistent.
  • Don't concatenate Tailwind classes with template strings when they might conflict; that's what cn()/tailwind-merge is for.
  • Don't animate width/height/top/left directly when you can use transform (x, y, scale) — transforms are GPU-cheap and won't trigger layout thrash. Reach for layout for size/position changes.
  • Don't forget: AnimatePresence only animates direct children that mount/unmount. Conditionally render the child, not a wrapper around it.
  • Don't add "use client" to a whole page just to animate one button — push the boundary down to the smallest component.

Exercises

  1. Add an icon size to Button — a square 40×40 variant for icon-only buttons, plus an aria-label reminder in a JSDoc comment. (Easy)
  2. Build a Badge primitive with cva that has tone (new, pro, beta) variants, reusing rounded-pill. (Easy)
  3. Make Chip interactive — add a selected boolean and a tap animation so tapping toggles between neutral and active. Hint: this one needs "use client". (Medium)
  4. Add a "liked" heart to each DiscoveryDeck card that scales up with a spring whileTap and changes color, persisting in local state. (Medium)
  5. Stagger the deck entrance — use a parent motion.ul with variants and staggerChildren so cards cascade in on first load. (Hard)

Mini-project: The Viby "Vibe Tags" picker

Build a self-contained onboarding step where a new user picks their interests.

Requirements:

  • A grid of Chips for ~12 hobbies ("Bouldering", "Lo-fi", "Matcha", "Anime", "Run club", ...).
  • Tapping a chip toggles it between neutral and active, with a whileTap={{ scale: 0.9 }} spring (so the Chip gets wrapped in a motion.button).
  • A live count: "3 selected" updating as you tap.
  • A Button variant="primary" size="lg" labeled "Start vibing", disabled until at least 3 are selected.
  • When enabled, the button should gently pulse once (animate scale from 1 → 1.05 → 1) using animate to draw the eye.

Stretch: persist the selection to Supabase with a Server Action ("use server") that writes hobbies to the user's profiles row, then redirect("/discover").

This wires together everything from the chapter: tokens, cva variants, cn(), primitive composition, and gesture + state-driven motion.

Key takeaways

  • A great component has a narrow API: enumerated variants for looks, native prop passthrough for behaviour.
  • cva maps variants to classes declaratively; cn() (clsx + tailwind-merge) resolves conflicts and lets callers override.
  • Compose small primitives (Button, Chip, Avatar) instead of building giant configurable components.
  • Keep primitives Server Components; add "use client" only at the smallest unit that needs interactivity or motion.
  • Framer Motion gives you gestures (whileHover/whileTap), enter/exit animation (AnimatePresence), and effortless reflow (layout) — the difference between a demo and a premium product.

Up next — Chapter 11: Forms, Validation & Server Actions, where we'll capture real user input with type-safe schemas and write it straight to Supabase.

Chapter 11 — Color, Theming & the Viby Design System

Color isn't decoration — it's the fastest message your app sends before a single word is read.

What you'll learn

  • The working color theory you actually need: hue/saturation/lightness, contrast, and 60-30-10.
  • How to turn a brand color (#FF6FB5) into a token-based palette that scales across light and dark.
  • How to define those tokens natively in Tailwind CSS v4 @theme — no tailwind.config.js.
  • Building Viby's signature gradients, glow, and glass surfaces, accessibly.
  • Shipping a design-token sheet: a single living page that documents every token in the app.

Prerequisites

  • Chapter 4 (Tailwind v4 setup, the @theme block, globals.css).
  • Chapter 7 (shadcn-style UI primitives and the cn() helper).
  • Chapter 9 (Framer Motion basics — motion components and gestures).
  • Comfort with Next.js 16 Server vs. Client Components (Chapter 6).

Theory

Color is a system, not a swatch

Beginners pick colors one at a time: "this pink looks nice here." Professionals define a system once and let every screen draw from it. Think of it like a kitchen. An amateur grabs whatever spice is closest; a chef builds a mise en place — everything measured, labeled, in reach. Our mise en place is a set of design tokens: named values (--color-primary, --radius-lg) that the whole codebase references instead of raw hex.

The payoff: change --color-primary in one place and every button, badge, and focus ring updates. Tokens are the difference between "we have a pink" and "we have a design system."

HSL: the only color model that thinks like a designer

Hex (#FF6FB5) is how computers store color; HSL is how humans reason about it:

  • Hue (0–360°): the color's identity. Viby's bubblegum pink sits around 332°.
  • Saturation (0–100%): how vivid vs. gray.
  • Lightness (0–100%): how close to white or black.

HSL makes palettes derivable. Keep the hue, walk the lightness up and down, and you get a tonal scale (50 → 950) that feels like one family because it is one family. That's exactly how Tailwind's built-in scales work, and how we'll build Viby's.

#FF6FB5 ≈ hsl(332, 100%, 72%). From that single seed we generate the whole ramp.

Contrast is non-negotiable

A pretty color that nobody can read is a bug. WCAG sets minimum contrast ratios: 4.5:1 for normal text, 3:1 for large text and UI components (borders, icons, focus states). Bubblegum pink on white is only ~2:1 — gorgeous for fills, illegal for body text. So the rule for Viby: pink is a surface and accent color; readable text rides on near-black ink (--color-foreground) or pure white on pink fills. We'll bake an accessible "on-primary" token in so nobody has to guess.

60-30-10: the proportion that makes brands feel calm

A balanced UI is roughly 60% dominant (white/neutral background), 30% secondary (cards, muted surfaces), 10% accent (the pink — buttons, links, highlights). The mistake new designers make is flooding the screen with brand color; restraint is what makes the 10% pop. Viby looks premium precisely because pink is a spark, not a wall.

Light and dark from one source of truth

We never hand-author two palettes. We define semantic tokens (--color-background, --color-foreground, --color-primary) and swap their values under a .dark class. Components only ever speak in semantics — bg-background, text-foreground — so they're theme-agnostic by construction.

In practice

1. The token sheet, defined in CSS

Tailwind v4 has no JS config. Tokens live in globals.css inside @theme, and every token there automatically becomes a utility class (--color-primary → bg-primary, text-primary, border-primary).

/* app/globals.css */
@import "tailwindcss";

/* 1) Raw brand ramp — the immutable source of truth.
   One hue (332°), walked across lightness. */
@theme {
  --color-pink-50:  hsl(330 100% 97%);
  --color-pink-100: hsl(331 100% 93%);
  --color-pink-200: hsl(332 100% 88%);
  --color-pink-300: hsl(332 100% 82%);
  --color-pink-400: hsl(332 100% 77%);
  --color-pink-500: hsl(332 100% 72%); /* #FF6FB5 — the brand */
  --color-pink-600: hsl(332 78%  60%);
  --color-pink-700: hsl(332 65%  48%);
  --color-pink-800: hsl(332 60%  38%);
  --color-pink-900: hsl(332 55%  28%);
  --color-pink-950: hsl(332 60%  16%);

  --color-ink-50:  hsl(320 16% 97%);
  --color-ink-500: hsl(320 8%  46%);
  --color-ink-900: hsl(322 24% 12%);

  /* 2) Semantic tokens — what components actually reference.
     These point AT the ramp, so a rebrand is a one-line change. */
  --color-background: white;
  --color-foreground: hsl(322 24% 12%);
  --color-primary: var(--color-pink-500);
  --color-primary-foreground: white;   /* text/icons ON pink — passes contrast */
  --color-muted: hsl(330 40% 97%);
  --color-muted-foreground: hsl(320 8% 46%);
  --color-card: white;
  --color-border: hsl(330 30% 91%);
  --color-ring: var(--color-pink-500);

  /* 3) Shape + motion tokens belong in the system too. */
  --radius-lg: 1rem;
  --radius-xl: 1.5rem;
  --shadow-glow: 0 0 0 1px hsl(332 100% 72% / 0.25),
                 0 8px 30px -8px hsl(332 100% 72% / 0.45);
}

2. Dark mode: same names, new values

We override the semantic layer only — the raw ramp never changes. Use a @custom-variant so dark: utilities work with a class toggle.

/* still in globals.css */
@custom-variant dark (&:where(.dark, .dark *));

.dark {
  --color-background: hsl(322 24% 8%);
  --color-foreground: hsl(330 40% 96%);
  --color-primary: var(--color-pink-400); /* lift lightness so it glows on dark */
  --color-primary-foreground: hsl(322 30% 10%);
  --color-muted: hsl(322 20% 14%);
  --color-muted-foreground: hsl(320 12% 65%);
  --color-card: hsl(322 22% 11%);
  --color-border: hsl(322 18% 20%);
}

Because --color-primary is referenced by the bg-primary utility, the same component renders pink-500 in light mode and pink-400 in dark — zero component changes.

3. A token-driven Button (shadcn-style)

// components/ui/button.tsx
import { cva, type VariantProps } from "class-variance-authority";
import { cn } from "@/lib/utils";

const button = cva(
  "inline-flex items-center justify-center rounded-lg font-medium " +
    "transition-colors focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 " +
    "focus-visible:ring-ring focus-visible:ring-offset-2 disabled:opacity-50",
  {
    variants: {
      variant: {
        primary:
          "bg-primary text-primary-foreground hover:bg-pink-600 shadow-glow",
        ghost: "text-foreground hover:bg-muted",
        outline: "border border-border text-foreground hover:bg-muted",
      },
      size: { sm: "h-9 px-3 text-sm", md: "h-11 px-5", lg: "h-13 px-7 text-lg" },
    },
    defaultVariants: { variant: "primary", size: "md" },
  },
);

export function Button({
  className,
  variant,
  size,
  ...props
}: React.ComponentProps<"button"> & VariantProps<typeof button>) {
  return <button className={cn(button({ variant, size }), className)} {...props} />;
}

Notice: no hex anywhere. The button speaks only tokens, so it's automatically correct in both themes.

4. Gradients, glow & glass — the Viby signature

// components/viby-card.tsx  (Server Component — no interactivity needed)
export function VibyCard({ children }: { children: React.ReactNode }) {
  return (
    <div
      className={[
        "rounded-xl p-6",
        // Glass: translucent surface + blur + hairline border
        "bg-card/70 backdrop-blur-xl border border-border/60",
        // Glow: our shadow token
        "shadow-glow",
      ].join(" ")}
    >
      {children}
    </div>
  );
}

/* A reusable brand gradient, defined as a token-backed utility */
@layer utilities {
  .bg-viby-gradient {
    background-image: linear-gradient(
      135deg,
      var(--color-pink-400) 0%,
      var(--color-pink-600) 100%
    );
  }
  .text-viby-gradient {
    background: linear-gradient(135deg, var(--color-pink-500), var(--color-pink-700));
    -webkit-background-clip: text;
    background-clip: text;
    color: transparent;
  }
}

// A hero headline using the gradient text utility
<h1 className="text-4xl font-bold text-viby-gradient">
  Find your people.
</h1>

5. A theme toggle that respects the system

The toggle flips a class on <html> and persists choice. Reading from localStorage and matching system preference means we need a Client Component, but we keep it tiny.

// components/theme-toggle.tsx
"use client";
import { useEffect, useState } from "react";
import { motion } from "framer-motion";
import { Button } from "@/components/ui/button";

export function ThemeToggle() {
  const [dark, setDark] = useState(false);

  useEffect(() => {
    const stored = localStorage.getItem("viby-theme");
    const prefersDark = window.matchMedia("(prefers-color-scheme: dark)").matches;
    const isDark = stored ? stored === "dark" : prefersDark;
    setDark(isDark);
    document.documentElement.classList.toggle("dark", isDark);
  }, []);

  function toggle() {
    const next = !dark;
    setDark(next);
    document.documentElement.classList.toggle("dark", next);
    localStorage.setItem("viby-theme", next ? "dark" : "light");
  }

  return (
    <Button variant="ghost" size="sm" onClick={toggle} aria-label="Toggle theme">
      <motion.span key={dark ? "moon" : "sun"} initial={{ rotate: -90, opacity: 0 }}
        animate={{ rotate: 0, opacity: 1 }} transition={{ duration: 0.2 }}>
        {dark ? "🌙" : "☀️"}
      </motion.span>
    </Button>
  );
}

To avoid a flash of the wrong theme, set the class before React hydrates with a tiny inline script in the root layout's <head>:

// app/layout.tsx (excerpt)
<script
  dangerouslySetInnerHTML={{
    __html: `(function(){try{var s=localStorage.getItem('viby-theme');
      var d=s?s==='dark':matchMedia('(prefers-color-scheme:dark)').matches;
      document.documentElement.classList.toggle('dark',d);}catch(e){}})();`,
  }}
/>

Architecture / diagram

The flow from a single brand hue to a rendered, themeable pixel:

flowchart TD
  A["Brand seed<br/>#FF6FB5 = hsl(332 100% 72%)"] --> B["Raw ramp tokens<br/>--color-pink-50 ... 950"]
  B --> C["Semantic tokens<br/>--color-primary, --color-background, ..."]
  C -->|".dark overrides values"| D["Theme layer<br/>light / dark"]
  D --> E["Tailwind utilities<br/>bg-primary, text-foreground"]
  E --> F["Components<br/>Button, VibyCard"]
  F --> G["Rendered UI<br/>accessible in both themes"]
  C -.documents.-> H["/design page<br/>token sheet"]

The arrows only ever flow downward: components never reach past the semantic layer to raw hex. That one rule keeps the whole system maintainable.

Best practices & pitfalls

  • Do reference semantic tokens (bg-primary) in components, never raw ramp steps or hex. Raw steps are for defining the system, not consuming it.
  • Do keep one hue family per brand color and derive the ramp by lightness — it guarantees harmony.
  • Don't put brand pink behind body text. Reserve it for fills, accents, and large display text; check 4.5:1 with a contrast checker.
  • Don't create a separate dark palette by hand. Override semantic values under .dark only.
  • Do ship a no-flash inline script; a dark-mode flash on load reads as "amateur."
  • Don't overuse glow/glass. Glass needs a busy/colored backdrop to read; over a flat white it just looks blurry. Follow 60-30-10.
  • Do include radius, shadow, and spacing in your tokens — a design system is more than color.
  • Don't animate box-shadow directly in hot paths; prefer transitioning a pseudo-element's opacity for glow on hover to stay at 60fps.

Exercises

  1. Derive a step. Compute --color-pink-450 between 400 and 500 in HSL and add it to the ramp. Verify the utility bg-pink-450 works on a <div>.
  2. Audit contrast. Use a checker to test text-primary on bg-background in both themes. If it fails, adjust which ramp step --color-primary references in .dark and re-test.
  3. A semantic success token. Add --color-success / --color-success-foreground (a mint green ~150°), wire a success Button variant, and confirm it themes correctly.
  4. Glow on hover, performantly. Refactor VibyCard so the glow appears only on hover by transitioning a pseudo-element's opacity rather than the box-shadow property.
  5. System-preference live sync. Extend ThemeToggle so that when the user has not made an explicit choice, the app follows OS theme changes in real time via a matchMedia change listener.

Mini-project — The Viby Design Token Sheet

Build /design, a living page that renders every token so the whole team (and your future self) can see the system at a glance.

// app/design/page.tsx  — Server Component, zero client JS
const RAMP = [50, 100, 200, 300, 400, 500, 600, 700, 800, 900, 950];
const SEMANTIC = [
  "background", "foreground", "primary", "primary-foreground",
  "muted", "muted-foreground", "card", "border",
] as const;

export default function DesignSystemPage() {
  return (
    <main className="min-h-screen bg-background text-foreground p-8 space-y-12">
      <header>
        <h1 className="text-4xl font-bold text-viby-gradient">Viby Design Tokens</h1>
        <p className="text-muted-foreground">One source of truth for color, shape & glow.</p>
      </header>

      <section>
        <h2 className="text-2xl font-semibold mb-4">Pink ramp</h2>
        <div className="grid grid-cols-11 gap-2">
          {RAMP.map((step) => (
            <div key={step} className="text-center">
              <div className="h-16 rounded-lg border border-border"
                   style={{ background: `var(--color-pink-${step})` }} />
              <span className="text-xs text-muted-foreground">{step}</span>
            </div>
          ))}
        </div>
      </section>

      <section>
        <h2 className="text-2xl font-semibold mb-4">Semantic tokens</h2>
        <div className="grid grid-cols-2 sm:grid-cols-4 gap-4">
          {SEMANTIC.map((name) => (
            <div key={name} className="rounded-lg border border-border overflow-hidden">
              <div className="h-16" style={{ background: `var(--color-${name})` }} />
              <code className="block p-2 text-xs">--color-{name}</code>
            </div>
          ))}
        </div>
      </section>

      <section>
        <h2 className="text-2xl font-semibold mb-4">Surfaces</h2>
        <div className="grid sm:grid-cols-3 gap-4">
          <div className="rounded-xl p-6 bg-viby-gradient text-white">Gradient</div>
          <div className="rounded-xl p-6 shadow-glow bg-card">Glow</div>
          <div className="rounded-xl p-6 bg-card/70 backdrop-blur-xl border border-border/60">
            Glass
          </div>
        </div>
      </section>
    </main>
  );
}

Add a ThemeToggle to the page header and watch every swatch re-theme live. This page is your contract: if a new component doesn't match what's here, the component is wrong — not the system.

Key takeaways

  • Design with a system of tokens, not one-off hex values; change once, update everywhere.
  • Reason in HSL and derive ramps by walking lightness across a single hue.
  • Split tokens into raw ramp → semantic → theme overrides; components consume semantics only.
  • Tailwind v4 puts the whole token sheet in CSS @theme — every token becomes a utility for free.
  • Respect contrast and 60-30-10: pink is a spark, not a flood.
  • A token sheet page keeps the system honest and onboards teammates instantly.

Up next — Chapter 12: Typography & Spacing — A Type Scale That Breathes, where we give Viby a voice to match its color.

Chapter 12 — Typography, Spacing & Layout Systems

Great design isn't decoration you add at the end — it's a system of decisions you make once, then reuse a thousand times.

You've shipped working screens. Now we make them feel like a real product. The difference between "a student project" and "a startup app" is rarely the features — it's the rhythm: consistent type, breathing room, and a hierarchy that guides the eye without a single instruction. This chapter turns Viby's look into a system.

What you'll learn

  • How to build a type scale and pair a display font with a body font that actually works
  • Spacing rhythm — why a 4px-based scale beats eyeballed margins
  • A radius and elevation system for cards, sheets, and buttons
  • The mechanics of visual hierarchy: size, weight, color, and space working together
  • How to encode all of it as Tailwind v4 @theme tokens and apply them across real Viby screens

Prerequisites

  • Chapter 3 (project setup) and Chapter 4 (Tailwind v4 + the @theme block in globals.css)
  • Chapter 8 (shadcn-style UI primitives — Button, Card)
  • Comfort with Server Components vs. "use client" (Chapter 6)

Theory

Why a system and not just "good taste"

Imagine furnishing a house where every door is a different height and every wall a slightly different white. Nothing is wrong, but the whole thing feels off. UI is the same. When one card uses padding: 14px, the next uses 16px, and a third uses 1rem, your brain registers the inconsistency as "amateur" even if it can't name why.

A design system replaces a thousand ad-hoc choices with a small set of tokens — named, reusable values. You decide the spacing scale once. After that, you're never asking "16 or 18?"; you're picking from a menu. That's what makes design fast and consistent.

Type scales — the musical analogy

A type scale is like a musical scale: a fixed set of notes that sound good together. Instead of random sizes (13px, 15px, 22px, 31px), you generate sizes from a base by multiplying by a ratio.

  • Base: 16px (1rem) — your body text.
  • Ratio: a common choice is the Major Third (1.25) or Perfect Fourth (1.333). Bigger ratio = more dramatic contrast between headings and body.

With a 1.25 ratio from a 16px base: 16 → 20 → 25 → 31 → 39 → 49. Each step is "one note up." Viby is a punchy, mobile-first social app, so we want strong contrast: a big confident display size for moments like the onboarding hero, and a calm, readable body.

Pairing fonts. The reliable formula is one expressive display face + one neutral, highly-readable body face. Contrast is the goal — if both fonts are geometric sans-serifs, they fight. For Viby we'll pair Sora (a rounded, friendly geometric sans for display — on-brand Gen-Z energy) with Inter (the workhorse UI body font, superb at small sizes).

Rhythm and spacing — the 4px grid

Vertical and horizontal rhythm come from a single base unit. The industry standard is a 4px base (sometimes 8px). Every gap, padding, and margin is a multiple: 4, 8, 12, 16, 24, 32, 48, 64. Why? Multiples of 4 align cleanly on screens, and constraining yourself to the scale eliminates the "is this 15 or 16?" dithering. Tailwind already bakes this in (p-4 = 16px, gap-2 = 8px), so we lean on it rather than fighting it.

Think of spacing like the silence in music. The notes (your content) matter, but the rests (your whitespace) are what make it legible. Cramped UIs read as stressful; generous spacing reads as premium.

Radius and elevation

Radius sets personality. Sharp corners (0–2px) feel corporate and serious; soft corners (12–24px) feel friendly and modern. Viby is warm and social, so we go generously rounded.

Elevation is how you communicate stacking — what floats above what. In flat design, elevation isn't drop-shadow soup; it's a small, deliberate ramp of shadows (rest → raised → overlay → modal). Each level should feel like a real step up off the page. Like the floors of a building: the lobby, then a few defined storeys — not forty indistinguishable ones.

Visual hierarchy — the four levers

You guide attention with four tools, used together:

  1. Size — bigger = more important.
  2. Weight — bolder = louder.
  3. Color/contrast — high contrast pulls the eye; muted recedes.
  4. Space — isolation creates emphasis (a lone CTA surrounded by whitespace screams "click me").

The mistake beginners make is shouting with all four at once on everything. Hierarchy is relative: something is only "loud" because other things are quiet.

In practice

1. Load the fonts (Next.js 16, next/font)

In the App Router, next/font self-hosts Google Fonts at build time (zero layout shift, no network request) and exposes them as CSS variables.

// app/layout.tsx
import type { Metadata } from "next";
import { Sora, Inter } from "next/font/google";
import "./globals.css";

const sora = Sora({
  subsets: ["latin"],
  variable: "--font-display",
  display: "swap",
  weight: ["600", "700", "800"],
});

const inter = Inter({
  subsets: ["latin"],
  variable: "--font-body",
  display: "swap",
});

export const metadata: Metadata = {
  title: "Viby — find your people",
  description: "Social discovery through shared hobbies and lifestyle.",
};

export default function RootLayout({
  children,
}: {
  children: React.ReactNode;
}) {
  return (
    <html lang="en" className={`${sora.variable} ${inter.variable}`}>
      <body className="bg-background text-foreground antialiased">
        {children}
      </body>
    </html>
  );
}

2. Encode the system as Tailwind v4 @theme tokens

In Tailwind v4 there is no tailwind.config.js — design tokens live in CSS inside an @theme block. The font variables we just created get wired in here, alongside our type scale, radius, and shadow ramp.

/* app/globals.css */
@import "tailwindcss";

@theme {
  /* Brand */
  --color-primary: #ff6fb5;
  --color-primary-foreground: #ffffff;
  --color-background: #ffffff;
  --color-foreground: #18121c;
  --color-muted: #f4f1f4;
  --color-muted-foreground: #6b6470;

  /* Fonts — consume the next/font variables */
  --font-display: var(--font-display), ui-sans-serif, system-ui, sans-serif;
  --font-body: var(--font-body), ui-sans-serif, system-ui, sans-serif;

  /* Type scale — base 16px, ~1.25 ratio (Major Third) */
  --text-xs: 0.75rem;     /* 12 */
  --text-sm: 0.875rem;    /* 14 */
  --text-base: 1rem;      /* 16 */
  --text-lg: 1.25rem;     /* 20 */
  --text-xl: 1.563rem;    /* 25 */
  --text-2xl: 1.953rem;   /* 31 */
  --text-3xl: 2.441rem;   /* 39 */
  --text-4xl: 3.052rem;   /* 49 */

  /* Line-height pairs to each step (set via utilities below) */
  --leading-tight: 1.1;
  --leading-snug: 1.3;
  --leading-relaxed: 1.6;

  /* Radius system — friendly, generous */
  --radius-sm: 0.5rem;    /* 8  - inputs, chips */
  --radius-md: 0.875rem;  /* 14 - buttons */
  --radius-lg: 1.25rem;   /* 20 - cards */
  --radius-xl: 1.75rem;   /* 28 - sheets, modals */
  --radius-full: 9999px;  /* avatars, pills */

  /* Elevation ramp — soft, brand-tinted */
  --shadow-raised: 0 1px 2px rgba(24, 18, 28, 0.06),
                   0 2px 8px rgba(24, 18, 28, 0.05);
  --shadow-overlay: 0 8px 24px rgba(24, 18, 28, 0.10);
  --shadow-modal: 0 24px 64px rgba(24, 18, 28, 0.18);
  --shadow-glow: 0 8px 28px rgba(255, 111, 181, 0.35);
}

/* Two semantic type roles built on the scale */
@layer components {
  .text-display {
    font-family: var(--font-display);
    font-weight: 800;
    line-height: var(--leading-tight);
    letter-spacing: -0.02em;
  }
  .text-body {
    font-family: var(--font-body);
    line-height: var(--leading-relaxed);
  }
}

Because we named tokens like --color-primary, --radius-lg, and --shadow-overlay, Tailwind v4 auto-generates utilities: bg-primary, rounded-lg, shadow-overlay, text-3xl, and so on. No config file, no plugin — the CSS is the source of truth.

3. A profile card that uses the whole system

Here's a Viby discovery card — the thing you swipe through to find people who share your hobbies. Notice every value is a token: type from the scale, rounded-lg, shadow-overlay, spacing on the 4px grid.

// components/discovery/profile-card.tsx
import Image from "next/image";

type Person = {
  name: string;
  age: number;
  tagline: string;
  hobbies: string[];
  avatarUrl: string;
};

export function ProfileCard({ person }: { person: Person }) {
  return (
    <article className="overflow-hidden rounded-lg bg-background shadow-overlay">
      <div className="relative aspect-[4/5] w-full">
        <Image
          src={person.avatarUrl}
          alt={person.name}
          fill
          className="object-cover"
          sizes="(max-width: 480px) 100vw, 380px"
        />
        {/* gradient scrim so text stays readable over any photo */}
        <div className="absolute inset-x-0 bottom-0 h-2/5 bg-gradient-to-t from-foreground/70 to-transparent" />
        <div className="absolute inset-x-0 bottom-0 p-5 text-primary-foreground">
          <h2 className="text-display text-2xl">
            {person.name}{" "}
            <span className="font-normal opacity-80">{person.age}</span>
          </h2>
          <p className="text-body mt-1 text-sm opacity-90">{person.tagline}</p>
        </div>
      </div>

      <div className="flex flex-wrap gap-2 p-4">
        {person.hobbies.map((h) => (
          <span
            key={h}
            className="rounded-full bg-muted px-3 py-1 text-sm text-muted-foreground"
          >
            {h}
          </span>
        ))}
      </div>
    </article>
  );
}

Read it top to bottom: the name is the loudest thing (display font, text-2xl, full white), the age recedes (normal weight, reduced opacity), the tagline is quieter still (body font, text-sm), and hobby chips are calm muted pills. Four levers, one clear path for the eye — and not a single hand-tuned pixel.

4. A reusable elevation primitive

Wrapping elevation in a tiny component keeps the ramp consistent and gives you one place to tune it. Server Component by default — no client JS needed.

// components/ui/surface.tsx
import { cn } from "@/lib/utils";

const elevation = {
  flat: "",
  raised: "shadow-raised",
  overlay: "shadow-overlay",
  modal: "shadow-modal",
} as const;

export function Surface({
  level = "raised",
  className,
  ...props
}: React.ComponentProps<"div"> & { level?: keyof typeof elevation }) {
  return (
    <div
      className={cn("rounded-lg bg-background", elevation[level], className)}
      {...props}
    />
  );
}

5. Animate elevation on interaction (Framer Motion)

A card that lifts on hover feels tactile. Keep the motion subtle — premium, not bouncy-house. This needs the client.

// components/discovery/interactive-card.tsx
"use client";

import { motion } from "framer-motion";

export function InteractiveCard({ children }: { children: React.ReactNode }) {
  return (
    <motion.div
      whileHover={{ y: -4 }}
      whileTap={{ scale: 0.98 }}
      transition={{ type: "spring", stiffness: 320, damping: 26 }}
      className="rounded-lg bg-background shadow-raised hover:shadow-overlay"
    >
      {children}
    </motion.div>
  );
}

The Tailwind hover:shadow-overlay and Framer's y: -4 work together: the card both rises (transform) and casts a bigger shadow (the elevation ramp). That's elevation done right.

Architecture / diagram

How the system flows from a few token definitions out to every screen:

flowchart TD
    A["next/font<br/>Sora + Inter"] -->|CSS variables| B["@theme tokens<br/>globals.css"]
    B --> C["Auto-generated utilities<br/>text-3xl · rounded-lg · shadow-overlay"]
    C --> D["UI primitives<br/>Button · Card · Surface"]
    D --> E["Screen components<br/>ProfileCard · Onboarding · Feed"]
    E --> F["Viby app — consistent<br/>type · spacing · elevation"]

    subgraph "Single source of truth"
        B
    end

And the hierarchy levers, ranked by how strongly each pulls the eye on a Viby card:

LOUDEST  ┌────────────────────────────┐
  ▲      │  Name   (display, text-2xl) │  ← size + weight + contrast
  │      ├────────────────────────────┤
  │      │  Age    (normal, 80% opac)  │  ← same size, lower contrast
  │      ├────────────────────────────┤
  │      │  Tagline (body, text-sm)    │  ← smaller, quieter
  ▼      ├────────────────────────────┤
QUIETEST │  Hobby chips (muted pills)  │  ← grouped, low contrast, spaced
         └────────────────────────────┘

Best practices & pitfalls

  • Do pick a base size + ratio and generate the whole scale. Don't add one-off sizes "just this once" — that's how scales rot.
  • Do pair one display font with one body font. Don't ship three+ font families; it reads as chaos and bloats your font payload.
  • Do use font-display: swap and self-host via next/font to kill layout shift (CLS). Don't <link> to Google Fonts in <head> — it's slower and shifts the page.
  • Do snap every gap/padding to the 4px grid (Tailwind's defaults). Don't write arbitrary values like p-[13px] unless you have a documented reason.
  • Do keep a small elevation ramp (3–4 levels). Don't stack heavy shadows on everything — flat-with-purpose beats shadow soup.
  • Do tighten line-height on big headings (1.1) and loosen it on body (1.5–1.6). Don't use one line-height for all sizes; large tight, small loose.
  • Do check contrast: body text should hit WCAG AA (4.5:1). Bubblegum pink #FF6FB5 on white is not AA for small text — use it for fills/accents, and use --color-foreground for reading text.
  • Don't use color alone to convey state (a green vs. red dot is invisible to many users) — pair it with an icon or label.

Exercises

  1. Re-scale. Regenerate the type scale using a Perfect Fourth ratio (1.333) instead of 1.25. Update the @theme tokens and observe how the onboarding hero gets more dramatic. Which ratio suits Viby better, and why?
  2. Spacing audit. Open any screen you built earlier and find three places using off-grid values (e.g. mt-[18px]). Snap them to the nearest scale step and note whether the layout looks more cohesive.
  3. Add a level. Extend the elevation ramp with a --shadow-sheet value for a bottom sheet that sits between overlay and modal. Add it to the Surface primitive's elevation map.
  4. Hierarchy challenge. Take the ProfileCard and make the tagline the loudest element instead of the name — using only the four levers. Then argue (in a comment) which version serves a discovery app better.
  5. Dark mode token swap (stretch). Add a @media (prefers-color-scheme: dark) block that overrides --color-background, --color-foreground, and the shadow ramp. Confirm every component adapts without touching a single component file.

Mini-project — the Viby Design Tokens reference screen

Build a /styleguide route (a Server Component page) that renders your living design system, so the whole team designs from the same source.

Goal: one page that displays:

  • The full type scale — each step rendered at size with its name and px value.
  • The spacing scale — labeled bars showing 4 → 64.
  • The radius set — swatches from sm to full.
  • The elevation ramp — four Surface cards, one per level.
// app/styleguide/page.tsx
import { Surface } from "@/components/ui/surface";

const typeSteps = [
  ["text-4xl", "49px", "Find your people"],
  ["text-3xl", "39px", "Section heading"],
  ["text-2xl", "31px", "Card title"],
  ["text-xl", "25px", "Subhead"],
  ["text-lg", "20px", "Lead paragraph"],
  ["text-base", "16px", "Body — the workhorse"],
  ["text-sm", "14px", "Captions & chips"],
] as const;

const space = [1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 8, 12, 16] as const; // ×4px

export default function StyleguidePage() {
  return (
    <main className="mx-auto max-w-2xl space-y-12 p-6">
      <h1 className="text-display text-4xl text-primary">Viby Design System</h1>

      <section className="space-y-3">
        <h2 className="text-display text-xl">Type scale</h2>
        {typeSteps.map(([cls, px, sample]) => (
          <div key={cls} className="flex items-baseline gap-4">
            <code className="w-44 shrink-0 text-sm text-muted-foreground">
              {cls} · {px}
            </code>
            <span className={`text-display ${cls}`}>{sample}</span>
          </div>
        ))}
      </section>

      <section className="space-y-3">
        <h2 className="text-display text-xl">Spacing (×4px)</h2>
        {space.map((s) => (
          <div key={s} className="flex items-center gap-4">
            <code className="w-16 text-sm text-muted-foreground">{s * 4}px</code>
            <div className="h-3 rounded-sm bg-primary" style={{ width: s * 4 }} />
          </div>
        ))}
      </section>

      <section className="space-y-3">
        <h2 className="text-display text-xl">Radius</h2>
        <div className="flex flex-wrap gap-4">
          {["sm", "md", "lg", "xl", "full"].map((r) => (
            <div
              key={r}
              className={`grid h-20 w-20 place-items-center bg-muted text-sm text-muted-foreground rounded-${r}`}
            >
              {r}
            </div>
          ))}
        </div>
      </section>

      <section className="space-y-3">
        <h2 className="text-display text-xl">Elevation</h2>
        <div className="grid grid-cols-2 gap-6">
          {(["raised", "overlay", "modal"] as const).map((level) => (
            <Surface key={level} level={level} className="grid h-24 place-items-center">
              <span className="text-sm text-muted-foreground">{level}</span>
            </Surface>
          ))}
        </div>
      </section>
    </main>
  );
}

Ship this once and you'll reference it for the rest of the book. When a teammate asks "what's our card radius?" you point at /styleguide instead of arguing in Slack. (One caveat for the radius swatches: because rounded-${r} is built dynamically, add those class names to a safelist comment or list them statically if your build tree-shakes unused utilities.)

Key takeaways

  • A design system swaps a thousand ad-hoc choices for a small set of named tokens — fast and consistent.
  • Generate your type scale from a base size and a ratio; pair one display + one body font for contrast.
  • Snap all spacing to a 4px grid; whitespace is a feature, not wasted space.
  • Keep a small radius set and a 3–4 step elevation ramp — restraint reads as premium.
  • Drive hierarchy with size, weight, contrast, and space — relatively, never all at once.
  • In this stack the system lives in Tailwind v4 @theme tokens (no config file), fed by next/font variables, and is the single source of truth for every screen.

Up next — Chapter 13: Color, Theming & Dark Mode, where we turn that bubblegum pink into a full, accessible palette and make Viby beautiful in the dark.

Chapter 13 — Design Psychology, Gen-Z & Social App UX

Great social apps don't show you content — they make you feel something in under 400ms, and then make you want to come back tomorrow.

What you'll learn

  • Emotional design: why the first 5 seconds decide whether a user stays, and how to engineer warmth into pixels.
  • Cognitive load: how to make Viby feel effortless by giving the brain less to do.
  • Social & feedback loops: the mechanics behind likes, streaks, and "someone viewed your profile" — and how to use them ethically.
  • Onboarding psychology: progressive disclosure, the goal-gradient effect, and the dreaded empty state.
  • Premium feel: micro-interactions, motion, and the details that separate a $0 app from a startup-grade one.
  • Dark patterns to avoid: the manipulative tricks that work short-term and burn trust long-term.
  • Designing an identity system that is NOT a dating clone: how copy, taxonomy, and affordances steer Viby toward friendship and discovery.

Prerequisites

  • Chapter 6 (Tailwind CSS v4 with @theme tokens) — we'll lean on our design tokens here.
  • Chapter 8 (shadcn-style UI primitives) — Button, Card, Avatar.
  • Chapter 10 (Framer Motion) — motion, AnimatePresence, gestures.
  • Chapter 11 (Zustand demo state) and Chapter 12 (Supabase Realtime) — for the live feedback loop in the mini-project.

Theory

Emotional design: people decode feeling before function

Don Norman splits design into three levels: visceral (gut reaction to look/feel), behavioral (how good it is to use), and reflective (the story you tell yourself after — "I'm the kind of person who uses this"). Most beginners obsess over behavioral and ignore the other two. For a social app, that's fatal: the visceral layer fires in ~50ms, before a single word is read.

Think of your UI like meeting a person. Before they speak, you've judged their posture, their warmth, whether they're like you. Viby's bubblegum pink (#FF6FB5) on generous white space is a deliberate visceral signal: playful, soft, non-threatening — the opposite of a corporate blue LinkedIn or a sultry red dating app.

Cognitive load: the brain is lazy on purpose

Working memory holds only a handful of items at once. Every choice, every unlabeled icon, every "wait, what does this do?" spends a slice of that budget. Cognitive load comes in three flavors:

  • Intrinsic — inherent difficulty of the task (joining a hobby group).
  • Extraneous — difficulty you added with bad UI (a cluttered screen). This is the one we ruthlessly cut.
  • Germane — effort that builds the user's mental model (a good onboarding tour).

Analogy: a well-designed screen is a clean kitchen. Everything you need for this step is on the counter; everything else is in a drawer. Hick's Law says decision time grows with the number of options — so we show one primary action per screen, not five.

Social & feedback loops: the dopamine machine, used kindly

A feedback loop is action → reward → motivation to repeat. Social apps run on three engines:

  1. Reciprocity — you waved, they waved back. Humans are wired to return gestures.
  2. Social proof — "12 people share your love of bouldering" lowers the fear of joining.
  3. Variable reward — you don't know who will react, so you check. This is the most powerful and the most dangerous lever; we'll use it with restraint.

The ethical line: a loop is healthy when it helps the user reach their goal (find friends), and toxic when it exists only to maximize our session time.

Onboarding psychology: earn the right to ask

New users have zero investment and infinite exits. Two principles save us:

  • Progressive disclosure — reveal complexity only as needed. Don't ask for 14 interests up front; ask for 3, celebrate, then offer more.
  • Goal-gradient effect — motivation increases as a goal feels closer. A progress bar that starts at 20% (not 0%) makes people finish.

Premium feel: the invisible 10%

Premium isn't a gradient — it's responsiveness. Buttons that depress, lists that stagger in, optimistic UI that reacts before the server replies. The brain reads sub-100ms feedback as "this thing is alive."

NOT a dating clone: identity through constraint

A dating app and a friendship app can look identical in a Figma file — the difference lives in affordances and language. Swipe-to-judge-faces says "rate this body." A grid of shared interests says "find your people." We design Viby so the unit of attraction is a shared hobby, never a face. We'll codify this below.

In practice

1. Encoding emotion as design tokens (Tailwind v4 @theme)

Emotion should be systematized, not vibes. We define warmth in CSS tokens once (Chapter 6) and reuse everywhere.

/* app/globals.css */
@import "tailwindcss";

@theme {
  /* Brand — visceral warmth */
  --color-viby: #ff6fb5;
  --color-viby-soft: #ffe1f0;
  --color-viby-ink: #2a1622;

  /* Friendship-coded accents (NOT romance reds) */
  --color-spark: #ffd166;   /* shared-interest highlight */
  --color-calm: #7ee0c2;    /* "you're matched on a hobby" */

  /* Premium motion */
  --ease-spring: cubic-bezier(0.34, 1.56, 0.64, 1);
  --radius-bubble: 1.25rem;
}

2. Low cognitive load: one primary action per screen

A profile card that surfaces shared interests (social proof) and exactly one clear next step (Hick's Law).

// components/discover/PersonCard.tsx  (Server Component by default)
import { Card } from "@/components/ui/card";
import { Avatar } from "@/components/ui/avatar";
import { WaveButton } from "./WaveButton";

type Person = {
  id: string;
  name: string;
  avatarUrl: string;
  sharedInterests: string[]; // the unit of attraction — hobbies, not looks
};

export function PersonCard({ person }: { person: Person }) {
  return (
    <Card className="rounded-[--radius-bubble] p-5 bg-white">
      <div className="flex items-center gap-3">
        <Avatar src={person.avatarUrl} alt={person.name} className="size-14" />
        <div>
          <h3 className="font-semibold text-viby-ink">{person.name}</h3>
          {/* Social proof, not a hot-or-not score */}
          <p className="text-sm text-viby-ink/70">
            {person.sharedInterests.length} shared interests
          </p>
        </div>
      </div>

      <ul className="mt-3 flex flex-wrap gap-2">
        {person.sharedInterests.slice(0, 3).map((tag) => (
          <li
            key={tag}
            className="rounded-full bg-viby-soft px-3 py-1 text-xs font-medium text-viby"
          >
            {tag}
          </li>
        ))}
      </ul>

      {/* Exactly ONE primary action */}
      <WaveButton personId={person.id} className="mt-4 w-full" />
    </Card>
  );
}

3. The feedback loop: optimistic "Wave" with a Server Action

A wave is reciprocity-coded (friendly), not a swipe (judgmental). We make it feel instant with React 19's useOptimistic, then persist via a Server Action.

// app/discover/actions.ts
"use server";

import { createServerClient } from "@supabase/ssr";
import { cookies } from "next/headers";
import { revalidatePath } from "next/cache";

export async function sendWave(personId: string) {
  const cookieStore = await cookies(); // async in Next.js 16

  const supabase = createServerClient(
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL!,
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY!,
    {
      cookies: {
        getAll: () => cookieStore.getAll(),
        setAll: (toSet) =>
          toSet.forEach(({ name, value, options }) =>
            cookieStore.set(name, value, options),
          ),
      },
    },
  );

  const {
    data: { user },
  } = await supabase.auth.getUser();
  if (!user) throw new Error("Not authenticated");

  const { error } = await supabase
    .from("waves")
    .insert({ from_user: user.id, to_user: personId });

  if (error) throw error;
  revalidatePath("/discover");
}

// components/discover/WaveButton.tsx
"use client";

import { useOptimistic, useTransition } from "react";
import { motion } from "framer-motion";
import { Button } from "@/components/ui/button";
import { sendWave } from "@/app/discover/actions";

export function WaveButton({
  personId,
  className,
}: {
  personId: string;
  className?: string;
}) {
  const [waved, setWaved] = useOptimistic(false);
  const [isPending, startTransition] = useTransition();

  return (
    <motion.div whileTap={{ scale: 0.96 }} className={className}>
      <Button
        disabled={waved || isPending}
        className="w-full bg-viby text-white transition-[transform,background-color]"
        onClick={() =>
          startTransition(async () => {
            setWaved(true); // feels instant — premium responsiveness
            await sendWave(personId);
          })
        }
      >
        {waved ? "Wave sent 👋" : "Say hi"}
      </Button>
    </motion.div>
  );
}

4. Onboarding: progressive disclosure + goal-gradient

Ask for 3 interests, show a head-start progress bar, celebrate on completion.

// components/onboarding/InterestPicker.tsx
"use client";

import { useState } from "react";
import { motion, AnimatePresence } from "framer-motion";

const INTERESTS = ["Bouldering", "Lo-fi", "Film photography", "Ramen", "Indie games", "Plants"];
const GOAL = 3;

export function InterestPicker() {
  const [picked, setPicked] = useState<string[]>([]);
  // Goal-gradient: start the bar at 20%, never empty
  const progress = 20 + (picked.length / GOAL) * 80;
  const done = picked.length >= GOAL;

  function toggle(tag: string) {
    setPicked((p) =>
      p.includes(tag) ? p.filter((t) => t !== tag) : [...p, tag],
    );
  }

  return (
    <div className="space-y-5">
      <div className="h-2 w-full overflow-hidden rounded-full bg-viby-soft">
        <motion.div
          className="h-full bg-viby"
          animate={{ width: `${Math.min(progress, 100)}%` }}
          transition={{ type: "spring", stiffness: 180, damping: 22 }}
        />
      </div>

      <p className="text-sm text-viby-ink/70">Pick {GOAL} things you love</p>

      <div className="flex flex-wrap gap-2">
        {INTERESTS.map((tag) => {
          const active = picked.includes(tag);
          return (
            <motion.button
              key={tag}
              whileTap={{ scale: 0.94 }}
              onClick={() => toggle(tag)}
              className={`rounded-full px-4 py-2 text-sm font-medium transition-colors ${
                active ? "bg-viby text-white" : "bg-viby-soft text-viby"
              }`}
            >
              {tag}
            </motion.button>
          );
        })}
      </div>

      <AnimatePresence>
        {done && (
          <motion.p
            initial={{ opacity: 0, y: 8 }}
            animate={{ opacity: 1, y: 0 }}
            exit={{ opacity: 0 }}
            className="font-semibold text-viby"
          >
            Nice — we found 24 people who love these too ✨
          </motion.p>
        )}
      </AnimatePresence>
    </div>
  );
}

Note the celebration line: it's social proof delivered at the moment of investment — the strongest time to reduce drop-off.

Architecture / diagram

The healthy feedback loop, and where each psychological lever attaches:

flowchart TD
    A[User opens Discover] --> B[PersonCard: shared interests = social proof]
    B --> C{One clear action: Say hi}
    C -->|whileTap + useOptimistic| D[Instant visual reward < 100ms]
    D --> E[Server Action: insert wave -> Supabase]
    E --> F[Realtime: recipient gets 'someone waved' ping]
    F --> G[Reciprocity: they wave back]
    G --> H[Match on a HOBBY, not a face]
    H --> I[Conversation seeded with shared interest]
    I -->|healthy goal reached| A

    style D fill:#ffe1f0,stroke:#ff6fb5
    style H fill:#7ee0c2,stroke:#2a1622

The loop closes on a goal the user actually wants (a real conversation), not on raw session time. That single design decision is what keeps Viby out of dark-pattern territory.

Best practices & pitfalls

Do

  • Give every action sub-100ms feedback (optimistic UI, whileTap). Perceived speed is premium.
  • Use motion with intent: --ease-spring for playful, short durations (150–300ms). Animation should clarify, never delay.
  • Anchor matches to shared interests, never appearance scores.
  • Respect prefers-reduced-motion — wrap large animations so accessibility users aren't punished.
  • Write copy in a warm, second-person voice ("find your people"), not transactional ("browse users").

Don't

  • ❌ Confirmshaming ("No thanks, I hate making friends") — manipulative and off-brand.
  • ❌ Forced continuity / roach motel — easy to subscribe, impossible to leave. Make "delete account" findable.
  • ❌ Infinite variable-reward feeds with no natural stopping point. Add "you're all caught up" states.
  • ❌ Fake urgency / vanity counters ("5 people are looking at you NOW!") that aren't true.
  • ❌ Swipe-on-faces, hot-or-not scoring, or proximity-first matching — these silently turn a friendship app into a dating clone.
  • ❌ Dumping 10 onboarding fields on screen one. Disclose progressively.

Exercises

  1. Warm empty state (easy). The Discover feed has no people yet. Design and build an empty state with an illustration placeholder, one encouraging line of copy, and a single CTA ("Add interests"). No dead ends.
  2. Reduced-motion guard (medium). Refactor InterestPicker so all Framer Motion animations are disabled when the user has prefers-reduced-motion: reduce. Hint: Framer Motion's useReducedMotion() hook.
  3. Dark-pattern audit (medium). Take any onboarding flow from an app on your phone. List 3 dark patterns it uses and rewrite each as an ethical alternative in Viby's voice.
  4. Streak, ethically (hard). Add a "kept in touch" streak that rewards replying within 48h — but design it so missing a day produces gentle encouragement, never guilt or loss-aversion pressure. Justify your copy choices in comments.
  5. Taxonomy redesign (hard). Replace any one screen's "users" framing with "interests" framing. Show before/after and explain how the affordance shift moves Viby further from a dating clone.

Mini-project: the "Someone waved at you" live ping

Build a realtime feedback loop that closes the reciprocity cycle. When another user sends a wave (via the Server Action above), the recipient sees a warm, dismissible toast — no fake urgency, just genuine social signal.

// components/discover/WaveListener.tsx
"use client";

import { useEffect, useState } from "react";
import { createBrowserClient } from "@supabase/ssr";
import { motion, AnimatePresence } from "framer-motion";

export function WaveListener({ userId }: { userId: string }) {
  const [from, setFrom] = useState<string | null>(null);

  useEffect(() => {
    const supabase = createBrowserClient(
      process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL!,
      process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY!,
    );

    const channel = supabase
      .channel("waves-inbox")
      .on(
        "postgres_changes",
        {
          event: "INSERT",
          schema: "public",
          table: "waves",
          filter: `to_user=eq.${userId}`,
        },
        (payload) => setFrom((payload.new as { from_user: string }).from_user),
      )
      .subscribe();

    return () => {
      supabase.removeChannel(channel);
    };
  }, [userId]);

  return (
    <AnimatePresence>
      {from && (
        <motion.div
          initial={{ opacity: 0, y: 24, scale: 0.95 }}
          animate={{ opacity: 1, y: 0, scale: 1 }}
          exit={{ opacity: 0, y: 24 }}
          transition={{ type: "spring", stiffness: 220, damping: 20 }}
          className="fixed bottom-6 left-1/2 -translate-x-1/2 rounded-[--radius-bubble]
                     bg-white px-5 py-3 shadow-lg ring-1 ring-viby-soft"
        >
          <p className="text-sm font-medium text-viby-ink">
            Someone waved at you 👋{" "}
            <button
              onClick={() => setFrom(null)}
              className="ml-2 font-semibold text-viby"
            >
              Wave back
            </button>
          </p>
        </motion.div>
      )}
    </AnimatePresence>
  );
}

Mount it once in your authenticated layout:

// app/(app)/layout.tsx  (Server Component)
import { createServerClient } from "@supabase/ssr";
import { cookies } from "next/headers";
import { WaveListener } from "@/components/discover/WaveListener";

export default async function AppLayout({
  children,
}: {
  children: React.ReactNode;
}) {
  const cookieStore = await cookies();
  const supabase = createServerClient(
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL!,
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY!,
    {
      cookies: {
        getAll: () => cookieStore.getAll(),
        setAll: () => {}, // read-only in a layout
      },
    },
  );

  const {
    data: { user },
  } = await supabase.auth.getUser();

  return (
    <>
      {children}
      {user && <WaveListener userId={user.id} />}
    </>
  );
}

Why this is ethical by design: the toast appears only on a genuine event, it's instantly dismissible, and the CTA points back to a real human goal (reciprocate, start talking) — not "open the app again." That's the whole philosophy of this chapter compiled into one component.

Route-protection note: this layout reads auth but doesn't enforce it. In Next.js 16 we handle redirect logic in proxy.ts (Chapter 9), not a middleware.ts file.

Key takeaways

  • Users feel before they think — engineer the visceral layer (color, space, motion) deliberately.
  • Cut extraneous cognitive load: one primary action per screen, social proof over raw choice.
  • Build feedback loops around the user's goal (real connection), not around your session-time metric.
  • Onboard with progressive disclosure and a goal-gradient head start; celebrate investment with social proof.
  • Premium = responsiveness: optimistic UI and intentful, sub-300ms motion.
  • Avoid dark patterns — they win the week and lose the user.
  • Keep Viby not a dating clone through affordances and language: the unit of attraction is a shared hobby, never a face.

Up next — Chapter 14: Component Architecture & Design Systems at Scale, where we turn these psychological principles into a reusable, tokenized component library that stays consistent as Viby grows.

Chapter 14 — Databases & PostgreSQL Data Modeling

Your app is only as trustworthy as the data model underneath it — so let's design Viby's brain before we wire up its mouth.

Up to now, Viby has lived in memory. Zustand stores held your profiles, your swipes, your matches — and every refresh wiped the world clean. That was perfect for learning the front end. But a real social-discovery platform needs to remember: who you are, who you vibed with, who matched back, and what everyone is posting. This chapter is where Viby grows a permanent memory.

What you'll learn

  • What a relational database actually is, and why PostgreSQL is the industry default
  • Tables, rows, columns, and how to choose good data types
  • Primary keys and foreign keys — the glue that makes data relational
  • The three relationship shapes: one-to-one (1:1), one-to-many (1:N), and many-to-many (N:M)
  • Normalization — how to stop your data from rotting
  • Indexes — how to make queries fast (and when not to)
  • A complete, production-shaped schema for Viby: profiles, swipes, matches, posts, messages

Prerequisites

  • Chapter 11–13 (Backend foundations): you understand servers, environment variables, and Supabase basics.
  • Chapter 8 (Auth): you know Supabase Auth issues a user in auth.users with a UUID id. We'll build on top of that.
  • Comfort reading SQL is not assumed — we teach it here.

Theory

Why a relational database?

Imagine Viby's data as a giant shared spreadsheet workbook. Each sheet is a table (profiles, posts, messages). Each row is one record — one person, one post, one message. Each column is one attribute with a fixed type — a display name (text), an age (integer), a "created at" timestamp.

A relational database is that workbook with superpowers: it enforces rules (this column must be a valid email; this post must belong to a real user), it answers questions across sheets instantly ("show me every post by people I matched with this week"), and it guarantees that two requests at the same time don't corrupt each other (ACID transactions — Atomicity, Consistency, Isolation, Durability).

We use PostgreSQL because it's the open-source gold standard: rock-solid, feature-rich (JSON, full-text search, geospatial), and it's exactly what Supabase runs under the hood. When you write SQL for Supabase, you're writing real Postgres.

Keys: the difference between a list and a graph

A primary key (PK) is the unique fingerprint of a row. No two rows in a table may share it. In Viby, a profile's primary key is its id (a UUID).

A foreign key (FK) is a column that points at another table's primary key. A post.author_id is a foreign key to profiles.id. This is the magic word "relational": foreign keys turn isolated lists into a connected graph, and the database refuses to let you create a post whose author doesn't exist (referential integrity).

Analogy: a primary key is your passport number. A foreign key is your name written on a concert ticket — it only means something because it points back to a real passport.

The three relationship shapes

One-to-one (1:1) — each row in A matches at most one row in B. Example: an auth.users record has exactly one profiles record. We model this by giving profiles.id the same value as the user's auth id.

One-to-many (1:N) — one row in A owns many rows in B, but each B belongs to one A. Example: one profile authors many posts. We put the FK on the "many" side: posts.author_id.

Many-to-many (N:M) — rows on both sides connect to many on the other. Example: profiles ↔ interests (you have many hobbies; each hobby has many fans). You can't store this with a single FK, so you introduce a join table (also called a junction or bridge table) that holds one row per pairing.

Normalization — don't repeat yourself, with data

Normalization is the discipline of storing each fact exactly once. The classic smell is duplication: if you copy a user's display name into every post they write, then a name change means hunting down hundreds of rows — and you'll miss some, leaving Viby showing two names for one person.

The practical rules (first three normal forms, plain-English):

  1. 1NF — one value per cell. No comma-jammed lists like "music,hiking,art" in a single column.
  2. 2NF — every column depends on the whole primary key (matters for composite keys).
  3. 3NF — non-key columns depend only on the key, not on each other. Don't store birth_year and age — age is derivable.

Normalize first. Denormalize later, deliberately, only when measured performance demands it (e.g., a cached like_count).

Indexes — the book's table of contents

Without an index, finding "all posts by author X" forces Postgres to scan every post (a sequential scan). An index is a sorted lookup structure (a B-tree) that turns that scan into a near-instant jump — exactly like the index at the back of a textbook.

The trade-off: indexes cost disk space and slow down writes (every insert must update the index too). So index the columns you filter, join, or sort on — and not much else. Foreign keys are almost always worth indexing.

In practice

We'll build Viby's schema in the Supabase SQL Editor (Dashboard → SQL Editor) or as a migration file. Everything below is real Postgres.

1. The profiles table (1:1 with auth.users)

-- One profile per authenticated user.
create table public.profiles (
  id          uuid primary key references auth.users (id) on delete cascade,
  username    text unique not null,
  display_name text not null,
  bio         text,
  avatar_url  text,
  birth_year  smallint check (birth_year between 1900 and 2025),
  city        text,
  created_at  timestamptz not null default now(),
  updated_at  timestamptz not null default now()
);

Notice id is both the primary key and a foreign key to auth.users(id) — that's the 1:1 link. on delete cascade means: if the auth user is deleted, their profile vanishes automatically. username unique enforces no duplicate handles. check constraints reject bad data at the door.

2. Interests (N:M via a join table)

A profile has many interests; an interest has many fans. That's many-to-many.

create table public.interests (
  id    serial primary key,
  slug  text unique not null,    -- e.g. 'bouldering'
  label text not null            -- e.g. 'Bouldering'
);

-- The join table: one row per (profile, interest) pairing.
create table public.profile_interests (
  profile_id  uuid not null references public.profiles (id) on delete cascade,
  interest_id integer not null references public.interests (id) on delete cascade,
  primary key (profile_id, interest_id)   -- composite key prevents duplicates
);

The composite primary key (profile_id, interest_id) guarantees you can't add the same interest to a profile twice — and gives us a built-in index on the pairing.

3. Swipes and matches (the heart of discovery)

A swipe is a directed action: I vibed (or passed) on someone. A match is the mutual result.

create table public.swipes (
  id         uuid primary key default gen_random_uuid(),
  swiper_id  uuid not null references public.profiles (id) on delete cascade,
  swipee_id  uuid not null references public.profiles (id) on delete cascade,
  direction  text not null check (direction in ('vibe', 'pass')),
  created_at timestamptz not null default now(),
  -- you can only swipe a given person once
  unique (swiper_id, swipee_id),
  -- you can't swipe yourself
  check (swiper_id <> swipee_id)
);

create table public.matches (
  id          uuid primary key default gen_random_uuid(),
  profile_a   uuid not null references public.profiles (id) on delete cascade,
  profile_b   uuid not null references public.profiles (id) on delete cascade,
  created_at  timestamptz not null default now(),
  -- store the pair in a canonical order so (a,b) and (b,a) can't both exist
  check (profile_a < profile_b),
  unique (profile_a, profile_b)
);

The profile_a < profile_b trick is a pro move: by always storing the smaller UUID first, we make a match between two people unique regardless of who swiped first.

4. Posts (1:N) and messages (1:N within a match)

create table public.posts (
  id         uuid primary key default gen_random_uuid(),
  author_id  uuid not null references public.profiles (id) on delete cascade,
  body       text not null check (char_length(body) <= 2000),
  image_url  text,
  like_count integer not null default 0,  -- deliberate denormalization
  created_at timestamptz not null default now()
);

create table public.messages (
  id          uuid primary key default gen_random_uuid(),
  match_id    uuid not null references public.matches (id) on delete cascade,
  sender_id   uuid not null references public.profiles (id) on delete cascade,
  body        text not null check (char_length(body) between 1 and 4000),
  created_at  timestamptz not null default now()
);

Messages belong to a match (you can only DM someone you matched with) and record who sent them — two foreign keys, both meaningful.

5. Indexes for the queries we'll actually run

-- "Show me a feed: recent posts by author."
create index posts_author_created_idx on public.posts (author_id, created_at desc);

-- "Have these two already swiped each other?"
create index swipes_swipee_idx on public.swipes (swipee_id);

-- "Load this match's messages in order."
create index messages_match_created_idx on public.messages (match_id, created_at);

These are composite indexes ordered to match our access patterns: filter by match_id, then sort by created_at. Postgres uses the leftmost columns, so order matters.

6. Reading it from Next.js 16 (Server Component + @supabase/ssr)

Now the payoff — querying the schema from a React Server Component. We use the @supabase/ssr server client with getAll/setAll cookie handlers.

// app/lib/supabase/server.ts
import { createServerClient } from "@supabase/ssr";
import { cookies } from "next/headers";

export async function createClient() {
  const cookieStore = await cookies(); // Next.js 16: cookies() is async

  return createServerClient(
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL!,
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY!,
    {
      cookies: {
        getAll() {
          return cookieStore.getAll();
        },
        setAll(cookiesToSet) {
          cookiesToSet.forEach(({ name, value, options }) =>
            cookieStore.set(name, value, options)
          );
        },
      },
    }
  );
}

// app/feed/page.tsx — a Server Component, no "use client" needed
import { createClient } from "@/app/lib/supabase/server";

export default async function FeedPage() {
  const supabase = await createClient();

  // Join posts -> profiles in ONE query using the FK relationship.
  const { data: posts, error } = await supabase
    .from("posts")
    .select(
      `id, body, image_url, like_count, created_at,
       author:profiles ( username, display_name, avatar_url )`
    )
    .order("created_at", { ascending: false })
    .limit(20);

  if (error) throw error;

  return (
    <ul className="mx-auto max-w-md space-y-4 p-4">
      {posts.map((post) => (
        <li key={post.id} className="rounded-2xl bg-white p-4 shadow-sm">
          <p className="font-semibold text-[--color-brand]">
            {post.author.display_name}
          </p>
          <p className="mt-1 text-sm text-gray-700">{post.body}</p>
        </li>
      ))}
    </ul>
  );
}

Supabase's author:profiles ( ... ) syntax follows the author_id foreign key automatically — that's relational modeling paying dividends. The brand pink lives in a Tailwind v4 theme token (@theme { --color-brand: #FF6FB5; }), referenced as [--color-brand].

7. Creating a swipe with a Server Action

// app/actions/swipe.ts
"use server";

import { createClient } from "@/app/lib/supabase/server";
import { revalidatePath } from "next/cache";

export async function recordSwipe(swipeeId: string, direction: "vibe" | "pass") {
  const supabase = await createClient();
  const {
    data: { user },
  } = await supabase.auth.getUser();
  if (!user) throw new Error("Not authenticated");

  // Insert the swipe (the UNIQUE constraint blocks accidental double-swipes).
  const { error } = await supabase
    .from("swipes")
    .insert({ swiper_id: user.id, swipee_id: swipeeId, direction });

  if (error && error.code !== "23505") throw error; // 23505 = unique violation

  revalidatePath("/discover");
}

In production you'd promote the mutual-match check into a Postgres function/trigger so a match is created atomically — but the schema above is what makes that trigger trivial to write.

Architecture / diagram

erDiagram
    auth_users ||--|| profiles : "1:1"
    profiles ||--o{ posts : "authors (1:N)"
    profiles ||--o{ swipes : "swiper (1:N)"
    profiles ||--o{ profile_interests : ""
    interests ||--o{ profile_interests : ""
    profiles ||--o{ matches : "in (1:N)"
    matches ||--o{ messages : "contains (1:N)"
    profiles ||--o{ messages : "sends (1:N)"

    profiles {
        uuid id PK_FK
        text username
        text display_name
    }
    swipes {
        uuid id PK
        uuid swiper_id FK
        uuid swipee_id FK
        text direction
    }
    matches {
        uuid id PK
        uuid profile_a FK
        uuid profile_b FK
    }
    posts {
        uuid id PK
        uuid author_id FK
        text body
    }
    messages {
        uuid id PK
        uuid match_id FK
        uuid sender_id FK
    }

profile_interests is the N:M join table bridging profiles and interests. Every other arrow is a 1:N relationship anchored by a foreign key.

Best practices & pitfalls

  • Do put foreign keys on the "many" side and index them. An unindexed FK is the #1 cause of mysterious slow joins.
  • Do use timestamptz (timezone-aware), never naive timestamp. Store UTC, format in the UI.
  • Do use UUIDs (gen_random_uuid()) for public-facing IDs so you don't leak row counts or invite enumeration attacks.
  • Do enforce rules in the database with check, unique, and not null. The DB is your last line of defense; app code has bugs.
  • Don't store lists in a single column ("music,art"). That breaks 1NF and makes filtering miserable — use a join table.
  • Don't denormalize prematurely. Add a cached like_count only after you've measured the read cost.
  • Don't forget Row Level Security (RLS). In Supabase, a table with no RLS policy is wide open. Every table here needs alter table ... enable row level security; plus policies (covered next chapter).
  • Don't delete rows you might need for audit/abuse review — consider a soft-delete deleted_at timestamptz column.

Exercises

  1. Warm-up: Add a pronouns text column and an is_verified boolean not null default false column to profiles. Write the alter table statements.
  2. Relationships: Model a post_likes table so a user can like a post at most once. Which relationship shape is this, and what should the primary key be?
  3. Indexing: A query filters messages by match_id and sorts newest-first, but it's slow. Write the exact create index that fixes it and explain the column order.
  4. Normalization: You find profiles.city and profiles.country are repeated thousands of times with typos. Refactor into a cities table and an FK. Sketch the new schema.
  5. Challenge: Write a SQL check constraint (or trigger) that prevents a user from messaging in a match they don't belong to. Why is the FK alone insufficient?

Mini-project — "Vibes that matched"

Build a Server Component page at app/matches/page.tsx that shows the current user's matches with the other person's display name and avatar.

Requirements: 1. Query matches where the logged-in user is either profile_a or profile_b (hint: .or("profile_a.eq...,profile_b.eq...")). 2. For each match, resolve the other profile (the one that isn't you) and show their display_name + avatar_url. 3. For each match, fetch the most recent message body as a preview, ordered by created_at desc, limited to 1. 4. Render each match as a tappable card (mobile-first, brand-pink accent) linking to app/messages/[matchId]/page.tsx. 5. Wrap the list in a Framer Motion motion.ul so cards fade-and-rise in with <AnimatePresence> as data loads.

Stretch: add an index that makes the "latest message per match" lookup fast, and explain why your composite index helps.

This project forces you to traverse three tables (matches → profiles → messages) through foreign keys — exactly the relational thinking this chapter built.

Key takeaways

  • A relational database stores facts as tables of typed rows and columns, enforcing integrity the app can't be trusted to.
  • Primary keys uniquely identify rows; foreign keys connect tables — together they turn lists into a navigable graph.
  • The three shapes: 1:1 (shared/foreign key), 1:N (FK on the many side), N:M (a join table).
  • Normalize to store each fact once; denormalize only with evidence.
  • Indexes make reads fast at a small write cost — index what you filter, join, and sort on.
  • Viby's core is just six tables: profiles, interests/profile_interests, swipes, matches, posts, messages.

Up next — Chapter 15: Supabase Row Level Security & Authorization, where we lock these tables down so users can only ever read and write the rows they're allowed to.

Chapter 15 — Supabase Architecture & Setup

Up to now Viby has been a beautiful app pretending to have a backend. In this chapter we give it a real one — without breaking the demo for a single second.

What you'll learn

  • What Supabase actually is: managed Postgres + Auth + Storage + Realtime + Edge, and where each piece fits Viby.
  • How to create a Supabase project, find your URL and keys, and wire them into env vars safely.
  • How to use the SQL editor and run our schema.sql to stand up every table and Row Level Security policy at once.
  • The crucial difference between the browser client and the server client with @supabase/ssr, and why we need both.
  • The connected-vs-demo mode flag pattern that lets Viby run with zero backend or a full one — no code changes in your components.

Prerequisites

  • Chapter 6 (Next.js App Router) and Chapter 8 (Server vs Client Components) — we lean hard on the server/client boundary here.
  • Chapter 5 (TypeScript for React) — clients and rows are typed.
  • Chapter 11 (Color & theming) only insofar as you have a running Viby app you can npm run dev.
  • A free Supabase account (we'll create one together) and the Viby repo with @supabase/ssr already in package.json.

Theory

For the first fourteen chapters, Viby's data lived in a Zustand store seeded from lib/mock.ts — fake profiles, fake matches, fake messages, all in memory. That was deliberate: it let you build every screen, animation, and interaction without first becoming a database administrator. But an in-memory store forgets everything on refresh, can't be shared between two phones, and has no concept of "you." To ship a real social-discovery app, we need a backend that persists data, knows who's logged in, stores photos, and pushes live updates.

You could assemble that yourself: a Postgres server, an auth service, an object store like S3, a websocket layer, plus the glue between them. That's weeks of plumbing before you write a single feature. Supabase is that whole stack, managed for you, around a real Postgres database.

Think of Supabase as a fully-furnished apartment versus an empty lot. The empty lot (raw cloud infrastructure) is yours to build on, but you're pouring the foundation and running the pipes. The furnished apartment gives you a working kitchen, plumbing, and locks on the door — you move in and start living.
