@import "tailwindcss";

@theme {
  --color-viby: #ff6fb5;
  --color-viby-soft: #ffe1ef;
  --radius-card: 1.5rem;
}

3. Decision-after-scrolling: the gated Connect button

We track scroll progress and only enable Connect once the viewer reaches the end. This is the heart of the chapter — friction with a purpose.

"use client";

import { useState } from "react";
import { motion, useScroll, useMotionValueEvent } from "framer-motion";
import { PhotoCarousel } from "./photo-carousel";
import { ConnectModal } from "./connect-modal";

type Hobby = { id: string; label: string; emoji: string };

export function ImmersiveProfile({
  profile,
  mutual,
}: {
  profile: any;
  mutual: Hobby[];
}) {
  const { scrollYProgress } = useScroll();
  const [reachedEnd, setReachedEnd] = useState(false);
  const [open, setOpen] = useState(false);

  useMotionValueEvent(scrollYProgress, "change", (p) => {
    if (p > 0.85) setReachedEnd(true); // unlock near the bottom
  });

  return (
    <div className="mx-auto max-w-md pb-28">
      <PhotoCarousel photos={profile.photos} />

      <header className="px-5 pt-4">
        <h1 className="text-2xl font-bold">
          {profile.display_name}, {profile.age}
        </h1>
        <p className="text-sm text-neutral-500">{profile.city}</p>
        <p className="mt-3 leading-relaxed">{profile.bio}</p>
      </header>

      {/* Prompts */}
      <section className="mt-6 space-y-3 px-5">
        {profile.prompts.map((p: any) => (
          <div key={p.question} className="rounded-card bg-viby-soft p-4">
            <p className="text-xs font-semibold uppercase text-viby">
              {p.question}
            </p>
            <p className="mt-1 text-lg font-medium">{p.answer}</p>
          </div>
        ))}
      </section>

      {/* Lifestyle grid */}
      <section className="mt-6 grid grid-cols-2 gap-3 px-5">
        {profile.lifestyle.map((l: any) => (
          <div key={l.key} className="rounded-card border p-3">
            <p className="text-xs text-neutral-500">{l.key}</p>
            <p className="font-medium">{l.value}</p>
          </div>
        ))}
      </section>

      {/* Activity timeline */}
      <section className="mt-6 px-5">
        <h2 className="mb-3 font-semibold">Recently on Viby</h2>
        <ol className="relative border-l border-viby-soft pl-4">
          {profile.activity.map((a: any) => (
            <li key={a.created_at} className="mb-4">
              <span className="absolute -left-[5px] mt-1.5 h-2 w-2 rounded-full bg-viby" />
              <p className="text-sm">{a.label}</p>
            </li>
          ))}
        </ol>
      </section>

      {/* Floating gated Connect button */}
      <div className="fixed inset-x-0 bottom-4 mx-auto max-w-md px-5">
        <motion.button
          disabled={!reachedEnd}
          onClick={() => setOpen(true)}
          animate={{
            opacity: reachedEnd ? 1 : 0.4,
            scale: reachedEnd ? 1 : 0.98,
          }}
          whileTap={reachedEnd ? { scale: 0.96 } : undefined}
          className="w-full rounded-full bg-viby py-4 font-semibold text-white shadow-lg disabled:cursor-not-allowed"
        >
          {reachedEnd ? `Connect with ${profile.display_name}` : "Keep scrolling to connect"}
        </motion.button>
      </div>

      <ConnectModal
        open={open}
        onClose={() => setOpen(false)}
        profile={profile}
        mutual={mutual}
      />
    </div>
  );
}

4. The Connect modal: mutual interests, icebreakers, intro message

We generate icebreakers from the mutual hobbies, let a tap pre-fill the message, then submit through a Server Action.

"use client";

import { useState } from "react";
import { motion, AnimatePresence } from "framer-motion";
import { sendConnectRequest } from "@/app/actions/connect";

type Hobby = { id: string; label: string; emoji: string };

function icebreakersFor(name: string, mutual: Hobby[]) {
  return mutual.slice(0, 3).map(
    (h) => `You both love ${h.label.toLowerCase()} ${h.emoji} — what got ${name} into it?`
  );
}

export function ConnectModal({
  open,
  onClose,
  profile,
  mutual,
}: {
  open: boolean;
  onClose: () => void;
  profile: any;
  mutual: Hobby[];
}) {
  const [message, setMessage] = useState("");
  const [pending, setPending] = useState(false);
  const icebreakers = icebreakersFor(profile.display_name, mutual);

  async function handleSend() {
    setPending(true);
    await sendConnectRequest({ targetId: profile.id, message });
    setPending(false);
    onClose();
  }

  return (
    <AnimatePresence>
      {open && (
        <motion.div
          className="fixed inset-0 z-50 flex items-end bg-black/40"
          initial={{ opacity: 0 }}
          animate={{ opacity: 1 }}
          exit={{ opacity: 0 }}
          onClick={onClose}
        >
          <motion.div
            className="w-full rounded-t-3xl bg-white p-5"
            initial={{ y: "100%" }}
            animate={{ y: 0 }}
            exit={{ y: "100%" }}
            transition={{ type: "spring", damping: 30, stiffness: 300 }}
            onClick={(e) => e.stopPropagation()}
          >
            <h2 className="text-lg font-bold">
              Connect with {profile.display_name}
            </h2>

            {mutual.length > 0 ? (
              <div className="mt-3 flex flex-wrap gap-2">
                {mutual.map((h) => (
                  <span
                    key={h.id}
                    className="rounded-full bg-viby-soft px-3 py-1 text-sm text-viby"
                  >
                    {h.emoji} {h.label}
                  </span>
                ))}
              </div>
            ) : (
              <p className="mt-3 text-sm text-neutral-500">
                No shared hobbies yet — say hi and find out what clicks.
              </p>
            )}

            <p className="mt-4 text-xs font-semibold uppercase text-neutral-400">
              Icebreakers
            </p>
            <div className="mt-2 space-y-2">
              {icebreakers.map((ib) => (
                <button
                  key={ib}
                  onClick={() => setMessage(ib)}
                  className="w-full rounded-card border border-viby-soft p-3 text-left text-sm"
                >
                  {ib}
                </button>
              ))}
            </div>

            <textarea
              value={message}
              onChange={(e) => setMessage(e.target.value)}
              placeholder="Write your intro message..."
              rows={3}
              className="mt-4 w-full rounded-card border p-3 text-sm outline-none focus:border-viby"
            />

            <button
              onClick={handleSend}
              disabled={pending || message.trim().length === 0}
              className="mt-3 w-full rounded-full bg-viby py-3 font-semibold text-white disabled:opacity-50"
            >
              {pending ? "Sending..." : "Send connect request"}
            </button>
          </motion.div>
        </motion.div>
      )}
    </AnimatePresence>
  );
}

5. The Server Action

// app/actions/connect.ts
"use server";

import { revalidatePath } from "next/cache";
import { createClient } from "@/lib/supabase/server";

export async function sendConnectRequest({
  targetId,
  message,
}: {
  targetId: string;
  message: string;
}) {
  const supabase = await createClient();
  const {
    data: { user },
  } = await supabase.auth.getUser();
  if (!user) throw new Error("Not authenticated");

  const clean = message.trim();
  if (clean.length === 0 || clean.length > 1000) {
    throw new Error("Message must be 1–1000 characters.");
  }

  const { error } = await supabase.from("connect_requests").insert({
    from_id: user.id,
    to_id: targetId,
    intro_message: clean,
    status: "pending",
  });

  if (error) throw new Error(error.message);
  revalidatePath("/connections");
}

Row-Level Security (Chapter 17) does the real enforcement on the server; never trust the client to set from_id. The Server Action derives it from the authenticated session.

Architecture / diagram

flowchart TD
  A["Feed card tap"] --> B["/profile/[username] - Server Component"]
  B --> C["Supabase select: photos, prompts, lifestyle, interests, activity"]
  B --> D["Compute mutual = viewer.hobbies ∩ owner.hobbies"]
  C --> E["ImmersiveProfile - Client"]
  D --> E
  E --> F["Carousel + Prompts + Lifestyle + Timeline"]
  F --> G{"scrollYProgress > 0.85?"}
  G -- "no" --> H["Connect button dimmed + disabled"]
  G -- "yes" --> I["Connect button springs in"]
  I --> J["ConnectModal: mutual chips + icebreakers + intro"]
  J --> K["Server Action sendConnectRequest"]
  K --> L["Supabase insert connect_requests - RLS enforced"]

Best practices & pitfalls

  • Do keep data fetching in the Server Component and pass plain props down. Don't fetch the profile inside a "use client" component — you lose streaming and leak query logic to the browser.
  • Do derive from_id server-side from auth.getUser(). Don't accept a fromId from the client; it's the classic impersonation hole.
  • Do treat decision-after-scrolling as encouragement, not a wall. Unlock near the bottom (> 0.85), not at an exact pixel, and provide an accessible fallback — disabled buttons should still be reachable and explained by their label.
  • Don't stack every photo as a tall column of <img> — it tanks LCP. Render the active image (or use next/image with priority on the first) and lazy-load the rest.
  • Do stop click propagation on the modal sheet (e.stopPropagation()) so tapping inside doesn't dismiss it, while the backdrop tap still closes.
  • Don't forget key={index} on the AnimatePresence child — without a changing key, the enter/exit animation won't fire.
  • Do validate message length in the Server Action and rely on RLS. Client-side disabled is UX; the server is security.
  • Don't auto-send an icebreaker. Pre-fill, then let the human edit — a verbatim template message reads as spam.

Exercises

  1. Read progress bar. Add a thin pink progress bar fixed to the top that fills with scrollYProgress using motion.div and scaleX. (Hint: style={{ scaleX: scrollYProgress }} with transformOrigin: "left".)
  2. Tap-to-advance carousel. Add left/right tap zones over the photo so users can advance without dragging — match the story-style segments to the active index.
  3. Smarter icebreakers. When the viewer and owner share a lifestyle value (e.g. both "Early bird"), generate an extra icebreaker referencing it, ranked above the hobby ones.
  4. Optimistic Connect. Use useOptimistic to flip the button to "Request sent ✓" instantly while the Server Action runs, rolling back on error.
  5. Empty-state polish (harder). If mutual.length === 0, fetch the owner's most popular hobby and craft a discovery icebreaker ("You haven't tried pottery yet — they're obsessed. Curious?").

Mini-project

Build the "Profile Preview" self-view. Add a /profile/me route that renders the same ImmersiveProfile component with the logged-in user's data, but with two differences: (1) the gated Connect button is replaced by an "Edit profile" button that's always enabled, and (2) a banner at the top reads "This is how others see you." Reuse the carousel, prompts, lifestyle grid, and timeline untouched. This proves your profile is a genuinely reusable component and gives users the confidence-building preview every great social app ships. Bonus: add a motion shimmer on the banner so it feels alive.

Key takeaways

  • Profiles are trailers, not posters — they unfold over time to make the viewer feel the person.
  • Decision-after-scrolling is intentional, gentle friction that produces deeper connections; gate the action near the bottom, never wall it off.
  • Fetch in Server Components, animate in Client Components, and persist through Server Actions with server-derived identity + RLS.
  • Mutual interests (a set intersection) are the engine behind both the Venn chips and the icebreakers.
  • Pre-fill icebreakers, but always let the human edit — warmth beats automation.

Up next: Chapter 24 — Realtime Chat & Presence, where an accepted connect request opens a live conversation powered by Supabase Realtime, typing indicators, and read receipts.

Chapter 24 — The Activity Feed

Every great social app has a heartbeat. In this chapter, you build Viby's.

So far, Viby has been about who: profiles, matching, the people you might click with. Now we build the what they're doing — a persistent, scrollable Activity Feed where members post a photo from their morning trail run, tag it #hiking, drop a location, and watch reactions roll in live. Think Strava's "I did a thing" energy crossed with Instagram's visual scroll, minus the vanity-metric pressure. This is the feature that turns a directory of strangers into a living community.

What you'll learn

  • How to model a feed post in PostgreSQL with activity tags, geolocation, and timestamps
  • Building a composer with client-side image upload to Supabase Storage
  • Rendering an infinite-ish feed with Server Components, then hydrating interactivity
  • Reactions and comments wired through Server Actions
  • Activity-type filtering that keeps URL state shareable
  • Realtime inserts so new posts appear without a refresh
  • Row Level Security (RLS) so the feed is safe by default

Prerequisites

  • Chapter 9 (Supabase Auth and @supabase/ssr clients)
  • Chapter 12 (PostgreSQL schema design and RLS basics)
  • Chapter 15 (Supabase Storage and signed/public URLs)
  • Chapter 18 (Server Actions and revalidation)
  • Chapter 21 (Zustand for ephemeral client state)
  • Chapter 22 (Framer Motion: motion, AnimatePresence, gestures)

Theory

A feed looks simple — a list of cards — but it's secretly three hard problems stacked together: storage, freshness, and interaction.

Storage. A post is structured metadata (caption, tag, location, author, time) plus a heavy binary blob (the image). The golden rule: databases store pointers, not pixels. We push the image to Supabase Storage (object storage, served over a CDN) and save only its URL in PostgreSQL. Think of the database as a library card catalog and Storage as the shelves — the catalog card is tiny and fast to search; the book itself lives elsewhere.

Freshness. Traditional web apps are pull — you refresh, the server hands you the latest. A feed feels alive only when it's push: the server taps you on the shoulder and says "hey, Maya just posted." Supabase Realtime gives us this for free by streaming PostgreSQL's replication log (the same write-ahead log the database uses internally) over a WebSocket. When a row hits the posts table, every subscribed client hears about it in milliseconds. The analogy: instead of you walking to the mailbox every minute, the mail carrier rings your doorbell.

Interaction. Reactions and comments are writes triggered by reading. The instinct of a beginner is to fetch everything through client-side useEffect. The professional instinct in Next.js 16 is the opposite: read on the server, write through Server Actions, and only reach for the client when you need interactivity or realtime. Server Components fetch the initial feed (fast, SEO-friendly, no loading spinner), Server Actions mutate data with progressive enhancement, and a thin "use client" layer handles optimistic reactions and the live socket.

One more concept: optimistic UI. When you tap a heart, waiting 300ms for a round-trip feels broken. So we update the UI immediately (assume success), fire the mutation in the background, and only roll back if it fails. React 19's useOptimistic hook is built exactly for this.

In practice

1. The schema

Run this in the Supabase SQL editor. We model an activity_type enum so the tag set is constrained (no typo'd #hikingg), and we denormalize a reaction_count only if we measure a need — for now we count on read.

-- Constrained set of activity tags
create type activity_type as enum (
  'running', 'hiking', 'cycling', 'climbing',
  'yoga', 'gaming', 'cooking', 'music', 'art', 'other'
);

create table posts (
  id          uuid primary key default gen_random_uuid(),
  author_id   uuid not null references auth.users (id) on delete cascade,
  caption     text check (char_length(caption) <= 500),
  image_url   text,
  activity    activity_type not null default 'other',
  location    text,                          -- human label, e.g. "Griffith Park"
  created_at  timestamptz not null default now()
);

create table reactions (
  post_id     uuid not null references posts (id) on delete cascade,
  user_id     uuid not null references auth.users (id) on delete cascade,
  created_at  timestamptz not null default now(),
  primary key (post_id, user_id)            -- one reaction per user per post
);

create table comments (
  id          uuid primary key default gen_random_uuid(),
  post_id     uuid not null references posts (id) on delete cascade,
  author_id   uuid not null references auth.users (id) on delete cascade,
  body        text not null check (char_length(body) between 1 and 300),
  created_at  timestamptz not null default now()
);

-- Feed queries always sort by recency
create index posts_created_at_idx on posts (created_at desc);
create index comments_post_idx    on comments (post_id, created_at);

Now lock it down with RLS. The feed is public-read for signed-in members, but you can only write as yourself:

alter table posts     enable row level security;
alter table reactions enable row level security;
alter table comments  enable row level security;

create policy "feed is readable by authenticated users"
  on posts for select to authenticated using (true);

create policy "users insert their own posts"
  on posts for insert to authenticated
  with check (auth.uid() = author_id);

create policy "users delete their own posts"
  on posts for delete to authenticated
  using (auth.uid() = author_id);

create policy "reactions readable"
  on reactions for select to authenticated using (true);
create policy "users manage their own reactions"
  on reactions for all to authenticated
  using (auth.uid() = user_id) with check (auth.uid() = user_id);

create policy "comments readable"
  on comments for select to authenticated using (true);
create policy "users write their own comments"
  on comments for insert to authenticated
  with check (auth.uid() = author_id);

Then create a public storage bucket called post-images in the dashboard (Storage → New bucket → Public), and enable Realtime for the posts table (Database → Replication → toggle posts).

2. The feed (Server Component)

The page is a Server Component. It reads cookies, builds a server Supabase client, and fetches the first page. The activity filter comes from the URL search params so it's shareable and survives refresh. In Next.js 16, searchParams is async — you await it.

// app/feed/page.tsx
import { createClient } from "@/lib/supabase/server";
import { Composer } from "@/components/feed/composer";
import { FilterBar } from "@/components/feed/filter-bar";
import { FeedStream } from "@/components/feed/feed-stream";

export default async function FeedPage({
  searchParams,
}: {
  searchParams: Promise<{ activity?: string }>;
}) {
  const { activity } = await searchParams;
  const supabase = await createClient();

  let query = supabase
    .from("posts")
    .select(
      `id, caption, image_url, activity, location, created_at, author_id,
       author:profiles!author_id (username, avatar_url),
       reactions (user_id),
       comments (id)`,
    )
    .order("created_at", { ascending: false })
    .limit(20);

  if (activity) query = query.eq("activity", activity);

  const { data: posts } = await query;

  return (
    <main className="mx-auto max-w-xl px-4 pb-24">
      <Composer />
      <FilterBar active={activity ?? null} />
      <FeedStream initialPosts={posts ?? []} activity={activity ?? null} />
    </main>
  );
}

Our server client from Chapter 9, for reference — note the getAll/setAll cookie handlers required by @supabase/ssr:

// lib/supabase/server.ts
import { createServerClient } from "@supabase/ssr";
import { cookies } from "next/headers";

export async function createClient() {
  const cookieStore = await cookies();
  return createServerClient(
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL!,
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY!,
    {
      cookies: {
        getAll: () => cookieStore.getAll(),
        setAll: (toSet) => {
          try {
            toSet.forEach(({ name, value, options }) =>
              cookieStore.set(name, value, options),
            );
          } catch {
            // Called from a Server Component — safe to ignore; proxy.ts refreshes the session.
          }
        },
      },
    },
  );
}

3. The composer (Server Action + client upload)

We upload the image straight from the browser to Storage (keeps heavy bytes off your server function), then call a Server Action with the resulting URL. The Action runs on the server, so RLS and auth.uid() are authoritative.

// app/feed/actions.ts
"use server";

import { createClient } from "@/lib/supabase/server";
import { revalidatePath } from "next/cache";
import { redirect } from "next/navigation";

export async function createPost(formData: FormData) {
  const supabase = await createClient();
  const {
    data: { user },
  } = await supabase.auth.getUser();
  if (!user) redirect("/login");

  const caption = String(formData.get("caption") ?? "").slice(0, 500);
  const activity = String(formData.get("activity") ?? "other");
  const location = String(formData.get("location") ?? "") || null;
  const imageUrl = String(formData.get("imageUrl") ?? "") || null;

  const { error } = await supabase.from("posts").insert({
    author_id: user.id,
    caption,
    activity,
    location,
    image_url: imageUrl,
  });
  if (error) return { error: error.message };

  revalidatePath("/feed");
  return { ok: true };
}

The composer itself is a client component because it handles file selection and upload progress. Notice we use useTransition to keep the UI responsive while the Action runs:

// components/feed/composer.tsx
"use client";

import { useRef, useState, useTransition } from "react";
import { createBrowserClient } from "@supabase/ssr";
import { motion, AnimatePresence } from "framer-motion";
import { createPost } from "@/app/feed/actions";

const ACTIVITIES = ["running", "hiking", "cycling", "yoga", "gaming", "art"];

export function Composer() {
  const [preview, setPreview] = useState<string | null>(null);
  const [uploading, setUploading] = useState(false);
  const [activity, setActivity] = useState("running");
  const fileRef = useRef<HTMLInputElement>(null);
  const [pending, startTransition] = useTransition();

  const supabase = createBrowserClient(
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL!,
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY!,
  );

  async function uploadImage(file: File): Promise<string> {
    setUploading(true);
    const ext = file.name.split(".").pop();
    const path = `${crypto.randomUUID()}.${ext}`;
    const { error } = await supabase.storage
      .from("post-images")
      .upload(path, file, { cacheControl: "3600", upsert: false });
    setUploading(false);
    if (error) throw error;
    const { data } = supabase.storage.from("post-images").getPublicUrl(path);
    return data.publicUrl;
  }

  async function onSubmit(formData: FormData) {
    const file = fileRef.current?.files?.[0];
    if (file) formData.set("imageUrl", await uploadImage(file));
    formData.set("activity", activity);
    startTransition(async () => {
      await createPost(formData);
      setPreview(null);
      if (fileRef.current) fileRef.current.value = "";
    });
  }

  return (
    <form
      action={onSubmit}
      className="mb-6 rounded-2xl border border-pink-100 bg-white p-4 shadow-sm"
    >
      <textarea
        name="caption"
        placeholder="What are you up to?"
        maxLength={500}
        className="w-full resize-none rounded-xl border-0 text-base outline-none placeholder:text-gray-400"
        rows={2}
      />

      <AnimatePresence>
        {preview && (
          <motion.img
            src={preview}
            alt="preview"
            initial={{ opacity: 0, scale: 0.96 }}
            animate={{ opacity: 1, scale: 1 }}
            exit={{ opacity: 0 }}
            className="my-3 max-h-72 w-full rounded-xl object-cover"
          />
        )}
      </AnimatePresence>

      <div className="mt-2 flex items-center gap-2 overflow-x-auto pb-1">
        {ACTIVITIES.map((a) => (
          <button
            key={a}
            type="button"
            onClick={() => setActivity(a)}
            className={`shrink-0 rounded-full px-3 py-1 text-sm transition ${
              activity === a
                ? "bg-[var(--color-brand)] text-white"
                : "bg-pink-50 text-pink-600"
            }`}
          >
            #{a}
          </button>
        ))}
      </div>

      <input
        name="location"
        placeholder="Add a location"
        className="mt-2 w-full rounded-lg bg-gray-50 px-3 py-2 text-sm outline-none"
      />

      <div className="mt-3 flex items-center justify-between">
        <label className="cursor-pointer text-sm font-medium text-pink-600">
          + Photo
          <input
            ref={fileRef}
            type="file"
            accept="image/*"
            hidden
            onChange={(e) => {
              const f = e.target.files?.[0];
              if (f) setPreview(URL.createObjectURL(f));
            }}
          />
        </label>
        <button
          disabled={pending || uploading}
          className="rounded-full bg-[var(--color-brand)] px-5 py-2 text-sm font-semibold text-white disabled:opacity-50"
        >
          {uploading ? "Uploading…" : pending ? "Posting…" : "Post"}
        </button>
      </div>
    </form>
  );
}

Our Tailwind v4 brand token, defined once in CSS — no tailwind.config.js:

/* app/globals.css */
@import "tailwindcss";

@theme {
  --color-brand: #ff6fb5;
  --color-brand-soft: #ffe3f1;
}

4. Reactions, comments, and realtime

The feed stream is a client component so it can own the live socket and optimistic state. It seeds from initialPosts (already fetched on the server — no spinner), then listens for inserts.

// components/feed/feed-stream.tsx
"use client";

import { useEffect, useState } from "react";
import { createBrowserClient } from "@supabase/ssr";
import { motion, AnimatePresence } from "framer-motion";
import { PostCard, type Post } from "./post-card";

export function FeedStream({
  initialPosts,
  activity,
}: {
  initialPosts: Post[];
  activity: string | null;
}) {
  const [posts, setPosts] = useState<Post[]>(initialPosts);

  useEffect(() => setPosts(initialPosts), [initialPosts]);

  useEffect(() => {
    const supabase = createBrowserClient(
      process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL!,
      process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY!,
    );

    const channel = supabase
      .channel("feed-inserts")
      .on(
        "postgres_changes",
        { event: "INSERT", schema: "public", table: "posts" },
        (payload) => {
          const row = payload.new as Post;
          // Respect the active filter
          if (activity && row.activity !== activity) return;
          setPosts((prev) =>
            prev.some((p) => p.id === row.id) ? prev : [row, ...prev],
          );
        },
      )
      .subscribe();

    return () => {
      supabase.removeChannel(channel);
    };
  }, [activity]);

  return (
    <ul className="space-y-4">
      <AnimatePresence initial={false}>
        {posts.map((post) => (
          <motion.li
            key={post.id}
            layout
            initial={{ opacity: 0, y: -12 }}
            animate={{ opacity: 1, y: 0 }}
            exit={{ opacity: 0 }}
          >
            <PostCard post={post} />
          </motion.li>
        ))}
      </AnimatePresence>
    </ul>
  );
}

The reaction is the showcase for optimistic UI. We use React 19's useOptimistic so the heart fills instantly:

// components/feed/like-button.tsx
"use client";

import { useOptimistic, useTransition } from "react";
import { motion } from "framer-motion";
import { toggleReaction } from "@/app/feed/actions";

export function LikeButton({
  postId,
  count,
  reacted,
}: {
  postId: string;
  count: number;
  reacted: boolean;
}) {
  const [, startTransition] = useTransition();
  const [optimistic, setOptimistic] = useOptimistic(
    { count, reacted },
    (_state, next: boolean) => ({
      reacted: next,
      count: count + (next ? 1 : 0) - (reacted ? 1 : 0),
    }),
  );

  return (
    <button
      onClick={() =>
        startTransition(async () => {
          setOptimistic(!optimistic.reacted);
          await toggleReaction(postId, !optimistic.reacted);
        })
      }
      className="flex items-center gap-1 text-sm"
    >
      <motion.span
        key={String(optimistic.reacted)}
        initial={{ scale: 0.6 }}
        animate={{ scale: 1 }}
        className={optimistic.reacted ? "text-[var(--color-brand)]" : "text-gray-400"}
      >
        {optimistic.reacted ? "♥" : "♡"}
      </motion.span>
      <span className="tabular-nums">{optimistic.count}</span>
    </button>
  );
}

The matching Server Actions:

// app/feed/actions.ts  (append)
export async function toggleReaction(postId: string, on: boolean) {
  const supabase = await createClient();
  const {
    data: { user },
  } = await supabase.auth.getUser();
  if (!user) return;

  if (on) {
    await supabase
      .from("reactions")
      .upsert({ post_id: postId, user_id: user.id });
  } else {
    await supabase
      .from("reactions")
      .delete()
      .match({ post_id: postId, user_id: user.id });
  }
}

export async function addComment(postId: string, body: string) {
  const supabase = await createClient();
  const {
    data: { user },
  } = await supabase.auth.getUser();
  if (!user || !body.trim()) return;

  await supabase
    .from("comments")
    .insert({ post_id: postId, author_id: user.id, body: body.trim() });
  revalidatePath("/feed");
}

The FilterBar is just links that set the search param — no client JS needed, and the state lives in the URL:

// components/feed/filter-bar.tsx
import Link from "next/link";

const TAGS = ["running", "hiking", "cycling", "yoga", "gaming", "art"];

export function FilterBar({ active }: { active: string | null }) {
  return (
    <nav className="mb-4 flex gap-2 overflow-x-auto pb-1">
      <Link
        href="/feed"
        className={`shrink-0 rounded-full px-3 py-1 text-sm ${
          !active ? "bg-[var(--color-brand)] text-white" : "bg-gray-100"
        }`}
      >
        All
      </Link>
      {TAGS.map((t) => (
        <Link
          key={t}
          href={`/feed?activity=${t}`}
          className={`shrink-0 rounded-full px-3 py-1 text-sm ${
            active === t ? "bg-[var(--color-brand)] text-white" : "bg-gray-100"
          }`}
        >
          #{t}
        </Link>
      ))}
    </nav>
  );
}

Architecture / diagram

flowchart TD
  U[User in browser] -->|1. select image| ST[(Supabase Storage<br/>post-images bucket)]
  ST -->|2. public URL| C[Composer client component]
  C -->|3. createPost Server Action| SA[Next.js server]
  SA -->|4. INSERT row| DB[(PostgreSQL: posts)]
  DB -->|5. replication log| RT{{Supabase Realtime}}
  RT -->|6. WebSocket INSERT event| FS[FeedStream client]
  FS -->|7. prepend card| V[Live feed UI]
  SA -->|revalidatePath /feed| V

The two paths to the screen: the author sees their post via revalidatePath, while everyone else sees it pushed through Realtime. Same data, two delivery mechanisms.

Best practices & pitfalls

  • Do upload images from the client straight to Storage. Don't pipe binary through a Server Action — you'll hit body-size limits and waste serverless compute.
  • Do rely on RLS as your real authorization boundary. Don't trust author_id sent from the client; always derive identity from auth.getUser() server-side.
  • Do seed the client feed from Server-Component data. Don't fetch the first page in a useEffect — that gives users a blank flash and tanks perceived performance.
  • Do clean up your Realtime channel in the useEffect return. Don't leave channels open on unmount — you'll leak subscriptions and double-render new posts.
  • Pitfall: Realtime INSERT payloads contain only the raw row — no joined author or counts. Either fetch the full row by id when an event arrives, or render gracefully with the data you have and let the next refresh fill gaps.
  • Pitfall: forgetting to toggle Realtime replication on the table in the dashboard — the socket connects but never fires. Check the Replication tab first when "nothing happens."
  • Do constrain tags with an enum and caption length with a check. Validate at the database edge, not just in React.
  • Do add loading: "lazy" and proper sizes to feed images, or use next/image, so a long scroll doesn't download every photo at once.

Exercises

  1. Comment composer. Build a CommentSheet client component that lists a post's comments and posts new ones via addComment, with optimistic insert.
  2. Relative timestamps. Render created_at as "3m ago" / "2h ago" using Intl.RelativeTimeFormat, updating on an interval — without causing hydration mismatches.
  3. Hydrate realtime rows. When a Realtime INSERT arrives, fetch the full joined row (author, counts) by id before prepending, so live posts look identical to server-rendered ones.
  4. Infinite scroll. Add cursor pagination keyed on created_at, loading 20 more posts when an IntersectionObserver sentinel enters view.
  5. Delete with undo. Let authors delete their own post with a 5-second "Undo" toast (Framer Motion AnimatePresence) before the Server Action actually runs.

Mini-project — "Trail Drop"

Build a focused, shareable feed page for a single activity: /feed/hiking.

Requirements: - A dedicated route (app/feed/[activity]/page.tsx) that reads the dynamic params (remember: await params in Next.js 16) and filters server-side. - The composer pre-selects that activity and is locked to it. - Realtime is scoped so only matching posts stream in (use the filter option on the channel: filter: 'activity=eq.hiking'). - A header showing the live member count reacting today, animated with a Framer Motion count-up. - Empty state: a friendly "Be the first to drop a trail 🥾" card with a motion entrance.

Ship it, open two browser windows side by side, post from one, and watch it land in the other. That moment — the post appearing without a refresh — is the whole magic of realtime, and now you own it.

Key takeaways

  • A feed = structured metadata in Postgres + binary in Storage; the DB holds pointers, never pixels.
  • Read on the server (Server Components), write through Server Actions, reach for "use client" only for interactivity and realtime.
  • RLS is your real auth — identity comes from auth.getUser(), never the client payload.
  • useOptimistic makes reactions feel instant; roll back only on failure.
  • Supabase Realtime streams the Postgres replication log over WebSocket — remember to enable replication and clean up channels.
  • Keep filter state in the URL so feeds are shareable and refresh-safe.

Up next — Chapter 25: Direct Messages, where we turn one-to-many broadcasting into private, one-to-one realtime chat with read receipts and presence.

Chapter 25 — The Connections System

The moment two people both tap "Connect" is where Viby stops being an app and starts being a place people belong.

So far, Viby can show people to each other. In this chapter we build the beating heart of social discovery: the swipe, the mutual match, and the Circle of humans a user actually gets to talk to. This is the feature that turns browsing into belonging.

What you'll learn

  • How a swipe / connect / skip model works under the hood, and why we persist intent rather than UI state
  • Designing a connections table and a mutual-match rule in PostgreSQL (with a clean, race-safe way to create a match on a reciprocal connect)
  • Writing Server Actions to record connect/skip decisions safely
  • Building the Circle screen — the grid of confirmed matches — as a Server Component
  • Surfacing connections inside chat, so a new match instantly becomes a conversation
  • Using Framer Motion gestures for a tactile swipe deck without a heavyweight library

Prerequisites

  • Chapter 9 — Supabase Auth and the @supabase/ssr client helpers
  • Chapter 12 — Row Level Security (RLS) and policies
  • Chapter 18 — Server Actions and revalidatePath
  • Chapter 21 — The discovery feed (profiles, recommendation ordering)
  • Chapter 23 — Realtime chat and the messages table

If you can read and write a row with RLS enabled, you're ready.

Theory

A connection is directional intent. When Maya taps "Connect" on Theo, she is saying: "I'd like to be in Theo's Circle." That is a one-way arrow — Maya → Theo. It is meaningless to Theo until he reciprocates.

A match is the symmetric relationship that exists only when both arrows point at each other: Maya → Theo and Theo → Maya. Think of it like a handshake. One hand extended is an offer; the connection only "completes" when the other hand reaches back. Until then, nobody gets notified and no chat opens. This respects consent and keeps the experience pressure-free — a core promise of Viby being a discovery app, not a dating clone.

This gives us a clean mental model:

  • Connect = insert a directional connect row.
  • Skip = insert a directional skip row (we remember it so we never show that person again).
  • Match = the derived fact that two reciprocal connect rows exist.

Why persist skips? Two reasons. First, the feed must not resurface someone you've already passed on — that feels broken and a little insulting. Second, "intent data" is gold for recommendations later (Chapter 27). We store the decision, not the swipe animation. The UI is disposable; the data is forever.

The subtle part is when and how a match is born. Maya connects with Theo while Theo has already connected with Maya — at that instant a match should appear in both Circles, atomically, exactly once. Do it naively with "read, check, write" and two simultaneous requests can both think they're first and create duplicate matches. We'll solve this the boring, bulletproof way: let the database decide, with a unique constraint and an ON CONFLICT upsert.

In practice

1. The schema

We use one table for directional decisions and one for the symmetric match. The match table stores a canonical ordered pair (user_a < user_b) so a pair can only ever produce one match row.

-- supabase/migrations/0025_connections.sql

create type connection_kind as enum ('connect', 'skip');

create table public.connections (
  id          uuid primary key default gen_random_uuid(),
  actor_id    uuid not null references auth.users (id) on delete cascade,
  target_id   uuid not null references auth.users (id) on delete cascade,
  kind        connection_kind not null,
  created_at  timestamptz not null default now(),
  -- one decision per (actor -> target); re-deciding updates the row
  unique (actor_id, target_id),
  check (actor_id <> target_id)
);

create table public.matches (
  id          uuid primary key default gen_random_uuid(),
  user_a      uuid not null references auth.users (id) on delete cascade,
  user_b      uuid not null references auth.users (id) on delete cascade,
  created_at  timestamptz not null default now(),
  -- canonical order guarantees one row per pair
  check (user_a < user_b),
  unique (user_a, user_b)
);

create index on public.connections (actor_id, kind);
create index on public.matches (user_a);
create index on public.matches (user_b);

2. The match-on-mutual-connect function

We push the "is this mutual?" logic into a single SECURITY DEFINER function so it runs atomically. The ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING makes it idempotent: even if two reciprocal connects land in the same millisecond, exactly one match row survives.

-- still in 0025_connections.sql

create or replace function public.record_connection(p_target uuid, p_kind connection_kind)
returns table (matched boolean, match_id uuid)
language plpgsql
security definer
set search_path = public
as $$
declare
  v_actor uuid := auth.uid();
  v_a uuid;
  v_b uuid;
  v_match_id uuid;
  v_reciprocal boolean := false;
begin
  if v_actor is null then
    raise exception 'not authenticated';
  end if;
  if v_actor = p_target then
    raise exception 'cannot connect with yourself';
  end if;

  -- 1. persist this decision (idempotent: re-deciding overwrites)
  insert into public.connections (actor_id, target_id, kind)
  values (v_actor, p_target, p_kind)
  on conflict (actor_id, target_id)
  do update set kind = excluded.kind, created_at = now();

  -- skips never create matches
  if p_kind = 'skip' then
    return query select false, null::uuid;
    return;
  end if;

  -- 2. did the target already connect with us?
  select exists (
    select 1 from public.connections
    where actor_id = p_target and target_id = v_actor and kind = 'connect'
  ) into v_reciprocal;

  if not v_reciprocal then
    return query select false, null::uuid;
    return;
  end if;

  -- 3. mutual! create the canonical match row, idempotently
  v_a := least(v_actor, p_target);
  v_b := greatest(v_actor, p_target);

  insert into public.matches (user_a, user_b)
  values (v_a, v_b)
  on conflict (user_a, user_b) do nothing
  returning id into v_match_id;

  if v_match_id is null then
    select id into v_match_id from public.matches
    where user_a = v_a and user_b = v_b;
  end if;

  return query select true, v_match_id;
end;
$$;

3. Row Level Security

Users may only read their own decisions and the matches they're part of. Writes go exclusively through the function above, so we keep direct insert policies tight.

alter table public.connections enable row level security;
alter table public.matches enable row level security;

create policy "read own connections"
  on public.connections for select
  using (auth.uid() = actor_id);

create policy "read own matches"
  on public.matches for select
  using (auth.uid() = user_a or auth.uid() = user_b);

Note: because record_connection is SECURITY DEFINER, it bypasses RLS for its inserts — exactly what we want, since the user is acting on their own behalf and we validate auth.uid() inside.

4. The Server Action

Thin wrapper that calls the RPC, revalidates the feed and Circle, and tells the client whether a match just happened.

// app/(app)/discover/actions.ts
"use server";

import { revalidatePath } from "next/cache";
import { createServerClient } from "@/lib/supabase/server";

export type ConnectResult =
  | { ok: true; matched: boolean; matchId: string | null }
  | { ok: false; error: string };

export async function recordConnection(
  targetId: string,
  kind: "connect" | "skip",
): Promise<ConnectResult> {
  const supabase = await createServerClient();

  const { data, error } = await supabase
    .rpc("record_connection", { p_target: targetId, p_kind: kind })
    .single();

  if (error) return { ok: false, error: error.message };

  revalidatePath("/discover");
  if (kind === "connect" && data?.matched) revalidatePath("/circle");

  return { ok: true, matched: data?.matched ?? false, matchId: data?.match_id ?? null };
}

Here's the createServerClient helper from Chapter 9, for reference — note the getAll/setAll cookie handlers required by @supabase/ssr:

// lib/supabase/server.ts
import { cookies } from "next/headers";
import { createServerClient as createClient } from "@supabase/ssr";

export async function createServerClient() {
  const cookieStore = await cookies();
  return createClient(
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL!,
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY!,
    {
      cookies: {
        getAll: () => cookieStore.getAll(),
        setAll: (toSet) => {
          try {
            toSet.forEach(({ name, value, options }) =>
              cookieStore.set(name, value, options),
            );
          } catch {
            // called from a Server Component — safe to ignore; proxy.ts refreshes the session
          }
        },
      },
    },
  );
}

5. The swipe deck (client gesture)

A focused "use client" component handles the drag gesture and optimistic removal. We keep the data fetching on the server and hand the deck a plain array.

// app/(app)/discover/SwipeDeck.tsx
"use client";

import { useState, useTransition } from "react";
import { motion, AnimatePresence, type PanInfo } from "framer-motion";
import { recordConnection } from "./actions";
import { MatchCelebration } from "./MatchCelebration";

type Candidate = { id: string; displayName: string; avatarUrl: string; bio: string; vibes: string[] };

export function SwipeDeck({ initial }: { initial: Candidate[] }) {
  const [deck, setDeck] = useState(initial);
  const [matched, setMatched] = useState<Candidate | null>(null);
  const [, startTransition] = useTransition();

  function decide(card: Candidate, kind: "connect" | "skip") {
    setDeck((d) => d.filter((c) => c.id !== card.id)); // optimistic
    startTransition(async () => {
      const res = await recordConnection(card.id, kind);
      if (res.ok && res.matched) setMatched(card);
    });
  }

  const onDragEnd = (card: Candidate) => (_: unknown, info: PanInfo) => {
    if (info.offset.x > 120) decide(card, "connect");
    else if (info.offset.x < -120) decide(card, "skip");
  };

  return (
    <div className="relative mx-auto h-[520px] w-full max-w-sm">
      <AnimatePresence>
        {deck.slice(-3).map((card, i, arr) => {
          const top = i === arr.length - 1;
          return (
            <motion.div
              key={card.id}
              drag={top ? "x" : false}
              dragSnapToOrigin
              onDragEnd={top ? onDragEnd(card) : undefined}
              initial={{ scale: 0.95, y: 12 }}
              animate={{ scale: 1 - (arr.length - 1 - i) * 0.04, y: (arr.length - 1 - i) * -10 }}
              exit={{ x: 0, opacity: 0, scale: 0.8, transition: { duration: 0.2 } }}
              whileDrag={{ rotate: 0 }}
              className="absolute inset-0 rounded-3xl border border-pink-100 bg-white shadow-xl overflow-hidden"
            >
              <img src={card.avatarUrl} alt="" className="h-72 w-full object-cover" />
              <div className="p-5">
                <h3 className="text-xl font-bold text-gray-900">{card.displayName}</h3>
                <p className="mt-1 text-sm text-gray-500">{card.bio}</p>
                <div className="mt-3 flex flex-wrap gap-2">
                  {card.vibes.map((v) => (
                    <span key={v} className="rounded-full bg-pink-50 px-3 py-1 text-xs font-medium text-[--color-brand]">
                      {v}
                    </span>
                  ))}
                </div>
              </div>
              {top && (
                <div className="absolute inset-x-0 bottom-0 flex justify-center gap-6 p-4">
                  <button onClick={() => decide(card, "skip")} aria-label="Skip"
                    className="grid h-14 w-14 place-items-center rounded-full bg-gray-100 text-2xl">✕</button>
                  <button onClick={() => decide(card, "connect")} aria-label="Connect"
                    className="grid h-14 w-14 place-items-center rounded-full bg-[--color-brand] text-2xl text-white">♥</button>
                </div>
              )}
            </motion.div>
          );
        })}
      </AnimatePresence>

      {deck.length === 0 && (
        <p className="grid h-full place-items-center text-center text-gray-400">
          That's everyone for now — check back soon!
        </p>
      )}

      <AnimatePresence>
        {matched && <MatchCelebration person={matched} onClose={() => setMatched(null)} />}
      </AnimatePresence>
    </div>
  );
}

The brand color comes from our Tailwind v4 theme tokens — no config file:

/* app/globals.css */
@import "tailwindcss";

@theme {
  --color-brand: #ff6fb5;
  --color-brand-soft: #fff0f7;
}

6. The Circle screen (Server Component)

The Circle is the user's confirmed matches. Because it's a Server Component, we fetch with the user's session and RLS does the filtering for free.

// app/(app)/circle/page.tsx
import Link from "next/link";
import { createServerClient } from "@/lib/supabase/server";

export default async function CirclePage() {
  const supabase = await createServerClient();
  const { data: { user } } = await supabase.auth.getUser();

  const { data: matches } = await supabase
    .from("matches")
    .select(`
      id, created_at,
      a:profiles!matches_user_a_fkey ( id, display_name, avatar_url ),
      b:profiles!matches_user_b_fkey ( id, display_name, avatar_url )
    `)
    .order("created_at", { ascending: false });

  const people = (matches ?? []).map((m) => (m.a.id === user!.id ? m.b : m.a));

  return (
    <main className="mx-auto max-w-2xl p-4">
      <h1 className="text-2xl font-bold text-gray-900">Your Circle</h1>
      <p className="mt-1 text-sm text-gray-500">{people.length} people you both said yes to.</p>

      <ul className="mt-6 grid grid-cols-2 gap-4 sm:grid-cols-3">
        {people.map((p) => (
          <li key={p.id}>
            <Link href={`/chat/${p.id}`} className="block rounded-2xl border border-pink-100 p-3 transition hover:shadow-md">
              <img src={p.avatar_url} alt="" className="aspect-square w-full rounded-xl object-cover" />
              <span className="mt-2 block text-center text-sm font-semibold text-gray-800">{p.display_name}</span>
            </Link>
          </li>
        ))}
      </ul>

      {people.length === 0 && (
        <p className="mt-16 text-center text-gray-400">No matches yet. Head to Discover and find your people!</p>
      )}
    </main>
  );
}

The profiles!matches_user_a_fkey syntax disambiguates the two foreign keys both pointing at auth.users/profiles. Without it, PostgREST can't tell which join you mean.

7. Surfacing connections in chat

A match should open a door. We don't pre-create empty conversations — instead, the chat list is derived from matches. Tapping a Circle member routes to /chat/[peerId], and the messages query (Chapter 23) loads on demand. The chat list page reuses the same match query:

// app/(app)/chat/page.tsx (excerpt)
const people = (matches ?? []).map((m) => (m.a.id === user!.id ? m.b : m.a));
// render each as a chat row → Link href={`/chat/${person.id}`}

This keeps a single source of truth: you can only chat with someone in your Circle. Enforce it at the data layer too — your messages RLS policy should check that a matches row exists for the sender/recipient pair before allowing an insert.

8. The match celebration

// app/(app)/discover/MatchCelebration.tsx
"use client";
import Link from "next/link";
import { motion } from "framer-motion";

export function MatchCelebration({
  person, onClose,
}: { person: { id: string; displayName: string; avatarUrl: string }; onClose: () => void }) {
  return (
    <motion.div
      initial={{ opacity: 0 }} animate={{ opacity: 1 }} exit={{ opacity: 0 }}
      className="fixed inset-0 z-50 grid place-items-center bg-[--color-brand]/90 p-6 text-white"
      onClick={onClose}
    >
      <motion.div
        initial={{ scale: 0.6, y: 30 }} animate={{ scale: 1, y: 0 }}
        transition={{ type: "spring", stiffness: 260, damping: 18 }}
        className="text-center"
      >
        <p className="text-sm font-semibold uppercase tracking-widest">It's a match</p>
        <h2 className="mt-2 text-4xl font-black">You & {person.displayName}</h2>
        <img src={person.avatarUrl} alt="" className="mx-auto mt-6 h-32 w-32 rounded-full border-4 border-white object-cover" />
        <Link href={`/chat/${person.id}`}
          className="mt-8 inline-block rounded-full bg-white px-8 py-3 font-bold text-[--color-brand]">
          Say hi 👋
        </Link>
      </motion.div>
    </motion.div>
  );
}

Architecture / diagram

sequenceDiagram
    participant U as Maya (client)
    participant SA as Server Action
    participant DB as record_connection()
    participant T as connections / matches

    U->>SA: recordConnection(theoId, "connect")
    SA->>DB: rpc record_connection
    DB->>T: upsert connection (Maya→Theo)
    DB->>T: SELECT reciprocal? (Theo→Maya, connect)
    alt reciprocal exists
        DB->>T: INSERT match (least, greatest) ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING
        DB-->>SA: { matched: true, match_id }
        SA-->>U: revalidate /circle → show MatchCelebration
    else not yet
        DB-->>SA: { matched: false }
        SA-->>U: next card
    end

connections (directional intent)        matches (symmetric, canonical)
+----------+-----------+--------+        +--------+--------+
| actor_id | target_id | kind   |        | user_a | user_b |
+----------+-----------+--------+        +--------+--------+
| Maya     | Theo      | connect|  --->  | Maya   | Theo   |  (user_a < user_b)
| Theo     | Maya      | connect|  ---^  +--------+--------+
| Maya     | Ravi      | skip   |        (created only on mutual connect)
+----------+-----------+--------+

Best practices & pitfalls

  • DO let the database enforce uniqueness. The matches unique constraint + ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING is what makes simultaneous reciprocal connects safe. Never "SELECT then INSERT" in app code for this.
  • DO store the canonical pair (least/greatest) so a pair maps to exactly one match row regardless of who connected first.
  • DON'T trust the client for actor_id. Always derive it from auth.uid() inside the SECURITY DEFINER function — never accept it as a parameter.
  • DO make the action idempotent. Re-tapping connect (double-click, retry) must not create duplicate intent or duplicate matches. The unique (actor_id, target_id) upsert handles this.
  • DON'T pre-create chat rooms for every match — derive conversations from matches and lazy-load messages. Empty rooms are clutter and storage you'll regret.
  • DO gate messages inserts with RLS that requires an existing match. A connection feature isn't done until people can't message strangers.
  • PITFALL: optimistic UI desync. If the Server Action fails, reconcile — re-add the card or toast an error. Don't leave the deck silently out of sync with the DB.
  • PITFALL: forgetting set search_path in SECURITY DEFINER functions — it's a real privilege-escalation vector. Always pin it.
  • DON'T resurface skipped users. Your discover query must LEFT JOIN/NOT IN against connections for the current actor.

Exercises

  1. Filter the feed. Update the Discover server query so candidates exclude anyone the current user has already connected with or skipped, and exclude existing matches. (Hint: not in (select target_id from connections where actor_id = auth.uid()).)
  2. Undo a skip. Add a "Bring back" button that deletes the most recent skip row for the user and revalidates /discover. Keep it RLS-safe.
  3. Match count badge. Add a live badge to the Circle nav item showing the number of matches created in the last 24 hours. Try it first with a server query, then upgrade to Supabase Realtime on the matches table.
  4. Pending connections (intermediate). Build a "Liked you" screen that shows people who connected with the current user but whom the user hasn't decided on yet — without leaking it before mutual consent. Think carefully about which RLS policy makes this safe.
  5. Race-condition test (advanced). Write a SQL or pgTAP test that fires two reciprocal record_connection calls concurrently and asserts exactly one matches row exists afterward.

Mini-project

Ship the full connect → match → chat loop.

  1. Run the 0025_connections.sql migration (tables, function, RLS).
  2. Wire app/(app)/discover/page.tsx (Server Component) to fetch candidates excluding prior decisions, and render <SwipeDeck initial={candidates} />.
  3. Implement the recordConnection Server Action and confirm both button taps and drag gestures persist rows.
  4. Build the Circle screen and verify two test accounts that mutually connect both see each other (and that MatchCelebration fires for the second connector).
  5. From a Circle card, navigate into /chat/[peerId] and send the first message — then add the messages RLS policy that requires a match, and prove a non-matched user gets rejected.

Success criteria: a brand-new pair can go from two swipes to a live conversation, with zero duplicate matches even under rapid double-taps.

Key takeaways

  • A connection is directional intent; a match is the symmetric handshake derived from two reciprocal connects.
  • Persist both connects and skips — the UI animation is throwaway, the decision data is permanent and powers recommendations later.
  • Make match creation atomic and idempotent with a canonical ordered pair, a unique constraint, and ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING — never read-then-write in app code.
  • Keep writes inside a validated SECURITY DEFINER RPC and reads behind tight RLS; derive the Circle and chat list from matches as a single source of truth.
  • Optimistic UI + Server Actions gives a snappy swipe deck, but always reconcile on failure.

Up next — Chapter 26: Realtime Presence & Notifications, where we light up "online now" dots, typing indicators, and push a "You have a new match!" notification the instant that handshake completes.

Chapter 26 — Realtime Chat

The moment two strangers who love the same obscure board game start typing to each other — that's Viby working. Let's build the room where it happens.

What you'll learn

  • How to model conversations and messages so 1-on-1 chat scales cleanly
  • Sending messages with optimistic UI so the bubble appears the instant you hit send
  • Subscribing to Supabase Realtime so new messages stream in live
  • Typing indicators and online presence using Realtime broadcast & presence channels
  • Unread counters that survive refreshes and stay accurate
  • A polished conversation list that re-sorts itself as messages arrive

Prerequisites

  • Chapter 9 (Supabase Auth + @supabase/ssr clients)
  • Chapter 12 (Postgres schema design + Row Level Security)
  • Chapter 18 (Zustand for client state)
  • Chapter 22 (Server Actions and the proxy.ts auth gate)
  • Comfort with motion / AnimatePresence from Chapter 20

Theory

Chat feels simple — text goes in a box, appears on a screen. Under the hood it's the hardest UI in the whole app, because three different forces fight each other:

  1. Persistence — messages must survive a refresh, so they live in Postgres.
  2. Liveness — the other person's message must appear now, not after a poll.
  3. Perceived speed — your own message must appear before the server even confirms it.

Think of it like a restaurant kitchen with a pass. When you order (send a message), the waiter writes it on a ticket and immediately tells you "got it" — that's optimistic UI, the bubble shows up grey before the kitchen confirms. The ticket goes to the kitchen (Postgres insert). When any dish is plated, a bell rings on the pass and every waiter hears it — that's Realtime, a broadcast everyone subscribed to the channel receives. You don't keep walking back to ask "is it ready yet?" (that's polling, and it's wasteful) — you subscribe to the bell.

Three Realtime primitives matter here, and they're easy to confuse:

  • Postgres Changes — Supabase tails the database's write-ahead log and pushes INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE events. This is the source of truth: it fires after a row is committed. Use it for messages, because messages are durable.
  • Broadcast — a lightweight, ephemeral message bus. Nothing is stored. Perfect for "Alex is typing…", which nobody needs to remember.
  • Presence — a synchronized roster of who's currently on a channel, with automatic join/leave events. Perfect for the green "online" dot.

The rule of thumb: if losing it on refresh is fine, use Broadcast/Presence; if it must persist, write to Postgres and listen to Postgres Changes. Typing and online status are gloriously disposable. Messages and unread counts are not.

One more concept: the optimistic reconciliation problem. You insert a fake message locally with a temporary id, then the real row arrives via Realtime with a real id. If you're not careful you'll show it twice. We solve this with a stable client_id so the optimistic row and the database row are the same row.

In practice

1. The schema

Two tables, plus a read_state table for unread counts. Run this in the Supabase SQL editor (this builds on the migrations pattern from Chapter 12).

-- A conversation is a 1-on-1 thread between two users.
create table conversations (
  id uuid primary key default gen_random_uuid(),
  user_a uuid not null references auth.users (id) on delete cascade,
  user_b uuid not null references auth.users (id) on delete cascade,
  last_message text,
  last_message_at timestamptz not null default now(),
  created_at timestamptz not null default now(),
  -- order the pair so (a,b) and (b,a) can't both exist
  constraint ordered_pair check (user_a < user_b),
  constraint unique_pair unique (user_a, user_b)
);

create table messages (
  id uuid primary key default gen_random_uuid(),
  conversation_id uuid not null references conversations (id) on delete cascade,
  sender_id uuid not null references auth.users (id) on delete cascade,
  body text not null check (char_length(body) between 1 and 4000),
  client_id text not null,           -- stable id from the client for dedupe
  created_at timestamptz not null default now()
);

-- Per-user, per-conversation read cursor.
create table read_state (
  conversation_id uuid not null references conversations (id) on delete cascade,
  user_id uuid not null references auth.users (id) on delete cascade,
  last_read_at timestamptz not null default now(),
  primary key (conversation_id, user_id)
);

create index messages_conversation_idx on messages (conversation_id, created_at desc);

-- Realtime needs the table in the publication.
alter publication supabase_realtime add table messages;

Now lock it down with RLS so users only touch their own conversations:

alter table conversations enable row level security;
alter table messages enable row level security;
alter table read_state enable row level security;

create policy "see own conversations" on conversations for select
  using (auth.uid() in (user_a, user_b));

create policy "read messages in own conversations" on messages for select
  using (exists (
    select 1 from conversations c
    where c.id = conversation_id and auth.uid() in (c.user_a, c.user_b)
  ));

create policy "send as self in own conversations" on messages for insert
  with check (
    sender_id = auth.uid() and exists (
      select 1 from conversations c
      where c.id = conversation_id and auth.uid() in (c.user_a, c.user_b)
    )
  );

create policy "manage own read state" on read_state for all
  using (user_id = auth.uid()) with check (user_id = auth.uid());

Why user_a < user_b? It guarantees one canonical row per pair. Without it, Alex→Sam and Sam→Alex become two separate threads — a classic duplicate-conversation bug.

2. Server Action: send a message

We send through a Server Action so the write happens with the user's authenticated server client and the conversation's last_message updates atomically.

// app/chat/actions.ts
"use server";

import { createServerClient } from "@/lib/supabase/server";
import { revalidatePath } from "next/cache";

export async function sendMessage(
  conversationId: string,
  body: string,
  clientId: string
) {
  const supabase = await createServerClient();
  const { data: { user } } = await supabase.auth.getUser();
  if (!user) return { error: "Not authenticated" };

  const text = body.trim();
  if (!text) return { error: "Empty message" };

  const { error } = await supabase.from("messages").insert({
    conversation_id: conversationId,
    sender_id: user.id,
    body: text,
    client_id: clientId,
  });
  if (error) return { error: error.message };

  // Bump the conversation so the list re-sorts.
  await supabase
    .from("conversations")
    .update({ last_message: text, last_message_at: new Date().toISOString() })
    .eq("id", conversationId);

  revalidatePath("/chat");
  return { ok: true };
}

3. The optimistic message store (Zustand)

// stores/useChatStore.ts
import { create } from "zustand";

export type ChatMessage = {
  id: string;            // db id, or temp id while pending
  client_id: string;     // stable dedupe key
  body: string;
  sender_id: string;
  created_at: string;
  pending?: boolean;
};

type ChatState = {
  messages: ChatMessage[];
  setInitial: (m: ChatMessage[]) => void;
  addOptimistic: (m: ChatMessage) => void;
  upsertFromServer: (m: ChatMessage) => void;
};

export const useChatStore = create<ChatState>((set) => ({
  messages: [],
  setInitial: (m) => set({ messages: m }),
  addOptimistic: (m) => set((s) => ({ messages: [...s.messages, m] })),
  upsertFromServer: (incoming) =>
    set((s) => {
      // Reconcile by client_id: replace the optimistic row if present.
      const idx = s.messages.findIndex((x) => x.client_id === incoming.client_id);
      if (idx === -1) return { messages: [...s.messages, incoming] };
      const next = [...s.messages];
      next[idx] = incoming;
      return { messages: next };
    }),
}));

The upsertFromServer reconciliation is the whole trick: the optimistic row and the realtime row share a client_id, so the server version replaces the grey pending bubble instead of stacking on top of it.

4. The chat thread: realtime + optimistic send

// app/chat/[id]/Thread.tsx
"use client";

import { use, useEffect, useRef, useState, useTransition } from "react";
import { motion, AnimatePresence } from "framer-motion";
import { createBrowserClient } from "@/lib/supabase/client";
import { useChatStore, type ChatMessage } from "@/stores/useChatStore";
import { sendMessage } from "../actions";

export function Thread({
  conversationId,
  meId,
  initial,
}: {
  conversationId: string;
  meId: string;
  initial: ChatMessage[];
}) {
  const supabase = createBrowserClient();
  const { messages, setInitial, addOptimistic, upsertFromServer } = useChatStore();
  const [text, setText] = useState("");
  const [, startTransition] = useTransition();
  const bottomRef = useRef<HTMLDivElement>(null);

  useEffect(() => setInitial(initial), [initial, setInitial]);

  // Subscribe to new messages for this conversation.
  useEffect(() => {
    const channel = supabase
      .channel(`messages:${conversationId}`)
      .on(
        "postgres_changes",
        {
          event: "INSERT",
          schema: "public",
          table: "messages",
          filter: `conversation_id=eq.${conversationId}`,
        },
        (payload) => upsertFromServer(payload.new as ChatMessage)
      )
      .subscribe();

    return () => {
      supabase.removeChannel(channel);
    };
  }, [conversationId, supabase, upsertFromServer]);

  useEffect(() => {
    bottomRef.current?.scrollIntoView({ behavior: "smooth" });
  }, [messages.length]);

  function handleSend() {
    const body = text.trim();
    if (!body) return;
    const clientId = crypto.randomUUID();

    // 1. Show it instantly.
    addOptimistic({
      id: `temp-${clientId}`,
      client_id: clientId,
      body,
      sender_id: meId,
      created_at: new Date().toISOString(),
      pending: true,
    });
    setText("");

    // 2. Persist. Realtime will deliver the canonical row and reconcile.
    startTransition(async () => {
      const res = await sendMessage(conversationId, body, clientId);
      if (res?.error) {
        // mark the bubble as failed (left as an exercise)
        console.error(res.error);
      }
    });
  }

  return (
    <div className="flex h-full flex-col">
      <div className="flex-1 space-y-2 overflow-y-auto p-4">
        <AnimatePresence initial={false}>
          {messages.map((m) => {
            const mine = m.sender_id === meId;
            return (
              <motion.div
                key={m.client_id}
                initial={{ opacity: 0, y: 8, scale: 0.97 }}
                animate={{ opacity: m.pending ? 0.6 : 1, y: 0, scale: 1 }}
                className={`flex ${mine ? "justify-end" : "justify-start"}`}
              >
                <span
                  className={`max-w-[75%] rounded-2xl px-4 py-2 text-sm ${
                    mine
                      ? "bg-brand text-white"
                      : "bg-gray-100 text-gray-900"
                  }`}
                >
                  {m.body}
                </span>
              </motion.div>
            );
          })}
        </AnimatePresence>
        <div ref={bottomRef} />
      </div>

      <div className="flex gap-2 border-t p-3">
        <input
          value={text}
          onChange={(e) => setText(e.target.value)}
          onKeyDown={(e) => e.key === "Enter" && handleSend()}
          placeholder="Say hi…"
          className="flex-1 rounded-full border px-4 py-2 text-sm outline-none focus:border-brand"
        />
        <button
          onClick={handleSend}
          className="rounded-full bg-brand px-5 py-2 text-sm font-semibold text-white"
        >
          Send
        </button>
      </div>
    </div>
  );
}

The bg-brand token comes from our Tailwind v4 @theme block (Chapter 6):

/* app/globals.css */
@theme {
  --color-brand: #ff6fb5;
}

5. Typing indicators (Broadcast) + presence (Presence)

Both ride on a single channel. Typing is ephemeral broadcast; online status is presence.

// hooks/usePresenceAndTyping.ts
"use client";

import { useEffect, useRef, useState } from "react";
import { createBrowserClient } from "@/lib/supabase/client";

export function usePresenceAndTyping(conversationId: string, meId: string) {
  const supabase = createBrowserClient();
  const [peerTyping, setPeerTyping] = useState(false);
  const [peerOnline, setPeerOnline] = useState(false);
  const channelRef = useRef<ReturnType<typeof supabase.channel>>(null);
  const stopTimer = useRef<ReturnType<typeof setTimeout>>(null);

  useEffect(() => {
    const channel = supabase.channel(`room:${conversationId}`, {
      config: { presence: { key: meId } },
    });
    channelRef.current = channel;

    channel
      .on("broadcast", { event: "typing" }, ({ payload }) => {
        if (payload.userId === meId) return;
        setPeerTyping(true);
        clearTimeout(stopTimer.current!);
        stopTimer.current = setTimeout(() => setPeerTyping(false), 2500);
      })
      .on("presence", { event: "sync" }, () => {
        const state = channel.presenceState();
        // anyone present who isn't me => peer online
        setPeerOnline(Object.keys(state).some((k) => k !== meId));
      })
      .subscribe(async (status) => {
        if (status === "SUBSCRIBED") {
          await channel.track({ online_at: new Date().toISOString() });
        }
      });

    return () => {
      supabase.removeChannel(channel);
    };
  }, [conversationId, meId, supabase]);

  // Call this on each keystroke; it's a fire-and-forget broadcast.
  function emitTyping() {
    channelRef.current?.send({
      type: "broadcast",
      event: "typing",
      payload: { userId: meId },
    });
  }

  return { peerTyping, peerOnline, emitTyping };
}

Wire emitTyping() into the input's onChange, and render peerTyping as an animated three-dot bubble. The 2.5s timer auto-clears the indicator if keystrokes stop — broadcast has no "stopped typing" event, so we infer it.

6. Unread counters

Unread = messages newer than my last_read_at that I didn't send. We compute it server-side for accuracy, and reset it when the thread opens.

// app/chat/actions.ts (append)
export async function markRead(conversationId: string) {
  const supabase = await createServerClient();
  const { data: { user } } = await supabase.auth.getUser();
  if (!user) return;
  await supabase.from("read_state").upsert({
    conversation_id: conversationId,
    user_id: user.id,
    last_read_at: new Date().toISOString(),
  });
}

// lib/chat/unread.ts — used by the conversation list (Server Component)
import { createServerClient } from "@/lib/supabase/server";

export async function getUnreadCount(conversationId: string, meId: string) {
  const supabase = await createServerClient();
  const { data: rs } = await supabase
    .from("read_state")
    .select("last_read_at")
    .eq("conversation_id", conversationId)
    .eq("user_id", meId)
    .maybeSingle();

  const since = rs?.last_read_at ?? "1970-01-01";
  const { count } = await supabase
    .from("messages")
    .select("*", { count: "exact", head: true })
    .eq("conversation_id", conversationId)
    .neq("sender_id", meId)
    .gt("created_at", since);

  return count ?? 0;
}

Call markRead(conversationId) from the Thread once it mounts (in a useEffect via a transition). The badge in the list then reflects only genuinely-unseen messages.

Architecture / diagram

sequenceDiagram
    participant A as Alex (browser)
    participant DB as Supabase Postgres
    participant RT as Supabase Realtime
    participant S as Sam (browser)

    Note over A: types "hey!"
    A->>A: addOptimistic() — grey bubble appears
    A->>DB: sendMessage() Server Action → INSERT
    A-->>S: broadcast "typing" (ephemeral, no DB)
    DB->>RT: WAL change (committed row)
    RT-->>A: postgres_changes INSERT
    A->>A: upsertFromServer() — reconcile by client_id
    RT-->>S: postgres_changes INSERT → bubble appears
    S->>DB: markRead() on open → unread resets

 Channels per conversation
 ────────────────────────────────────────────
  messages:<id>   ── postgres_changes ──> durable message stream
  room:<id>       ── broadcast(typing) ──> ephemeral "typing…"
  room:<id>       ── presence ──────────> who's online right now

Best practices & pitfalls

  • Do dedupe by client_id, not by array index or db id. The optimistic row has no db id yet.
  • Do scope channels per conversation (messages:<id>) and always removeChannel in cleanup — leaked subscriptions silently multiply and you'll process the same insert five times.
  • Don't trust the filter on postgres_changes for security. RLS is what actually stops a user reading another thread; the filter is a performance convenience.
  • Don't store typing state in Postgres. A row write per keystroke will hammer your database and your bill. Broadcast is free-flowing and ephemeral for a reason.
  • Do compute unread counts on the server. A purely client-side counter drifts the moment a tab was closed when a message arrived.
  • Don't forget alter publication supabase_realtime add table messages; — the single most common "why isn't realtime firing?" cause.
  • Do debounce or throttle emitTyping() if you broadcast on every keystroke at scale; once per ~300ms is plenty.
  • Do handle the failed-send case — flip pending to a failed flag and offer a retry, rather than leaving a ghost bubble.

Exercises

  1. Failed-send retry. Add a status: "sending" | "sent" | "failed" to ChatMessage. When sendMessage returns an error, mark the bubble failed and render a tap-to-retry icon.
  2. Typing dots component. Build a <TypingBubble /> with three dots that bounce using Framer Motion's animate and staggered transition.delay. Show it only when peerTyping is true, wrapped in AnimatePresence.
  3. Read receipts. Using the peer's read_state.last_read_at, render a subtle "Seen" under the last message you sent if its created_at is older than their read cursor.
  4. Live unread badge. Make the conversation-list badge update without a refresh by subscribing to messages inserts at the list level and incrementing the count for non-active threads.
  5. Pagination. The thread loads only the latest 50 messages. Add "load older" that fetches the previous page by created_at cursor and prepends without breaking scroll position.

Mini-project — "Viby DMs" end to end

Build a working two-pane chat at /chat:

  • Left pane: a ConversationList Server Component that fetches the user's conversations ordered by last_message_at desc, renders each with the peer's avatar, the last_message preview, a relative timestamp, and an unread badge from getUnreadCount. The bubblegum-pink badge only shows when count > 0.
  • Right pane: the Thread from this chapter, with optimistic send, realtime delivery, a presence dot in the header ("Online" in pink when peerOnline), and a typing bubble.
  • On open, call markRead. On send, watch the optimistic bubble reconcile.

Acceptance test: open two browsers (one incognito) signed in as two users who matched in an earlier chapter. Type in one — the dots appear in the other within a beat, the message lands live, the unread badge clears on open, and your own bubble never duplicates. Ship it to Vercel and DM a classmate.

Key takeaways

  • Persist messages in Postgres; stream them with postgres_changes. That's your durable backbone.
  • Optimistic UI + client_id reconciliation gives instant-feeling sends with zero duplicates.
  • Broadcast for typing, Presence for online — ephemeral signals never touch the database.
  • Unread counts belong on the server, derived from a per-user read cursor.
  • RLS is the real security boundary; realtime filters are only for efficiency.

Up next — Chapter 27: Push Notifications & Background Sync, so the conversation pulls people back in even when Viby is closed.

Chapter 27 — Groups & Notifications

A social app stops being a feed and starts being a community the moment people can gather in rooms and feel the tap on the shoulder when something happens.

So far Viby connects people one-to-one. In this chapter we give them places to belong — Groups (hobby communities people create and join) — and the nervous system that keeps everyone in the loop: a realtime notifications system that fires on new connections, messages, comments, reactions, and group invites, surfacing them as live toasts.

What you'll learn

  • How to model many-to-many membership (groups, group_members) and a group feed in PostgreSQL with Row Level Security.
  • How to build create/join flows with Server Actions and optimistic UI.
  • The architecture of a notifications table and why notifications are materialized rows, not computed on the fly.
  • How to subscribe to Supabase Realtime changes from a Client Component and pop Framer Motion toasts.
  • How to keep an unread badge accurate without hammering the database.

Prerequisites

  • Chapter 9–11: Supabase Auth, the @supabase/ssr client helpers, and RLS basics.
  • Chapter 18: Server Actions and revalidatePath.
  • Chapter 22: the connections/friendship model (connections table).
  • Chapter 24: Realtime fundamentals (you built the live chat there — we reuse the same postgres_changes subscription pattern).
  • Chapter 26: the Zustand store and our toast primitive.

Theory

Groups are a join table wearing a costume

A "community" feels like a big, fuzzy concept, but underneath it's the most classic relational pattern there is: many-to-many. A user can be in many groups; a group has many users. You never store an array of member IDs on the group row (that path leads to race conditions and unqueryable data). Instead you put a thin join table between them — group_members — where each row says "user X is in group Y, with role R."

Think of it like a coat-check: the group is the cloakroom, each group_members row is a numbered ticket. The ticket carries a little metadata (your role: member, admin, owner) and lets either side look the other up instantly.

The group feed is then just posts scoped to a group_id — the same posts table you've used, with one extra foreign key.

Notifications are events you saved, not events you compute

Here's the single most important idea in this chapter, and the one beginners get wrong: a notification is a stored row, not a query.
