It's tempting to think "the user's notifications are: all unread messages + all new comments on their posts + …" and compute that with a giant join on every page load. Don't. That's slow, it's impossible to mark individual items read, and it can't represent "Sara reacted then un-reacted."

Instead, treat a notification as a fact that happened at a point in time — like a stamped letter dropped in a mailbox. When an event occurs (someone comments, reacts, sends an invite), you write a row into a notifications table addressed to the recipient. That row is immutable history. The mailbox (the bell icon) just lists rows and tracks which envelopes have been opened (read_at).

This "write on event" model is called a materialized or fan-out-on-write notification system. It costs a tiny write when the event happens and makes every read cheap.

Realtime is a phone line you leave off the hook

Polling ("any new notifications yet? …yet? …yet?") is the rude way. Supabase Realtime lets the client subscribe to inserts on the notifications table filtered to its own rows. Postgres streams the new row down a WebSocket the instant it's committed. Your React component receives it and shows a toast. No polling, no refresh — the tap on the shoulder.

In practice

1. The schema

-- Groups (communities)
create table public.groups (
  id          uuid primary key default gen_random_uuid(),
  slug        text unique not null,
  name        text not null,
  description text,
  cover_url   text,
  created_by  uuid not null references auth.users (id) on delete cascade,
  created_at  timestamptz not null default now()
);

create table public.group_members (
  group_id  uuid not null references public.groups (id) on delete cascade,
  user_id   uuid not null references auth.users (id) on delete cascade,
  role      text not null default 'member' check (role in ('member','admin','owner')),
  joined_at timestamptz not null default now(),
  primary key (group_id, user_id)
);

-- Group feed: posts already exist; add an optional group scope
alter table public.posts
  add column group_id uuid references public.groups (id) on delete cascade;

-- Notifications: one row per event, addressed to a recipient
create table public.notifications (
  id           uuid primary key default gen_random_uuid(),
  recipient_id uuid not null references auth.users (id) on delete cascade,
  actor_id     uuid references auth.users (id) on delete set null,
  type         text not null
                 check (type in ('connection','message','comment','reaction','group_invite')),
  entity_id    uuid,            -- the post/message/group this is about
  metadata     jsonb not null default '{}',
  read_at      timestamptz,
  created_at   timestamptz not null default now()
);

create index notifications_recipient_idx
  on public.notifications (recipient_id, created_at desc);

A few deliberate choices: actor_id is nullable with on delete set null so a deleted account doesn't erase your history (the notification just becomes "someone"). entity_id + type together tell the UI where to deep-link. read_at IS NULL is our definition of unread — cleaner than a boolean because it records when it was read.

2. Row Level Security

alter table public.groups          enable row level security;
alter table public.group_members   enable row level security;
alter table public.notifications   enable row level security;

-- Anyone signed in can discover groups; only members see member lists
create policy "groups are public to read"
  on public.groups for select using (true);

create policy "members read their memberships"
  on public.group_members for select
  using (
    exists (
      select 1 from public.group_members gm
      where gm.group_id = group_members.group_id
        and gm.user_id = auth.uid()
    )
  );

create policy "users join groups as themselves"
  on public.group_members for insert
  with check (user_id = auth.uid());

-- Notifications: you can ONLY ever read your own
create policy "read own notifications"
  on public.notifications for select
  using (recipient_id = auth.uid());

create policy "update own notifications"
  on public.notifications for update
  using (recipient_id = auth.uid());

Notice there's no client-side INSERT policy on notifications. Notifications are written by Server Actions using the server client (or, even better, a Postgres trigger / service role) so a malicious client can't forge a notification addressed to someone else.

3. A reusable "notify" helper (Server)

// lib/notifications.ts
import "server-only";
import { createServerSupabase } from "@/lib/supabase/server";

type NotifyInput = {
  recipientId: string;
  actorId: string;
  type: "connection" | "message" | "comment" | "reaction" | "group_invite";
  entityId?: string;
  metadata?: Record<string, unknown>;
};

export async function notify(input: NotifyInput) {
  // Never notify yourself about your own action.
  if (input.recipientId === input.actorId) return;

  const supabase = await createServerSupabase();
  const { error } = await supabase.from("notifications").insert({
    recipient_id: input.recipientId,
    actor_id: input.actorId,
    type: input.type,
    entity_id: input.entityId ?? null,
    metadata: input.metadata ?? {},
  });
  if (error) console.error("[notify] failed:", error.message);
}

Recall from Chapter 11 that createServerSupabase wraps createServerClient from @supabase/ssr with the getAll/setAll cookie handlers. We await it because Next.js 16 cookies() is async.

4. Server Actions: join a group, comment, and fan out

// app/groups/[slug]/actions.ts
"use server";

import { revalidatePath } from "next/cache";
import { createServerSupabase } from "@/lib/supabase/server";
import { notify } from "@/lib/notifications";

export async function joinGroup(groupId: string) {
  const supabase = await createServerSupabase();
  const { data: { user } } = await supabase.auth.getUser();
  if (!user) throw new Error("Not authenticated");

  const { error } = await supabase
    .from("group_members")
    .insert({ group_id: groupId, user_id: user.id });
  if (error && error.code !== "23505") throw error; // ignore "already a member"

  revalidatePath(`/groups`);
}

export async function commentOnPost(postId: string, body: string) {
  const supabase = await createServerSupabase();
  const { data: { user } } = await supabase.auth.getUser();
  if (!user) throw new Error("Not authenticated");

  const { data: post } = await supabase
    .from("posts")
    .select("id, author_id, group_id")
    .eq("id", postId)
    .single();
  if (!post) throw new Error("Post not found");

  await supabase.from("comments").insert({
    post_id: postId,
    author_id: user.id,
    body,
  });

  // Fan out: tell the post's author someone commented.
  await notify({
    recipientId: post.author_id,
    actorId: user.id,
    type: "comment",
    entityId: postId,
    metadata: { groupId: post.group_id },
  });

  revalidatePath(`/posts/${postId}`);
}

5. The Join button — optimistic with useTransition

// components/groups/join-button.tsx
"use client";

import { useState, useTransition } from "react";
import { motion } from "framer-motion";
import { joinGroup } from "@/app/groups/[slug]/actions";

export function JoinButton({ groupId, joined }: { groupId: string; joined: boolean }) {
  const [isMember, setIsMember] = useState(joined);
  const [isPending, startTransition] = useTransition();

  function handleJoin() {
    setIsMember(true); // optimistic
    startTransition(async () => {
      try {
        await joinGroup(groupId);
      } catch {
        setIsMember(false); // roll back on failure
      }
    });
  }

  return (
    <motion.button
      whileTap={{ scale: 0.94 }}
      onClick={handleJoin}
      disabled={isMember || isPending}
      className="rounded-full px-5 py-2 text-sm font-semibold text-white
                 bg-[var(--color-brand)] disabled:opacity-60 transition"
    >
      {isMember ? "Joined" : "Join group"}
    </motion.button>
  );
}

Our brand pink lives in a Tailwind v4 theme token (defined once in app/globals.css):

@import "tailwindcss";

@theme {
  --color-brand: #FF6FB5;
  --color-brand-ink: #B11D6E;
}

6. The Notification Bell — subscribe + toast

This is the payoff. A Client Component opens a Realtime subscription scoped to the current user and renders a live unread badge. New rows trigger a Framer Motion toast.

// components/notifications/notification-bell.tsx
"use client";

import { useEffect, useState } from "react";
import { motion, AnimatePresence } from "framer-motion";
import { createBrowserSupabase } from "@/lib/supabase/client";

type Notification = {
  id: string;
  type: string;
  actor_id: string | null;
  metadata: Record<string, unknown>;
  read_at: string | null;
};

const COPY: Record<string, string> = {
  connection: "wants to connect with you",
  message: "sent you a message",
  comment: "commented on your post",
  reaction: "reacted to your post",
  group_invite: "invited you to a group",
};

export function NotificationBell({
  userId,
  initialUnread,
}: {
  userId: string;
  initialUnread: number;
}) {
  const [unread, setUnread] = useState(initialUnread);
  const [toast, setToast] = useState<Notification | null>(null);

  useEffect(() => {
    const supabase = createBrowserSupabase();

    const channel = supabase
      .channel(`notifications:${userId}`)
      .on(
        "postgres_changes",
        {
          event: "INSERT",
          schema: "public",
          table: "notifications",
          filter: `recipient_id=eq.${userId}`,
        },
        (payload) => {
          const n = payload.new as Notification;
          setUnread((c) => c + 1);
          setToast(n);
          setTimeout(() => setToast(null), 4000);
        }
      )
      .subscribe();

    return () => {
      supabase.removeChannel(channel);
    };
  }, [userId]);

  return (
    <>
      <button className="relative" aria-label={`Notifications, ${unread} unread`}>
        <BellIcon />
        {unread > 0 && (
          <span
            className="absolute -top-1 -right-1 min-w-5 h-5 px-1 grid place-items-center
                       rounded-full bg-[var(--color-brand)] text-white text-xs font-bold"
          >
            {unread > 9 ? "9+" : unread}
          </span>
        )}
      </button>

      <AnimatePresence>
        {toast && (
          <motion.div
            initial={{ opacity: 0, y: 24, scale: 0.96 }}
            animate={{ opacity: 1, y: 0, scale: 1 }}
            exit={{ opacity: 0, y: 12, scale: 0.98 }}
            transition={{ type: "spring", stiffness: 380, damping: 28 }}
            className="fixed bottom-6 right-6 z-50 max-w-xs rounded-2xl bg-white
                       shadow-lg ring-1 ring-black/5 px-4 py-3 text-sm"
          >
            <span className="font-semibold text-[var(--color-brand-ink)]">Someone </span>
            {COPY[toast.type] ?? "did something"}
          </motion.div>
        )}
      </AnimatePresence>
    </>
  );
}

function BellIcon() {
  return (
    <svg width="22" height="22" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor"
      strokeWidth="2" strokeLinecap="round" strokeLinejoin="round">
      <path d="M6 8a6 6 0 0 1 12 0c0 7 3 9 3 9H3s3-2 3-9" />
      <path d="M10.3 21a1.94 1.94 0 0 0 3.4 0" />
    </svg>
  );
}

The bell receives its initialUnread count from the Server Component that renders the layout, so the badge is correct on first paint and Realtime keeps it live afterward:

// app/(app)/layout.tsx  (excerpt — Server Component)
import { createServerSupabase } from "@/lib/supabase/server";
import { NotificationBell } from "@/components/notifications/notification-bell";

export default async function AppLayout({ children }: { children: React.ReactNode }) {
  const supabase = await createServerSupabase();
  const { data: { user } } = await supabase.auth.getUser();

  const { count } = await supabase
    .from("notifications")
    .select("id", { count: "exact", head: true })
    .is("read_at", null);

  return (
    <div>
      <header className="flex items-center justify-end p-4">
        {user && <NotificationBell userId={user.id} initialUnread={count ?? 0} />}
      </header>
      {children}
    </div>
  );
}

One setup step: in the Supabase dashboard, enable Realtime for the notifications table (Database → Replication → add the table to the supabase_realtime publication). RLS still applies to Realtime, so a user only ever receives their own rows even though everyone subscribes to the same table.

7. Marking read

// app/(app)/notifications/actions.ts
"use server";
import { createServerSupabase } from "@/lib/supabase/server";
import { revalidatePath } from "next/cache";

export async function markAllRead() {
  const supabase = await createServerSupabase();
  const { data: { user } } = await supabase.auth.getUser();
  if (!user) return;

  await supabase
    .from("notifications")
    .update({ read_at: new Date().toISOString() })
    .is("read_at", null); // RLS already scopes this to the current user

  revalidatePath("/notifications");
}

Architecture / diagram

sequenceDiagram
    participant A as Actor (Sara)
    participant SA as Server Action
    participant DB as Postgres (notifications)
    participant RT as Supabase Realtime
    participant B as Recipient browser (Max)

    A->>SA: commentOnPost(postId, "love this!")
    SA->>DB: insert comment
    SA->>DB: notify(recipient=Max, type=comment)
    DB-->>RT: WAL change (INSERT)
    RT-->>B: postgres_changes (filtered recipient_id=Max)
    B->>B: setUnread(+1) + spring toast

groups ──< group_members >── auth.users
  │                              │
  └──< posts (group_id) >──┐     │
                           │     │
        comments/reactions ┘     │
                                 │
        notifications >──────────┘   (recipient_id, actor_id)
        ^ one immutable row per event, addressed to recipient

Best practices & pitfalls

  • Do write notifications server-side only. Never give clients INSERT rights — they'd forge notifications for other users.
  • Do guard against self-notification (recipientId === actorId). Nobody wants "You commented on your own post."
  • Don't compute notifications with a giant join at read time. Materialize one row per event.
  • Do rely on RLS for Realtime security — the filter in .on() is a convenience, not a security boundary. The policy is what actually protects rows.
  • Don't forget to removeChannel in the useEffect cleanup, or you'll leak WebSocket subscriptions and double-fire toasts in React Strict Mode.
  • Do collapse noisy events (e.g., 12 reactions → "Sara and 11 others reacted") by updating metadata on an existing unread row instead of inserting 12 rows. Add this once you feel the volume.
  • Don't store member IDs as an array column on groups. Use the join table — it's queryable, RLS-able, and race-free.
  • Do index notifications(recipient_id, created_at desc) — the bell query sorts on exactly this.
  • Don't trust count from a stale client; seed from the server on load, then let Realtime adjust deltas.

Exercises

  1. Member avatars. On the group page, render a stacked avatar row of the first 5 members plus a "+N" bubble. Query group_members joined to profiles, ordered by joined_at.
  2. Leave a group. Add a leaveGroup Server Action and toggle the JoinButton between Join/Leave with optimistic state and a roll-back on error.
  3. Deep links. Make each notification clickable: build a hrefFor(notification) helper that routes comment/reaction to /posts/{entity_id}, group_invite to /groups/{slug}, and connection to the actor's profile.
  4. Group invites. Add an inviteToGroup(groupId, userId) action that inserts a group_invite notification, and accept/decline buttons in the toast that call back into Server Actions.
  5. Reaction collapsing (hard). Before inserting a reaction notification, check for an existing unread reaction notification on the same entity_id; if found, bump a count in its metadata and update created_at instead of inserting a new row.

Mini-project — "Viby Circles"

Build a complete community page at /groups/[slug]:

  1. Server Component loads the group by slug, its member count, and the latest 20 posts where group_id = group.id (newest first).
  2. A header with the cover image, name, description, member count, and the optimistic JoinButton.
  3. A group feed of posts; each post has a comment box wired to commentOnPost. Posting a comment fans out a comment notification to the post's author.
  4. The shared NotificationBell in the layout: open two browser profiles, comment as one user, and watch the spring toast and badge increment live for the other — with zero page refresh.
  5. Bonus: add an "Invite a friend" button that writes a group_invite notification, and confirm it toasts on the recipient's screen in realtime.

Ship it, then DM the link to a friend — that loop (join → post → get notified) is the heartbeat of every social product you'll ever build.

Key takeaways

  • Communities are a many-to-many relationship: groups + a group_members join table, never an array column.
  • A notification is a stored, immutable row addressed to a recipient — fan-out-on-write, not computed-on-read.
  • read_at IS NULL is a cleaner "unread" definition than a boolean, because it records when.
  • Server Actions (or triggers) write notifications; clients never get INSERT rights.
  • Supabase Realtime + RLS streams each user only their own new rows; Framer Motion turns them into delightful toasts.
  • Seed counts from the server for a correct first paint, then let Realtime apply live deltas.

Up next — Chapter 28: Search & Discovery, where we make Viby findable: full-text search on people and groups, trending hobbies, and a "people you might vibe with" recommendation feed.

Chapter 28 — Performance & Optimization

A fast app feels like magic; a slow one feels broken — even when every feature works perfectly.

By now your Viby app does a lot: profiles, hobby feeds, realtime chat, image uploads. This chapter is where you make it feel premium. Performance isn't a final polish step — it's a product feature your users feel in their thumbs.

What you'll learn

  • What Core Web Vitals (LCP, INP, CLS) actually measure and why Google ranks you on them
  • Shipping optimized images with next/image and Supabase Storage
  • Code-splitting & lazy loading heavy client components with next/dynamic
  • Memoization and how to stop React from re-rendering the whole feed
  • Analyzing your bundle to find the 200KB library you forgot about
  • Virtualizing Viby's infinite hobby feed so 5,000 cards don't crater the page
  • Keeping Framer Motion animations buttery at 60fps

Prerequisites

  • Chapter 11 (Server vs. Client Components) — we lean hard on the Server Component default
  • Chapter 17 (Supabase Storage & uploads) — we optimize the avatars you stored there
  • Chapter 22 (Framer Motion) — we revisit those animations through a performance lens
  • Chapter 25 (the infinite feed) — the list we're about to virtualize

Theory

Why "fast" is not one thing

Performance is really three separate questions a user asks subconsciously:

  1. Did it show up? (loading)
  2. Did it respond when I tapped? (interactivity)
  3. Did it stop jumping around? (visual stability)

Google formalized these as Core Web Vitals:

  • LCP — Largest Contentful Paint: when the biggest thing in the viewport (usually a hero image or heading) finishes rendering. Target: under 2.5s. Analogy: how long until the restaurant actually puts food on your table.
  • INP — Interaction to Next Paint: how quickly the UI visibly reacts to taps/clicks across the whole session. Target: under 200ms. (INP replaced the old FID metric in 2024.) Analogy: how fast the waiter looks up when you wave.
  • CLS — Cumulative Layout Shift: how much content jumps around as things load. Target: under 0.1. Analogy: the table not wobbling every time a dish arrives.

These aren't vanity numbers. They affect SEO ranking, and they correlate directly with bounce rate. A Gen-Z user on a mid-range Android over spotty 4G is unforgiving.

Where time actually goes

The two biggest performance sins in a React app are almost always:

  • Shipping too much JavaScript — every KB must be downloaded, parsed, and executed on a phone CPU far slower than your MacBook.
  • Re-rendering too often — React re-running components that didn't need to change.

The good news: Next.js 16's App Router defaults fight both for you. Server Components ship zero JS to the browser. A component is server-rendered unless you opt out with "use client". So the cheapest optimization is simply not reaching for "use client" out of habit.

The mental model for re-renders

React re-renders a component when its state changes, its props change, or its parent re-renders. That last one surprises people: a parent re-rendering re-renders all its children by default, even children whose props are identical. Memoization (React.memo, useMemo, useCallback) is how you tell React "skip this — nothing it depends on changed."

But memoization isn't free, and premature memoization is clutter. Measure first. React 19's compiler (when enabled) auto-memoizes a lot of this, but understanding the manual tools makes you a better engineer.

In practice

1. Images: the single biggest LCP win

Viby is image-heavy — avatars, hobby cover photos, profile galleries. Unoptimized images are the #1 cause of bad LCP. Always use next/image: it serves modern formats (AVIF/WebP), generates responsive srcsets, lazy-loads off-screen images, and reserves space so nothing shifts (helping CLS).

First, allow your Supabase Storage domain in next.config.ts:

// next.config.ts
import type { NextConfig } from "next";

const nextConfig: NextConfig = {
  images: {
    remotePatterns: [
      {
        protocol: "https",
        hostname: "YOUR_PROJECT.supabase.co",
        pathname: "/storage/v1/object/public/**",
      },
    ],
  },
};

export default nextConfig;

Now a hobby card. Note priority on above-the-fold images (skips lazy-loading so LCP fires sooner), explicit sizes so the browser downloads the right resolution, and fill with a sized parent to prevent layout shift:

// components/feed/HobbyCard.tsx  (Server Component — no "use client")
import Image from "next/image";

type Hobby = { id: string; title: string; coverUrl: string };

export function HobbyCard({ hobby, priority = false }: { hobby: Hobby; priority?: boolean }) {
  return (
    <article className="overflow-hidden rounded-2xl bg-white shadow-sm">
      <div className="relative aspect-[4/3] w-full">
        <Image
          src={hobby.coverUrl}
          alt={hobby.title}
          fill
          priority={priority}
          sizes="(max-width: 640px) 100vw, (max-width: 1024px) 50vw, 33vw"
          className="object-cover"
        />
      </div>
      <h3 className="p-4 font-semibold text-zinc-900">{hobby.title}</h3>
    </article>
  );
}

Mark only the first few cards priority; lazy-load the rest (the default).

2. Code-splitting heavy client components

Viby's emoji picker for chat is ~150KB and most users never open it. Don't make everyone pay for it on first load. Split it with next/dynamic, and skip SSR for browser-only widgets:

// components/chat/MessageComposer.tsx
"use client";

import { useState } from "react";
import dynamic from "next/dynamic";

// Loaded only when needed; never server-rendered.
const EmojiPicker = dynamic(() => import("./EmojiPicker"), {
  ssr: false,
  loading: () => <div className="h-72 w-72 animate-pulse rounded-xl bg-pink-50" />,
});

export function MessageComposer() {
  const [showEmoji, setShowEmoji] = useState(false);

  return (
    <div className="relative">
      <button
        onClick={() => setShowEmoji((v) => !v)}
        className="rounded-full px-3 py-2 text-[#FF6FB5]"
        aria-label="Add emoji"
      >
        😊
      </button>
      {showEmoji && <EmojiPicker />}
    </div>
  );
}

The emoji bundle is now a separate chunk fetched on first click — not in your initial page load.

3. Memoization: stop the feed from re-rendering

Suppose Viby's feed has a like counter in a parent. Every time the count changes, the parent re-renders — and so does every HobbyCard, even though their data didn't change. Wrap the card in React.memo, and stabilize the callback with useCallback:

// components/feed/LikeableCard.tsx
"use client";

import { memo, useCallback, useState } from "react";

type Props = { id: string; title: string; onLike: (id: string) => void };

// Re-renders only when its own props change by reference.
export const LikeableCard = memo(function LikeableCard({ id, title, onLike }: Props) {
  console.log("render", id); // watch this stop firing for unchanged cards
  return (
    <button onClick={() => onLike(id)} className="rounded-2xl bg-white p-4 shadow-sm">
      {title} 💖
    </button>
  );
});

export function Feed({ hobbies }: { hobbies: { id: string; title: string }[] }) {
  const [likes, setLikes] = useState(0);

  // Stable reference across renders → memo'd children don't re-render.
  const handleLike = useCallback((id: string) => {
    setLikes((n) => n + 1);
    console.log("liked", id);
  }, []);

  return (
    <>
      <p className="mb-3 text-sm text-zinc-500">{likes} likes</p>
      {hobbies.map((h) => (
        <LikeableCard key={h.id} id={h.id} title={h.title} onLike={handleLike} />
      ))}
    </>
  );
}

Without useCallback, handleLike would be a new function every render, breaking memo. Use useMemo similarly for expensive computed values (e.g., filtering/sorting a big hobby list) — not for cheap ones.

4. List virtualization for the infinite feed

When Viby's feed grows to thousands of cards, the browser chokes on DOM nodes — even memoized ones, because they all exist. Virtualization renders only the rows currently in (and near) the viewport. We'll use @tanstack/react-virtual:

npm install @tanstack/react-virtual

// components/feed/VirtualFeed.tsx
"use client";

import { useRef } from "react";
import { useVirtualizer } from "@tanstack/react-virtual";
import { HobbyCard } from "./HobbyCard";

type Hobby = { id: string; title: string; coverUrl: string };

export function VirtualFeed({ hobbies }: { hobbies: Hobby[] }) {
  const parentRef = useRef<HTMLDivElement>(null);

  const virtualizer = useVirtualizer({
    count: hobbies.length,
    getScrollElement: () => parentRef.current,
    estimateSize: () => 320, // estimated card height in px
    overscan: 5, // render a few extra above/below for smooth scroll
  });

  return (
    <div ref={parentRef} className="h-[100dvh] overflow-auto">
      <div style={{ height: virtualizer.getTotalSize(), position: "relative" }}>
        {virtualizer.getVirtualItems().map((item) => (
          <div
            key={hobbies[item.index].id}
            style={{
              position: "absolute",
              top: 0,
              left: 0,
              width: "100%",
              transform: `translateY(${item.start}px)`,
            }}
          >
            <HobbyCard hobby={hobbies[item.index]} priority={item.index < 3} />
          </div>
        ))}
      </div>
    </div>
  );
}

5,000 hobbies, ~12 DOM cards at a time. The scrollbar still feels full because the spacer div has the total height.

5. Analyzing your bundle

You can't optimize what you can't see. Add the analyzer:

npm install --save-dev @next/bundle-analyzer

// next.config.ts
import type { NextConfig } from "next";
import withBundleAnalyzer from "@next/bundle-analyzer";

const nextConfig: NextConfig = {
  images: { /* ...as above... */ },
};

const analyzer = withBundleAnalyzer({ enabled: process.env.ANALYZE === "true" });
export default analyzer(nextConfig);

# package.json script: "analyze": "ANALYZE=true next build"
ANALYZE=true npm run build

A treemap opens in your browser. Look for surprises — a date library, an icon set imported whole, a "use client" component that dragged a server-only dependency into the client. Fix by importing narrowly (import { format } from "date-fns/format") or moving logic server-side.

6. Animation performance

Framer Motion is fast if you animate the right properties. Animating transform (x, y, scale, rotate) and opacity is GPU-accelerated and never triggers layout. Animating width, height, top, or margin forces layout recalculation on every frame and causes jank.

// components/feed/CardReveal.tsx
"use client";

import { motion } from "framer-motion";

export function CardReveal({ children }: { children: React.ReactNode }) {
  return (
    <motion.div
      initial={{ opacity: 0, y: 16 }}   // ✅ transform + opacity = 60fps
      animate={{ opacity: 1, y: 0 }}
      transition={{ duration: 0.3, ease: "easeOut" }}
      // Respect users who get motion sick:
      style={{ willChange: "transform, opacity" }}
    >
      {children}
    </motion.div>
  );
}

Avoid animating height: 0 → auto; animate scaleY or use a fixed/measured height instead. And honor prefers-reduced-motion — Framer Motion's MotionConfig with reducedMotion="user" does this app-wide.

Architecture / diagram

How a request becomes a fast, stable paint:

flowchart TD
    A[Request /feed] --> B[Server Component renders on server]
    B --> C{Component needs browser APIs / interactivity?}
    C -- No --> D[Ship HTML only — 0 KB JS]
    C -- Yes --> E[Mark 'use client' → JS chunk]
    E --> F{Heavy & rarely used?}
    F -- Yes --> G[next/dynamic → separate lazy chunk]
    F -- No --> H[Bundled in route chunk]
    D --> I[next/image streams AVIF/WebP, reserves space]
    G --> I
    H --> I
    I --> J[Virtualizer renders only visible rows]
    J --> K[Good LCP / low CLS / fast INP ✅]

The throughline: default to the server, opt into the client deliberately, and only ship what the user can actually see.

Best practices & pitfalls

  • Do keep components as Server Components until you genuinely need state, effects, or browser APIs. "use client" is a cost, not a default.
  • Do set priority on the one or two largest above-the-fold images — and only those.
  • Do always provide sizes with fill images, or you'll download desktop-sized images to phones.
  • Don't memoize everything reflexively. memo on a component whose props change every render just adds an extra comparison.
  • Don't put useCallback/useMemo around trivial values — the bookkeeping can cost more than the work.
  • Don't animate layout-affecting properties (width, top, margin). Use transform.
  • Do virtualize any list that can exceed a few hundred items. Pagination + virtualization compound nicely.
  • Don't import whole libraries (import _ from "lodash"). Import the function (import debounce from "lodash/debounce").
  • Do measure with the Network tab throttled to "Slow 4G" and CPU 4x slowdown — that's your real user.
  • Pitfall: ssr: false in next/dynamic only works inside Client Components. Calling it from a Server Component throws.

Exercises

  1. Audit one route. Run ANALYZE=true npm run build on the /feed route. Screenshot the treemap and name the three largest chunks. Replace one whole-library import with a narrow one and re-measure the delta.
  2. Kill a layout shift. Find an image in Viby rendered with a plain <img> and no dimensions. Convert it to next/image with fill + aspect-ratio parent. Confirm CLS drops in Lighthouse.
  3. Lazy-load a modal. Pick a rarely-used component (settings panel, report dialog) and convert it to next/dynamic with ssr: false and a skeleton loading state.
  4. Catch a rogue re-render. Add a console.log to a feed card. Trigger a parent state change and watch every card log. Fix it with memo + useCallback so only relevant cards re-render.
  5. Virtualize & profile. Render 2,000 mock hobbies without virtualization, record the React Profiler commit time, then add VirtualFeed and compare.

Mini-project: The Buttery Feed

Build a production-grade /discover page that ties the whole chapter together.

Requirements:

  1. A Server Component page that fetches the first page of hobbies from Supabase (createServerClient with getAll/setAll cookie handlers from Chapter 14).
  2. Cards rendered with next/image, correct sizes, and priority on the first three.
  3. The list passed into a VirtualFeed client component using @tanstack/react-virtual.
  4. Each card wrapped in a memo'd CardReveal using Framer Motion (opacity + y only).
  5. A lazy-loaded filter sheet (next/dynamic, ssr: false) that opens on tap.
  6. Wrap the app in <MotionConfig reducedMotion="user"> to respect accessibility.

Definition of done: Lighthouse mobile run scores LCP < 2.5s, CLS < 0.1, INP < 200ms on a throttled profile, and the bundle analyzer shows the filter sheet in its own chunk — not the initial load.

Starter for the page shell:

// app/discover/page.tsx  (Server Component)
import { createServerClient } from "@supabase/ssr";
import { cookies } from "next/headers";
import { VirtualFeed } from "@/components/feed/VirtualFeed";

export default async function DiscoverPage() {
  const cookieStore = await cookies();
  const supabase = createServerClient(
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL!,
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY!,
    {
      cookies: {
        getAll: () => cookieStore.getAll(),
        setAll: (toSet) => toSet.forEach(({ name, value, options }) =>
          cookieStore.set(name, value, options)),
      },
    },
  );

  const { data: hobbies } = await supabase
    .from("hobbies")
    .select("id, title, coverUrl:cover_url")
    .limit(200);

  return (
    <main className="mx-auto max-w-3xl">
      <h1 className="px-4 py-6 text-2xl font-bold text-[#FF6FB5]">Discover your people</h1>
      <VirtualFeed hobbies={hobbies ?? []} />
    </main>
  );
}

Key takeaways

  • Core Web Vitals — LCP (loading), INP (interactivity), CLS (stability) — are the metrics that define "fast" and affect your ranking.
  • Server Components ship zero JS. The cheapest optimization is not opting into "use client".
  • next/image with priority, sizes, and reserved space is your biggest single LCP + CLS win.
  • next/dynamic keeps rarely-used heavy code out of the initial bundle.
  • memo + useCallback/useMemo stop needless re-renders — but measure before reaching for them.
  • Virtualize long lists; analyze your bundle; animate only transform and opacity.

Up next — Chapter 29: Testing & Quality Assurance, where we make sure all this speed never ships a regression: unit tests, component tests, and end-to-end flows for Viby.

Chapter 29 — Caching, Server Components & Scalable Architecture

The difference between an app that feels instant and one that feels broken is almost never the database — it's where you decided to do the work.

By now Viby works. People sign up, build hobby profiles, discover their people, and chat in realtime. But "works on my laptop with three users" and "works on Vercel with three thousand" are different sports. This chapter is where you stop being someone who makes features and start being someone who makes systems.

What you'll learn

  • The four caching layers in Next.js 16 and how revalidate, tags, and dynamic actually behave
  • Streaming UI with <Suspense> so slow data never blocks a fast page
  • A decision framework for where logic lives: client, Server Component, Server Action, or data layer
  • A scalable feature-first folder architecture that survives 50+ screens
  • A clean data-access layer (DAL) that centralizes every query and auth check
  • How the demo (Zustand) vs. connected (Supabase) seam holds up as the app grows

Prerequisites

  • Chapter 7 (Server vs. Client Components) and Chapter 11 (Server Actions & forms)
  • Chapter 18 (Supabase Auth with @supabase/ssr) and Chapter 21 (Realtime chat)
  • Chapter 24 (the demo/connected pattern with Zustand) — we extend that seam here

Theory

Caching is just "remembering so you don't redo work"

Imagine a barista who memorizes your regular order. The first time, they ask everything. After that, you say "the usual" and coffee appears. Caching is that — but you have to decide how long "the usual" stays valid before they re-ask. Too short and you're slow; too long and you serve a stale order.

Next.js 16 gives you four places to remember things:

  1. Request Memoization — within a single render pass, identical fetch() calls (or React cache()-wrapped functions) run once. Free, automatic, lasts one request. This is why you can call your "get current user" helper in five components without five queries.
  2. Data Cache — persists fetched data across requests and deployments until you invalidate it. This is the big one for performance.
  3. Full Route Cache — caches the rendered HTML/RSC payload of static routes at build time.
  4. Router Cache — a client-side, in-memory cache of visited routes for snappy back/forward navigation.

The mental shift in the App Router: rendering is dynamic by default unless you opt into caching. In Next.js 16, fetch is not cached unless you say so, and reading dynamic inputs (cookies, headers, searchParams) makes a route dynamic. You choose static behavior deliberately.

Static, dynamic, and revalidation

Three knobs control a route segment:

  • Static — rendered once, reused. Perfect for a marketing page or a public hobby directory.
  • Dynamic — rendered per request. Required when output depends on the logged-in user.
  • Revalidate (ISR) — static, but refreshed on a timer or on demand. "Cache this, but re-bake it every 60 seconds" or "re-bake it the moment someone edits their profile."

On-demand revalidation uses tags. You tag cached data, then call revalidateTag('hobbies') from a Server Action after a mutation. The barista forgets only that order, not the whole menu.

Streaming and Suspense: don't let the slow guest hold the door

Picture a dinner party. Some dishes are ready instantly; the soufflé takes ten minutes. You don't make every guest stand outside until the soufflé is done — you seat them, serve bread, and bring the soufflé when it's ready.

<Suspense> is that. You send the fast shell (header, nav, profile card) immediately, show a skeleton where the slow part will go, and stream the slow component in when its data resolves. The user sees a meaningful page in ~100ms instead of staring at a white screen for a slow query.

Where does logic go?

This is the question that separates juniors from seniors. The rule of thumb:

Concern Lives in
Reading data for first paint Server Component (calls the DAL)
Mutations (create/update/delete) Server Action ("use server")
Interactivity, animation, local UI state Client Component ("use client")
Database queries + auth checks Data-access layer (one place)
Cross-cutting routing rules (auth redirects) proxy.ts

Push data fetching down to the leaf that needs it (so Suspense can isolate it) and push secrets/queries into the DAL (so nothing leaks to the client and auth is never forgotten).

In practice

The data-access layer (one front door for your data)

Everything that touches Supabase goes through src/data/. No component imports the Supabase client directly. This is your security boundary and your caching boundary.

// src/data/supabase/server.ts
import { createServerClient } from "@supabase/ssr";
import { cookies } from "next/headers";

export async function createClient() {
  const cookieStore = await cookies();

  return createServerClient(
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL!,
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY!,
    {
      cookies: {
        getAll() {
          return cookieStore.getAll();
        },
        setAll(cookiesToSet) {
          try {
            cookiesToSet.forEach(({ name, value, options }) =>
              cookieStore.set(name, value, options),
            );
          } catch {
            // Called from a Server Component render — safe to ignore;
            // proxy.ts refreshes the session cookie instead.
          }
        },
      },
    },
  );
}

// src/data/profiles.ts
import "server-only";
import { cache } from "react";
import { createClient } from "@/data/supabase/server";

// React cache(): dedupes within a single request (Request Memoization).
export const getCurrentUser = cache(async () => {
  const supabase = await createClient();
  const {
    data: { user },
  } = await supabase.auth.getUser();
  return user;
});

export async function getProfileByHandle(handle: string) {
  const supabase = await createClient();
  const { data, error } = await supabase
    .from("profiles")
    .select("id, handle, display_name, bio, avatar_url, hobbies(name, emoji)")
    .eq("handle", handle)
    .single();

  if (error) throw new Error("Profile not found");
  return data;
}

The import "server-only" line is a tripwire: if anyone ever imports this into a Client Component, the build fails loudly instead of shipping your service logic to the browser.

Discover feed: static directory, fresh on demand

Viby's public Discover page lists hobby communities. It rarely changes, so cache it — but refresh instantly when an admin adds a hobby.

// src/data/hobbies.ts
import "server-only";
import { unstable_cache } from "next/cache";
import { createClient } from "@/data/supabase/server";

export const getHobbyCommunities = unstable_cache(
  async () => {
    const supabase = await createClient();
    const { data } = await supabase
      .from("hobbies")
      .select("id, name, emoji, member_count")
      .order("member_count", { ascending: false });
    return data ?? [];
  },
  ["hobby-communities"], // cache key parts
  { tags: ["hobbies"], revalidate: 3600 }, // refresh hourly OR on demand
);

// src/app/discover/page.tsx  (Server Component — no "use client")
import { getHobbyCommunities } from "@/data/hobbies";
import { HobbyCard } from "@/features/discover/components/hobby-card";

export default async function DiscoverPage() {
  const communities = await getHobbyCommunities();

  return (
    <main className="mx-auto max-w-2xl px-4 py-8">
      <h1 className="text-2xl font-bold text-brand">Find your people</h1>
      <div className="mt-6 grid grid-cols-2 gap-3">
        {communities.map((c) => (
          <HobbyCard key={c.id} community={c} />
        ))}
      </div>
    </main>
  );
}

When an admin creates a hobby, the Server Action busts only the hobby cache:

// src/features/discover/actions.ts
"use server";

import { revalidateTag } from "next/cache";
import { createHobby } from "@/data/hobbies";
import { getCurrentUser } from "@/data/profiles";

export async function addHobbyAction(formData: FormData) {
  const user = await getCurrentUser();
  if (!user) throw new Error("Unauthorized");

  await createHobby({
    name: String(formData.get("name")),
    emoji: String(formData.get("emoji")),
  });

  revalidateTag("hobbies"); // every reader of "hobbies" re-bakes
}

Streaming a slow profile section

A profile page should paint instantly. The "people you might vibe with" matcher is slow (it scores shared hobbies), so we stream it.

// src/app/u/[handle]/page.tsx
import { Suspense } from "react";
import { getProfileByHandle } from "@/data/profiles";
import { ProfileHeader } from "@/features/profile/components/profile-header";
import { VibeMatches } from "@/features/profile/components/vibe-matches";
import { MatchesSkeleton } from "@/features/profile/components/matches-skeleton";

// Next.js 16: params is async — await it.
export default async function ProfilePage({
  params,
}: {
  params: Promise<{ handle: string }>;
}) {
  const { handle } = await params;
  const profile = await getProfileByHandle(handle); // fast, blocks paint

  return (
    <main className="mx-auto max-w-2xl px-4 py-8">
      <ProfileHeader profile={profile} />

      {/* Fast shell renders now; matches stream in when ready */}
      <Suspense fallback={<MatchesSkeleton />}>
        <VibeMatches profileId={profile.id} />
      </Suspense>
    </main>
  );
}

// src/features/profile/components/vibe-matches.tsx  (async Server Component)
import { getVibeMatches } from "@/data/matches";
import { MatchRow } from "./match-row";

export async function VibeMatches({ profileId }: { profileId: string }) {
  const matches = await getVibeMatches(profileId); // the slow query

  return (
    <section className="mt-8">
      <h2 className="text-lg font-semibold">People you might vibe with</h2>
      <ul className="mt-3 space-y-2">
        {matches.map((m) => (
          <MatchRow key={m.id} match={m} />
        ))}
      </ul>
    </section>
  );
}

The skeleton is a Client Component only if it animates; otherwise keep it on the server:

// src/features/profile/components/matches-skeleton.tsx
"use client";
import { motion } from "framer-motion";

export function MatchesSkeleton() {
  return (
    <div className="mt-8 space-y-2">
      {[0, 1, 2].map((i) => (
        <motion.div
          key={i}
          className="h-14 rounded-xl bg-pink-100"
          animate={{ opacity: [0.5, 1, 0.5] }}
          transition={{ duration: 1.2, repeat: Infinity, delay: i * 0.15 }}
        />
      ))}
    </div>
  );
}

Cross-cutting auth in proxy.ts

Middleware-style logic lives in proxy.ts (not middleware.ts). It refreshes the Supabase session cookie and guards private routes.

// proxy.ts
import { type NextRequest, NextResponse } from "next/server";
import { createServerClient } from "@supabase/ssr";

export async function proxy(request: NextRequest) {
  let response = NextResponse.next({ request });

  const supabase = createServerClient(
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL!,
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY!,
    {
      cookies: {
        getAll() {
          return request.cookies.getAll();
        },
        setAll(cookiesToSet) {
          cookiesToSet.forEach(({ name, value, options }) =>
            response.cookies.set(name, value, options),
          );
        },
      },
    },
  );

  const {
    data: { user },
  } = await supabase.auth.getUser();

  const isPrivate = request.nextUrl.pathname.startsWith("/app");
  if (!user && isPrivate) {
    return NextResponse.redirect(new URL("/login", request.url));
  }

  return response;
}

export const config = {
  matcher: ["/((?!_next/static|_next/image|favicon.ico).*)"],
};

The demo/connected seam at scale

From Chapter 24, Viby runs in demo mode (Zustand, in-memory) or connected mode (Supabase). At scale you don't if (demo) inside components — you choose the implementation at the DAL boundary:

// src/data/matches.ts
import "server-only";
import { isDemo } from "@/lib/runtime";
import { demoMatches } from "@/data/demo/matches";
import { fetchVibeMatches } from "@/data/supabase/matches";

export async function getVibeMatches(profileId: string) {
  return isDemo() ? demoMatches(profileId) : fetchVibeMatches(profileId);
}

Components stay identical in both modes; only the data source swaps. One seam, enforced in one folder.

Architecture / diagram

flowchart TD
  Browser["Browser (Client Components: motion, Zustand UI state)"]
  Proxy["proxy.ts — session refresh + route guards"]
  RSC["Server Components (render, await DAL)"]
  Action["Server Actions ('use server') — mutations"]
  DAL["Data-Access Layer (src/data, 'server-only')"]
  Cache["Caching: cache() · unstable_cache(tags) · revalidateTag"]
  SB[("Supabase: Postgres · Auth · Storage")]
  Demo[("Demo store (in-memory)")]

  Browser -->|request| Proxy --> RSC
  Browser -->|form submit| Action
  RSC --> DAL
  Action --> DAL
  Action -.revalidateTag.-> Cache
  DAL --> Cache
  Cache --> SB
  DAL --> Demo

Feature-first folder layout — group by domain, not by file type, so a feature is self-contained and deletable:

src/
  app/                      # routes only (thin: compose features)
    discover/page.tsx
    u/[handle]/page.tsx
  features/                 # one folder per domain
    discover/
      components/
      actions.ts
      schema.ts
    profile/
      components/
      actions.ts
    chat/
  data/                     # the ONLY place that talks to data
    supabase/               # client factories + raw queries
    demo/                   # in-memory equivalents
    profiles.ts hobbies.ts matches.ts
  components/ui/            # shadcn-style primitives (Button, Card)
  lib/                      # pure helpers (runtime flags, formatters)
  stores/                   # Zustand stores (client UI state)

Best practices & pitfalls

  • Do keep all queries + auth checks in src/data/. Don't call createClient() inside page components.
  • Do add import "server-only" to every DAL module. It turns an accidental client leak into a build error.
  • Do wrap per-request reads (like getCurrentUser) in React cache(). Don't assume fetch is cached — in Next.js 16 it isn't unless configured.
  • Do use tags + revalidateTag for surgical invalidation after mutations. Don't reach for revalidatePath("/") as a sledgehammer — it nukes far more than you mean to.
  • Do stream slow, independent sections with <Suspense>. Don't wrap your whole page in one Suspense boundary — you lose the fast shell.
  • Don't cache anything user-specific with a shared key. Either scope the cache key to the user or keep the route dynamic.
  • Do await params and await cookies() — they're async in Next.js 16. Don't add "use client" just to silence an error; understand which boundary you actually need.
  • Don't scatter if (demo) through the UI. Resolve it once at the DAL.

Exercises

  1. Tag a cache. Convert Viby's events list to unstable_cache with the tag "events", then add a createEventAction that calls revalidateTag("events"). Confirm the list updates without a full reload.
  2. Stream a second section. Add a <Suspense>-wrapped "Recent activity" feed to the profile page with its own skeleton. Verify the header paints before the feed.
  3. Find the leak. Import a src/data/ module into a Client Component and watch the build fail. Explain in a comment why server-only caught it.
  4. Scope a cache key. Cache a user's saved-hobbies list correctly so two different users never see each other's data. (Hint: include the user id in the key array.)
  5. Refactor a seam. Take one component still doing if (isDemo()) inline and move that branch into its DAL function.

Mini-project — "Trending Communities" with on-demand revalidation

Build a /discover/trending page that ranks hobby communities by weekly join count.

  1. In src/data/hobbies.ts, add getTrendingCommunities() wrapped in unstable_cache with key ["trending"], tag "hobbies", and revalidate: 600.
  2. Build src/app/discover/trending/page.tsx as a Server Component that maps results into animated HobbyCards.
  3. Wrap the list in <Suspense> with a TrendingSkeleton (Framer Motion pulse).
  4. Add a "Join community" Server Action that inserts a membership row and calls revalidateTag("hobbies"), so trending re-ranks within the same session.
  5. Wire it for both modes: a demoTrending() branch and a Supabase branch, selected in the DAL — zero if (demo) in the page.

Ship it to Vercel and watch the page serve from cache, then re-bake the instant you tap "Join."

Key takeaways

  • Next.js 16 renders dynamically by default; caching is an intentional opt-in across four layers (request, data, route, router).
  • Use tags + revalidateTag for precise, on-demand cache busting after mutations.
  • Stream slow, independent sections with <Suspense> so the fast shell never waits.
  • Logic has a home: reads in Server Components, mutations in Server Actions, queries + auth in the DAL, routing rules in proxy.ts.
  • A feature-first structure plus a single data-access boundary keeps Viby scalable — and keeps the demo/connected seam to exactly one place.

Up next — Chapter 30: Observability & Performance — measuring Core Web Vitals, tracing slow queries, and proving Viby is fast under real load.

Chapter 30 — Security, RLS & Rate Limiting

The moment Viby has one real user, it has one real attacker — and the only code you can trust runs on a machine you control.

What you'll learn

  • Why the client is never a security boundary, and where the real boundary lives
  • How Row-Level Security (RLS) turns Postgres itself into your authorization layer
  • Validating input with Zod in Server Actions, and trusting nothing from the request
  • Handling secrets correctly (and the difference between NEXT_PUBLIC_ and server-only env)
  • The mechanics of XSS and CSRF, and why the App Router defends you by default
  • Adding rate limiting to protect signups, messages, and uploads

Prerequisites

  • Supabase Auth and @supabase/ssr clients (Chapter 12)
  • Server Actions and the "use server" directive (Chapter 15)
  • proxy.ts for edge/middleware-style logic (Chapter 18)
  • The Viby profiles, posts, and messages tables (Chapters 10–14)

Theory

The client is enemy territory

Here's the mental model that prevents 90% of security bugs: everything that runs in the browser can be edited, replayed, and lied to. The JavaScript you ship, the disabled attribute on a button, the if (user.isAdmin) check in a React component — all of it is suggestion, not enforcement. A determined user opens DevTools, edits your Zustand store, replays your fetch with a different user_id, and your "hidden" admin action fires.

Think of it like a nightclub. The styling on the door — the velvet rope, the sign saying "members only" — is the client. It sets expectations. But the bouncer is the server, and the guest list is the database. If the bouncer waves anyone through because the rope looked closed, the rope was decoration. Security lives at the door you control, every single time, for every single request.

So the rule: validate and authorize on the server, ideally as close to the data as possible. The closer the check sits to the data, the harder it is to bypass.

RLS: authorization that lives in the data

Most apps put authorization in application code: "before returning posts, filter by the logged-in user." That works until you have three API routes, two Server Actions, and a cron job — and one of them forgets the filter. Now any user can read anyone's private messages.

Row-Level Security moves the rule into Postgres. You write a policy — a SQL predicate — that the database evaluates on every query touching that table, no matter which client, route, or action issued it. Forget to filter in your code? The database filters for you. It's the difference between asking every employee to remember the safe combination versus installing a lock that physically can't open for the wrong key.

With Supabase, the magic is that the user's JWT flows into Postgres, and auth.uid() returns the authenticated user's id inside the policy. The policy becomes: "you may only see rows where user_id = auth.uid()." Even if your Server Action has a bug, the database refuses.

Critical: RLS only protects you when queries run with the anon or authenticated role (the keys exposed to users). The service role key bypasses RLS entirely. Treat it like the master key to the building — server-only, never in the browser, used sparingly.

Validation, secrets, XSS, CSRF — the supporting cast

  • Input validation: even authenticated users send garbage — a 50,000-character bio, a role: "admin" field you never asked for. Parse incoming data against a strict schema and reject everything else.
  • Secrets: API keys belong in server-only environment variables. Anything prefixed NEXT_PUBLIC_ is baked into the browser bundle — public forever.
  • XSS (cross-site scripting): an attacker gets their script to run in another user's browser, usually by smuggling <script> into content you later render as raw HTML. React escapes by default; you only get burned via dangerouslySetInnerHTML.
  • CSRF (cross-site request forgery): a malicious site silently submits a request as the logged-in victim, riding their cookies. The App Router's Server Actions are POST-only with Origin checks, which neutralizes the classic attack.
  • Rate limiting: without it, one script can attempt 10,000 logins or flood your DMs in a minute. Limits cap the blast radius of abuse and runaway bots.

In practice

1. Turn on RLS for every user-facing table

In the Supabase SQL editor, enable RLS and write explicit policies for the Viby posts table. Enabling RLS with no policy denies all access — that's the safe default.

-- Enable RLS (default-deny once on)
alter table public.posts enable row level security;

-- Anyone authenticated can read PUBLIC posts; you always see your own.
create policy "read public or own posts"
on public.posts for select
to authenticated
using (
  visibility = 'public' or author_id = (select auth.uid())
);

-- You may only INSERT a post authored by yourself.
create policy "insert own posts"
on public.posts for insert
to authenticated
with check ( author_id = (select auth.uid()) );

-- You may only UPDATE / DELETE your own posts.
create policy "modify own posts"
on public.posts for update
to authenticated
using ( author_id = (select auth.uid()) )
with check ( author_id = (select auth.uid()) );

create policy "delete own posts"
on public.posts for delete
to authenticated
using ( author_id = (select auth.uid()) );

Two things worth burning into memory:

  • using filters which existing rows the operation can see/touch; with check validates the row's new values. Inserts need with check; deletes need using; updates need both.
  • Wrapping auth.uid() in (select auth.uid()) lets Postgres evaluate it once per query instead of per row — a real performance win on large tables.

2. Read data with the SSR client — RLS does the rest

Because RLS enforces ownership, your Server Component query stays refreshingly dumb. No manual .eq('author_id', user.id) needed for security — though you may still add filters for correctness.

// app/feed/page.tsx — Server Component (no "use client")
import { createServerClient } from "@supabase/ssr";
import { cookies } from "next/headers";

async function getSupabase() {
  const cookieStore = await cookies(); // Next.js 16: async
  return createServerClient(
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL!,
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY!, // anon key → RLS applies
    {
      cookies: {
        getAll: () => cookieStore.getAll(),
        setAll: (toSet) =>
          toSet.forEach(({ name, value, options }) =>
            cookieStore.set(name, value, options)
          ),
      },
    }
  );
}

export default async function FeedPage() {
  const supabase = await getSupabase();
  // RLS guarantees we only get public posts + our own private ones.
  const { data: posts } = await supabase
    .from("posts")
    .select("id, body, visibility, author_id")
    .order("created_at", { ascending: false });

  return (
    <ul className="space-y-3">
      {posts?.map((p) => (
        <li key={p.id} className="rounded-2xl bg-white p-4 shadow-sm">
          {p.body}
        </li>
      ))}
    </ul>
  );
}

3. Validate in a Server Action — never trust the payload

A Server Action runs on the server, but the arguments still come from the client. Parse them. Here we validate a new Viby post with Zod, then let RLS confirm ownership at write time.

// app/feed/actions.ts
"use server";

import { z } from "zod";
import { revalidatePath } from "next/cache";
import { createServerClient } from "@supabase/ssr";
import { cookies } from "next/headers";

const PostInput = z.object({
  body: z.string().trim().min(1, "Say something!").max(500),
  visibility: z.enum(["public", "friends", "private"]),
});

export async function createPost(formData: FormData) {
  // 1. Validate shape + bounds. Reject anything extra/oversized.
  const parsed = PostInput.safeParse({
    body: formData.get("body"),
    visibility: formData.get("visibility"),
  });
  if (!parsed.success) {
    return { ok: false, error: parsed.error.issues[0].message };
  }

  const cookieStore = await cookies();
  const supabase = createServerClient(
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL!,
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY!,
    {
      cookies: {
        getAll: () => cookieStore.getAll(),
        setAll: (toSet) =>
          toSet.forEach(({ name, value, options }) =>
            cookieStore.set(name, value, options)
          ),
      },
    }
  );

  // 2. Get the trusted server-side identity. NEVER take author_id from the form.
  const { data: { user } } = await supabase.auth.getUser();
  if (!user) return { ok: false, error: "Not signed in." };

  // 3. Insert. RLS's with-check will also reject a mismatched author_id.
  const { error } = await supabase.from("posts").insert({
    body: parsed.data.body,
    visibility: parsed.data.visibility,
    author_id: user.id, // derived from the session, not the request body
  });

  if (error) return { ok: false, error: "Could not post." };
  revalidatePath("/feed");
  return { ok: true };
}

Use supabase.auth.getUser() for trust decisions, not getSession(). getUser() re-validates the token with the Auth server; getSession() reads the (potentially spoofable) cookie.

4. Secrets: public vs. server-only

# .env.local
# Safe to expose — shipped to the browser bundle:
NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL=https://xxxx.supabase.co
NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY=eyJhbGci...   # anon key is meant to be public

# SERVER ONLY — bypasses RLS. Never NEXT_PUBLIC_, never imported in a client file:
SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY=eyJhbGci...
RESEND_API_KEY=re_...

A service-role client must only ever be constructed in server code (Server Actions, Route Handlers, proxy.ts):

// lib/supabase-admin.ts — import ONLY from server code
import { createClient } from "@supabase/supabase-js";

export const supabaseAdmin = createClient(
  process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL!,
  process.env.SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY!, // bypasses RLS — handle with care
  { auth: { persistSession: false } }
);

5. XSS: render content as text, never as HTML

React already escapes {post.body}. The danger is opting out:

// ❌ DANGER: if body contains "<img src=x onerror=stealCookies()>", it runs.
<div dangerouslySetInnerHTML={{ __html: post.body }} />

// ✅ Safe: React escapes this. A user typing "<script>" sees literal text.
<p>{post.body}</p>

If you genuinely must render user-authored rich text (e.g. a Markdown bio), sanitize first with a library like isomorphic-dompurify, allowlisting tags — never trust raw input.

6. Rate limiting in proxy.ts

We protect the auth routes with a sliding-window limiter (Upstash Redis here; any shared store works). Putting it in proxy.ts means it runs before the request ever reaches your action.

// proxy.ts (project root) — runs before matched routes
import { NextResponse, type NextRequest } from "next/server";
import { Ratelimit } from "@upstash/ratelimit";
import { Redis } from "@upstash/redis";

const ratelimit = new Ratelimit({
  redis: Redis.fromEnv(), // UPSTASH_REDIS_REST_URL / _TOKEN (server-only)
  limiter: Ratelimit.slidingWindow(5, "60 s"), // 5 attempts / minute / IP
  prefix: "viby:auth",
});

export async function proxy(request: NextRequest) {
  const ip =
    request.headers.get("x-forwarded-for")?.split(",")[0]?.trim() ?? "anon";
  const { success, limit, remaining, reset } = await ratelimit.limit(ip);

  if (!success) {
    return NextResponse.json(
      { error: "Too many attempts. Slow down a sec." },
      {
        status: 429,
        headers: {
          "Retry-After": String(Math.ceil((reset - Date.now()) / 1000)),
          "X-RateLimit-Remaining": String(remaining),
        },
      }
    );
  }
  return NextResponse.next();
}

export const config = {
  matcher: ["/login", "/signup", "/api/auth/:path*"],
};

IP-based limits are a first line of defense, not a fortress (IPs are shared and spoofable on some hops). For sensitive actions, also limit per authenticated user.id.

Architecture / diagram

flowchart TD
    A[Browser / Untrusted] -->|request + cookies| B[proxy.ts<br/>rate limit + origin]
    B -->|429 if over limit| A
    B --> C[Server Component / Server Action<br/>'use server']
    C --> D{Zod validation}
    D -->|invalid| E[Reject 4xx]
    D -->|valid| F[supabase.auth.getUser<br/>trusted identity]
    F --> G[(Postgres + RLS)]
    G -->|policy: author_id = auth.uid| H{Allowed?}
    H -->|no| I[Row denied by DB]
    H -->|yes| J[Data returned]

    subgraph TRUST["Server / Trusted boundary"]
        B
        C
        D
        F
        G
    end

The takeaway the diagram encodes: a request passes through three independent gates — rate limit, validation, and RLS. Each is enforced server-side, so a bypass of one is still caught by the next.

Best practices & pitfalls

Do

  • Enable RLS on every table exposed to the anon/authenticated roles, before launch.
  • Derive identity from auth.getUser() server-side; never accept user_id from the body.
  • Validate every Server Action input with a strict schema (.max(), .enum(), no passthrough).
  • Keep the service-role key server-only; construct admin clients in lib/ files imported solely by server code.
  • Rate-limit auth, messaging, and upload endpoints — by IP and by user id for sensitive ones.

Don't

  • Don't rely on hidden/disabled UI, client-side role checks, or Zustand state for authorization.
  • Don't use getSession() for trust decisions — it reads spoofable cookies.
  • Don't dangerouslySetInnerHTML raw user content. Sanitize, or render as text.
  • Don't enable RLS and forget the policies — you'll lock yourself out (default-deny). And don't write a using (true) policy "to fix it."
  • Don't put any secret behind a NEXT_PUBLIC_ prefix; it ships to the browser.

Exercises

  1. Lock down profiles. Add RLS policies to profiles: anyone authenticated can select any profile, but only the owner can update their own row. Verify by trying to update another user's row from the SQL editor with a different auth.uid().
  2. Schema-harden a Server Action. Take the Viby "edit bio" action and add a Zod schema capping the bio at 280 chars and stripping a sneaky injected role field. Confirm extra fields are ignored.
  3. Find the leak. Given a Route Handler that builds its Supabase client with SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY, explain in two sentences why RLS no longer protects it, then refactor it to use the anon key + user session.
  4. Per-user limiting. Extend the limiter so sending a DM is capped at 20 messages/minute per authenticated user id, not per IP. (Hint: build the limiter key from user.id.)
  5. (Stretch) Sanitize rich text. Allow Markdown bios: render them, but pipe the HTML through isomorphic-dompurify with an allowlist of b, i, em, strong, a, p, br. Prove that <script> and onerror= are stripped.

Mini-project — "Vibe Reports": a secure abuse-report flow

Build a feature letting users report a post. It must be safe end to end:

  1. Table + RLS. Create reports (id, post_id, reporter_id, reason, created_at). Policy: a user may insert only with reporter_id = (select auth.uid()), and may select only their own reports. No update/delete policies (reports are immutable to users).
  2. Server Action. submitReport(formData) validates reason with Zod (z.enum(["spam","harassment","other"]) plus an optional note capped at 300 chars), derives reporter_id from getUser(), and inserts.
  3. Rate limit. In proxy.ts, cap report submissions to 10/hour per user id, returning a friendly 429.
  4. UI. A "use client" modal (Framer Motion AnimatePresence for the fade/scale) with a reason dropdown. On success, animate a pink ✓ and a "Thanks, our team will review this" toast. Render the confirmation text with {} — never dangerouslySetInnerHTML.
  5. Admin view (optional). A server-only page using supabaseAdmin (service role) that lists all reports — demonstrating the one legitimate place the master key belongs, and why that page must itself check for an admin role.

Ship it and try to break it: open DevTools, change the reporter_id in the network payload, and confirm RLS rejects the write.

Key takeaways

  • The client is never a security boundary — enforce on the server, close to the data.
  • RLS is your real authorization layer: a default-deny lock the database evaluates on every query.
  • Derive identity from getUser(); never trust user_id/role sent from the browser.
  • Validate every input with a strict schema; the service-role key bypasses RLS and stays server-only.
  • React escapes by default (beware dangerouslySetInnerHTML); Server Actions blunt CSRF; rate limit to cap abuse.

Up next — Chapter 31: Realtime & Presence, where we'll wire up Supabase Realtime so Viby feeds, typing indicators, and "who's online" update live — securely, with RLS still standing guard over every subscription.

Chapter 31 — Testing, Debugging & Error Handling

Shipping a feature is the easy part. Shipping a feature that stays shipped — that survives refactors, weird inputs, and 2 a.m. production traffic — is what separates a hobbyist from a professional. This chapter is where Viby grows a spine.

What you'll learn

  • How to debug the App Router like a surgeon: where code actually runs (server vs. client), and how to read the right logs
  • How error boundaries (error.tsx, global-error.tsx) and loading boundaries (loading.tsx, Suspense) catch failures and stalls gracefully
  • What a hydration mismatch really is — and the exact bug Viby's TimeAgo component hit, plus how we fixed it
  • A realistic testing pyramid: unit (Vitest), integration (Testing Library), and end-to-end (Playwright) — and which to use when
  • Structured logging that survives the leap from console.log to production

Prerequisites

  • Chapter 6 (Next.js App Router fundamentals) and Chapter 8 (Server vs. Client Components) — non-negotiable here
  • Chapter 16 (Auth, sessions, RLS) and Chapter 17 (Server Actions) — we'll test and harden them
  • Chapter 24 (Activity Feed) — the TimeAgo and PostCard components live here
  • Comfort running terminal commands and reading a stack trace

Theory

Where does my code even run?

The single biggest source of App Router confusion is forgetting that your code runs in two different universes. A Server Component executes once, on a machine in a data center, and streams HTML to the browser. A Client Component ("use client") executes there too (for the initial HTML), and then again in the browser to become interactive.

Think of it like a theater production. The Server Component is the script and the printed program — written once, handed to the audience. The Client Component is the live actor — they perform from that same script, but they breathe, move, and react. Debugging means first asking: am I looking at the script or the actor? A console.log in a Server Component prints to your terminal. The same log in a Client Component prints to the browser console. Confuse the two and you'll stare at an empty console for an hour.

Errors and the boundary metaphor

A React app is a tree. When a component throws during render, React unwinds the whole subtree above it — by default, all the way to a blank white screen. An error boundary is a circuit breaker: it stops the cascade at a known point and shows a fallback instead of taking down the building. The App Router gives you this declaratively. Drop an error.tsx into a route folder and Next wraps that segment in a boundary automatically. One bad post in the feed no longer nukes the whole tab bar.

Loading boundaries are the same idea for time instead of failure. When a Server Component awaits slow data, a loading.tsx (which is just a Suspense fallback) shows instantly while the real content streams in behind it. The mental model: error boundaries catch what broke, Suspense boundaries cover what's not ready yet.

Hydration: the script must match the performance

Hydration is React attaching event listeners to server-rendered HTML. For it to work, the HTML React produces on the client during the first render must byte-for-byte match what the server sent. If they differ, React panics — it can't tell which version is "right" — and you get the dreaded "Hydration failed because the server rendered HTML didn't match the client."

The classic trigger is anything non-deterministic: Date.now(), Math.random(), window, locale-dependent formatting, or reading localStorage during render. The server renders at 10:00:00 and says "2m ago"; the client hydrates at 10:00:01 and computes "3m ago" — mismatch. We'll see Viby's real version of this below.

The testing pyramid

You can't manually click every flow on every commit. Tests do it for you. The industry model is a pyramid: a wide base of fast unit tests (pure functions, one thing each), a thinner band of integration tests (a component plus its real children), and a few precious end-to-end tests (a real browser walking a full user journey). The ratio matters: e2e tests are slow and flaky, so you want few but high-value ones — the signup → onboarding → first connection path, not every button.

In practice

We're on Next.js 16, React 19, Tailwind v4, Supabase via @supabase/ssr. All code below targets that stack.

1. The hydration bug Viby actually hit

Early on, the feed rendered relative timestamps with a naive component:

// ❌ The buggy version — DO NOT ship this
export function TimeAgo({ iso }: { iso: string }) {
  return <span>{timeAgo(iso)}</span>; // timeAgo() calls Date.now() internally
}

timeAgo uses Date.now(). The server computed the label at request time; the browser recomputed it milliseconds (or minutes, behind a CDN cache) later. Result: a console full of hydration errors on every feed load. Here's the fix we actually shipped (components/common/TimeAgo.tsx):

"use client";

import { useEffect, useState } from "react";
import { timeAgo } from "@/lib/utils";

/**
 * Relative timestamps depend on the current time, which differs between the
 * server render and client hydration — a guaranteed mismatch. We render the
 * initial value with suppressHydrationWarning and refresh it on the client.
 */
export function TimeAgo({ iso, className }: { iso: string; className?: string }) {
  const [label, setLabel] = useState(() => timeAgo(iso));

  useEffect(() => {
    setLabel(timeAgo(iso));
    const id = setInterval(() => setLabel(timeAgo(iso)), 60_000);
    return () => clearInterval(id);
  }, [iso]);

  return (
    <span className={className} suppressHydrationWarning>
      {label}
    </span>
  );
}

Two things make this correct:

  1. suppressHydrationWarning tells React, for this element only, "I know the text may differ across server and client — don't warn." This is the sanctioned escape hatch, not a hack. Use it surgically on the element whose content is intentionally time-dependent.
  2. The useEffect runs only on the client, after hydration, so it can freely call Date.now() and even tick every 60 seconds without ever causing a mismatch.

The general escape hatch for "client-only" values is the mounted flag:

const [mounted, setMounted] = useState(false);
useEffect(() => setMounted(true), []);
if (!mounted) return null; // or a stable skeleton

Use suppressHydrationWarning when the shape matches but the text differs (timestamps); use the mounted flag when the element shouldn't render server-side at all (e.g. something reading localStorage).

2. Error boundaries per route segment

error.tsx must be a Client Component — it receives the error and a reset() function to retry the segment. Here's one for the feed (app/(app)/feed/error.tsx):

"use client";

import { useEffect } from "react";
import { Button } from "@/components/ui/button";

export default function FeedError({
  error,
  reset,
}: {
  error: Error & { digest?: string };
  reset: () => void;
}) {
  useEffect(() => {
    // Send to your logger; `digest` correlates with the server-side stack.
    console.error("[feed] render error", error.digest, error.message);
  }, [error]);

  return (
    <div className="flex flex-col items-center gap-4 py-20 text-center">
      <p className="text-2xl">😵‍💫</p>
      <p className="text-sm text-neutral-500">
        Your feed hit a snag. It&apos;s not you, it&apos;s us.
      </p>
      <Button onClick={reset}>Try again</Button>
    </div>
  );
}

error.tsx only catches errors below it in the tree, and not in the layout at the same level. To catch a crash in the root layout itself, use app/global-error.tsx (it must render its own <html> and <body>):

"use client";

export default function GlobalError({ reset }: { reset: () => void }) {
  return (
    <html lang="en">
      <body className="grid min-h-dvh place-items-center bg-white">
        <button onClick={reset} className="text-[#FF6FB5] underline">
          Reload Viby
        </button>
      </body>
    </html>
  );
}

3. Loading boundaries with Suspense

A loading.tsx is the zero-config Suspense fallback for the whole segment. For the feed, show skeleton cards in Viby's pink-on-white aesthetic (app/(app)/feed/loading.tsx):

export default function FeedLoading() {
  return (
    <div className="space-y-4 p-4">
      {Array.from({ length: 4 }).map((_, i) => (
        <div key={i} className="animate-pulse rounded-2xl bg-neutral-100 p-4">
          <div className="mb-3 h-4 w-1/3 rounded bg-neutral-200" />
          <div className="h-24 rounded-xl bg-neutral-200" />
        </div>
      ))}
    </div>
  );
}

For finer control — say, streaming the feed while the profile header renders instantly — wrap just the slow part in <Suspense>:

import { Suspense } from "react";

export default async function FeedPage() {
  return (
    <>
      <FeedHeader /> {/* instant */}
      <Suspense fallback={<FeedLoading />}>
        <FeedStream /> {/* async Server Component, streams in */}
      </Suspense>
    </>
  );
}

4. Defensive Server Actions

Server Actions are public HTTP endpoints in disguise — never trust the input. Notice how Viby's saveProfile already returns a typed result instead of throwing, so the client can show a friendly toast (app/actions/profile.ts):

"use server";

import { createClient } from "@/lib/supabase/server";

export async function saveProfile(input: ProfileEdit) {
  const supabase = await createClient();
  const {
    data: { user },
  } = await supabase.auth.getUser();
  if (!user) return { ok: false as const, error: "Not signed in" };

  const { error } = await supabase
    .from("profiles")
    .update({ display_name: input.displayName /* ...rest */ })
    .eq("id", user.id);

  if (error) return { ok: false as const, error: error.message };
  return { ok: true as const };
}

The pattern — return a discriminated union ({ ok: true } | { ok: false, error }) rather than throwing — means the error boundary is your last line of defense, not your first. Predictable failures get handled inline; only the truly unexpected ones bubble up to error.tsx.

5. Unit tests with Vitest

Install the toolchain:

npm i -D vitest @vitejs/plugin-react jsdom \
  @testing-library/react @testing-library/jest-dom @testing-library/user-event

// vitest.config.ts
import { defineConfig } from "vitest/config";
import react from "@vitejs/plugin-react";
import { resolve } from "node:path";

export default defineConfig({
  plugins: [react()],
  test: { environment: "jsdom", globals: true, setupFiles: ["./vitest.setup.ts"] },
  resolve: { alias: { "@": resolve(__dirname, ".") } },
});

// vitest.setup.ts
import "@testing-library/jest-dom/vitest";

Pure functions are the cheapest, highest-leverage tests. Lock down timeAgo (lib/utils.test.ts):

import { describe, it, expect, vi, afterEach } from "vitest";
import { timeAgo } from "@/lib/utils";

describe("timeAgo", () => {
  afterEach(() => vi.useRealTimers());

  it("says 'just now' under a minute", () => {
    vi.setSystemTime(new Date("2026-05-30T12:00:00Z"));
    expect(timeAgo("2026-05-30T11:59:30Z")).toBe("just now");
  });

  it("formats hours and days", () => {
    vi.setSystemTime(new Date("2026-05-30T12:00:00Z"));
    expect(timeAgo("2026-05-30T09:00:00Z")).toBe("3h");
    expect(timeAgo("2026-05-28T12:00:00Z")).toBe("2d");
  });
});
