vi.setSystemTime freezes Date.now(), making a time-dependent function deterministic — the same trick that prevents the hydration class of bug also makes it testable.

6. Integration test of a Client Component

Render PostCard with its real children and assert behavior (components/feed/PostCard.test.tsx):

import { render, screen } from "@testing-library/react";
import userEvent from "@testing-library/user-event";
import { PostCard } from "@/components/feed/PostCard";

it("toggles the like count when tapped", async () => {
  const post = { id: "p1", author: "Mia", body: "anyone climb?", likes: 3, createdAt: new Date().toISOString() };
  render(<PostCard post={post} />);

  await userEvent.click(screen.getByRole("button", { name: /like/i }));
  expect(screen.getByText("4")).toBeInTheDocument();
});

Test what the user sees (getByRole, getByText), never internal state. If a refactor keeps the behavior, the test keeps passing — that's the whole point.

7. End-to-end with Playwright

npm i -D @playwright/test && npx playwright install

// e2e/connect.spec.ts
import { test, expect } from "@playwright/test";

test("a new user discovers and connects with someone", async ({ page }) => {
  await page.goto("/discover");
  await expect(page.getByRole("heading", { name: /discover/i })).toBeVisible();

  await page.getByRole("button", { name: /connect/i }).first().click();
  await expect(page.getByText(/request sent/i)).toBeVisible();
});

One golden-path e2e test per critical journey. They're slow — run them in CI and before release, not on every save.

8. Structured logging

console.log is fine in dev but useless in production (no context, no levels, no correlation). Wrap it once so you can swap in a real sink (Vercel Logs, Sentry, Axiom) later without touching call sites (lib/logger.ts):

type Level = "debug" | "info" | "warn" | "error";

function log(level: Level, msg: string, meta?: Record<string, unknown>) {
  const entry = { level, msg, at: new Date().toISOString(), ...meta };
  // In prod, JSON lines are machine-parseable; in dev, readable.
  console[level === "debug" ? "log" : level](
    process.env.NODE_ENV === "production" ? JSON.stringify(entry) : `[${level}] ${msg}`,
    process.env.NODE_ENV === "production" ? "" : meta ?? "",
  );
}

export const logger = {
  info: (m: string, meta?: Record<string, unknown>) => log("info", m, meta),
  warn: (m: string, meta?: Record<string, unknown>) => log("warn", m, meta),
  error: (m: string, meta?: Record<string, unknown>) => log("error", m, meta),
};

Now logger.error("saveProfile failed", { userId: user.id, error: error.message }) gives you searchable, structured events instead of a lonely string.

Architecture / diagram

How a request flows through Viby's safety nets — from proxy auth refresh down to the boundaries that catch trouble:

flowchart TD
  A[Request] --> B[proxy.ts: refresh Supabase session]
  B --> C[Layout renders]
  C --> D{Suspense boundary}
  D -- data not ready --> E[loading.tsx skeleton]
  D -- data ready --> F[Server Component renders]
  F -->|throws| G[error.tsx fallback + reset]
  F -->|ok| H[HTML streamed to browser]
  H --> I[Hydration: client re-renders]
  I -- text matches --> J[Interactive ✅]
  I -- mismatch --> K[Hydration error ⚠️]
  K -. fix .-> L[suppressHydrationWarning / useEffect / mounted flag]
  L --> J

Best practices & pitfalls

  • Do ask "server or client?" first when debugging — check the terminal for server logs, the browser console for client logs. Most lost hours are looking in the wrong place.
  • Do return typed { ok } results from Server Actions; reserve throw for the genuinely exceptional. Boundaries are a safety net, not flow control.
  • Don't slap suppressHydrationWarning on whole subtrees to silence errors — that hides real bugs. Apply it to the single element whose content is intentionally non-deterministic.
  • Don't read window, localStorage, or Date.now() during render in a component that runs on the server. Move it into useEffect.
  • Do put error.tsx and loading.tsx at meaningful segments (feed, chat, profile) so one failure doesn't blank the whole shell — but remember error.tsx can't catch its sibling layout.tsx.
  • Don't test implementation details (state, props, internals). Test what the user sees and does.
  • Do keep e2e tests few and golden-path; lean on unit and integration for coverage. An inverted pyramid (lots of e2e) is slow and flaky.
  • Don't ship console.log. Route everything through one logger so production has structure and you have one place to add a real sink.

Exercises

  1. Reproduce the bug. Temporarily replace TimeAgo with the naive <span>{timeAgo(iso)}</span> version, load /feed, and read the hydration error in the console. Then revert and confirm it's gone. Understanding the failure cements the fix.
  2. Add a chat error boundary. Create app/(app)/chat/[id]/error.tsx with a "Couldn't load this conversation" fallback and a working reset() button styled in brand pink.
  3. Cover the matching logic. Write a Vitest suite for lib/matching.ts (the discover algorithm from Chapter 22). Include an edge case: two profiles with zero shared interests should score 0, not NaN.
  4. Stream the feed. Wrap the async feed list in <Suspense> with the skeleton fallback, and prove the header paints before the posts by throttling the network in DevTools.
  5. Harden a Server Action. Add input validation (reject empty displayName, cap bio length) to saveProfile and write an integration test asserting it returns { ok: false } for bad input.

Mini-project — A typed <ErrorState> + tested logger pipeline

Build a small, reusable resilience kit for Viby:

  1. Create components/common/ErrorState.tsx — a Client Component taking { title, message, onRetry }, styled with the bubblegum-pink retry button and a friendly emoji. Use it as the body of both app/(app)/feed/error.tsx and app/(app)/chat/[id]/error.tsx so every boundary looks consistent.
  2. Wire lib/logger.ts (from §8) into each error.tsx's useEffect, logging error.digest plus the segment name.
  3. Add a Vitest test for logger that spies on console.error (via vi.spyOn) and asserts that logger.error("boom", { userId: "u1" }) produces valid JSON containing userId when NODE_ENV === "production".
  4. Add one Playwright test that forces the feed action to fail (e.g. point it at a bad route) and asserts your ErrorState title appears and the retry button is clickable.

When you're done, you'll have a failure path that's designed, consistent, logged, and tested — the exact difference between a demo and a product.

Key takeaways

  • App Router code runs in two places; always determine server vs. client before debugging, and look in the matching console.
  • error.tsx / global-error.tsx are circuit breakers; loading.tsx / Suspense cover not-yet-ready data. Place them at meaningful segments.
  • Hydration mismatches come from non-deterministic render values. Viby's TimeAgo fix — suppressHydrationWarning plus a client-side useEffect — is the canonical pattern.
  • Favor the testing pyramid: many fast unit tests, fewer integration tests, a handful of golden-path e2e tests. Freeze time with vi.setSystemTime to make time-dependent code deterministic.
  • Server Actions should return typed results, not throw, and everything should flow through one structured logger.

Up next — Chapter 32: Performance & Optimization, where we make Viby not just correct, but fast: bundle splitting, image optimization, caching, and squeezing the App Router for buttery-smooth Gen-Z scroll.

Chapter 32 — Environment, Config & Deploying to Vercel

Your app finally leaves your laptop and meets the world — let's make that moment boring, repeatable, and safe.

You've built Viby. It runs beautifully at localhost:3000. But a social-discovery app that only you can open isn't a product — it's a rehearsal. This chapter is the dress rehearsal becoming opening night: we'll wire up configuration the professional way, produce a real production build, and ship Viby to Vercel with both preview and production environments plus a custom domain.

What you'll learn

  • The difference between public and server-only environment variables, and why mixing them up leaks secrets
  • How .env.local, .env, and Vercel's environment system fit together
  • How to run and read a real production build (next build) and what changes vs. dev
  • Deploying Viby to Vercel two ways: the CLI and GitHub integration
  • How preview deployments differ from production, and how to use them in a team
  • Attaching a custom domain with HTTPS

Prerequisites

  • Chapter 8 (Next.js App Router & Server Components) — you'll lean on the server/client boundary here
  • Chapter 19 (Supabase Auth with @supabase/ssr) — we configure its keys for production
  • Chapter 27 (proxy.ts for edge logic) — we'll confirm it ships correctly
  • A GitHub account and the Vercel free (Hobby) tier — both free

Theory

Why environment variables exist

Imagine your app's recipe (the code) and its ingredients list for this kitchen (the configuration). The recipe is identical whether you cook at home or in a restaurant. The ingredients — which Supabase project, which database URL, which API keys — change per kitchen. Environment variables are how we keep the recipe fixed while swapping ingredients between your laptop, a teammate's laptop, a preview deploy, and production.

Hard-coding your Supabase keys into a .ts file is like writing your home address on every copy of the recipe and then handing the recipe to strangers. Config belongs outside the code.

The one rule that prevents most disasters: public vs. server

This is the single most important idea in the chapter, so let's be precise.

In Next.js, an environment variable is exposed to the browser only if its name begins with NEXT_PUBLIC_. Everything else stays server-side and is never shipped in the JavaScript bundle.

  • NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL → safe to expose. The browser needs it to talk to Supabase.
  • NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY → safe to expose. It's the anonymous key, designed for the client and protected by Row-Level Security (you set this up in Chapter 20).
  • SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY → never prefix this with NEXT_PUBLIC_. It bypasses RLS and can read/write any row. If it lands in the browser bundle, anyone can pull your entire users table.

The analogy: NEXT_PUBLIC_ is the lobby of an apartment building — public, fine for strangers to see. Everything without the prefix is behind the locked front door of an apartment. The prefix is the only thing deciding which side of that door a value lives on. Get it wrong and you either break the app (a secret the browser needed wasn't exposed) or leak a master key.

A subtle gotcha: NEXT_PUBLIC_ values are inlined at build time. They're baked into the bundle when next build runs, not read fresh at runtime. Change one, and you must rebuild.

The .env file family

Next.js loads several files automatically. Order of precedence (highest wins):

.env.local        ← your machine's secrets, NEVER committed
.env.production    ← committed, non-secret production defaults
.env.development   ← committed, non-secret dev defaults
.env               ← committed, shared non-secret defaults

The golden rule: .env.local is git-ignored and holds anything secret. Everything committed should be safe for the whole world to read.

In practice

1. Define Viby's local environment

Create .env.local at the project root:

# .env.local — git-ignored, never commit this file

# Public: shipped to the browser, protected by RLS
NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL="https://abcdefgh.supabase.co"
NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY="eyJhbGciOiJIUzI1NiIs..."
NEXT_PUBLIC_SITE_URL="http://localhost:3000"

# Server-only: NEVER prefixed with NEXT_PUBLIC_
SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY="eyJhbGciOiJIUzI1NiIs...service..."

Confirm .gitignore already ignores it (Next.js scaffolds this for you):

# .gitignore
.env*.local

2. Validate config at startup (fail loud, fail early)

A missing variable in production is a 3am pager incident. Catch it the moment the server boots instead. Create a typed, validated config module:

// lib/env.ts
import { z } from "zod";

const serverSchema = z.object({
  SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY: z.string().min(1),
});

const publicSchema = z.object({
  NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL: z.string().url(),
  NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY: z.string().min(1),
  NEXT_PUBLIC_SITE_URL: z.string().url(),
});

// Public vars must be referenced statically so Next.js can inline them.
export const publicEnv = publicSchema.parse({
  NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL: process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL,
  NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY: process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY,
  NEXT_PUBLIC_SITE_URL: process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SITE_URL,
});

// Only parse server vars on the server — guard so the browser never touches them.
export const serverEnv =
  typeof window === "undefined"
    ? serverSchema.parse({
        SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY: process.env.SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY,
      })
    : ({} as z.infer<typeof serverSchema>);

Now your Supabase browser client (from Chapter 19) reads from validated config:

// lib/supabase/client.ts
import { createBrowserClient } from "@supabase/ssr";
import { publicEnv } from "@/lib/env";

export function createClient() {
  return createBrowserClient(
    publicEnv.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL,
    publicEnv.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY,
  );
}

And the server client uses getAll/setAll cookie handlers — the only correct pattern with @supabase/ssr:

// lib/supabase/server.ts
import { createServerClient } from "@supabase/ssr";
import { cookies } from "next/headers";
import { publicEnv } from "@/lib/env";

export async function createClient() {
  const cookieStore = await cookies(); // async in Next.js 16

  return createServerClient(
    publicEnv.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL,
    publicEnv.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY,
    {
      cookies: {
        getAll() {
          return cookieStore.getAll();
        },
        setAll(cookiesToSet) {
          try {
            cookiesToSet.forEach(({ name, value, options }) =>
              cookieStore.set(name, value, options),
            );
          } catch {
            // Called from a Server Component — safe to ignore; proxy.ts refreshes.
          }
        },
      },
    },
  );
}

The service-role key only ever appears in trusted server code — for example a Server Action that needs to bypass RLS for an admin task:

// app/admin/actions.ts
"use server";

import { createClient as createAdminClient } from "@supabase/supabase-js";
import { publicEnv, serverEnv } from "@/lib/env";

export async function banUser(userId: string) {
  // service_role bypasses RLS — server-only, never in a "use client" file
  const admin = createAdminClient(
    publicEnv.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL,
    serverEnv.SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY,
  );

  const { error } = await admin
    .from("profiles")
    .update({ banned: true })
    .eq("id", userId);

  if (error) throw new Error("Could not ban user");
}

3. Run a real production build

Dev mode (next dev) is forgiving: hot reload, unminified, extra warnings. Production is the truth. Run it locally before you ever deploy:

npm run build   # runs `next build`
npm run start   # serves the production build on :3000

next build type-checks, lints, statically pre-renders what it can, and inlines every NEXT_PUBLIC_ value. If a public env var is missing, the build is where you'll find out — exactly what you want. Read the output: it tells you which routes are static (○), dynamic (ƒ), and how big each bundle is. A surprise dynamic route often means you accidentally read a cookie or header in a page you expected to be static.

4. Deploy via the Vercel CLI

The fastest path from laptop to live URL:

npm i -g vercel
vercel login

# First run links the folder to a Vercel project (answer the prompts)
vercel              # creates a PREVIEW deployment

vercel --prod       # promotes to PRODUCTION

Push your environment variables to Vercel so the cloud build has them. You can do this per environment:

vercel env add SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY production
vercel env add NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL production
# ...repeat for preview and development as needed

# Pull cloud env vars down into a local .env.local for parity:
vercel env pull .env.local

5. Deploy via GitHub (the way real teams ship)

The CLI is great for solo experiments, but the professional default is git-based deploys. Connect once and every push becomes a deployment:

  1. Push Viby to a GitHub repo.
  2. In the Vercel dashboard: Add New → Project → Import your repo.
  3. Vercel auto-detects Next.js — no build config needed.
  4. Add your environment variables in Settings → Environment Variables, choosing the Production, Preview, and Development scopes per variable.
  5. Deploy.

From now on:

  • Push to main → Production deployment.
  • Open a Pull Request → unique Preview deployment with its own URL.

Vercel even comments the preview URL on the PR so reviewers can click and test the actual running app.

Architecture / diagram

flowchart TD
    Dev["Developer laptop\n.env.local (secrets)"] -->|git push| GH["GitHub repo"]

    GH -->|push to feature branch / PR| PrevBuild["Vercel Build\n(Preview env vars)"]
    GH -->|push to main| ProdBuild["Vercel Build\n(Production env vars)"]

    PrevBuild --> PrevURL["viby-git-feat-x.vercel.app\nephemeral preview"]
    ProdBuild --> ProdURL["viby.app\nproduction + custom domain"]

    subgraph Build["Inside every build"]
      direction LR
      Inline["NEXT_PUBLIC_* inlined\ninto browser bundle"]
      Secret["server-only vars kept\nin Node runtime only"]
    end

    PrevBuild -.-> Build
    ProdBuild -.-> Build

    ProdURL --> Supabase[("Supabase\nAuth · Postgres · Storage")]
    PrevURL --> Supabase

The key insight the diagram encodes: the same code flows down both paths; only the environment variables injected at build time differ. Public vars get inlined into the bundle; secrets stay in the Node runtime and never reach the browser.

Best practices & pitfalls

  • Do keep .env.local git-ignored. Don't ever commit a service-role key — if you do, rotate it immediately in the Supabase dashboard.
  • Do prefix browser-needed values with NEXT_PUBLIC_. Don't prefix secrets with it "just to make the error go away."
  • Do set NEXT_PUBLIC_SITE_URL per environment so OAuth redirects and email links point to the right host. A preview deploy redirecting to localhost is a classic broken-auth bug.
  • Do run npm run build locally before pushing. The cloud build will fail on the same TypeScript and lint errors — find them on your machine first.
  • Don't assume changing a NEXT_PUBLIC_ value in the dashboard updates the live site. It's inlined at build time — you must redeploy.
  • Do add your production and preview domains to Supabase → Authentication → URL Configuration (Site URL + Redirect URLs). Auth silently fails otherwise.
  • Don't read secrets in a "use client" component or pass them as props from a Server Component — props are serialized to the browser.
  • Do trust proxy.ts to deploy automatically; Vercel runs it at the edge with no extra config. Don't rename it to middleware.ts — Next.js 16's convention here is proxy.ts.
  • Do use Preview deployments as your QA environment for every PR. Don't test risky changes by pushing straight to main.

Exercises

  1. Audit the boundary. Add a console.log(process.env.SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY) inside a "use client" component, run npm run build && npm run start, open the browser, and confirm it logs undefined. Then move it to a Server Action and confirm it's present on the server. Delete both logs when done.

  2. Break the build on purpose. Remove NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL from .env.local and run npm run build. Read the Zod error from lib/env.ts. Restore it. You've just proven your fail-loud guard works.

  3. Two environments, two values. In the Vercel dashboard, set NEXT_PUBLIC_SITE_URL to different values for Preview and Production. Deploy both and confirm a page that renders publicEnv.NEXT_PUBLIC_SITE_URL shows the correct host in each.

  4. Ship a preview from a PR. Create a branch, change Viby's hero headline, open a PR, and click the Vercel preview link in the PR comment to see your change live before merging.

  5. (Stretch) Custom domain. Buy or reuse a domain, add it under Vercel → Settings → Domains, set the DNS records Vercel shows you, and confirm HTTPS provisions automatically. Update NEXT_PUBLIC_SITE_URL and the Supabase redirect URLs to match.

Mini-project — A "Build Info" badge for Viby

Ship a tiny diagnostics widget that proves your environment wiring is correct in every deployment. It shows which environment the running build belongs to and the git commit it was built from — invaluable when a teammate asks "is the fix live yet?"

Vercel injects system environment variables automatically — including VERCEL_ENV (production | preview | development) and VERCEL_GIT_COMMIT_SHA. Expose just what the badge needs by re-mapping them to public vars in next.config.ts:

// next.config.ts
import type { NextConfig } from "next";

const nextConfig: NextConfig = {
  env: {
    NEXT_PUBLIC_VERCEL_ENV: process.env.VERCEL_ENV ?? "development",
    NEXT_PUBLIC_COMMIT_SHA: process.env.VERCEL_GIT_COMMIT_SHA ?? "local",
  },
};

export default nextConfig;

Then build the badge as a client component with a subtle Framer Motion entrance:

// components/build-badge.tsx
"use client";

import { motion, AnimatePresence } from "framer-motion";
import { useState } from "react";

const env = process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_VERCEL_ENV;
const sha = (process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_COMMIT_SHA ?? "local").slice(0, 7);

export function BuildBadge() {
  const [open, setOpen] = useState(true);

  // Hide in production so end users never see it.
  if (env === "production") return null;

  return (
    <AnimatePresence>
      {open && (
        <motion.button
          onClick={() => setOpen(false)}
          initial={{ opacity: 0, y: 12 }}
          animate={{ opacity: 1, y: 0 }}
          exit={{ opacity: 0, y: 12 }}
          whileTap={{ scale: 0.95 }}
          className="fixed bottom-4 left-4 z-50 rounded-full bg-[--color-brand] px-3 py-1.5 text-xs font-semibold text-white shadow-lg"
        >
          {env} · {sha}
        </motion.button>
      )}
    </AnimatePresence>
  );
}

Define the brand token in your Tailwind v4 theme (no tailwind.config.js):

/* app/globals.css */
@import "tailwindcss";

@theme {
  --color-brand: #ff6fb5;
}

Drop it into your root layout so it appears on previews and locally, but auto-hides in production:

// app/layout.tsx
import { BuildBadge } from "@/components/build-badge";

export default function RootLayout({
  children,
}: {
  children: React.ReactNode;
}) {
  return (
    <html lang="en">
      <body>
        {children}
        <BuildBadge />
      </body>
    </html>
  );
}

Open a PR, click the preview URL, and you'll see a bubblegum-pink badge reading preview · a1b2c3d. Merge to main, visit production, and it's gone. You've now got a visible, deploy-aware signal — and proof that your public/server boundary and environment scoping all work end to end.

Key takeaways

  • Config lives outside code in environment variables; the recipe stays fixed while ingredients change per environment.
  • NEXT_PUBLIC_ is the only switch deciding browser vs. server exposure — and public values are inlined at build time, so changing them means redeploying.
  • .env.local is git-ignored and holds secrets; everything committed must be safe for the world.
  • Validate env vars at startup so missing config fails the build, not production at 3am.
  • Vercel ships the same code to preview and production, differing only by injected env vars; PRs get free preview URLs and main is production.
  • Add your deploy domains to Supabase's auth URL config, and remember proxy.ts deploys to the edge automatically.

Up next — Chapter 33: Observability & Error Tracking, where we wire Viby up with logging, performance monitoring, and Sentry so you know when something breaks before your users tell you.

Chapter 33 — Supabase in Production

Your localhost Supabase has been a cozy sandbox — now we wire Viby to a real, hardened backend that thousands of strangers will hammer at 2am.

What you'll learn

  • How to stand up a dedicated production Supabase project (separate from dev)
  • Shipping your schema with migrations instead of clicking around the dashboard
  • Creating Storage buckets and writing RLS-style storage policies for avatars and post photos
  • Configuring auth providers, the Site URL, and redirect allow-lists so OAuth and magic links land where they should
  • Why connection pooling matters on serverless (Vercel) and which connection string to use
  • Setting up backups and a sane disaster-recovery posture

Prerequisites

  • Chapter 9 (Supabase fundamentals & the @supabase/ssr clients)
  • Chapter 12 (Auth flows: OAuth, magic links, sessions)
  • Chapter 18 (Row Level Security and policies)
  • Chapter 24 (Storage: uploading avatars and post images)
  • Chapter 32 (Deploying Viby to Vercel and environment variables)

You should already have Viby running locally against a dev Supabase project. We're now creating its grown-up twin.

Theory

Think of your dev Supabase project as a kitchen at home: if you spill flour, nobody cares. Production is a restaurant kitchen during a Saturday dinner rush — health inspectors, paying customers, and zero tolerance for "oops I dropped the users table." The skills are the same; the discipline is not.

Three ideas separate hobby backends from production ones.

1. Environments are isolated, not shared. You never point dev and prod at the same database. A bad migration or a delete from profiles you ran "just to test" must never be able to touch real users. So we keep at least two Supabase projects: viby-dev and viby-prod. They have different URLs, different keys, different data. This is the single most important habit in this chapter.

2. Schema is code, not clicks. In dev it's tempting to add a column in the dashboard table editor. That works once. But production schema must be reproducible and reviewable — you want the exact same tables to exist whether you rebuild from scratch tomorrow or onboard a teammate next year. The answer is migrations: ordered SQL files, committed to git, applied in sequence. The dashboard is for looking, not changing.

3. Serverless changes how you connect. Traditional servers open a handful of long-lived database connections and reuse them. Vercel runs your code as functions that spin up and die constantly — each cold start could try to open a brand-new Postgres connection. Postgres has a hard connection limit (often ~60 on small plans), so a traffic spike can exhaust it and your app starts throwing too many connections. The fix is a connection pooler (Supabase ships PgBouncer/Supavisor in front of Postgres): your functions talk to the pooler, the pooler multiplexes a small set of real connections to the database. Analogy: instead of every customer getting their own dedicated waiter forever, a small pool of waiters serves everyone, table by table.

A subtlety that trips everyone up: for the Viby app's normal runtime requests we use the Supabase JS client over HTTP (PostgREST) — that goes through Supabase's API layer, not a raw Postgres socket, so pooling is handled for you. You only think hard about pooling/connection strings when you connect to Postgres directly (an ORM like Prisma/Drizzle, a migration tool, or a cron worker). Keep that distinction in your head.

In practice

1. Create the production project

In the Supabase dashboard: New project → name it viby-prod, pick a region close to your Vercel deployment region (latency between your functions and the DB is real), and set a strong database password (store it in your password manager — you'll need it for the direct connection string).

Once it's provisioning, grab three things from Project Settings → API:

  • Project URL → https://<ref>.supabase.co
  • anon public key
  • service_role secret key (server-only — never ships to the browser)

2. Wire Viby's environment variables

On Vercel (Chapter 32), set these for the Production environment only. Your .env.local keeps the dev project's values.

# .env.production (managed in Vercel → Settings → Environment Variables)
NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL=https://YOUR_PROD_REF.supabase.co
NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY=eyJhbGci...prod-anon...
SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY=eyJhbGci...prod-service-role...   # server only, NOT NEXT_PUBLIC

Our @supabase/ssr clients from Chapter 9 don't change — they read those env vars. As a reminder, the server client uses the modern getAll/setAll cookie handlers:

// lib/supabase/server.ts
import { createServerClient } from "@supabase/ssr";
import { cookies } from "next/headers";

export async function createClient() {
  const cookieStore = await cookies(); // Next.js 16: cookies() is async

  return createServerClient(
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL!,
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY!,
    {
      cookies: {
        getAll() {
          return cookieStore.getAll();
        },
        setAll(cookiesToSet) {
          try {
            cookiesToSet.forEach(({ name, value, options }) =>
              cookieStore.set(name, value, options)
            );
          } catch {
            // Called from a Server Component — safe to ignore; proxy refreshes the session.
          }
        },
      },
    }
  );
}

3. Ship the schema with migrations

Install and link the Supabase CLI, then keep all SQL in versioned files.

npm install -D supabase
npx supabase login

# Link your local repo to the PROD project (ref is in the dashboard URL)
npx supabase link --project-ref YOUR_PROD_REF

# Create a migration file
npx supabase migration new init_viby_schema

That creates supabase/migrations/<timestamp>_init_viby_schema.sql. Put real Viby schema in it:

-- supabase/migrations/20260530120000_init_viby_schema.sql

create table public.profiles (
  id uuid primary key references auth.users(id) on delete cascade,
  username text unique not null,
  display_name text,
  bio text,
  avatar_url text,
  hobbies text[] default '{}',
  created_at timestamptz not null default now()
);

create table public.posts (
  id uuid primary key default gen_random_uuid(),
  author_id uuid not null references public.profiles(id) on delete cascade,
  caption text,
  image_url text,
  created_at timestamptz not null default now()
);

-- RLS is OFF by default on new tables — turn it ON or your data is public.
alter table public.profiles enable row level security;
alter table public.posts enable row level security;

create policy "Profiles are viewable by everyone"
  on public.profiles for select using (true);

create policy "Users can update their own profile"
  on public.profiles for update using (auth.uid() = id);

create policy "Posts are viewable by everyone"
  on public.posts for select using (true);

create policy "Users can insert their own posts"
  on public.posts for insert with check (auth.uid() = author_id);

create policy "Users can delete their own posts"
  on public.posts for delete using (auth.uid() = author_id);

Apply it to production:

npx supabase db push

The CLI runs only migrations the prod DB hasn't seen yet, in order. From now on, every schema change is a new migration file in a PR — never a dashboard edit. To pull an out-of-band change someone made in the dashboard back into code, run npx supabase db pull and commit the generated migration.

4. Storage buckets & policies

Avatars and post images live in Storage. Create buckets and lock them down with policies — also as migration SQL so they're reproducible:

-- supabase/migrations/20260530121000_storage.sql

-- Public bucket for avatars (readable by anyone, writable only by the owner)
insert into storage.buckets (id, name, public)
values ('avatars', 'avatars', true)
on conflict (id) do nothing;

insert into storage.buckets (id, name, public)
values ('posts', 'posts', true)
on conflict (id) do nothing;

-- Anyone can read avatar files
create policy "Avatar images are publicly readable"
  on storage.objects for select
  using (bucket_id = 'avatars');

-- A user may only write into a folder named after their own uid: avatars/<uid>/...
create policy "Users upload their own avatar"
  on storage.objects for insert
  with check (
    bucket_id = 'avatars'
    and (storage.foldername(name))[1] = auth.uid()::text
  );

create policy "Users update their own avatar"
  on storage.objects for update
  using (
    bucket_id = 'avatars'
    and (storage.foldername(name))[1] = auth.uid()::text
  );

The folder-prefix trick (avatars/<uid>/avatar.png) is the standard pattern: the policy checks that the first path segment equals the caller's auth.uid(), so a user can never overwrite someone else's file.

Uploading from Viby's profile editor (a Server Action) looks like:

// app/(app)/settings/actions.ts
"use server";

import { createClient } from "@/lib/supabase/server";
import { revalidatePath } from "next/cache";

export async function uploadAvatar(formData: FormData) {
  const supabase = await createClient();
  const {
    data: { user },
  } = await supabase.auth.getUser();
  if (!user) throw new Error("Not authenticated");

  const file = formData.get("avatar") as File;
  const path = `${user.id}/avatar-${Date.now()}.${file.name.split(".").pop()}`;

  const { error } = await supabase.storage
    .from("avatars")
    .upload(path, file, { upsert: true, cacheControl: "3600" });
  if (error) throw error;

  const {
    data: { publicUrl },
  } = supabase.storage.from("avatars").getPublicUrl(path);

  await supabase.from("profiles").update({ avatar_url: publicUrl }).eq("id", user.id);
  revalidatePath("/settings");
}

5. Auth providers & redirect URLs

This is where most "it worked locally but breaks in prod" bugs live. In Authentication → URL Configuration:

  • Site URL: https://viby.app (your real production domain — used as the default redirect)
  • Redirect URLs (allow-list — Supabase rejects any redirect not listed): https://viby.app/** https://*-yourteam.vercel.app/** # Vercel preview deployments http://localhost:3000/** # local dev

For OAuth (say Google), enable the provider under Authentication → Providers, paste the Client ID/Secret from Google Cloud Console, and add Supabase's callback URL — https://YOUR_PROD_REF.supabase.co/auth/v1/callback — to Google's Authorized redirect URIs.

Your sign-in Server Action passes a redirectTo that must match the allow-list:

// app/(auth)/login/actions.ts
"use server";

import { createClient } from "@/lib/supabase/server";
import { redirect } from "next/navigation";
import { headers } from "next/headers";

export async function signInWithGoogle() {
  const supabase = await createClient();
  const origin = (await headers()).get("origin")!; // works in prod, preview, and local

  const { data, error } = await supabase.auth.signInWithOAuth({
    provider: "google",
    options: { redirectTo: `${origin}/auth/callback` },
  });
  if (error) throw error;
  if (data.url) redirect(data.url);
}

And the session refresh lives in proxy.ts (Next.js 16's middleware-style entry — not a middleware.ts):

// proxy.ts
import { createServerClient } from "@supabase/ssr";
import { NextResponse, type NextRequest } from "next/server";

export async function proxy(request: NextRequest) {
  let response = NextResponse.next({ request });

  const supabase = createServerClient(
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL!,
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY!,
    {
      cookies: {
        getAll() {
          return request.cookies.getAll();
        },
        setAll(cookiesToSet) {
          cookiesToSet.forEach(({ name, value }) => request.cookies.set(name, value));
          response = NextResponse.next({ request });
          cookiesToSet.forEach(({ name, value, options }) =>
            response.cookies.set(name, value, options)
          );
        },
      },
    }
  );

  await supabase.auth.getUser(); // refreshes the token cookie on every request
  return response;
}

export const config = {
  matcher: ["/((?!_next/static|_next/image|favicon.ico).*)"],
};

6. Connection pooling (only for direct Postgres access)

If you add Drizzle, Prisma, or a cron worker that opens raw Postgres connections, use the pooler connection string from Project Settings → Database → Connection string, not the direct 5432 one:

# Transaction-mode pooler (port 6543) — best for serverless / Vercel functions
DATABASE_URL="postgresql://postgres.YOUR_REF:PASSWORD@aws-0-REGION.pooler.supabase.com:6543/postgres?pgbouncer=true"

# Direct connection (port 5432) — use ONLY for migrations / long-lived workers
DIRECT_URL="postgresql://postgres.YOUR_REF:PASSWORD@aws-0-REGION.pooler.supabase.com:5432/postgres"

Transaction mode (6543) doesn't support prepared statements or session features, which is why the ?pgbouncer=true flag (and Prisma's separate directUrl for migrations) exists. The Viby app itself, using @supabase/ssr over HTTP, needs none of this — it's already pooled at the API layer.

7. Backups

On paid plans Supabase takes daily automated backups with Point-in-Time Recovery (PITR) available as an add-on — PITR lets you restore to any second, which is what you want the day someone fat-fingers a delete. Free tier projects can be paused and have limited backups, so production should be on at least the Pro plan. Verify under Project Settings → Database → Backups. Belt-and-suspenders: schedule a periodic pg_dump of critical tables to object storage you control, and test a restore before you need one — an untested backup is a rumor.

Architecture / diagram

flowchart TD
  subgraph Vercel["Vercel (Production)"]
    SC[Server Components / Server Actions]
    PX[proxy.ts session refresh]
  end

  subgraph SB["Supabase viby-prod"]
    API[PostgREST + Auth API<br/>pooled at HTTP layer]
    POOL[Supavisor / PgBouncer<br/>:6543 transaction mode]
    PG[(PostgreSQL)]
    ST[Storage buckets<br/>avatars / posts]
  end

  Browser -->|cookies| PX --> SC
  SC -->|anon key over HTTPS| API
  SC -->|signed uploads| ST
  API --> PG
  ST --> PG

  Worker[Cron / ORM worker] -->|raw Postgres| POOL --> PG

  GOOGLE[Google OAuth] -->|callback URL| API

Best practices & pitfalls

  • Do keep viby-dev and viby-prod as separate projects with separate keys. Don't ever run experiments against prod.
  • Do treat every schema change as a committed migration. Don't edit tables in the prod dashboard — it desyncs your migration history.
  • Do enable RLS on every table immediately (new tables ship with it off). A forgotten enable row level security is the #1 Supabase data leak.
  • Don't expose the service_role key to the browser — never prefix it with NEXT_PUBLIC_. It bypasses RLS entirely.
  • Do include preview-deployment URLs (https://*-yourteam.vercel.app/**) in the redirect allow-list, or OAuth silently fails on PR previews.
  • Do use the 6543 transaction pooler for any serverless direct-Postgres access; reserve 5432 for migrations. Don't run app traffic on 5432 — you'll exhaust connections.
  • Do put production on a plan with PITR and test a restore. Don't trust a backup you've never restored.
  • Do call supabase.auth.getUser() (verifies with the auth server) for authorization decisions, not getSession() (reads the cookie, spoofable).

Exercises

  1. Two-project hygiene. Create viby-prod, copy its three API values into Vercel's Production env scope, and confirm vercel env pull shows prod values differ from your .env.local.
  2. Migrate, don't click. Move Viby's full schema into supabase/migrations/, run db push against prod, then supabase db reset locally and confirm the app rebuilds identically from migrations alone.
  3. Storage lockdown. Write a policy so a user can delete only their own files in the posts bucket. Verify by trying (and failing) to delete another user's object with the anon key.
  4. Redirect allow-list. Deploy a PR to a Vercel preview URL, attempt Google sign-in, watch it fail, then add the wildcard preview pattern and confirm it works.
  5. Pooling proof (stretch). Add Drizzle to a cron route, wire it to the 6543 pooler, and load-test it; then switch to 5432 and observe too many connections under concurrency.

Mini-project — Production launch checklist for Viby

Build a one-time, admin-only /admin/health Server Component that verifies your production wiring at a glance. It should, on the server:

  • Confirm NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL points at the prod ref (not dev).
  • Query select count(*) from profiles to prove DB connectivity and RLS-as-service-role works.
  • List Storage buckets via supabase.storage.listBuckets() and assert avatars and posts exist and are public.
  • Render a green/red checklist (Tailwind v4 tokens, a little Framer Motion AnimatePresence for rows fading in) covering: env scope correct, RLS enabled on profiles/posts, redirect URLs configured (manual confirm checkbox), backups/PITR enabled (manual confirm).

Ship it behind an is_admin check in your profiles table. The goal: a single page you load right after deploy that tells you Viby's backend is truly production-ready — no guessing.

Key takeaways

  • Production = a separate, isolated Supabase project; never share keys or data with dev.
  • Migrations are the source of truth for schema — dashboard edits desync your history.
  • New tables ship with RLS off; turn it on everywhere, and never leak the service_role key.
  • Storage policies use the folder-prefix-equals-auth.uid() pattern to scope writes per user.
  • Auth breaks in prod when Site URL and the redirect allow-list (including preview URLs) aren't configured.
  • Use the 6543 transaction pooler for serverless direct-Postgres access; the @supabase/ssr HTTP client is already pooled.
  • Put prod on a plan with PITR backups — and test a restore before you need one.

Up next: Chapter 34 — Observability & Error Tracking: wiring Sentry, structured logs, and uptime alerts so you know the moment Viby misbehaves in production.

Chapter 34 — Monitoring, Logging & Analytics

You can't fix what you can't see — once Viby is live, your real users become your best (and most brutal) QA team.

What you'll learn

  • The three pillars of observability — logs, metrics, traces — and how they differ
  • Wiring Sentry into Next.js 16 for error tracking and performance monitoring
  • Reading Vercel's built-in Web Analytics and Speed Insights (Core Web Vitals)
  • Capturing meaningful product events (not vanity metrics) with PostHog
  • Doing privacy-respectful server-side event tracking from Server Actions
  • Building a lightweight ops dashboard from Supabase data
  • Closing the loop: turning a funnel drop-off into a shipped fix

Prerequisites

  • Chapter 9 (Server Components & Server Actions) — we'll instrument actions
  • Chapter 12 (proxy.ts request handling) — we'll tag requests there
  • Chapter 31 (Deploying to Vercel) — analytics ride on the deployment
  • Chapter 33 (Environment variables & secrets) — DSNs and keys live in env

Theory

When Viby ran on localhost, you were the monitoring system: you saw every error in the terminal, every slow render in DevTools. In production, thousands of strangers on flaky phones across the world hit your app and you see... nothing. Unless you build the nerves to feel it.

Observability is the property of a system that lets you ask new questions about its behavior without shipping new code. It rests on three pillars:

  • Logs — discrete, timestamped events. "User a1b2 failed to upload an avatar at 14:03." Like a diary: great for what exactly happened in one case.
  • Metrics — numbers aggregated over time. "Avatar uploads: p95 latency 1.2s, error rate 0.4%." Like a car dashboard: great for how healthy is the whole system right now.
  • Traces — the path of a single request across functions and services. "This signup took 900ms: 120ms in proxy.ts, 600ms in Supabase auth, 180ms rendering." Like a flight recorder: great for where the time went.

Sitting on top of these are two business-facing layers:

  • Error tracking turns raw exceptions into grouped, deduplicated, alertable issues with stack traces and user context. The difference between "something broke 4,000 times" and "this one null check broke for 4,000 users on iOS Safari."
  • Product analytics answers why people behave the way they do: Do new users find their first community? Where does the onboarding funnel leak?

A useful mental model: operational telemetry (errors, latency, uptime) tells you whether the machine is healthy; product analytics tells you whether the experience is working. You need both. A perfectly stable app that nobody completes onboarding in is still failing.

One rule above all: measure what changes a decision. "Total signups: 41,300" feels good but changes nothing. "Onboarding completion dropped from 68% to 51% after Tuesday's release" sends you to a specific PR. The first is a vanity metric; the second closes the loop.

In practice

1. Error tracking & performance with Sentry

Sentry is the industry standard for error tracking and has first-class Next.js support. Install and run the wizard, then we'll look at the files it expects.

npm install @sentry/nextjs
npx @sentry/wizard@latest -i nextjs

In Next.js 16's App Router, client and edge config live in instrumentation-client.ts and instrumentation.ts (the wizard wires next.config.ts for you). Here's a hand-tuned client config for Viby:

// instrumentation-client.ts
import * as Sentry from "@sentry/nextjs";

Sentry.init({
  dsn: process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SENTRY_DSN,
  // Sample 10% of transactions for perf monitoring in prod; 100% locally.
  tracesSampleRate: process.env.NODE_ENV === "production" ? 0.1 : 1.0,
  // Session Replay: record 10% of sessions, 100% of sessions WITH an error.
  replaysSessionSampleRate: 0.1,
  replaysOnErrorSampleRate: 1.0,
  integrations: [
    Sentry.replayIntegration({
      // Privacy first: never leak DMs or emails into a replay.
      maskAllText: true,
      blockAllMedia: true,
    }),
  ],
  environment: process.env.VERCEL_ENV ?? "development",
});

// Required in Next.js 16 so navigation transactions are captured.
export const onRouterTransitionStart = Sentry.captureRouterTransitionStart;

// instrumentation.ts — runs on the server & edge runtimes
import * as Sentry from "@sentry/nextjs";

export async function register() {
  if (process.env.NEXT_RUNTIME === "nodejs") {
    await import("./sentry.server.config");
  }
  if (process.env.NEXT_RUNTIME === "edge") {
    await import("./sentry.edge.config");
  }
}

export const onRequestError = Sentry.captureRequestError;

The onRequestError export is the magic hook: Next.js 16 calls it for every uncaught Server Component / Server Action / route handler error, so you get server stack traces automatically.

Now, context is everything. A naked stack trace is a riddle; a stack trace tagged with the user and the community they were viewing is a bug report. Identify the user once you know who they are:

// lib/observability/identify.ts
import * as Sentry from "@sentry/nextjs";
import type { User } from "@supabase/supabase-js";

export function identifyUser(user: User | null) {
  if (!user) {
    Sentry.setUser(null);
    return;
  }
  // Send the id — NEVER the email or phone. Privacy by default.
  Sentry.setUser({ id: user.id });
}

When you expect an error might happen (a third-party image host timing out, say), capture it explicitly instead of swallowing it:

// In a Viby avatar-upload Server Action
"use server";

import * as Sentry from "@sentry/nextjs";
import { createServerClient } from "@supabase/ssr";

export async function uploadAvatar(formData: FormData) {
  try {
    // ...supabase storage upload...
  } catch (error) {
    Sentry.captureException(error, {
      tags: { feature: "profile", action: "avatar_upload" },
    });
    return { ok: false, message: "Couldn't save your photo — try again." };
  }
}

2. Vercel Web Analytics & Speed Insights

Vercel gives you privacy-friendly traffic analytics and real-user Core Web Vitals (LCP, INP, CLS) with two small packages and zero cookies.

npm install @vercel/analytics @vercel/speed-insights

Drop both components into the root layout — they're Client Components that Vercel renders as tiny scripts:

// app/layout.tsx
import { Analytics } from "@vercel/analytics/next";
import { SpeedInsights } from "@vercel/speed-insights/next";

export default function RootLayout({
  children,
}: {
  children: React.ReactNode;
}) {
  return (
    <html lang="en">
      <body>
        {children}
        <Analytics />
        <SpeedInsights />
      </body>
    </html>
  );
}

That's it — deploy, and the Vercel dashboard fills with pageviews and field-data Web Vitals from your actual users (far more honest than a Lighthouse score on your fast laptop).

3. Product analytics with PostHog

For behavioral events we'll use PostHog. Initialize it client-side and expose a typed track helper so event names stay consistent — drifting event names ("signup", "Sign Up", "signed_up") are the silent killer of analytics.

// app/providers.tsx
"use client";

import posthog from "posthog-js";
import { PostHogProvider } from "posthog-js/react";
import { useEffect } from "react";

export function AnalyticsProvider({ children }: { children: React.ReactNode }) {
  useEffect(() => {
    posthog.init(process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_POSTHOG_KEY!, {
      api_host: "/ingest", // proxy through our own domain (see proxy.ts)
      person_profiles: "identified_only",
      capture_pageview: false, // App Router needs manual pageviews
    });
  }, []);

  return <PostHogProvider client={posthog}>{children}</PostHogProvider>;
}

// lib/analytics/events.ts — the single source of truth for event names
import posthog from "posthog-js";

// A closed union means a typo becomes a compile error, not a lost insight.
type VibyEvent =
  | { name: "onboarding_started"; props?: Record<string, never> }
  | { name: "interests_selected"; props: { count: number } }
  | { name: "community_joined"; props: { communityId: string; source: "search" | "suggested" } }
  | { name: "first_post_created"; props: { communityId: string } };

export function track<E extends VibyEvent>(event: E["name"], props?: E["props"]) {
  posthog.capture(event, props);
}

// Using it in a "Join community" button
"use client";

import { track } from "@/lib/analytics/events";

export function JoinButton({ communityId }: { communityId: string }) {
  return (
    <button
      className="rounded-full bg-brand px-5 py-2 font-semibold text-white"
      onClick={() => {
        track("community_joined", { communityId, source: "suggested" });
        // ...call the join Server Action...
      }}
    >
      Join
    </button>
  );
}

The bg-brand utility comes from our Tailwind v4 theme token defined in CSS:

/* app/globals.css */
@import "tailwindcss";

@theme {
  --color-brand: #ff6fb5;
}

4. Proxying analytics through your own domain

Ad-blockers eat requests to posthog.com, silently destroying 20–40% of your data. Route them through a first-party path using Viby's proxy.ts (Next.js 16's middleware-style file) so requests go to viby.app/ingest:

// proxy.ts
import { NextResponse, type NextRequest } from "next/server";

export function proxy(request: NextRequest) {
  if (request.nextUrl.pathname.startsWith("/ingest")) {
    const url = new URL(request.url);
    const target = "https://us.i.posthog.com";
    return NextResponse.rewrite(
      new URL(url.pathname.replace("/ingest", "") + url.search, target),
    );
  }
  return NextResponse.next();
}

export const config = {
  matcher: ["/ingest/:path*"],
};

5. Server-side events & structured logging

Some events should never depend on a browser — money, signups, and anything you'd hate to lose to an ad-blocker. Fire those from the server with posthog-node, and emit a structured JSON log line that Vercel's log drains can parse:

// lib/analytics/server.ts
import { PostHog } from "posthog-node";

const client = new PostHog(process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_POSTHOG_KEY!, {
  host: "https://us.i.posthog.com",
  flushAt: 1, // serverless is short-lived — don't batch
  flushInterval: 0,
});

export async function trackServer(
  distinctId: string,
  event: string,
  properties?: Record<string, unknown>,
) {
  client.capture({ distinctId, event, properties });
  await client.flush(); // ensure it sends before the function freezes
}

// lib/observability/log.ts — structured logs beat console.log("here")
type Level = "info" | "warn" | "error";

export function log(level: Level, message: string, meta: Record<string, unknown> = {}) {
  // One JSON object per line = queryable in Vercel/Datadog/Logflare.
  console[level === "error" ? "error" : "log"](
    JSON.stringify({ level, message, ts: new Date().toISOString(), ...meta }),
  );
}

// app/(auth)/actions.ts
"use server";

import { trackServer } from "@/lib/analytics/server";
import { log } from "@/lib/observability/log";

export async function completeOnboarding(userId: string, interestCount: number) {
  // ...persist to Supabase...
  await trackServer(userId, "onboarding_completed", { interestCount });
  log("info", "onboarding_completed", { userId, interestCount });
  return { ok: true };
}

Architecture / diagram

flowchart TD
  subgraph Client["Browser (React 19)"]
    UI["Viby UI<br/>track() + Sentry"]
  end
  subgraph Edge["Vercel Edge / proxy.ts"]
    PX["/ingest rewrite"]
  end
  subgraph Server["Server Actions / Route Handlers"]
    SA["completeOnboarding()<br/>trackServer() + log()"]
  end

  UI -->|behavioral events| PX --> PH[(PostHog<br/>product analytics)]
  UI -->|exceptions + replays| SN[(Sentry<br/>errors + perf)]
  UI -->|pageviews + Web Vitals| VA[(Vercel<br/>Analytics)]
  SA -->|server events| PH
  SA -->|JSON logs| LD[(Vercel Log Drain)]
  SA -->|onRequestError| SN

  PH --> DASH["Dashboards & Funnels"]
  SN --> ALERT["Alerts → Slack"]
  DASH --> DECIDE{"Decision:<br/>ship a fix"}
  ALERT --> DECIDE

The loop is the point: telemetry flows up from client and server into tools, tools surface dashboards and alerts, and those drive the next code change — which you then measure again.

Best practices & pitfalls

  • Do sample performance traces in production (tracesSampleRate: 0.1). 100% sampling at scale is expensive and rarely more insightful.
  • Do define event names in one typed file. Don't sprinkle raw string literals across components — typos silently fragment your funnels.
  • Don't log PII. No emails, phone numbers, DM contents, or full auth tokens in Sentry, PostHog, or logs. Send IDs and let your DB resolve the rest.
  • Do mask text and media in Session Replay. A replay that captures someone's private messages is a breach waiting to happen.
  • Don't rely on client-only events for critical funnels — ad-blockers and crashed tabs lose them. Mirror the load-bearing ones server-side.
  • Do proxy analytics through a first-party route, or accept losing a chunk of data to blockers.
  • Don't alert on everything — alert fatigue means real fires get ignored. Page a human only for user-facing breakage (error-rate spikes, p95 latency cliffs).
  • Do set flushAt: 1 for posthog-node in serverless; otherwise events vanish when the function freezes.
  • Don't confuse a Lighthouse score (lab) with Speed Insights (field). Optimize for the real-user numbers.

Exercises

  1. Tag the request. In proxy.ts, read the user-agent and attach an is_mobile boolean as a request header, then surface it as a Sentry tag. (Easy)
  2. Funnel it. Instrument the full onboarding flow — onboarding_started → interests_selected → community_joined → first_post_created — and build a PostHog funnel. Where's the biggest drop? (Medium)
  3. Custom Web Vital. Use the useReportWebVitals hook to send INP to PostHog as a custom event, so you can correlate poor responsiveness with specific pages. (Medium)
  4. Synthetic check. Write a Vercel Cron route handler that pings /api/health, and have it Sentry.captureMessage if Supabase is unreachable. (Hard)
  5. Cohort retention. Build a PostHog retention report answering: of users who community_joined, what fraction return and post within 7 days? (Hard)

Mini-project — The Viby Onboarding Health dashboard

Build an internal /admin/health page (gated to admins) that closes the loop end-to-end.

Requirements:

  1. Instrument the onboarding funnel with the four typed events from Exercise 2, mirroring onboarding_completed server-side via trackServer.
  2. Create a Supabase view onboarding_daily that aggregates, per day: signups, completed onboardings, and completion rate.
  3. Build a Server Component that reads the view and renders a simple bar chart of the last 14 days' completion rate.
  4. Wrap each bar in a Framer Motion motion.div with a staggered initial={{ scaleY: 0 }} → animate={{ scaleY: 1 }} entrance so the dashboard feels alive.
  5. Add a Sentry alert: if the onboarding Server Action's error rate exceeds 2% over 5 minutes, post to a Slack channel.

Stretch: annotate the chart with deploy timestamps (pull from the Vercel API) so a completion-rate dip lines up visibly with the release that caused it — the literal picture of closing the loop.

You'll know it works when you can answer, in one glance, "Is onboarding healthier or worse than last week, and did our last deploy help?"

Key takeaways

  • Observability stands on logs, metrics, and traces; error tracking and product analytics build the business view on top.
  • Sentry (instrumentation-client.ts + onRequestError) gives you grouped errors, performance, and replays across client and server.
  • Vercel Analytics + Speed Insights deliver cookieless traffic data and real-user Core Web Vitals for free.
  • PostHog with a single typed track() helper keeps event names clean; mirror critical events server-side and proxy through your own domain to survive ad-blockers.
  • Never log PII; sample traces; alert only on user-facing pain.
  • The whole point is the loop: measure → surface → decide → ship → measure again.

Up next — Chapter 35: Scaling & Performance Optimization, where we take everything these dashboards reveal and make Viby fast and cheap under real load.

Chapter 35 — MVP Strategy & Product Thinking

You don't ship a product. You ship a hypothesis — and then you go find out if you were right.

What you'll learn

  • What an MVP actually is (and the three things people mistake it for)
  • How to scope ruthlessly so you ship in weeks, not quarters
  • Problem/solution fit vs. product/market fit — and why the order matters
  • The Build–Measure–Learn loop, made concrete with instrumentation
  • Two prioritization frameworks you'll reach for constantly: RICE and MoSCoW
  • Exactly how Viby was scoped from a wishlist of 40 features down to a shippable core

Prerequisites

  • You've built Viby's core flows: auth (Chapter 11), profiles, the discovery feed, and Realtime chat (Chapters 22–28)
  • You're comfortable with Server Components, Server Actions, and the @supabase/ssr clients
  • Nice to have: the analytics groundwork from Chapter 31 — we'll lean on event tracking here

Theory

What an MVP really is

MVP stands for Minimum Viable Product. Every word is load-bearing, and most beginners get two of the three wrong.

  • Minimum — the smallest thing you can build. Not "small but polished everywhere." Smallest.
  • Viable — it must actually deliver the core value. A car with no engine is minimum, but it's not viable. A skateboard is.
  • Product — a real person uses it and gets a real outcome. A landing page that collects emails is a test, not yet a product.

The famous analogy (from Henrik Kniberg): if the goal is transportation, don't ship a wheel, then a chassis, then a half-car, then a car. Each intermediate step is useless — nobody can ride a chassis. Instead ship a skateboard, then a scooter, then a bike, then a motorbike, then a car. Every step is usable and teaches you something about how people actually want to move.

WRONG: wheel -> axle -> chassis -> car      (nothing works until the end)
RIGHT: skateboard -> scooter -> bike -> car (every step is rideable & teaches you)

An MVP is not:

  • A prototype (throwaway, internal, proves a technical risk)
  • A beta (a near-complete product, just earlier)
  • "Version 1 with fewer features" (that's just a smaller waterfall)

An MVP is: the smallest experiment that lets you learn whether real users get real value — before you've spent six months building the wrong thing.

Problem/solution fit comes before product/market fit

There are two milestones, in order:

  1. Problem/solution fit — "Do people actually have this problem, and does my approach plausibly solve it?" This is qualitative. You learn it from interviews, a clickable prototype, a concierge MVP.
  2. Product/market fit (PMF) — "Is there a market that pulls the product out of my hands?" This is quantitative: retention curves that flatten instead of decaying to zero, organic growth, people upset if you took it away.

The number-one startup killer is building a beautiful solution to a problem nobody has. For Viby, the problem hypothesis is sharp: "Adults past college find it genuinely hard to make new platonic friends who share their hobbies." Notice it's not dating — that's a deliberate scoping decision that shapes every feature.

The Build–Measure–Learn loop

Eric Ries's Lean Startup loop is the engine of product thinking:

        ┌──────────┐
        │  LEARN   │◀────────────┐
        └────┬─────┘             │
   (insights)│                   │(data)
        ┌────▼─────┐        ┌─────┴────┐
        │  BUILD   │───────▶│ MEASURE  │
        └──────────┘ (code) └──────────┘

The trick everyone misses: you go around the loop backwards when planning. Start from what you want to learn, decide what to measure to learn it, then build the minimum needed to generate that measurement. Building first and asking "what should we measure?" afterward is how you ship features that move nothing.

Each loop should produce a validated learning — a sentence like: "Users who join a Circle in their first session retain at 3x the rate of those who don't." That sentence changes your roadmap. A loop that produces only "we shipped it" produced nothing.

Prioritization: turning opinions into decisions

When everyone in the room has a favorite feature, you need a shared scoring rubric so the loudest voice doesn't win.

RICE scores each idea: (Reach × Impact × Confidence) / Effort.

  • Reach — how many users per quarter does it touch?
  • Impact — per-user effect (3 = massive, 1 = medium, 0.25 = minimal)
  • Confidence — how sure are you? (100% / 80% / 50%)
  • Effort — person-weeks

MoSCoW buckets scope for a single release: Must / Should / Could / Won't. The discipline is the Won't column — writing down what you're explicitly not doing this cycle is the whole point.

In practice

Theory is worthless without instrumentation. An MVP you can't measure is just a guess with a deploy URL. Let's wire the Measure half of the loop into Viby.

1. A typed, minimal analytics event

Keep your event vocabulary tiny and intentional. For the MVP you might track only a handful of events — the ones tied to your core learning question.

// lib/analytics/events.ts
export type VibyEvent =
  | { name: "signup_completed"; props: { method: "email" | "oauth" } }
  | { name: "circle_joined"; props: { circleId: string; hobby: string } }
  | { name: "match_messaged"; props: { matchId: string } }
  | { name: "discovery_card_swiped"; props: { direction: "save" | "skip" } };

export type EventName = VibyEvent["name"];

2. Record events with a Server Action

Write events to Supabase from a Server Action. Note the @supabase/ssr createServerClient with getAll/setAll cookie handlers — the modern, correct API.

// app/actions/track.ts
"use server";

import { cookies } from "next/headers";
import { createServerClient } from "@supabase/ssr";
import type { VibyEvent } from "@/lib/analytics/events";

export async function trackEvent(event: VibyEvent) {
  // Next.js 16: cookies() is async — await it.
  const cookieStore = await cookies();

  const supabase = createServerClient(
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL!,
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY!,
    {
      cookies: {
        getAll: () => cookieStore.getAll(),
        setAll: (cookiesToSet) => {
          cookiesToSet.forEach(({ name, value, options }) =>
            cookieStore.set(name, value, options)
          );
        },
      },
    }
  );

  const {
    data: { user },
  } = await supabase.auth.getUser();

  await supabase.from("analytics_events").insert({
    user_id: user?.id ?? null,
    name: event.name,
    props: event.props,
  });
}

3. Fire it from a client component

The discovery feed is a client component (it needs gestures via Framer Motion). When a user saves or skips a card, we record it — that swipe ratio is a measurement feeding the loop.

// components/discovery/swipe-card.tsx
"use client";

import { motion, useMotionValue, useTransform } from "framer-motion";
import { trackEvent } from "@/app/actions/track";

export function SwipeCard({ profile }: { profile: { id: string; name: string } }) {
  const x = useMotionValue(0);
  const rotate = useTransform(x, [-200, 200], [-12, 12]);
  const opacity = useTransform(x, [-200, 0, 200], [0.4, 1, 0.4]);

  function onDragEnd(_: unknown, info: { offset: { x: number } }) {
    if (Math.abs(info.offset.x) < 120) return; // not a decisive swipe
    const direction = info.offset.x > 0 ? "save" : "skip";
    // Fire-and-forget: measurement must never block the interaction.
    void trackEvent({ name: "discovery_card_swiped", props: { direction } });
  }

  return (
    <motion.div
      style={{ x, rotate, opacity }}
      drag="x"
      dragConstraints={{ left: 0, right: 0 }}
      onDragEnd={onDragEnd}
      className="rounded-3xl bg-white p-6 shadow-lg"
    >
      <h3 className="text-xl font-semibold text-[--color-brand]">{profile.name}</h3>
    </motion.div>
  );
}

4. The brand token, defined the Tailwind v4 way

That [--color-brand] resolves to a token defined in CSS — no tailwind.config.js in Tailwind v4.

/* app/globals.css */
@import "tailwindcss";

@theme {
  --color-brand: #ff6fb5; /* Viby bubblegum pink */
  --color-brand-ink: #1f2937;
}

5. Read the funnel back

The point of measuring is to learn. A simple Server Component dashboard turns raw events into the activation funnel that tells you whether the MVP works.

// app/(admin)/funnel/page.tsx  — Server Component, no "use client"
import { cookies } from "next/headers";
import { createServerClient } from "@supabase/ssr";

export default async function FunnelPage() {
  const cookieStore = await cookies();
  const supabase = createServerClient(
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL!,
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY!,
    {
      cookies: {
        getAll: () => cookieStore.getAll(),
        setAll: () => {}, // read-only page; no cookies to write
      },
    }
  );

  // One RPC that returns counts per funnel step (defined in SQL).
  const { data: funnel } = await supabase.rpc("activation_funnel");

  return (
    <div className="mx-auto max-w-md space-y-3 p-6">
      <h1 className="text-2xl font-bold text-[--color-brand]">Activation funnel</h1>
      {funnel?.map((step: { label: string; count: number }) => (
        <div key={step.label} className="flex justify-between rounded-xl bg-pink-50 p-4">
          <span>{step.label}</span>
          <span className="font-mono">{step.count}</span>
        </div>
      ))}
    </div>
  );
}

Gate this admin route in proxy.ts (Viby's middleware-style file from Chapter 18) so only staff session cookies reach /funnel. Don't ship a public dashboard of your raw metrics.

Architecture / diagram

How Viby's MVP scope maps onto the Build–Measure–Learn loop:

flowchart TD
    H[Hypothesis: hobby-based Circles drive friendship] --> B[BUILD: auth + profile + Circles + 1:1 chat]
    B --> U[Real users use Viby]
    U --> M[MEASURE: signup -> circle_joined -> match_messaged]
    M --> D[(analytics_events in Supabase)]
    D --> F[activation_funnel RPC]
    F --> L{LEARN: does joining a Circle lift retention?}
    L -->|Yes, double down| H
    L -->|No, pivot scope| H
    style H fill:#ff6fb5,color:#fff
    style L fill:#ff6fb5,color:#fff

The loop is a cycle, not a line. Each pass tightens the hypothesis or kills it.

Best practices & pitfalls

Do

  • Write your learning goal before you write code. "We'll learn X by measuring Y."
  • Keep an explicit Won't-do-this-cycle list. Saying no is the job.
  • Instrument the core funnel on day one — retroactive analytics is guesswork.
  • Ship to real users fast, even 10 of them. Ten engaged users beat a thousand imaginary ones.
  • Measure retention, not just signups. A flattening retention curve is the only real signal of PMF.

Don't

  • Don't confuse "minimum" with "low quality." The slice you ship must feel premium — just narrow.
  • Don't build features because a competitor has them. Build for your hypothesis.
  • Don't gold-plate the parts no one has validated (Viby's video calls, gamified badges — all v2).
  • Don't let analytics block the UI. Track fire-and-forget (void trackEvent(...)).
  • Don't ship a metrics dashboard publicly or skip auth on /funnel.
  • Don't mistake activity (we shipped 8 features!) for progress (we learned 1 true thing).

Exercises

  1. Scope the cut. List 10 features you'd want in Viby. Sort them into MoSCoW buckets for a 3-week MVP. Defend your "Won't" column in two sentences each.
  2. RICE a feature. Pick "voice notes in chat." Estimate Reach, Impact, Confidence, Effort and compute its RICE score. Compare it to "profile photo cropping." Which ships first?
  3. Add an event. Extend the VibyEvent union with profile_completed and fire it from the profile form's Server Action. Keep the type exhaustive.
  4. Write the learning sentence. For the discovery swipe data, write the one-sentence validated learning you'd hope to extract, and the SQL query that would confirm or deny it.
  5. (Stretch) Build a retention_cohort Supabase RPC that buckets users by signup week and reports week-1 return rate. Render it in a Server Component.

Mini-project: The Viby "One-Metric Dashboard"

Build a single Server Component page, /insights, that answers one question: Do users who join a Circle in their first session come back?

Steps

  1. Create the analytics_events table in Supabase with columns id, user_id, name, props jsonb, created_at default now(), and Row Level Security allowing inserts for authenticated users.
  2. Wire trackEvent (above) into your signup action (signup_completed) and Circle join action (circle_joined).
  3. Write a SQL function circle_retention() returning two numbers: 7-day return rate for users who fired circle_joined in session one vs. those who didn't.
  4. Render both numbers side by side with the Viby pink token, plus a one-line verdict: "Joining a Circle lifts week-1 retention by N%."
  5. Protect /insights in proxy.ts.

Definition of done: the page renders a single comparison and a plain-English verdict. If the verdict surprises you, congratulations — you just closed a Build–Measure–Learn loop.

Key takeaways

  • An MVP is the smallest experiment that delivers real value and produces validated learning — not a small waterfall.
  • Chase problem/solution fit first, then product/market fit; retention is your truest PMF signal.
  • Plan the Build–Measure–Learn loop backwards: learn → measure → build the minimum.
  • Use RICE to rank ideas and MoSCoW to scope a release; the Won't column is sacred.
  • Viby was scoped to one hypothesis — hobby-based Circles create friendships — and everything off that path (dating, video, badges) was deliberately deferred.
  • Instrument from day one; activity is not progress.

Up next — Chapter 36: Growth, Analytics & Retention Engineering, where we turn these raw events into cohort retention curves, activation metrics, and the growth loops that make Viby spread.

Chapter 36 — Growth, Retention, Scaling & Engineering Workflows

You shipped a beautiful app. Now let's make it grow, keep the people who join, and survive the day 50,000 of them show up at once.

This is the chapter where Viby stops being a project and starts behaving like a startup. Code quality got you to launch. Growth loops, retention discipline, and a calm engineering workflow get you to traction.

What you'll learn

  • Growth loops & network effects — why some products compound and most don't, and how to design loops into Viby.
  • Activation & retention metrics — the North Star, the AARRR funnel, cohort retention curves, and how to instrument them.
  • Virality — the viral coefficient (k-factor), invite loops, and why "share" buttons usually fail.
  • Scaling — the product (database, realtime, caching) and the team (ownership, on-call).
  • Modern startup engineering workflows — trunk-based git, fast reviews, CI/CD on Vercel, and feature flags so you can ship daily without fear.

Prerequisites

  • Chapters 1–10: the Next.js 16 App Router, Server Components, and TypeScript foundations.
  • Chapter 14–18: Supabase Auth, PostgreSQL, RLS, and Realtime.
  • Chapter 28: Viby's "Find your people" discovery feed and the hobby-match graph.
  • Chapter 33–35: deployment to Vercel and basic observability.

Theory

Growth loops beat funnels

A funnel is linear: an ad sends a stranger in one end, a fraction trickles out the other as a user. You pay for every new person. A growth loop is circular: the output of using the product becomes the input that brings the next person.

Analogy: a funnel is a bucket you keep pouring water into. A loop is a sourdough starter — feed it once and it keeps making more of itself.

Viby's natural loop:

A new user joins → finds a hobby circle (e.g. "Bouldering Berlin")
→ posts a meetup → invites 2 friends who also boulder
→ those friends join, see the circle is alive → they post → invite more

Every active user manufactures the reason for the next user to join. That's a loop. Funnels you buy; loops you build.

Network effects (the moat)

A network effect means the product gets more valuable as more people use it. Viby's value is "find people near you who share your hobby." With 3 users in your city, that's worthless. With 3,000, it's magic. This is why social-discovery is hard to start (the cold-start problem) but defensible once warm — a competitor with better code but no users still loses.

Beat cold-start by going narrow and dense: don't launch "everyone, everywhere." Launch one hobby in one city until it's saturated, then expand. Density, not breadth, creates the magic moment.

Activation, retention, and the North Star

  • Activation is the moment a new user first feels the value — the "aha." For Viby it's not signup; it's joining a circle and seeing it's active. Define it precisely, because everything optimizes toward it.
  • Retention is whether they come back. It's the single best predictor of a real business. A leaky bucket can't be filled by more marketing.
  • North Star Metric (NSM): the one number that captures delivered value. For Viby: Weekly Connected Users — users who had a real interaction (joined a circle, RSVP'd, or messaged) in the last 7 days. Not signups, not page views — value delivered.

The classic frame is AARRR ("pirate metrics"): Acquisition → Activation → Retention → Referral → Revenue.

Virality and the k-factor

The viral coefficient k = (invites sent per user) × (conversion rate per invite).

  • k > 1: each user brings more than one new user → exponential growth (rare, magical).
  • k < 1: invites amplify other channels but don't sustain growth alone — still hugely valuable.

A "Share to Instagram" button is almost always k ≈ 0. What works is an invite that is load-bearing: the recipient gets something real (a pre-joined circle, a friend already inside). Make the invite part of using the product, not a bolt-on.

Scaling — product and team

  • Product: add database indexes before you need them, push reads to caches, keep Realtime channels scoped, and move slow work to background jobs.
  • Team: as you grow past ~3 engineers, informal coordination breaks. You need clear ownership, small PRs, automated checks, and a deploy process anyone can run safely at 5pm on a Friday.

In practice

1. Instrument activation with a typed event helper

Track events from a single, typed module so naming stays consistent. We log to a Supabase events table (cheap, queryable) and could fan out to a product-analytics tool later.

-- supabase/migrations/0042_events.sql
create table public.events (
  id          bigint generated always as identity primary key,
  user_id     uuid references auth.users (id) on delete set null,
  name        text not null,
  props       jsonb not null default '{}'::jsonb,
  created_at  timestamptz not null default now()
);

create index events_name_created_idx on public.events (name, created_at desc);
create index events_user_idx on public.events (user_id, created_at desc);

alter table public.events enable row level security;

-- Users may insert their own events; reads are server/service-role only.
create policy "insert own events" on public.events
  for insert to authenticated
  with check (auth.uid() = user_id);

A Server Action is the clean way to record events — it runs on the server, so the service context and cookies are already available.

// app/_actions/track.ts
"use server";

import { createServerClient } from "@supabase/ssr";
import { cookies } from "next/headers";

export type VibyEvent =
  | "signup_completed"
  | "circle_joined"      // our activation event
  | "meetup_rsvp"
  | "message_sent"
  | "invite_sent"
  | "invite_accepted";

export async function track(name: VibyEvent, props: Record<string, unknown> = {}) {
  const cookieStore = await cookies();

  const supabase = createServerClient(
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL!,
    process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY!,
    {
      cookies: {
        getAll: () => cookieStore.getAll(),
        setAll: (toSet) =>
          toSet.forEach(({ name, value, options }) =>
            cookieStore.set(name, value, options),
          ),
      },
    },
  );

  const {
    data: { user },
  } = await supabase.auth.getUser();

  await supabase.from("events").insert({ user_id: user?.id ?? null, name, props });
}

Fire activation right where it happens — when a user joins a circle:

// app/circles/[id]/_actions/join.ts
"use server";

import { revalidatePath } from "next/cache";
import { track } from "@/app/_actions/track";
import { getServerClient } from "@/lib/supabase/server";

export async function joinCircle(circleId: string) {
  const supabase = await getServerClient();
  const { error } = await supabase
    .from("circle_members")
    .insert({ circle_id: circleId });

  if (error) return { ok: false as const, error: error.message };

  await track("circle_joined", { circleId }); // ← activation moment
  revalidatePath(`/circles/${circleId}`);
  return { ok: true as const };
}

2. Measure retention with a cohort query

Retention is best read as a cohort curve: group users by the week they signed up, then see what fraction were "connected" in each later week.

-- Weekly retention by signup cohort
with cohorts as (
  select id as user_id, date_trunc('week', created_at) as cohort_week
  from auth.users
),
activity as (
  select distinct user_id, date_trunc('week', created_at) as active_week
  from public.events
  where name in ('circle_joined', 'meetup_rsvp', 'message_sent')
)
select
  c.cohort_week,
  count(distinct c.user_id)                                   as cohort_size,
